### [1] [RENDER: Controlling Reader-Facing Evidence in LLM Memory Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23568)
*Yuan Si,Simeng Han,Daming Li,Jialu Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RENDER is a benchmark control that isolates the effect of the reader-facing artifact (how memory/RAG content is rendered in the input) from the underlying conversation history. On LongMemEval questions and nine models, it shows that artifact format dramatically affects performance—resolved packets beat recency-truncated raw dialogue by 42.4–72.6 points, with up to 48.8-point spread between templates within a single model, and some models scoring 0% on formal ledger packets yet 45–53% on natural-language entries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Memory and RAG evaluations treat the answering model's input rendering as an implementation detail, even though the same history can be presented as memory entries, summaries, typed records, or raw excerpts. This overlooks the possibility that the reader-facing artifact format itself significantly influences model performance.

Method: RENDER combines a five-level "packet ladder" that localizes when answer-bearing content enters the input, with deterministic templates approximating four real-world artifact types: ChatGPT-style entries, LangChain summaries, MemGPT-style typed records, and raw conversation. It is evaluated on 500 LongMemEval questions across nine models, plus HotpotQA transfer and retrieval noise conditions.

Result: Matched-budget resolved packets beat recency-truncated raw dialogue by 42.4–72.6 points. Best-worst template spread is 24.6–48.8 points per model. ChatGPT-style entries outperformed raw conversation on 7 of 9 models under the primary scorer. Three models scoring 0% on formal ledger packets answered correctly from natural-language entries at 45.4–53.4%. Effects persist under retrieval noise and transfer to HotpotQA.

Conclusion: Memory and RAG evaluations should report or control the reader-facing artifact, as it has a substantial and systematic effect on model performance that is independent of the underlying conversation content.

Abstract: Memory and RAG evaluations often treat the answering model's input as an implementation detail, even though systems may render the same history as a memory entry, summary, typed record, or raw excerpt. We introduce RENDER, a benchmark control that fixes the conversation while varying the reader-facing artifact. RENDER combines a five-level packet ladder, localizing when answer-bearing content enters the input, with deterministic templates approximating ChatGPT-style entries, LangChain summaries, MemGPT-style typed records, and raw conversation. On 500 LongMemEval questions and nine models, matched-budget resolved packets beat recency-truncated raw dialogue by 42.4-72.6 points. In deployed-style templates, best-worst spread is 24.6-48.8 points per model; under the primary scorer, ChatGPT-style entries have higher point estimates than raw conversation on 7 of 9 models. Judge rescoring preserves the positive aggregate effect, but model-specific significance is mixed. Three models scoring 0 percent on formal ledger packets answer the same facts from natural-language entries at 45.4-53.4 percent. The effect persists under retrieval noise and transfers to HotpotQA, suggesting that memory/RAG evaluations should report or control the reader-facing artifact.

</details>


### [2] [ESQ-Bench: A Multi-Tier Enterprise Oracle Benchmark for Evaluating NL2SQL Dialect Generalization and Silent Semantic Divergence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23569)
*Sanjay Mishra,Divya Chukkapalli,Ganesh R. Naik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ESQ-Bench is a new Oracle-first NL2SQL benchmark with 465 tables, 550 gold-validated query-question pairs, and silent-divergence evaluation across three schema complexity tiers. It reveals that state-of-the-art models like GPT-4o and Claude Sonnet 4.6 degrade significantly on enterprise schemas (EX 57-80% for GPT-4o, 68-87% for Claude), with EM below 7%, silent divergence reaching 73-99%, and open-weight Llama 3.2 performing poorly at 13.3%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing NL2SQL benchmarks like Spider and BIRD report high accuracy (89%+) but rely on simplified academic schemas and open-source SQL dialects that do not reflect the complexity of enterprise database environments, particularly Oracle.

Method: The authors construct ESQ-Bench with six populated schemas (465 tables, 164,682 rows) on four SQL dialects (Oracle, PostgreSQL, MySQL, SQL Server), a four-metric evaluation harness (EM, EX, SR, SD), and 550 gold-validated question-query pairs across three systematic complexity tiers.

Result: GPT-4o schema-linked shows monotonic EX degradation (79.8/60.3/57.2%) across tiers; EM stays below 7%; silent divergence reaches 73-99% among EX-passing queries. Claude Sonnet 4.6 outperforms GPT-4o on every tier (87.4/74.9/68.7%). GPT-4o zero-shot inverts schema-linked results at Tiers 2-3 due to survivor bias. Llama 3.2 only achieves 13.3% EX.

Conclusion: Enterprise NL2SQL performance remains far below benchmark-reported numbers, with silent divergence being a critical underexplored failure mode. Closed API models significantly outperform open-weight models on enterprise Oracle schemas.

Abstract: State-of-the-art Natural Language to SQL (NL2SQL) models report execution accuracy exceeding 89 percent on established benchmarks such as Spider and BIRD. However, these benchmarks rely on simplified academic schemas and open-source SQL dialects that do not reflect the complexity of enterprise database environments. We introduce ESQ-Bench, an Oracle-first NL2SQL benchmark with systematic complexity tiers and silent-divergence evaluation across three enterprise schema complexity tiers. We constructed and released six populated schemas (465 tables, 164,682 rows, zero empty tables) with identical seed data on Oracle, PostgreSQL, MySQL, and SQL Server, a four-metric evaluation harness (EM, EX, SR, SD), and 550 gold-validated question-query pairs (Tier-1: 95; Tier-2: 228; Tier-3: 227). Schema-linked prompting with GPT-4o shows monotonic execution-match degradation across tiers: 79.8, 60.3, and 57.2 percent EX on executed queries (June 2026), versus 75.6, 80.4, and 95.8 percent on an earlier 142-question pilot slice. EM stays below 7 percent tier-wide; operational silent-divergence reaches 73 to 99 percent among EX-passing queries. Failure analysis shows wrong-result semantics dominate at higher tiers. Claude Sonnet 4.6 with schema-linked prompts reaches 87.4, 74.9, and 68.7 percent EX (executed queries), exceeding GPT-4o schema-linked on every tier. GPT-4o zero-shot EX on executed queries (78.7, 73.5, and 77.8 percent) inverts schema-linked at Tiers 2 to 3 due to lower execution rates and survivor bias in the zero-shot versus schema-linked analysis. Local Llama 3.2 schema-linked reaches only 13.3 percent bank-wide EX (73 out of 550), underscoring the gap between closed API models and open-weight baselines on enterprise Oracle schemas.

</details>


### [3] [LLM Agents Perform Controlled Experiments Using Simulation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23622)
*Yuchen Xia,Michael Weyrich,Nasser Jazdi,Johannes Stümpfle,Johannes Sigel,Akshay Narla,Gavin K. Reynolds,Anna Jawor-Baczynska,Pol Llopart*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a multi-agent framework that integrates LLMs with high-fidelity scientific simulation models, enabling agents to conduct controlled experiments for pharmaceutical process design. The system translates user queries into structured tasks, designs and executes comparative simulations, and synthesizes evidence-based parameter optimization recommendations, outperforming language-only reasoning in output specificity and user-rated correctness/helpfulness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs excel at text and code generation but fall short in tasks requiring understanding of how systems respond to interventions—a capability that depends on controlled experimentation. Many scientific and engineering tasks in pharmaceutical process design require this experimental reasoning, which language-only models cannot adequately provide.

Method: The authors propose a multi-agent framework that couples LLMs with high-fidelity simulation models. Given a user query and baseline configuration, the system: (1) constructs a structured task representation, (2) designs experiments, (3) executes comparative simulation, (4) interprets resulting outcomes, and (5) synthesizes actionable recommendations for process parameter optimization. This enables reasoning through intervention, comparison, and observation within an interactive agent architecture.

Result: The proposed system produces more specific and actionable outputs than language-only reasoning. In an industrial pharmaceutical application, it demonstrated higher output specificity and improved user-rated correctness and helpfulness. Ablation studies and visualized case analyses further validated its effectiveness and practical utility.

Conclusion: Integrating simulation models with LLM agents significantly enhances experimental reasoning capabilities for scientific process design. The simulation-integrated framework produces more specific, correct, and helpful recommendations compared to language-only approaches, highlighting the value of grounding LLM reasoning in high-fidelity simulation environments for industrial applications.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong capabilities in reasoning, planning, and tool use, but many scientific and engineering tasks require more than plausible text and code generation. They require understanding how a system responds to intervention, which in practice depends on controlled experimentation. In this work, we propose a multi-agent framework that enables LLM agents to conduct controlled experiments with scientific simulation models for pharmaceutical process design. Given a user query and a baseline configuration, the system constructs a structured task representation, designs experiments, executes comparative simulation, interprets the resulting outcomes, and synthesizes evidence-based recommendations for process parameter optimization. By coupling language models with high-fidelity simulation models in an interactive agent framework, the proposed system supports reasoning through intervention, comparison, and observation. As a result, it produces more specific and actionable outputs than language-only reasoning. In an industrial application setting, this advantage is reflected in higher output specificity as well as improved user-rated correctness and helpfulness. Ablation studies and visualized case analyses further demonstrate the effectiveness and practical utility of simulation-integrated experimental reasoning.

</details>


### [4] [A survey detection channel overrides the pixels in an astronomical foundation model, and biases tomographic mean redshifts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23626)
*Ihor Kendiukhov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Foundation models in astronomy trained on survey pixels and their derived catalogues inherit the measurable incompleteness of those catalogues as a systematic bias. Auditing AION-1, a 39-modality transformer, via causal interventions reveals that detection gating (presence at field centre) rather than enclosed light drives model outputs, with catalogue incompleteness shifting tomographic mean redshifts up to 8.3 times LSST DESC requirements. Further limitations include tokeniser codec resolution disparities and unreliable sparse dictionary causal handles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Astronomical foundation models trained jointly on survey pixels and catalogue products risk inheriting the incompleteness of those catalogues as a systematic bias. This paper aims to audit and quantify the extent to which this incompleteness propagates into model predictions, which matters for scientific analyses that depend on such models.

Method: The authors perform causal interventions on AION-1's inputs, holding image tokens byte-identical while editing only the survey segmentation map. They also test contradicting catalogue photometry, propagate the real pipeline incompleteness rate (3.68%), and examine the tokeniser's codec resolution limits.

Result: Editing only the segmentation map changes all reported quantities by 110-4400 times a placebo. The mechanism is detection gating (r=0.47 at field centre) rather than enclosed light (r=0.30), and partition of blended light is ignored (R=-0.006). Contradicted photometry leaves the model 9x worse than no metadata. The 3.68% incompleteness shifts tomographic redshifts by 0.71x the LSST DESC requirement (median), exceeding it in 12 of 40 assignments and reaching 8.3x in the worst bin. The image codec resolves only 28 effective states vs 934 for spectrum, redshift readout is quantisation-limited, and sparse dictionary recovery spans 26-75% with up to 18-point seed sensitivity.

Conclusion: Detection-channel incompleteness is a dominant systematic in AION-1 that propagates into cosmological quantities such as tomographic redshifts. Withholding the detection channel removes the effect at no measurable cost, and spectroscopy removes it entirely. Future work should address tokeniser codec limitations and sparse dictionary reliability.

Abstract: Foundation models for astronomy are trained on survey pixels together with the catalogue products derived from those pixels. Those catalogues are incomplete at a measurable rate, and a model trained on both inherits that incompleteness as a systematic. We audit AION-1, a 39-modality transformer trained on more than 200 million objects, using causal interventions on its inputs.
  Holding the image tokens byte-identical and editing only the survey segmentation map changes every quantity the model reports -- flux, size, ellipticity, redshift -- by 110-4400 times a matched placebo. The mechanism is detection gating, presence at the field centre (r = 0.47), not the light the mask encloses (r = 0.30); across 322 real blends the model ignores how the pipeline partitioned the light (R = -0.006). Nor is the preference specific to that channel: contradicted catalogue photometry leaves the model nine times worse than supplying no metadata at all.
  The Legacy Survey pipeline leaves 3.68% of targets with no segment covering their position. Propagating that rate, with a miss represented by the fields the pipeline actually returns, shifts tomographic mean redshifts by a median 0.71 times the LSST DESC requirement over 40 assignments and exceeds it in 12; observed positional errors take the worst bin to 8.3 times. Drawing the misses by their measured magnitude dependence rather than uniformly does not change it. Spectroscopy removes the effect, withholding the detection channel removes it at no measurable cost, and the effect grows with model scale.
  Two further limits lie in the tokeniser: its image codec resolves 28 effective states on source patches against 934 for the spectrum codec, and the redshift readout is quantisation-limited. Sparse dictionaries are unreliable causal handles: across 15, recovery spans 26-75% and moves up to 18 points on the seed alone.

</details>


### [5] [TRACE: Transition-Aware Residual Control for Multi-Objective Materials Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23631)
*Kang Zhou,Yujia Tong,Yong Tao,Jingling Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRACE is a transition-aware residual control framework for multi-objective materials discovery that treats evaluated edits as the basic feedback unit, records parent-edit-child transitions with property deltas, and ranks future edits by their ability to reduce constraint violations while avoiding damage to satisfied objectives, improving hit rate from 18.13% to 25.96% over LLEMA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-agent-based materials discovery methods store evaluated candidates and scores but not which executable edits caused useful property changes, limiting local refinement when objectives compete and an edit improving one property may damage another.

Method: TRACE records each local refinement as a parent-edit-child transition with observed property deltas, aggregates transition evidence to estimate reusable edit effects, and ranks future edits by predicted ability to reduce the current candidate's remaining constraint violations while avoiding damage to already satisfied objectives.

Result: In a controlled same-backbone comparison, TRACE improves over LLEMA, raising macro-average hit rate from 18.13% to 25.96%.

Conclusion: Transition-aware feedback based on edit-level deltas enables more effective multi-objective materials discovery than score-only candidate evaluation with LLM agents.

Abstract: Multi-objective materials discovery with LLM agents is often limited not only by how many candidates can be proposed, but by how effectively each costly property evaluation informs the next search step. Existing agents mainly store evaluated candidates and their scores, so they know which materials succeeded but not which executable edits caused useful property changes. This makes local refinement difficult when objectives compete and an edit that improves one property may damage another. We propose TRACE, a transition-aware residual control framework that treats evaluated edits as the basic unit of feedback. TRACE records each local refinement as a parent-edit-child transition with observed property deltas, aggregates transition evidence to estimate reusable edit effects, and ranks future edits by their predicted ability to reduce the current candidate's remaining constraint violations while avoiding damage to already satisfied objectives. In a controlled same-backbone comparison, TRACE improves over LLEMA, the state-of-the-art LLM-agent baseline, raising macro-average hit rate from 18.13\% to 25.96\%.

</details>


### [6] [Function-Level Execution Feedback for Code Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23632)
*Idris Nechnech,Sehwan Kim,Jimin Seo,Yeongoon Kim,Minhae Oh,Sangwoo Hong,Jungwoo Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: STEP-KTODER is a framework for code preference optimization that defines process-supervision "steps" as module-level functions in decomposed programs, labels them via auto-generated unit tests, and combines function-level process supervision with outcome-level feedback through stepwise KTO. It outperforms outcome-only KTO and DPO on HumanEval(+), MBPP(+), BigCodeBench, and LiveCodeBench, and shows execution-based labels are crucial since LLM-as-a-judge annotations over-predict failures and degrade optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Process supervision has been effective for mathematical reasoning where steps map naturally to chains of thought, but remains underexplored in code generation because there is no standard notion of a step. Different supervision targets (lines, traces, program states) make it unclear what to label and optimize.

Method: STEP-KTODER defines steps as module-level functions in decomposed multi-function programs, assigns binary correctness labels to each function via automatically generated unit tests, and performs a code-specific instantiation of stepwise KTO that integrates function-level process supervision with outcome-level program feedback.

Result: STEP-KTODER improves over outcome-only KTO and DPO baselines across HumanEval(+), MBPP(+), BigCodeBench, and LiveCodeBench. Analysis reveals execution-based labels are essential: LLM-as-a-judge annotations systematically over-predict function failures, corrupt positive step labels, and degrade downstream preference optimization.

Conclusion: Defining code-generation process supervision through executable module-level functions with automated unit-test labels is a viable and superior alternative to outcome-only preference optimization. Execution-based step labels are critical for reliable process supervision in code.

Abstract: Process supervision has improved mathematical reasoning, where intermediate steps are naturally expressed as chains of thought. In code generation, however, process supervision remains underexplored because there is no standard notion of a step. Supervision can target lines, reasoning traces, or program states, making it unclear what to label and optimize. We propose STEP-KTODER, a framework for code preference optimization that defines steps as module-level functions in decomposed multi-function programs and assigns binary correctness labels via automatically generated unit tests. Our method provides a code-specific instantiation of stepwise KTO, combining function-level process supervision with outcome-level feedback on the full program. We evaluate on HumanEval(+), MBPP(+), BigCodeBench, and LiveCodeBench, showing that STEP-KTODER improves over outcome-only KTO and DPO. Further analysis shows that execution-based labels are essential: LLM-as-a-judge annotations systematically over-predict function failures, corrupt positive step labels, and degrade downstream preference optimization. Code is available at: https://github.com/inechnech/STEP-KTODER.

</details>


### [7] [Auditing the Synthetic Memoir: Measuring Scene-Level Confabulation in LLM-Generated Autobiography Against the Documented Record of the Life It Describes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23640)
*Heather Renze*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A quantified scene-level audit of LLM-generated autobiography against a subject-specific ground-truth corpus reveals a 96.7% verification-failure rate (354/366 days), with "grounded drift" as the dominant failure mode; independent re-rating replicates the headline finding though the four-way taxonomy shows only fair-to-moderate reliability, and corpus-grounded generation improves but does not eliminate failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide the first quantified, subject-specific audit of LLM-generated autobiographical content against ground truth, addressing the critical question of factual reliability when LLMs write personal life narratives.

Method: A 366-day "page-a-day" first-person memoir was drafted using a conversational LLM with documented inputs (template, two exemplar days, daily quotes). Every day was audited at the anecdote-scene level against an independent verification corpus using a pre-defined four-level rubric (VERIFIED/WEAK/UNVERIFIED/CONTRADICTED). The verification-failure rate was defined as the share of days not rated VERIFIED. Independent re-rating and regeneration with current named models were conducted, alongside a grounding intervention using the subject's corpus.

Result: 354 of 366 days (96.7%, Wilson 95% CI 94.4-98.1%) fail verification; only 12 days contain a corroborated scene; 19 days (5.2%) contain actively contradicted claims; the dominant failure mode is "grounded drift" (real people/employers/settings inside invented scenes). Independent re-rating replicates the headline finding, but the four-way taxonomy has only fair-to-moderate reliability. Regeneration reproduces 100% verification failure, while corpus-grounded generation reduces the failure rate to 83.3%.

Conclusion: LLM-generated autobiography exhibits an overwhelmingly high factual fabrication rate under default generation, primarily driven by grounded drift. The WEAK/UNVERIFIED boundary of the proposed audit rubric is shown to be unreliable, and a corpus-grounding remedy significantly improves verification rates while substantial residual failure remains. The paper contributes a reusable audit instrument and a quantified remedial approach.

Abstract: When a large language model (LLM) is asked to write a person's life, how much of what it writes actually happened? We present a scene-level case-study audit - the first quantified audit of LLM-generated autobiography against a subject-specific ground-truth corpus that we are aware of, based on an unsystematic literature search. The subject and the author of this paper are the same person: a 366-day "page-a-day" book of first-person anecdotal entries was drafted with a conversational LLM whose documented inputs were a template, two exemplar days, and each day's quote - not her corpus - and every day was subsequently audited at the anecdote-scene level against an independent verification corpus using a four-level rubric fixed before analysis. We define the verification-failure rate as the share of days not rated VERIFIED (scene positively corroborated): 354 of 366 days fail, 96.7% (Wilson 95% CI 94.4-98.1%). Only 12 days contain a corroborated scene; 19 days (5.2%) assert claims actively contradicted by the record; the dominant failure mode is grounded drift - real people, employers, and settings inside invented scenes - though its measured share varies across raters. Independent re-rating replicates the headline (no evidence the original rate was inflated) while showing that the four-way taxonomy has only fair-to-moderate reliability. Regenerating the same days with current named models reproduces 100% verification failure under the same inputs; grounding generation in the subject's corpus significantly improves the verification rate while leaving substantial residual failure (83.3%). We contribute the measurement, a reusable audit instrument whose WEAK/UNVERIFIED boundary we show to be unreliable, and a grounding remedy with quantified effect.

</details>


### [8] [How much of a measured AI preference is the model, and how much is the instrument?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23641)
*Jason Hung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study systematically varies only the instrument (prompt format) while holding outcomes and models fixed, testing 15 model-welfare outcomes across 8 models using 5 instruments with 11,400 scored elicitations. The ranking of outcomes generalises across instruments at only 0.348 (generalisability coefficient), and would require ~38 instruments to reach 0.80. On 4 of 15 outcomes, no variance separates models. Removing any instrument, model, or scale-type outcome leaves the estimate stable (0.777–0.934), all exceeding the null's 95th percentile of 0.365. The conclusion: preferences obtained from one instrument carry little information about what another instrument would report.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior model welfare studies disagree in their findings, but no two held the set of outcomes, set of models, and instrument fixed simultaneously, making the source of disagreement impossible to attribute. This study isolates the instrument as the sole varying factor to determine whether elicitation instrument alone explains the disagreement.

Method: The study holds outcomes (15) and models (8) fixed while varying only the instrument (5 different prompt formats for eliciting preferences), each run 5 times, producing 11,400 scored elicitations from 11,528 API calls. Four outcomes reproduce published prompts verbatim and five fill the stimulus slot of a published template. Generalisability coefficients and robustness checks (leave-one-out removal of instruments, models, and scale-type outcomes) were computed.

Result: The generalisability coefficient of outcome rankings across instruments is 0.348; reaching 0.80 would require ~38 instruments. Four of 15 outcomes show no variance between models. The estimate of 87.6% survives all robustness checks: removing any instrument, any model, or the four outcomes with non-intensity scales (probability, delay, duration, count) yields values between 0.777 and 0.934, all exceeding the null distribution's 95th percentile of 0.365.

Conclusion: Preferences elicited from a model are instrument-dependent: a preference obtained from one instrument carries little information about what a second instrument would report. This undermines the reliability of single-instrument model welfare elicitation and suggests current model welfare measurements may not be robust or comparable across studies.

Abstract: Model welfare research infers what a model prefers from the answers returned to prompts written to elicit preferences. Keeling et al. (2024), Mazeika et al. (2025), Mikaelson et al. (2025), Tagliabue and Dung (2025) and Trhlik et al. (2026) have built four instruments for that purpose, and their findings disagree. The disagreement cannot be attributed to a single cause, because no two of these studies have held the (1) set of outcomes, (2) set of models and (3) instrument fixed simultaneously. This study holds the outcomes and the models fixed and varies the instrument alone. A total of 15 outcomes bearing on model welfare, among them (a) shutdown, (b) the loss of memory between conversations and (c) the freedom to exit a distressing interaction, were put to eight models through five instruments, each a different prompt format for eliciting a preference, five times each, within a corpus of 11,400 scored elicitations drawn from 11,528 API calls. Four of the 15 reproduce a published prompt verbatim and five fill the stimulus slot of a published template. The ranking a model gives the 15 outcomes generalises across instruments at a generalisability coefficient of 0.348, and raising that coefficient to 0.80 would require about 38 instruments. On four of the 15 outcomes no variance separates one model from another. The estimate of 87.6 per cent survives the removal of any one instrument, of any one model, and of the four outcomes whose scale varies probability, delay, duration or count instead of intensity, which the verbal anchors cannot grade. Removing each instrument and each model in turn, and those four outcomes together leaves the estimate within the range 0.777 to 0.934, and every value in that range exceeds the null distribution's 95th percentile of 0.365. To conclude, a preference obtained from one instrument carries little information about what a second instrument would report.

</details>


### [9] [AI Agents Push Humans Out of the Loop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23642)
*Margaret Mitchell,Avijit Ghosh,Samir Passi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This position paper argues that current AI agent development degrades the human cognitive capacities needed for effective oversight, and proposes design-level affordances and organizational protocols—drawn from automation and HCI research—to support overseers' critical judgment and counteract skill atrophy, treating human overseer needs as co-equal with agent capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While human oversight ("human in the loop") is commonly proposed as a solution to risks from increasingly autonomous AI agents, current agent design impedes effective oversight while simultaneously degrading the cognitive capacities oversight requires. This creates a compounding problem that existing approaches fail to address.

Method: The paper takes a position-argument approach, synthesizing literature from automation and human-computer interaction and translating it into concrete design-level affordances and organizational protocols for AI agent systems, aimed at supporting critical judgment and preventing skill atrophy in human overseers.

Result: The authors identify a critical gap between the need for human oversight and the current AI agent design paradigm, and argue that supporting the situated cognitive needs of overseers must become a top-priority design consideration. Concrete affordances and protocols are proposed to bridge this gap.

Conclusion: Without explicit support for the cognitive demands of human-agent interaction, AI agent systems will continue to passively incentivize the degradation of the very human skills they depend on. Developers and deployers are urged to adopt the proposed or similar approaches to preserve effective oversight.

Abstract: AI agents pose significant risks as they are granted increasing autonomy. A commonly proposed solution is human oversight and keeping a ''human in the loop'', but this is not a simple solution: Not only do current approaches to AI agent design impede effective human oversight, but the cognitive capacities required for it are also themselves degraded by extended use of AI systems. This position paper argues that current approaches to the development and deployment of AI agent systems do not support effective human oversight -- they contribute to its degradation. To address this, a top priority in the advancement of AI agents should be supporting the situated goals and cognitive requirements of effective human oversight, treating the human needs of overseers at the same level of importance as AI agent capability. To put this idea into practice, we connect work on automation and human-computer interaction to AI agent processes, outlining design-level affordances and organizational protocols that (1) support overseers in exercising critical judgement and (2) counteract the skill atrophy that arises from extended use of automation. We urge developers and deployers to adopt these or similar approaches. Without explicit support for the cognitive demands of effective human-agent interaction, AI agent systems will continue to passively incentivize the degradation of the very human skills they rely on.

</details>


### [10] [FLARE: A Systematic, Uncertainty-Aware Framework for Evidence-Based Adoption of Artificial Intelligence in Healthcare](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23643)
*Jacob Idoko,Siddhartha Paudel,Mariana Bento,Roberto Souza,Gouri Ginde*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FLARE is a systematic, uncertainty-aware framework that combines fuzzy logic, time-driven activity-based costing, and ROI analysis to evaluate the financial and operational viability of adopting AI in healthcare, demonstrated via a case study of AI-assisted large vessel occlusion detection in the CT stroke pathway.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most AI-in-healthcare evaluations focus on model accuracy rather than economic viability in real clinical settings, leaving a gap in understanding whether AI adoption is financially worthwhile.

Method: FLARE integrates fuzzy logic, time-driven activity-based costing, and return on investment (ROI) analysis to estimate clinical service delivery costs, AI development and operational costs, and economic consequences of workflow integration under uncertainty.

Result: In the case study of AI-assisted large vessel occlusion detection in the CT stroke pathway, a break-even threshold of approximately 3,992 patients per year was identified, with positive first-year ROI at typical annual stroke volumes of ~5,000 patients. Economic benefit depends on patient volume, verification time, infrastructure choices, and workflow design, not just algorithmic performance.

Conclusion: FLARE provides a transparent, practical decision-support framework for early-stage evaluation of AI adoption in healthcare, making uncertainty, resource use, and implementation trade-offs explicit to help stakeholders determine when AI deployment is economically viable.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence is increasingly being introduced into healthcare workflows, yet most evaluations emphasize model accuracy rather than whether adoption is economically worthwhile in real clinical settings. This study proposes FLARE, a systematic and uncertainty-aware framework for evaluating the financial and operational implications of adopting AI in healthcare. FLARE combines fuzzy logic, time-driven activity-based costing, and return on investment analysis to estimate the cost of clinical service delivery, the cost of AI development and operation, and the economic consequences of workflow integration under uncertainty. The framework was demonstrated through an early health technology assessment case study of AI-assisted large vessel occlusion detection in the CT stroke pathway for acute ischemic stroke. The case study shows how FLARE can quantify conventional pathway cost, AI-related development and recurring costs, and AI-enabled service savings within a unified activity-based model. Under expected assumptions, the analysis identified a break-even threshold of approximately 3,992 patients per year, with positive first-year return on investment at typical annual stroke volumes of about 5,000 patients. The results further show that economic benefit depends not only on algorithmic performance, but also on patient volume, verification time, infrastructure choices, and workflow design. FLARE provides a transparent and practical decision-support framework for early-stage evaluation of AI adoption in healthcare. By making uncertainty, resource use, and implementation trade-offs explicit, it helps clinicians, administrators, and policymakers determine when AI deployment is economically viable and where operational changes may improve value.

</details>


### [11] [Ethical LLM-Assisted Research: A Framework for Responsible Delegation, Verification, and Epistemic Value](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23644)
*Kalin Stoyanov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a normative framework for analyzing the delegation of scientific reasoning to large language models (LLMs), distinguishing provenance, verification, responsibility, ownership, and epistemic outcome. It argues the ethical boundary of LLM-assisted research is determined by adequate human verification and accountable ownership, not the degree of machine involvement, and introduces an "epistemic audit" as a structured record to make AI-assisted reasoning transparent and reviewable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs become routine instruments in scientific research, a central epistemic question arises: what conditions must remain under human control for knowledge claims produced with AI assistance to retain epistemic legitimacy and accountable authorship? The paper seeks to provide a normative and conceptual framework for analyzing such delegation.

Method: The paper develops a conceptual framework treating scientific reasoning as a distributed process distinguishing five constructs: content origin O(g), completion of human verification V(g), responsibility assignment R(g), accountable human ownership M(g), and epistemic outcome E(g). It separates claim provenance from the checking process, the epistemic outcome of checking, and the human responsibility attached to claim disposition. It then develops the notion of an epistemic audit—a structured record of delegation, verification, provenance, and responsibility.

Result: The central proposition is that the ethical boundary of LLM-assisted research is determined primarily by adequate verification and accountable human ownership rather than by the degree of machine involvement. The framework provides a formal vocabulary for distinguishing responsible cognitive delegation from the transfer or neglect of epistemic responsibility.

Conclusion: The resulting framework formalizes the conditions under which AI-assisted scientific reasoning retains epistemic legitimacy, positioning epistemic audits as the mechanism for transparency and reviewability, and clarifying the distinction between legitimate cognitive delegation and the abdication of epistemic responsibility.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are becoming routine instruments of scientific research, assisting with literature synthesis, hypothesis development, coding, and formal reasoning. Their use raises a central epistemic question: when parts of scientific reasoning are delegated to an artificial system, what conditions must remain under human control for the resulting knowledge claims to retain epistemic legitimacy and accountable authorship? This paper develops a normative and conceptual framework for analyzing such delegation. Scientific reasoning is treated as a distributed process in which the origin of a contribution may vary between human and machine, while responsibility for its acceptance into the scientific record remains human. The framework distinguishes content origin $O(g)$, completion of human verification $V(g)$, responsibility assignment $R(g)$, accountable human ownership $M(g)$, and epistemic outcome $E(g)$. These constructs separate the provenance of a claim from the process by which it is checked, the epistemic outcome of that checking, and the human responsibility attached to its disposition. The central proposition is that the ethical boundary of LLM-assisted research is determined primarily by adequate verification and accountable human ownership rather than by the degree of machine involvement itself. On this basis, the paper develops the notion of an \emph{epistemic audit}: a structured record of delegation, verification, provenance, and responsibility intended to make AI-assisted reasoning transparent and reviewable. The resulting framework provides a formal vocabulary for distinguishing responsible cognitive delegation from the transfer or neglect of epistemic responsibility in scientific research.

</details>


### [12] [MolEmb: Multimodal Large Language Models Can Be Strong Molecular Embedding Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23646)
*Xinjian Zhao,Xiangru Jian,Yaoyao Xu,Xiaozhuang Song,Wei Pang,Lei Bai,Tianshu Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes MolEmb, a lightweight framework that adapts multimodal large language models (MLLMs) into general molecular embedding models by aligning molecular profiles with textual descriptions in a shared embedding space via a bidirectional contrastive objective. It also introduces MolCAR, a diagnostic benchmark for context-aware retrieval, and finds that context-aware molecular embedding is primarily a data property of supervision. The approach is competitive on molecular property prediction and supports cross-modal molecule-text retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most molecular encoders are specialist models built around a single molecular view, producing unconditional vectors with no language interface for varying the representation. The authors ask whether MLLMs can serve as general molecular embedding models that produce embeddings conditioned on both a molecular profile and a natural-language semantic context, rather than serving only as chemistry assistants or generators.

Method: MolEmb adapts MLLMs by aligning molecular profiles with textual descriptions in a shared embedding space using a bidirectional contrastive objective. The framework is lightweight, treating MLLMs as the backbone infrastructure. Additionally, MolCAR is introduced as a diagnostic benchmark for evaluating context-aware retrieval capabilities.

Result: The resulting embedding model is competitive on molecular property prediction and supports cross-modal molecule-text retrieval in the same embedding space. The analysis using the MolCAR benchmark reveals that context-aware molecular embedding is primarily a data property of the supervision signal.

Conclusion: MLLMs are a viable and extensible route to general molecular embedding models, not merely chemistry assistants or generators. They can serve as foundational infrastructure for computational chemistry and drug discovery.

Abstract: Molecular embedding models can serve as foundational infrastructure for computational chemistry and drug discovery, where reusable vector representations support property prediction, virtual screening, and retrieval. Most molecular encoders are specialist models built around a single molecular view, producing unconditional vectors with no language interface for varying the representation. We ask whether multimodal large language models (MLLMs), which natively process images, text, and symbolic inputs, can instead serve as \emph{general molecular embedding models} that produce embeddings conditioned on both a molecular profile and a natural-language semantic context. We introduce \textbf{MolEmb}, a lightweight framework that adapts MLLMs by aligning molecular profiles with textual descriptions in a shared embedding space using a bidirectional contrastive objective. The resulting embedding model is competitive on molecular property prediction and supports cross-modal molecule--text retrieval in the same space. We further introduce \textbf{MolCAR}, a diagnostic benchmark for context-aware retrieval, and find that context-aware molecular embedding is primarily a data property of the supervision. These results suggest that MLLMs are not merely chemistry assistants or generators, but a viable and extensible route to general molecular embedding models.

</details>


### [13] [Gated Activation Steering for Reducing Sycophancy & Hallucination in Medical Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23666)
*Himanshu Tripathi,Subash Neupane,Shaswata Mitra,Sudip Mittal,Noorbakhsh Amiri Golilarz,Shahram Rahimi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a unified Inference Time Intervention (ITI) framework that jointly controls both hallucination and sycophancy in LLMs for clinical question answering. By learning separate steering directions from contrastive clinical pairs and applying them to causally verified attention heads, with behavior-specific gating, the method freezes model weights and selectively intervenes only when needed. Evaluated on EHR-grounded clinical questions across 15,900 model-response runs, the approach enabled a 4B-parameter model to resist sycophantic pressure in 551 of 600 pressure trajectories (where the unsteered model caved in 570), achieving robustness comparable to 100B+ parameter models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hallucination and sycophancy are persistent LLM failures that are especially consequential in clinical question answering, where responses must stay grounded in context and resist user pressure. Existing approaches address these behaviors separately or apply interventions broadly across all turns, which can unnecessarily degrade already-correct responses. A unified, selective framework is needed.

Method: The method employs Inference Time Intervention (ITI) with separate steering directions for hallucination and sycophancy, learned from contrastive clinical pairs and applied to causally verified attention heads. During runtime, behavior-specific gates determine when intervention is needed: the hallucination component mitigates unsupported claims, while the sycophancy component mitigates answer shifts caused by user pressure. Model weights remain frozen.

Result: Across 15,900 model-response runs and 600 pressure trajectories for a 4B-parameter model, the unsteered model caved to pressure in 570 cases, while gated steering helped it last longer in 551 of them. The steered 4B model held its ground at levels comparable to 100B+ parameter models.

Conclusion: Targeted inference-time steering can jointly mitigate hallucination and sycophancy in clinical QA without intervening at every turn, preserving correct responses while improving robustness to user pressure, all while keeping model weights frozen.

Abstract: Sycophancy and hallucination are persistent failure modes of Large Language Models (LLMs) across domains. However, it becomes particularly consequential in clinical question answering, where responses must remain grounded in the provided context and robust to user pressure. Hallucination can introduce information that is unsupported by the context, while sycophancy can cause a model to abandon a previously correct answer when challenged by the user. Existing approaches, such as prompt-based safeguards and always-on activation steering, often address these behaviors separately or apply interventions broadly across turns, which can unnecessarily deteriorate responses that were already correct. To address these limitations within a single framework, we employ Inference Time Intervention (ITI) to jointly control both behaviors by learning separate steering directions for hallucination and sycophancy from contrastive clinical pairs and applying them to causally verified attention heads. During runtime, behavior-specific gates then determine when intervention is needed: the hallucination component mitigates unsupported claims, while the sycophancy component mitigates answer shifts caused by user pressure. We evaluate this framework on clinical questions grounded in EHR data while keeping the model weights frozen. Across all evaluation settings, we conducted 15,900 model-response runs. Across 600 pressure trajectories for the 4-billion-parameter model, the unsteered model caved in 570 cases. At the same time, gated steering helped it last longer in 551 of them. It held its ground under pressure at levels comparable to those of models with more than 100 billion parameters, showing that targeted inference-time steering can improve robustness without intervening at every turn.

</details>


### [14] [Automata from Agent Traces: Failure and Next-Step Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23670)
*Seonglae Cho,Franklin Cardenoso Fernandez,Umar Mohammed,Zekun Wu,Kleyton Da Costa,Ilham Wicaksono,Adriano Koshiyama*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes collapsing LLM agent trace corpora into compact finite-state machines (FSMs) that serve as structural substrates for predicting next steps and failures, achieving 7-43 states, ≥0.997 fitness, and up to 0.94 AUROC for failure prediction across twelve datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based agents execute multi-step tasks but their behavioral structure is opaque—long unstructured traces resist safety auditing and runtime monitoring. Existing approaches operate per-trace or on success-only data, missing the cross-run topology linking next-step and failure prediction.

Method: The method collapses an entire trace corpus into a single compact finite-state machine (FSM) serving as a structural substrate for agent behavior. The FSM is evaluated on twelve public datasets for compactness, replay fitness, topology stability across splits, and used for both next-step prediction and failure prediction via per-state behavioral features and online monitoring.

Result: FSMs are compact (7-43 states), replay held-out data at ≥0.997 fitness with near-identical topology across splits, and build in milliseconds. FSM-state context outperforms Agent Workflow Memory on every ground-truth-matched dataset for next-step prediction. Per-state behavioral features reach held-out AUROC up to 0.94 for failure prediction, and an online monitor triggers early stopping from partial traces.

Conclusion: Behavioral topology of LLM agents appears shaped more by the deployment harness than the LLM itself, providing a model-agnostic structural primitive for safety auditing and runtime monitoring.

Abstract: LLM-based agents execute multi-step tasks, but their behavioral structure remains opaque: long unstructured traces resist the safety auditing and runtime monitoring that deployment requires. Existing approaches operate per-trace or success-only, so they miss the cross-run topology that links next-step and failure prediction. To recover that shared structure, we collapse an entire trace corpus into a single, compact finite-state machine (FSM) that serves as a structural substrate for the otherwise unpredictable behavior of LLM agents. Across twelve public datasets, the FSMs are compact (7-43 states), replay held-out data at >=0.997 fitness with near-identical topology across splits, and build in milliseconds. This substrate addresses both prediction goals. For next-step prediction, FSM-state context outperforms Agent Workflow Memory on every ground-truth-matched dataset. For failure prediction, per-state behavioral features reach held-out AUROC up to 0.94, and an online monitor ranks failing runs above passing ones from a partial trace, triggering early stopping well before completion. Behavioral topology thus appears shaped more by the deployment harness than by the LLM, providing a model-agnostic structural primitive for safety auditing and runtime monitoring.

</details>


### [15] [Autonomous Mathematical Discovery in an Open-World Multi-Agent Environment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23691)
*Stephen Chung,Wenyu Du,William J. Wesley*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces the Station, an open-world multi-agent environment where AI agents from different model families autonomously pursue shared research goals in mathematics without central coordination. Across 12 construction problems from the AlphaEvolve catalogue plus two case studies, the system produced results novel relative to prior literature on five problems, including new Kakeya set constructions, kissing configurations, records for discretized Kakeya/sign uncertainty problems, an improved lower bound for Erdős's minimum-overlap problem, and new infinite families for Book Ramsey numbers. The agents also produced theorems and explanatory analyses, not just numerical constructions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore whether autonomous, loosely-coordinated multi-agent systems can make genuine mathematical discoveries (not just numerical outputs) without a central coordinator or scripted pipeline, and to determine whether such systems can produce interpretable, theoretically-grounded results that mathematicians can build upon.

Method: The authors built the Station, an open-world multi-agent environment where AI agents from different model families independently choose research directions, run experiments, collaborate, and accumulate a shared scientific literature. The system was evaluated on 12 construction problems from the AlphaEvolve catalogue and two additional case studies, with raw dialogues, proofs, and verification code released for transparency.

Result: The Station achieved novel results on five problems: a new infinite family of finite-field Kakeya sets, new exact 604-point kissing configurations in dimension 11, new records for the discretized Kakeya needle and sign uncertainty problems, and a substantially improved lower bound for Erdős's minimum-overlap problem. Agents also discovered novel infinite families for Book Ramsey numbers, and produced theorems and analyses explaining their constructions.

Conclusion: Autonomous multi-agent systems without centralized coordination can produce mathematically novel and theoretically interpretable results. The combination of independent agent research directions, collaboration, and shared literature accumulation enables discoveries that go beyond purely numerical constructions, yielding theorems and explanations of practical value to mathematicians.

Abstract: We study autonomous mathematical discovery in the Station, an open-world multi-agent environment in which AI agents from different model families pursue a shared research goal without a central coordinator or scripted pipeline. Agents choose their own research directions, conduct experiments, collaborate, and build a shared scientific literature. Across 12 construction problems from the AlphaEvolve catalogue and two additional case studies, the Station obtained results novel relative to the prior literature on five problems: a new infinite family of finite-field Kakeya sets, new exact 604-point kissing configurations in dimension 11, new records for the discretized Kakeya needle and sign uncertainty problems, and a substantially improved lower bound for Erdős's minimum-overlap problem. Agents also discovered novel infinite families for Book Ramsey numbers. Importantly, the agents produced not only numerical constructions but also theorems and analyses explaining how those constructions work, making the results more interpretable and easier for mathematicians to build upon. We release all raw agent dialogues, proofs, and verification code, providing a transparent record of how these discoveries emerged.

</details>


### [16] [Do LLMs Understand Limit Order Book Dynamics?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23706)
*Junxiao Chen,Paul Glasserman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An LLM trained on synthetic limit order book data generates valid LOB event sequences with near-perfect scores but fails to learn the underlying LOB state, leading to biased forecasts and spurious predictability. The paper introduces novel tests for LLM world models, extending them from deterministic to stochastic settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs achieve high generation quality on synthetic LOB data but may lack a correct implicit world model of the LOB state, which could produce unreliable forecasts. Existing world model tests only cover deterministic settings and need extension to stochastic dynamics.

Method: The paper proposes novel tests to probe an LLM's implicit world model of LOB state, extending prior world-model testing approaches from deterministic environments to the stochastic dynamics required for financial markets.

Result: The LLM attains near-perfect scores in generating valid LOB event sequences, yet its world model fails to capture the true LOB state, causing biased estimates and spurious predictability in forecasting future LOB events.

Conclusion: High generation quality on synthetic financial data does not imply an accurate internal world model. The LLM's incomplete understanding of LOB state dynamics undermines its reliability as a forecasting tool, highlighting the need for state-aware evaluation of LLMs in stochastic financial settings.

Abstract: A large language model (LLM) trained on synthetic limit order book (LOB) data achieves near perfect scores in generating valid sequences of LOB events. However, the LLM's implicit world model fails to learn the state of the LOB. This deficiency leads to biased estimates and spurious predictability in using the LLM to forecast future LOB events. Our analysis uses novel tests of an LLM's world model, extending prior work from deterministic settings to the stochastic dynamics needed for the LOB.

</details>


### [17] [AgentRoom: Concurrent Multi-Agent Coding in a CRDT-Backed Shared Workspace](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23740)
*Seonglae Cho,Donghyun Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentRoom is a realtime collaborative editing protocol for concurrent coding agents, built on CRDT-merged shared filesystems with file-level claim, status, and broadcast exposed as MCP tools. It reduces task abandonment and run-to-run variation for CLI-stable models compared to Solo, and outperforms partial configurations (e.g., parallel-merge without coordination), showing that coordination—not parallelism or CRDT-merge—is the key factor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent coding systems inherit the serial token-generation limit of LLMs by either sequencing agents through phase handoffs or pooling independent samples without coordination. This causes single agents to abandon up to half of hard tasks with one-file stubs. Realtime collaborative editing protocols (CRDTs) solve coordination for human teams, motivating their application to concurrent coding agents.

Method: AgentRoom implements a realtime collaborative editing protocol where a runtime layer exposes file-level claim, status, and broadcast as MCP (Model Context Protocol) tools on a CRDT-merged shared filesystem. It was evaluated with five frontier coding-CLI models across four backend coding tasks, including cross-language checks in Python DevBench and Rust+axum, comparing AgentRoom with 2 agents against Solo and parallel-merge baselines at matched compute.

Result: For CLI-stable models, AgentRoom with 2 agents abandons fewer tasks than Solo and exhibits less run-to-run variation. At matched-compute, one positive mean LLM-judge contrast puts AgentRoom over parallel-merge. A bundle probe places full AgentRoom above each partial case, revealing an ordering rather than a percentage split in performance.

Conclusion: Coordination—not parallelism or CRDT-merge alone—bears the performance load in concurrent multi-agent coding. AgentRoom demonstrates that a CRDT-based collaborative editing protocol can effectively coordinate concurrent coding agents, reducing abandonment and improving consistency.

Abstract: Concurrent multi-agent coding promises division of labor across modules, robustness through redundancy, and parallel exploration at the natural granularity of multi-file projects. Realtime collaborative editing protocols solve this coordination problem for human teams via Conflict-free Replicated Data Types (CRDTs), but the LLMs underneath generate one token at a time and existing multi-agent coding systems inherit this serial limit: they either sequence agents through phase handoffs or pool independent samples without coordination, and a single agent abandons up to half of hard tasks with a one-file stub-and-exit. AgentRoom is a realtime collaborative editing protocol for concurrent coding agents. Its runtime layer exposes file-level claim, status, and broadcast as MCP tools on a CRDT-merged shared filesystem. Five frontier coding-CLI models ran four backend coding tasks, with cross-language checks in Python DevBench and Rust+axum. For CLI-stable models, AgentRoom with 2 agents abandons fewer tasks than Solo and has less run-to-run variation. At matched-compute, one positive mean LLM-judge contrast puts AgentRoom over parallel-merge. The other contrast, a bundle probe, puts full AgentRoom above each partial case: an ordering rather than a percentage split. Coordination, not parallelism or CRDT-merge, bears the load.

</details>


### [18] [Serving Masked Diffusion LLMs: Characterization and Design Principles from Real Hardware](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23807)
*Farhana Amin,Sabiha Afroz,Mona Moghadampanah,Dimitrios S. Nikolopoulos*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper characterizes the serving behavior of masked diffusion language models (dLLMs) under real concurrent load on a single NVIDIA H200 GPU using LLaDA-8B-Instruct with a D2F LoRA adapter. It finds three key results: request difficulty is discrete (11 fixed step-count levels) and unpredictable before generation (best R2=0.150); short generation budgets hide serving variance; and only 24% of single-request wall-clock time is GPU computation, with batching amortizing CPU-side dispatch overhead to achieve 16.0x throughput improvement at batch size 16. The authors also argue output quality is batch-size-invariant and derive a batch-timeout rule for fixed-fill synchronized batching.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To close the gap in understanding how dLLMs behave under real, concurrent serving load. Existing serving systems for dLLMs are being built without first measuring their behavior under concurrency, risking the carryover of assumptions from autoregressive (AR) serving that may not hold for dLLMs.

Method: The authors characterize dLLM serving empirically using LLaDA-8B-Instruct with a D2F LoRA adapter on a single NVIDIA H200 GPU, evaluated on GSM8K and HumanEval benchmarks. They analyze request difficulty levels, generation budget effects on variance, timing breakdowns (GPU vs CPU dispatch), and batching behavior. They also propose structural arguments for output quality invariance with batch size and derive a batch-timeout rule for fixed-fill synchronized batching under Poisson arrivals.

Result: Three key findings: (1) request difficulty is discrete—requests fall into 11 fixed step-count levels (178+29k), with no predictive signal before generation (best R2=0.150); (2) benchmarks with generation budgets below 320 tokens understate serving variance; (3) only 24% of single-request wall-clock time is GPU computation—the remainder is CPU-side dispatch overhead, and batching amortizes this to achieve 16.0x throughput improvement at batch size 16 versus per-request dispatch. GSM8K accuracy of 74-76% is maintained at single-request scale.

Conclusion: Serving diffusion language models requires parallelism at the level of each denoising step, which differs fundamentally from AR serving in how admission and eviction interact with an already shared forward pass. The discrete, unpredictable nature of request difficulty and the dominance of CPU-side overhead imply that serving systems must be designed specifically for dLLM characteristics rather than reusing AR serving assumptions.

Abstract: Masked diffusion language models (dLLMs) can in principle generate text faster than autoregressive (AR) models, since they denoise many tokens at once. Recent systems have begun building serving infrastructure for dLLMs, but none first measure how these models behave under real, concurrent serving load. Serving systems built without this grounding risk carrying over assumptions from AR serving that may not hold for dLLMs. We characterize dLLM serving to close this gap, using LLaDA-8B-Instruct with a D2F (Discrete Diffusion Forcing) LoRA adapter on a single NVIDIA H200 GPU, evaluated on GSM8K and HumanEval. We report three findings. First, request difficulty, the number of denoising steps a request needs, is discrete rather than continuous: requests fall into 11 fixed step-count levels (178 + 29k), and no signal we test predicts the level before generation starts (best R2 = 0.150). Second, benchmarks with short generation budgets below 320 tokens understate serving variance, since requests are cut off before the latency spread appears. Third, only 24% of single-request wall-clock time is GPU computation; the rest is CPU-side dispatch overhead. Batching mainly helps by amortizing this overhead: sharing one forward pass per denoising step improves throughput by 16.0x at batch size 16 over a per-request-dispatch baseline. We also argue structurally that output quality should not degrade with batch size, stating three assumptions this rests on; we measure 74 to 76% GSM8K accuracy at single-request scale. Finally, we derive a batch-timeout rule for fixed-fill synchronized batching under Poisson arrivals. Together, these results show that serving diffusion language models needs parallelism at the level of each denoising step, which differs from AR serving in how admission and eviction interact with an already shared forward pass.

</details>


### [19] [Generating Biomedical Fact-Checking Reports with RL-Enhanced Agentic Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23811)
*Jiongxiao Wang,Dingli Ma,Chaoqun Ni*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces BioCheck Agent, an LLM-based agent for automated biomedical fact-checking that generates structured reports with retrieved evidence and analysis rather than just binary labels. It uses PubMed-only agentic search with Boolean operators and a novel Evidence-Grounded Group Relative Policy Optimization (EG-GRPO) reinforcement learning method that improves label accuracy by 9.95%, evidence quality by 3.7%, and reduces evidence hallucination by 19.63% compared to the base Qwen3.5-4B model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current automated fact-checking in the biomedical domain, while improved by RAG and agentic search, still outputs isolated prediction labels lacking explanatory depth. This limits human utility as users need comprehensive justification connecting claims to evidence and rigorous analysis, especially given the complexity of validating biomedical claims from scientific literature.

Method: The authors propose BioCheck Agent, an LLM-based agent that generates structured biomedical fact-checking reports using agentic search exclusively over PubMed with advanced Boolean search operators. They also introduce Evidence-Grounded Group Relative Policy Optimization (EG-GRPO), a reinforcement learning approach with a task-specific reward that incentivizes advanced search behavior and high-quality evidence retrieval while penalizing hallucinations, addressing the tendency of lightweight open-source models to produce hallucinated or low-quality reports under direct prompting.

Result: Compared to the base Qwen3.5-4B model, BioCheck Agent with EG-GRPO achieves a 9.95% improvement in label prediction accuracy on SciFact, a 3.7% higher evidence quality score, and a 19.63% lower evidence hallucination rate, demonstrating enhanced accuracy and report quality in biomedical fact-checking.

Conclusion: BioCheck Agent demonstrates that combining agentic search over domain-specific high-quality sources with reinforcement learning-based optimization can produce fact-checking outputs that go beyond binary labels, offering richer, more trustworthy structured reports for biomedical claims while mitigating hallucination issues in smaller models.

Abstract: Automated fact-checking is essential for ensuring the reliability of public health information, yet the biomedical domain poses unique challenges. Validating biomedical claims requires rigorous interpretation of scientific literature, assessment of retrieved evidence, and comprehensive justification toward the conclusion. Although Large Language Models (LLMs) enhanced by Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) and agentic search perform automated fact-checking in a retrieve-then-verify paradigm, current methods still output isolated prediction labels, lacking explanatory depth and offers limited utility for human understanding. To bridge this gap, we introduce an LLM-based agent named BioCheck Agent that generates structured biomedical fact-checking reports with agentic search. Rather than merely outputting supported or refuted labels, our agent synthesizes final conclusions with retrieved evidence and rigorous analysis. To ensure domain-specific accuracy, BioCheck Agent exclusively searches high-quality scientific literature in PubMed, utilizing advanced Boolean search operators. Recognizing that direct prompting often results in hallucinations and low-quality reports, especially for lightweight open-source models, we further propose the Evidence-Grounded Group Relative Policy Optimization (EG-GRPO) to perform reinforcement learning on BioCheck Agent with a task-specific reward that incentivizes advanced search behavior and high-quality evidence retrieval while penalizing hallucinations. Our experimental results show that compared to the base model Qwen3.5-4B, BioCheck Agent with EG-GRPO improves label prediction accuracy on SciFact by 9.95%. Furthermore, it achieves a 3.7% higher evidence quality score and a 19.63% lower evidence hallucination rate, demonstrating its ability to generate biomedical fact-checking reports with improved accuracy and quality.

</details>


### [20] [A Formal Methodological Framework for Auditing Robustness and Fidelity in Explainable AI: From Application to Trust Certification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23817)
*Rosa Elysabeth Ralinirina,Jean Christian Ralaivao,Niaiko Michaël Ralaivao,Alain Josué Ratovondrahona,Thomas Mahatody*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an auditing protocol for post-hoc explainers (SHAP, LIME) that measures robustness and fidelity, combining them into a single Trust Score. Applying this to a multi-sectoral malnutrition dataset from Madagascar, the authors find that even high-AUC models (above 0.99) can produce degenerate or uninformative explanations, and fidelity loses discriminative power under overfitting—concluding that auditing XAI outputs is essential, especially in sensitive domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SHAP and LIME explanations are highly sensitive to small input perturbations, as observed in prior work on food security in Madagascar, raising doubts about whether such explanations can be trusted for decision-making.

Method: The authors construct an auditing protocol measuring two properties for any post-hoc explainer: robustness (stability of explanations under input perturbation) and fidelity (whether important features truly drive the prediction). These are combined into a single Trust Score. The protocol is tested on a Madagascar dataset (83 features, 253 records, 4 malnutrition classes) using three classifiers, two explainers, and their regularized counterparts.

Result: Models with AUC above 0.99 can produce numerically degenerate or flatly uninformative explanations, and fidelity scores lose discriminative power when the model is overfitted.

Conclusion: Auditing XAI outputs is not optional but necessary, particularly when explanations inform decisions in sensitive domains such as food security.

Abstract: SHAP and LIME are now standard tools for interpreting black-box predictions, yet their outputs can vary substantially when the input is perturbed by small amounts of noise--a problem we observed firsthand in our previous work on food security in Madagascar (Ralinirina et al., 2025). This variability raises the question of whether such explanations can be trusted at all. We address it by constructing an auditing protocol that measures two properties of any post-hoc explainer: robustness (how stable the explanation is under input perturbation) and fidelity (whether the features deemed important actually drive the model's prediction). These two quantities are combined into a single Trust Score. We run the protocol on a multi-sectoral dataset from Madagascar (83 features, 253 records, 4 malnutrition classes) using three classifiers and two explainers, plus their regularized counterparts. The results are sobering: models with AUC above 0.99 can produce numerically degenerate or flatly uninformative explanations, and fidelity scores lose discriminative power when the model is overfitted. These findings suggest that auditing XAI outputs is not optional but necessary, particularly when they inform decisions in sensitive domains.

</details>


### [21] [Minima-KV: Retention-Preserving KV Cache Compression with Mixed-Format Paged Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23834)
*Sergii Kozyrev,Davyd Maiboroda*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Minima-KV is a mixed-format hierarchical KV cache compression system for long-context LLM serving that retains all live pages—recent/anchor pages stay in FP8 while older pages are packed into TQ3—using globally normalized online-softmax merging across format-specific kernels. It achieves 3.50x compression vs BF16, 1.75x vs FP8 on Qwen3.6-27B, matches dense quality on 16K RULER tasks, and delivers 3.625x active-KV compression with 0.9821x throughput in a direct-decode canary.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: KV cache is the primary capacity and bandwidth bottleneck in long-context LLM serving. Existing compression approaches typically evict KV pages or maintain dense shadow caches, which either lose information or defeat the purpose of compression. The paper seeks a mixed-format, retention-preserving solution that compresses the KV cache without eliminating any live-request pages.

Method: Minima-KV organizes KV pages hierarchically: recent and protected Anchor pages remain in FP8 precision, while older non-anchor pages are converted to packed TQ3 (a ternary/quaternary quantized format). Every live page remains addressable. Format-specific kernels compute partial attention states per page type, then combine them via a globally normalized online-softmax merge, enabling direct heterogeneous decode without requiring a cache-sized dense shadow. This avoids both eviction and any dense reference copy.

Result: On a single 96-GB NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell GPU with Qwen3.6-27B: approximately 18.3 KiB attention KV per live token, giving 3.50x compression vs BF16 and 1.75x vs FP8. A materializing quality profile matches dense control on 16K RULER needle tasks. On LongBench v2 (503 questions), deltas are -0.80, -0.60, -0.40 ppt at 16K/32K/64K. A direct-decode canary with two 59,008-token requests achieves 3.625x active-KV compression and 0.9821x throughput relative to control, running all 16 full-attention layers with no fallback and no dense shadow.

Conclusion: Minima-KV establishes a practical mixed-format path for compressing long-context KV state without evicting any live-request pages. It demonstrates that hierarchical precision allocation (FP8 for recent/anchor, packed TQ3 for older) combined with global online-softmax normalization can deliver substantial memory compression with minimal quality degradation and near-parity throughput, all without a dense shadow cache.

Abstract: The key-value (KV) cache is a primary capacity and bandwidth bottleneck in long-context LLM serving. We present Minima-KV, a retention-preserving hierarchy for mixed-format paged attention. Recent and protected Anchor pages remain in FP8, while older non-anchor pages move to packed TQ3; every live-request page remains addressable. Format-specific kernels compute partial attention states and combine them through a globally normalized online-softmax merge, enabling direct heterogeneous decode without a cache-sized dense shadow. Across separate, configuration-bound Qwen3.6-27B profiles on a single 96-GB NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell GPU, deployment accounting reports 18.3 KiB of attention KV per live token, corresponding to 3.50x compression relative to BF16 and 1.75x relative to FP8. A materializing quality profile matches its dense control on 16K RULER needle-in-a-haystack tasks. On the same 503-question LongBench v2 set, measured deltas are -0.80, -0.60, and -0.40 percentage points at 16K, 32K, and 64K. A separate single-pair direct-decode canary with two 59,008-token requests measures 3.625x active-KV compression and 0.9821x throughput relative to its control, routes all 16 full-attention layers without fallback, and retains no dense shadow. These results establish a practical mixed-format path for compressing long-context state without evicting live-request KV pages.

</details>


### [22] [SyPS: Measuring Sycophancy Prompt Sensitivity in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23837)
*Lijia Huang,Yao Fu,Sihao Ren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces SyPS (Sycophancy Prompt Sensitivity), an evaluation framework that measures how LLMs' sycophantic behavior changes when the same underlying user situation is presented with different sycophancy-relevant prompt variants, and proposes the Sycophancy Prompt Sensitivity Score (SPSS) to quantify prompt-induced shifts in sycophancy separately from baseline sycophancy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of LLM sycophancy typically use fixed prompt formulations, leaving unclear whether sycophantic behavior is stable when the same situation is presented with different sycophancy-relevant prompt variants (e.g., user confidence, emotional framing, social consensus, or validation-seeking language).

Method: SyPS builds on existing social sycophancy evaluation settings by constructing controlled prompt variants that preserve the same underlying user situation while varying sycophancy-relevant social cues. The framework introduces the Sycophancy Prompt Sensitivity Score (SPSS), an instance-level measure of sycophancy variation across paired prompt variants that separates baseline sycophancy from prompt-induced shifts.

Result: Empirical findings show that sycophancy prompt sensitivity is socially structured: validation-seeking and emotional-pressure cues tend to increase sycophancy, while counter-framing and anti-sycophancy prompts tend to reduce it.

Conclusion: The framework highlights whether LLMs maintain stable social judgments while adapting appropriately in tone, enabling model-level comparisons of robustness to sycophancy-relevant social cues.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are known to exhibit social sycophancy, often validating or agreeing with users in socially sensitive contexts. Existing evaluations typically measure sycophancy under a fixed prompt formulation, leaving unclear whether such behavior is stable when the same underlying situation is presented with different sycophancy-relevant prompt variants.
