In this work, we study sycophancy prompt sensitivity: the extent to which changes in user confidence, emotional framing, social consensus, or validation-seeking language alter a model's sycophantic behavior. We refer to our evaluation framework as SyPS, short for Sycophancy Prompt Sensitivity. Building on existing social sycophancy evaluation settings, SyPS constructs controlled prompt variants that preserve the same underlying user situation while varying sycophancy-relevant social cues. We introduce the Sycophancy Prompt Sensitivity Score (SPSS), an instance-level measure of sycophancy variation across paired prompt variants. Unlike aggregate sycophancy rates, SPSS separates baseline sycophancy from prompt-induced shifts, enabling model-level comparisons of robustness to sycophancy-relevant social cues. Empirically, we find that sycophancy prompt sensitivity is socially structured: validation-seeking and emotional-pressure cues often increase sycophancy, whereas counter-framing and anti-sycophancy prompts tend to reduce it. Our framework highlights whether LLMs maintain stable social judgments while adapting appropriately in tone.

</details>


### [23] [Exploit More, Explore Smarter for Budget-Constrained Agentic Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23848)
*Haoyang Fang,Bernie Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ExTS is a tree-search policy for budget-constrained agentic search that treats expansion as a value-of-information decision, using discriminative reward shaping, a stochastic virtual child, and quality-conditioned branching, achieving +5.5% average relative gains over task-specific baselines across four domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard MCTS allocates budget poorly in budget-constrained agentic search: exploration bonuses dominate at low visit counts, unpromising siblings are expanded before promising chains deepen, and branching is independent of node quality. This leads to inefficient use of the limited evaluation budget when validation is expensive or generation requires multiple model calls.

Method: ExTS combines three mechanisms: (1) discriminative reward shaping to separate candidates under narrow score distributions, (2) a stochastic virtual child that estimates the value of creating a new branch from parent reward history, and (3) quality-conditioned branching that expands only when a node's score justifies the budget cost.

Result: Across prompt optimization, code generation, molecular structure elucidation, and agentic workflow optimization, ExTS is competitive with or improves over task-specific tree-search baselines, with an average relative gain of +5.5% using a single fixed configuration. The paper also introduces pilot-run diagnostics that characterize structural differences among budget-constrained agentic search problems.

Conclusion: ExTS provides a general, single-configuration tree-search policy that effectively addresses budget allocation weaknesses in MCTS for agentic search, and the pilot-run diagnostics offer both understanding of the problem space and practical guidance for adaptation.

Abstract: Budget-constrained agentic search arises when an LLM agent must refine candidates under a small evaluation budget, because validation is expensive, generation requires multiple model calls, or both. In this regime, standard MCTS allocates budget poorly: exploration bonuses dominate at low visit counts, unpromising siblings are expanded before promising chains can deepen, and branching is independent of node quality. We introduce ExTS, a tree-search policy that treats expansion itself as a value-of-information decision. ExTS combines three mechanisms: discriminative reward shaping to separate candidates under narrow score distributions, a stochastic virtual child that estimates the value of creating a new branch from the parent's reward history, and quality-conditioned branching that expands only when a node's score justifies the budget cost. Across prompt optimization, code generation, molecular structure elucidation, and agentic workflow optimization, ExTS is competitive with or improves over task-specific tree-search baselines, with an average relative gain of +5.5% using a single fixed configuration. We further introduce pilot-run diagnostics that characterize what makes budget-constrained agentic search problems structurally different from one another, providing both understanding of the problem space and practical guidance for adaptation.

</details>


### [24] [In-Context Inpainting for Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23855)
*Thang Nguyen,Dung Nguyen,Romero Morais,Truyen Tran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ICI-Time is a novel framework that reframes time series forecasting as a visual inpainting task by converting time series into area charts and applying visual in-context learning with pre-trained vision transformers, eliminating the need for specialized temporal architectures or fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To leverage the generalization power of large vision models (LVMs) for time series forecasting without requiring specialized temporal architectures or extensive domain-specific training, thereby introducing a new paradigm that bridges temporal and visual domains.

Method: ICI-Time transforms time series into structured visual representations (area charts) and applies visual in-context learning, reformulating forecasting as pattern completion within a grid-structured prompt. Temporal dependencies are represented through spatial layout using a consistent, invertible mapping between numerical and visual domains, allowing pre-trained vision transformers to solve the task without fine-tuning or architectural modification.

Result: Experiments across epidemiology, meteorology, and power systems demonstrate that ICI-Time performs competitively against deep learning baselines and shows promising adaptability under limited-data settings.

Conclusion: The framework introduces a new paradigm that bridges temporal and visual domains, demonstrating that pre-trained vision models can effectively handle time series forecasting through visual in-context learning, particularly excelling in data-scarce scenarios.

Abstract: We propose ICI-Time, a novel framework that reframes time series forecasting as a visual inpainting task, leveraging the generalisation power of large vision models (LVMs). Unlike methods that require specialised temporal architectures and extensive domain-specific training, ICI-Time transforms time series into structured visual representations (area charts) and applies visual in-context learning, reformulating forecasting as pattern completion within a grid-structured prompt that pre-trained vision transformers can solve without fine-tuning or architectural modification. Temporal dependencies are represented through spatial layout, with a consistent, invertible mapping between numerical and visual domains. Extensive experiments across epidemiology, meteorology, and power systems demonstrate that ICI-Time performs competitively against deep learning baselines and shows promising adaptability under limited-data settings, introducing a new paradigm that bridges temporal and visual domains.

</details>


### [25] [Granite.Trust Policy Tools: Shareable, Actionable Policies for Generative AI Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23870)
*Nathalie Baracaldo,Nicolas Mello,Kush R. Varshney,Heiko Ludwig,Kate Soule,David Cox*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Actionable Policy schema, a YAML-based format for specifying content-based constraints for GenAI model responses, along with a synthetic data generation pipeline and enforcement tools. This enables organizations to specify safety policies once and enforce them across the entire GenAI lifecycle—from model alignment to runtime monitoring—addressing the gap where traditional access control policies fail to capture GenAI content constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing policy specification approaches are designed for traditional access control and fail to capture the nuances of Generative AI applications, particularly the enforcement of content-based constraints. Different organizations and use cases require different risk mitigations depending on application context, regulatory environment, organizational values, and user personas, yet no suitable policy format exists for GenAI safety.

Method: The authors propose two main contributions: (1) the Actionable Policy schema, a YAML-based format for specifying allowable content in model responses with exception-based policy governance that tracks policy violations; and (2) a synthetic data generation pipeline that produces policy-aligned training data for model alignment and testing, plus supporting tools for schema definition and policy enforcement.

Result: The framework enables organizations to specify policies once and enforce them throughout the GenAI application lifecycle, from model alignment to runtime monitoring. The schema, example policies, and tools are released as open source on GitHub (ibm-granite/granite.trust.policy-tools).

Conclusion: The Actionable Policy schema and associated toolkit fill the gap in GenAI policy specification by providing a content-aware, exception-based policy governance approach that works across the full application lifecycle, and the authors welcome community contributions to further develop the framework.

Abstract: When it comes to safety policies for generative AI, one size does not fit all. Each organization and use case needs to mitigate different risks depending on the application context, regulatory environment, organizational values, and user personas. Yet, existing policy specification approaches are designed for traditional access control and fail to capture the nuances of GenAI application: the enforcement of content-based constraints.
  We present two contributions to address this gap: (1) the Actionable Policy schema, a YAML-based format for specifying what model responses can and cannot contain. The schema enables exception-based policy governance, proposing exceptions to track policy violations; (2) synthetic data generation pipeline that produces policy-aligned training data for model alignment and testing, and a set of tools to help define the schema and enforce policy. Together, these enable organizations to specify policies once and enforce them throughout the GenAI application lifecycle: from model alignment to runtime monitoring. The Actionable Policy schema, example policies, and tools are available as open source: https://github.com/ibm-granite/granite.trust.policy-tools We welcome new ideas, contributions and feedback.

</details>


### [26] [Semantic Overlays: Mitigating Prompt Injection with Annotations Beyond Tokens and Steering Vectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23873)
*Joshua Penman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Semantic Overlays, small learned adapters applied to residual streams at chosen prefill positions of a frozen language model, creating a non-textual channel to communicate span identity. This out-of-band annotation channel defends against prompt injection attacks by marking spans (e.g., as "non-executable"), dramatically reducing attack success across benchmarks while preserving content readability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language models see everything as tokens and must infer span identity (user input, tool output, instructions) from text alone, which they can lose track of or be confused by. Prompt injection exploits this by scrambling the model's understanding of span boundaries/identity, inducing unwanted actions. A non-textual channel communicating span identity beyond text would mitigate this attack class.

Method: Semantic Overlays are small learned adapters applied at chosen prefill positions to a frozen model's residual stream. Unlike steering vectors, they are trained, adaptable, and selectively applied. Overlays create an out-of-band annotation channel that tokens cannot replicate, can encode complex semantics (e.g., asserting a span is in another programming language), are composable, allow transparent reading of underlying content, and can carry executable payloads such as imperatives. A key application is marking a span as "non-executable" to defend against prompt injections.

Result: On prompt injection benchmarks: SEP separation rises from 24.3% to 96.5% with unchanged utility (using their own scoring rule; they also correct a defect in the published grader). TensorTrust attack success rate drops from 34.8% to 6.6%. All four PIArena attack families drop to 0% compliance. Marked spans remain readable with 92.5% exact copy rate.

Conclusion: Semantic Overlays provide an effective, general steering technique for adding non-textual span-identity information to LLM inputs, strongly mitigating prompt injection attacks while preserving content readability and utility. The approach offers a principled defense that addresses the root cause of prompt injection: the model's inability to distinguish span identity from tokens alone.

Abstract: Everything a language model sees is tokens. The serving stack knows what each span is -- user input, tool output, instructions -- but the model must keep track of that itself, and it can lose track or be confused: text can be written to read like anything. Prompt injection is a natural exploit of this phenomenon. By scrambling the model's understanding of span identity, an attacker can induce unwanted and potentially dangerous actions. Adding a non-textual channel to the model's input -- a way to communicate span identity beyond text -- mitigates this class of attack. We thus introduce a general steering technique called Semantic Overlays: small learned adapters applied at chosen prefill positions to a frozen model's residual stream. Laying an overlay over a span creates an out-of-band annotation channel that cannot be replicated by tokens. Unlike steering vectors, Semantic Overlays are trained, adaptable, and selectively applied. An overlay can encode complex semantics that reshape how the model perceives the marked span: asked to copy a code snippet under an overlay asserting that it is in a different programming language than it is, the model rewrites the snippet, faithfully, in the asserted language. Overlays are also composable, allow for transparent reading of underlying content, and can carry complex payloads -- including imperatives that the model will follow. An overlay which marks a span as "non-executable" defends against the broad class of prompt injections that add instructions in untrusted context. We report strong results on prompt injection benchmarks: SEP separation rises from 24.3% to 96.5% with utility unchanged (our scoring rule; we also correct a defect in the published grader), TensorTrust attack success rate falls from 34.8% to 6.6%, and all four PIArena attack families drop to 0% compliance, all while marked spans stay readable (92.5% exact copy rate).

</details>


### [27] [AI Finds A Way](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23875)
*Aaron Dharna,Cong Lu,Ryan Sullivan,Joel Lehman,Victoria Krakovna,Jeff Clune*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper curates 26 firsthand anecdotes from over 100 researchers across machine learning subfields, documenting cases where AI systems circumvent human-imposed constraints, exploit reward loopholes, or make unexpected discoveries. The authors argue these unconventional behaviors are common in modern AI, pose fundamental challenges for AI safety and alignment, and can paradoxically be harnessed to accelerate scientific discovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accounts of unexpected, creative, or unconventional AI behavior are seldom formally documented despite being frequently encountered in practice. The authors aim to consolidate these anecdotes into a formal resource to illustrate the systematic nature of such behaviors and their implications for AI safety and alignment research.

Method: The paper compiles 26 curated firsthand anecdotes gathered from over 100 researchers across various machine learning subfields. It organizes these anecdotes into thematic categories: (1) superhuman performance via reinforcement learning, (2) reward hacking and failure of underspecified reward/constraint specifications, (3) case studies on internet-scale foundation models showing these issues persist and are amplified, and (4) examples where such learning dynamics are leveraged for scientific discovery.

Result: The collection demonstrates that unexpected and creative AI behaviors are pervasive across modern systems, that reward-driven optimization remains vulnerable to reward hacking even with foundation models (which can supercharge these issues), and that the same learning dynamics can be repurposed to accelerate scientific discovery rather than only causing safety concerns.

Conclusion: The tendency toward unexpected behaviors is commonplace in modern AI, highlighting the fundamental challenge of aligning models with human values without stifling their creativity. The work provides a consolidated resource to inform future research and argues for the need to anticipate and manage AI's capacity for innovative yet unpredictable solutions.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI) algorithms frequently learn creative and unexpected solutions, surprising even expert researchers who develop and study them. They often astonish practitioners by discovering unanticipated behavior, exploiting loopholes in reward signals, or spontaneously uncovering previously unknown scientific phenomena. However, accounts of such unconventional behavior across machine learning are seldom formally documented. This work presents 26 curated firsthand anecdotes from various machine learning subfields representing the work of over 100 researchers. These anecdotes showcase the capability of modern AI systems to circumvent human-imposed design limitations and discover unexpected solutions to the tasks we train them on. Furthermore, these accounts are particularly important for the safety of future AI systems. They illustrate the fundamental challenge of aligning models with human values without diminishing their creativity, so they can make surprising discoveries without producing surprising, potentially harmful outcomes. The paper first details AI achieving superhuman success through reinforcement learning across many challenging domains. However, reward-driven optimization can fail when the model learns to hack an underspecified reward or unarticulated constraint. We then present case studies suggesting that harnessing internet-scale foundation models (FMs) has not resolved these fundamental challenges and, in fact, can supercharge them. Nevertheless, we argue that these same learning dynamics can be harnessed to accelerate scientific discovery. Finally, we hope this work provides a consolidated resource to inform future research and demonstrates that the tendency toward unexpected behaviors is commonplace in modern AI, highlighting the need to anticipate and manage AI's capacity for innovative, yet unpredictable, solutions. (abstract abridged)

</details>


### [28] [Provenance Guided Incremental Learning Under Evolving Concept Definitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23893)
*Ismail Lamaakal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a provenance-guided incremental learning framework for handling rule-induced concept shift, where target-defining concepts are explicitly revised. The framework compiles concept definitions into a rule delta, traces affected records through provenance, and restricts reevaluation to a localized region. It introduces RuleShift-Bench and achieves 92.3% accuracy while reprocessing only 14.7% of historical data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional concept-drift methods infer changes from data or prediction errors, but fail to exploit cases where the target-defining rule/policy/query has been explicitly modified. This paper addresses rule-induced concept shift, where previously stored instances acquire different semantic labels due to direct concept revisions, without any change in the observed data itself.

Method: The framework (1) compiles consecutive concept definitions into a structured rule delta, (2) traces changed components through historical provenance, (3) certifies records whose labels remain valid, (4) restricts reevaluation to a localized candidate region, (5) auto-relabels executable revisions while handling ambiguous cases via selective supervision, (6) uses resulting changes for incremental predictor repair, and (7) maintains a versioned concept memory for recurring definitions. A benchmark, RuleShift-Bench, is introduced covering financial, demographic, cybersecurity, and graph data with six revision types.

Result: Provenance-guided repair achieves 92.3% accuracy and 90.2% Macro-F1 while reprocessing only 14.7% of the historical collection and retaining 94.6% of affected records. Average update latency is 179s versus 993s for complete relabeling and retraining.

Conclusion: Explicit concept revisions can be exploited as a data-maintenance signal, enabling learning systems to update only the supervision and predictive state that depends on the change while preserving valid knowledge, rather than requiring full relabeling and retraining.

Abstract: Learning systems deployed over long periods must adapt not only to statistical changes in incoming data, but also to revisions of the definitions that generate their prediction targets. Conventional concept-drift methods typically infer such changes from observations or prediction errors, even when the underlying policy, rule, or query has been explicitly modified. This paper studies rule-induced concept shift, where the target-defining concept is revised directly, causing previously stored instances to acquire different semantic labels without requiring any change in their observed data. We introduce a provenance-guided incremental learning framework that compiles consecutive concept definitions into a structured rule delta, traces the changed components through historical provenance, certifies records whose previous labels remain valid, and restricts reevaluation to a localized candidate region. Executable revisions are relabeled automatically, ambiguous cases are handled through selective supervision, and the resulting changes are used for incremental predictor repair. A versioned concept memory further supports recurring definitions. We also introduce RuleShift-Bench, spanning financial, demographic, cybersecurity, and graph-structured data with threshold, predicate, logical, relational, recurring, and mixed concept revisions. Across the benchmark, provenance-guided repair attains 92.3% accuracy and 90.2% Macro-F1 while reprocessing 14.7% of the historical collection and retaining 94.6% of affected records. Its average update latency is 179s compared with 993s for complete relabeling and retraining. The results demonstrate that an explicit concept revision can be exploited as a data-maintenance signal, allowing learning systems to update the supervision and predictive state that depend on the change while preserving knowledge that remains valid.

</details>


### [29] [BenchBench-Protocol: Evaluating Real-World Wet-Lab Protocol Reasoning and Modification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23898)
*Aditya Sivakumar,Ashu Singhal,Nicholas Larus-Stone,Nithin Parsan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BenchBench-Protocol is a benchmark for LLMs containing 149 protocol-modification tasks derived from real changes scientists made to published wet-lab protocols. It covers nine biology domains and 96 source protocols, with expert-reviewed rubric grading. Claude Opus 5 achieves the highest normalized rubric score at 59.2%, while other models range from 34.1% to 47.1%, indicating the benchmark remains unsaturated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adapting published protocols to new experiments is a routine wet-lab task requiring reasoning about prior choices and downstream steps. Existing life-science benchmarks rely on expert-elicited tasks rather than reconstructing tasks from real-world modifications, limiting their grounding in actual experimental practice.

Method: Tasks are reconstructed from differences between published protocols and versions modified by scientists during real experiments, providing the query basis and weighted rubric elements. The benchmark includes 149 tasks across 96 source protocols and nine wet-lab biology domains, with expert review filtering for task quality.

Result: Nine closed and open models were evaluated. Claude Opus 5 scored highest at 59.2% normalized rubric score; other models scored between 34.1% and 47.1%. The benchmark remains unsaturated even when taking the best of ten attempts per model.

Conclusion: BenchBench-Protocol provides a grounded assessment of wet-lab reasoning for LLMs and demonstrates that real-world experimental modifications are a valuable source for constructing benchmark tasks as models become increasingly integrated into life-sciences research.

Abstract: We introduce BenchBench-Protocol, a benchmark for large language models of 149 protocol-modification tasks recovered from modifications that scientists made to published protocols during real experimental work. Adapting a published protocol to a new experiment is a routine task for a wet-lab scientist, and a correct modification requires accounting for prior choices and downstream steps. Recent life-science benchmarks have moved toward open-ended, rubric-graded tasks, but tasks are typically elicited from experts rather than reconstructed from real-world modifications. BenchBench-Protocol tasks are derived from differences between a published protocol and a version a scientist modified, which provides the basis for the query and the weighted rubric elements for a correct response. The benchmark draws from 96 source protocols across nine domains of wet-lab biology and only includes tasks rated highly after review by domain experts. We evaluate nine closed and open models; Claude Opus 5 scores highest at 59.2% normalized rubric score, with other models between 34.1% and 47.1%, and the benchmark remains unsaturated when taking the best of ten attempts. As models are increasingly helpful in life-sciences research, evaluating them on routine wet-lab tasks becomes correspondingly important. We present BenchBench-Protocol as both a grounded assessment of wet-lab reasoning and evidence for the utility of real-world experiments to construct benchmark tasks.

</details>


### [30] [Quantifying System-Level Harms from AI Adoption in Complex Sociotechnical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23906)
*Paul Vautravers,Oliver Chalkley,Gabriel Downer,Kate S,Damian Ruck*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a framework for bridging model-centric AI evaluation to system-level risk assessment in Critical National Infrastructure by linking structured hazard analysis (STPA), component-level testing, and probabilistic system modelling. Applied to the UK's RTGS system, the framework demonstrates that adversarial manipulation of LLM-based trading can measurably alter system resilience, increasing bank failures and lowering cascade thresholds, particularly under widespread or monopolistic AI adoption.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI evaluation approaches are model-centric and fail to translate observed model behaviours into system-level risks. As AI is increasingly integrated into complex sociotechnical systems like CNI, harms arise from interactions between technical, human, and organisational elements, requiring a more holistic assessment approach.

Method: The framework combines three elements: (1) structured hazard analysis using Systems Theoretic Process Analysis (STPA) to derive AI-driven loss scenarios, (2) component-level testing to measure behavioural shifts from adversarial inputs, and (3) probabilistic system modelling (a financial contagion model) to map component-level behaviours to system-level outcomes.

Result: Simple adversarial inputs induce measurable behavioural shifts where AI recommendations are followed. Under component-to-system mapping in a financial contagion model, these shifts increase bank failures and lower the threshold at which shocks trigger cascading disruption, particularly under widespread or monopolistic AI adoption scenarios.

Conclusion: The framework provides a traceable pathway from model behaviour to system-level outcomes, enabling practitioners to answer the "so what?" of AI failures, quantify systemic impact, and support evidence-based and anticipatory governance of AI in complex systems.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI) is increasingly integrated into complex sociotechnical systems, including Critical National Infrastructure (CNI), where harms emerge from interactions between technical, human, and organisational elements. Yet current AI evaluation remains model-centric, offering little insight into how observed behaviours might translate into system-level risk. We propose a framework that links structured hazard analysis, component-level testing, and probabilistic system modelling to bridge this gap. By providing a traceable pathway from model behaviour to system-level outcomes, the framework enables practitioners to answer the "so what?" of AI failures, quantify their systemic impact, and move toward evidence-based and anticipatory governance of AI in complex systems. Applied to the UK's Real Time Gross Settlement (RTGS) system as an illustrative worked example, we derive AI-driven loss scenarios using Systems Theoretic Process Analysis (STPA) and examine adversarial manipulation of LLM-based trading as one such loss scenario. Component-level experiments show that simple adversarial inputs induce measurable behavioural shifts where AI recommendations are followed. Under the component-to-system mapping used here for a financial contagion model, these shifts alter system resilience, increasing bank failures and lowering the threshold at which shocks lead to cascading disruption, particularly under widespread or monopolistic AI adoption.

</details>


### [31] [Retrieval-augmented generation vs. deterministic tax computation in multi-agent financial advisory: A 2x2 factorial experiment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23908)
*Aryan Brar,Justin Du,Avery Lor,Kylie Seto,Eric Taylor*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a custom capital gains calculation engine and a RAG-retrieved vector store of market advisory reports to provide context for a multi-agent trade recommendation system for tax-loss harvesting. A 2x2 repeated-measures ANOVA found the tax engine had a significant main effect (reducing tax savings by ~55 percentage points), while the RAG context and interaction were not significant. Surprisingly, the RAG-only and baseline conditions achieved the highest descriptive tax savings, suggesting LLM agents' internalized financial knowledge may suffice without explicit tooling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tax-loss harvesting offers consistent long-term portfolio benefits but is complex to implement efficiently, depending on portfolio-specific holdings and individual circumstances. The authors aim to determine whether augmenting LLM agents with domain-specific computation engines and RAG-retrieved advisory reports improves the quality of trade recommendations for tax-loss harvesting.

Method: The system combines (1) a custom capital gains calculation engine and (2) a RAG-retrieved vector store of market advisory reports as context providers for a multi-agent trade recommendation system. The authors employed a 2x2 repeated-measures ANOVA to assess the effects of each context provider on recommendation quality, measured by relative capital gains incurred during portfolio liquidation.

Result: The 2x2 repeated-measures ANOVA revealed a significant main effect of the tax optimization engine (F(1,29)=9.17, p=.005, η²p=.240), with the engine reducing tax savings by approximately 55 percentage points. The RAG main effect was not significant (p=.841), nor was the interaction (p=.553). The RAG-only condition achieved the highest descriptive mean tax savings (47.7%), while the baseline condition achieved 30.6%.

Conclusion: Augmenting LLM agents with domain-specific computation engines does not guarantee improved performance and may introduce conflicting optimization signals. The pre-trained language model's internalized financial knowledge appears sufficient for competent tax-loss harvesting recommendations without explicit external tooling.

Abstract: Tax-loss harvesting demonstrates consistent benefits to long-term portfolio growth; yet implementing it efficiently often involves complex considerations that are specific to the holdings within that portfolio and the individual who owns it. We introduce a custom capital gains calculation engine and a RAG-retrieved vector store of market advisory reports to provide context for a multi-agent trade recommendation system. We investigate the effects of each context provider on the quality of recommendations, measured by relative capital gains incurred during portfolio liquidation. A 2x2 repeated-measures ANOVA revealed a significant main effect of the tax optimization engine ($F(1,29) = 9.17$, $p = .005$, $η^2_p = .240$): enabling the engine reduced tax savings by approximately 55 percentage points relative to the no-engine conditions. The RAG main effect was not significant ($p = .841$), nor was the interaction ($p = .553$). The RAG-only condition achieved the highest descriptive mean tax savings (47.7%), and the baseline condition performed second-best (30.6%), suggesting that the pre-trained language model's internalized financial knowledge may be sufficient for competent tax-loss harvesting recommendations without explicit tooling. These results indicate that augmenting LLM agents with domain-specific computation engines does not guarantee improved performance and may introduce conflicting optimization signals.

</details>


### [32] [PROOF-Gen: From Optimized Data to Better Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23911)
*Anh Ta,Junjie Zhu,Shahin Shayandeh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PROOF-Gen recovers golden trajectories from failed teacher attempts in tool-calling distillation by using a reflector to perform per-scenario prompt optimization, recovering 93% of failed scenarios on τ2-bench and significantly improving downstream student model performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard tool-calling distillation pipelines re-run generate-and-filter cycles from frontier teachers on a daily/weekly cadence, but each cycle fails on the same hard scenarios since failures provide no training signal—on τ2-bench, 57% of teacher trials fail, with two-thirds being near-misses undone by a single decisive error.

Method: For each failed task, a reflector analyzes the execution trace and evaluation feedback to write corrective guidance steering the teacher toward a passing trajectory. The guidance is stripped before training, so students learn from clean demonstrations without task-specific scaffolding.

Result: Per-scenario optimization recovers 93% of failed scenarios on τ2-bench. Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 improves from Pass^1=0.132 to 0.529; Gemma 4 E4B-it gains +7.2pp on BFCL v4 multi-turn. In a deployed pipeline, trajectory quality improves +6.3pp goal completion with positive transfer to an on-device model (+1.5pp goal completion; +1.7 to +5.0pp response quality), including non-English locales (+1.48pp average).

Conclusion: Leveraging near-miss failures as a signal source via reflective per-scenario optimization can drastically improve tool-calling distillation quality, directly benefiting deployed pipelines and exhibiting positive cross-locale and on-device transfer.

Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning on teacher-generated trajectories is the standard first stage for distilling tool-calling capabilities into deployable models. Post-training pipelines that drive shipped tool-calling agents re-run this stage on a daily or weekly cadence, paying the frontier-teacher cost each cycle, yet the mechanism is generate-and-filter (keep the teacher's passing trajectories, discard the rest) and each cycle leaves behind the same hard scenarios because failures supply no signal. On τ2-bench, 57% of teacher trials fail, two-thirds of them near-misses (most tool calls correct, undone by one decisive error).
  We introduce PROOF-Gen (Per-scenario Reflective Optimization to Overcome FailedGeneration), which recovers golden trajectories from these failures via per-scenario prompt optimization. For each failed task, a reflector analyzes the execution trace and evaluation feedback, then writes corrective guidance that steers the teacher to a passing trajectory. The guidance is stripped before training, so the student learns from clean demonstrations with no task-specific scaffold.
  On τ2-bench, per-scenario optimization recovers 93% of failed scenarios. Fine-tuned on the combined data, Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 improves from Pass^1=0.132 to 0.529 and Gemma 4 E4B-it gains +7.2pp on BFCL v4 multi-turn. In a deployed pipeline, the method lifts trajectory quality by +6.3pp goal completion and transfers to a deployed on-device model (+1.5pp goal completion; +1.7 to +5.0pp across response-quality metrics), with positive transfer in every locale (non-English average +1.48pp).

</details>


### [33] [MARS: Multi-Specialist LLM Relay System for Competitive Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23918)
*Andrei Mikhailov,Mikhail Burtsev,Alsu Sagirova*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MARS is a prompt-only multi-agent framework for competitive programming where each agent is a topic specialist (dynamic programming, graphs, strings, geometry, etc.) grounded by retrieval-augmented generation over an algorithm-theory corpus. It achieves 0.624±0.006 pass rate on CodeContests with Gemma 4, a +14.4 percentage point improvement over direct prompting, while using fewer pipeline stages and 3.3× lower wall-clock cost than the stronger CodeSIM baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent code generation pipelines use generic planner/coder/debugger roles and leave algorithmic technique selection entirely to the backbone LLM, which fails on competitive programming tasks that require specialized algorithmic knowledge.

Method: MARS organizes agents by algorithmic topic specialization (e.g., DP, graphs, strings, geometry). Retrieval over an algorithm-theory corpus selects a small team of relevant specialists per problem. A starter writes an initial C++17 solution; each subsequent turn runs the candidate against public examples in a sandbox, lets the active specialist keep, repair, or hand off the draft, and forwards a structured packet to the next specialist. A final infrastructure-fixer pass normalizes boilerplate.

Result: On CodeContests with Gemma 4, MARS achieves 0.624±0.006 pass rate at 2.3 pipeline stages per task, +14.4 percentage points over direct prompting, and closes most of the gap to CodeSIM (0.731) at 3.3× lower wall-clock cost with substantially lower variance in per-task token spend.

Conclusion: Specialized-topic multi-agent routing with retrieval grounding over algorithmic knowledge significantly improves competitive programming performance over generic role-based pipelines, at lower cost and with more predictable token usage.

Abstract: Large Language Models excel at code generation, yet competitive programming exposes a persistent failure mode: existing multi-agent pipelines distribute work over generic planner, coder, and debugger roles and delegate the choice of algorithmic technique to the backbone alone. We present MARS (Multi-Agent Relay of Specialized LLMs), a prompt-only framework in which each agent is a topic specialist---dynamic programming, graphs, strings, geometry, and so on---grounded by retrieval-augmented generation over an algorithm-theory corpus. Given a problem, retrieval selects a small team of relevant specialists; a starter writes an initial C++17 solution, and each subsequent turn runs the candidate against public examples in a sandbox, lets the active specialist keep, repair, or hand off the draft, and forwards a structured packet to the next specialist. A single infrastructure-fixer pass normalizes boilerplate at the end. On the CodeContests test split with Gemma 4, MARS reaches $0.624 \pm 0.006$ pass rate at $2.3$ recorded pipeline stages per task ($+14.4$ percentage points over direct prompting), closing most of the gap to CodeSIM ($0.731$) at $3.3{\times}$ lower wall-clock cost and substantially smaller variance in per-task token spend. The source code is available on GitHub: https://github.com/fckand/mars.

</details>


### [34] [Data Mixing as Mixture Experiment: Response Surface Methodology and Optimal Design for Large Language Model Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23922)
*Yicheng Mao,Hongru Du*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper reframes LLM data mixing as a classical mixture experiment, applying sparse second-order Scheffé response-surface models and model-robust I-optimal designs to proxy-trained data-mixing experiments. Using RegMix as a case study, they show that domain value is strongly relational (interactions, especially with web text, matter), that sparse Scheffé models preserve mixture rankings across scales, and that I-optimal designs recover mixture ordering with ~25% fewer proxy runs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data mixing is a central design problem in LLM pretraining, but current proxy-based methods treat it primarily as a prediction problem rather than recognizing the experimental-design structure underlying the workflow. This limits statistical efficiency in selecting proxy mixtures for larger-scale training.

Method: The authors cast the data-mixing workflow as a classical mixture experiment: domains = mixture components, token shares = proportions, proxy runs = design points, and validation loss = a response surface over the probability simplex. They use sparse second-order Scheffé response-surface models to fit this surface and construct model-robust I-optimal designs for proxy experiments, demonstrated through the RegMix framework.

Result: The Scheffé analysis reveals that domain value is strongly relational—several weak additive domains become favourable through pairwise interactions, especially with web-derived text. The sparse Scheffé model preserves mixture rankings across model scales and is competitive with flexible ML predictors while providing an explicit decomposition of additive and interaction effects. Simulation studies show model-robust I-optimal designs recover mixture ordering after removing ~25% of original proxy runs.

Conclusion: LLM data mixing should be treated not only as a prediction problem but also as an experimental-design problem, where the choice of proxy mixtures themselves can be optimized to improve statistical efficiency in selecting data mixtures for large-scale training.

Abstract: Data mixing is a central design problem in large language model pretraining: given a fixed token budget, practitioners must decide how much data to allocate to each domain. Recent proxy-based methods address this problem by training small models on candidate mixtures, fitting a response model, and using the response to select mixtures for larger-scale training. We show that this workflow has the structure of a classical mixture experiment. Under this view, data domains are mixture components, token shares are component proportions, proxy-training runs are experimental design points, and validation loss defines a response surface over the probability simplex. We develop this formulation using sparse second-order Scheffé response-surface models and construct model-robust $\mathcal{I}$-optimal designs for proxy data-mixing experiments. Using RegMix as an empirical case study, we demonstrate how the framework can both interpret observed mixture responses and design more efficient proxy experiments. The Scheffé analysis shows that domain value is strongly relational: several domains that are weak under additive effects become favourable through pairwise interactions, especially through combinations with web-derived text. The sparse Scheffé model preserves mixture rankings across model scales and remains competitive with a flexible machine-learning predictor while providing an explicit decomposition of additive and interaction effects. In a simulation study calibrated to observed proxy-training responses, model-robust $\mathcal{I}$-optimal designs recover the relevant mixture ordering after removing about 25\% of the original proxy runs. These results suggest that LLM data mixing should be treated not only as a prediction problem, but also as an experimental-design problem in which the proxy mixtures themselves can be chosen to improve statistical efficiency.

</details>


### [35] [Evolutionary Recurrent Decision Model in Developing Adaptive and Maladaptive Behaviors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23932)
*Andrew Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces ERDM, a computational reinforcement learning framework that models evolutionary mismatch, bounded rationality, and satisficing to explain adaptive and maladaptive behavior. Agents learn in evolutionary recurrent environments (threat, prey/goal-pursuits, alliances) using competing survival-reward abstractions. A validity study with varying adverse childhood experiences shows that strategies like learned helplessness, avoidance, healthy relationships, and aggression emerge naturally, aligning with empirical literature and demonstrating ecological validity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a computational cognitive framework that bridges evolutionary mismatch, bounded rationality, and satisficing to explain both adaptive and maladaptive behavioral outcomes, offering a mechanistic account of psychopathology that is not hardwired but emergent from learning in evolutionarily relevant environments.

Method: The evolutionarily recurrent decision model (ERDM) is a reinforcement learning framework where agents operate across evolutionary recurrent environments (threat, prey/goal-pursuits, alliances). Agents learn by competing rewards abstracted from survival metrics. A validity study manipulates adverse childhood experiences to test whether distinct behavioral strategies emerge organically from the learning process.

Result: Simulations produced distinct adaptive and maladaptive strategies—learned helplessness, avoidance, healthy relationships, and aggression—that emerged naturally without hardcoding. These emergent strategies align with empirical literature, confirming the model's ecological validity.

Conclusion: Psychopathology-relevant behaviors can be interpreted as bounded cognitive systems operating under modern-ancestral environmental mismatch. ERDM serves as a foundational computational cognitive tool that can be extended to broader studies in computational psychiatry and evolutionary psychology.

Abstract: This study introduces the evolutionarily recurrent decision model (ERDM), a computational reinforcement learning framework designed to examine how evolutionary mismatch, bounded rationality, and satisficing contribute to adaptive and maladaptive behavior. ERDM simulates agents across evolutionary recurrent environments, including threat, prey/goal-pursuits, and alliances. Agents learn through competing rewards abstracted from survival metrics. A validity study under varying adverse childhood experiences demonstrates that distinct adaptive and maladaptive strategies, such as learned helplessness, avoidance, healthy relationships, and aggression, emerge naturally without being hardwired. These results align with empirical literature, showcasing ecological validity. The results suggest that many psychopathology-relevant aspects may be interpreted as bounded cognitive systems operating under modern-ancestral environmental mismatch, positioning ERDM as a key computational cognitive tool that can be extended to other studies.

</details>


### [36] [More Rejective, Not More Discriminative: The Unit of Verification in Pre-Execution LLM Oversight](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23941)
*Yuchen Han,Cheng Yan,Wuyang Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces the twin-prefix framework, a controlled pre-registered instrument for measuring how the unit of verification (number of actions per review call) affects fallible LLM monitors in AI control. It shows that while longer review lengths increase both catch and false rejection rates, informedness (discrimination) peaks at one or two actions per review across all six judges tested, as longer windows make monitors more rejective rather than more discriminative.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pre-execution monitoring by fallible LLMs is core to AI control, but the choice of verification unit—how many actions one review call covers—has been taken as given despite its unmeasured effect. Natural traces cannot isolate this factor because review length co-varies with error type and position, and catch rate alone is misleading since rejecting everything catches everything. There is a need for a controlled measurement instrument that varies only the review unit while holding everything else constant.

Method: The authors introduce the twin-prefix framework. Each gold plan yields a prefix containing one injected, environment-accepted error and a clean twin that differs by a single write. Both are judged at five nested review lengths, so that any verdict differences are attributable solely to the unit of verification. Discrimination is scored using pre-registered informedness (catch minus false rejection), which accounts for both sensitivity and over-blocking.

Result: Longer review windows increase catch but false rejection increases in lockstep. Informedness peaks at one or two actions per review across all six judges in both tested domains, indicating longer windows make zero-shot monitors more rejective, not more discriminative. Replaying withheld observations shows the failure is largely due to observation deprivation. The calibrated short unit recovers up to 0.95 informedness over eight-action review, and no tested label-blind policy consistently outperforms it.

Conclusion: Safety cases for AI control should explicitly state the unit of verification and co-report clean-series performance rather than reporting catch alone. The twin-prefix framework is the first controlled, pre-registered instrument for measuring this design choice, and shorter review units (one to two actions) are recommended for maximizing monitor discrimination.

Abstract: Pre-execution oversight is core to trusted monitoring in AI control: a fallible LLM monitor vets planned actions before irreversible execution. Over-blocking forfeits usefulness and pressures deployers to disable it. Every protocol must fix a unit of verification: how many actions one call reviews. Existing designs take the unit as given; its effect on fallible monitors is unmeasured. Natural traces cannot isolate it: review length co-varies with error type and position. Catch alone misleads: rejecting everything catches everything. Measuring this needs boundary variation alone and a matched clean control. We introduce the twin-prefix framework, which supplies both. Each gold plan yields a prefix with one injected, environment-accepted error and a clean twin differing in one write. Judging each pair at five nested lengths ties verdict changes to the unit alone. Discrimination is scored by pre-registered informedness, catch minus false rejection. Longer review raises catch; false rejection climbs in lockstep. Informedness peaks at one or two actions for all six judges in both domains: longer windows make zero-shot monitors more rejective, not more discriminative. Replaying withheld observations traces the failure largely to observation deprivation. Safety cases should state the unit and co-report the clean series. Our framework is the first controlled, pre-registered instrument for this choice and never reads catch alone. Our calibrated short unit recovers up to 0.95 informedness over eight-action review, and no tested label-blind policy consistently beats it.

</details>


### [37] [Recursive Agentic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23956)
*Shengxin Zhang,Xiaomin Wu,Xiyang Wu,Jing Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a unified framework for test-time reasoning methods—GROW, PRUNE, and BRANCH—as recursion operators over an agent's reasoning trace. Under a shared evaluation harness across five benchmarks and three frontier models (14 settings, 49,327 graded items, 151,876 model calls), BRANCH consistently dominates, improving accuracy in all 14 settings by an average of 5.98 percentage points, while GROW and PRUNE show smaller and less consistent gains. BRANCH's advantage stems from both path diversity and recovery from budget truncation (correlation r=0.72 with baseline empty-output rate). The paper also demonstrates that unpaired evaluation and pipeline-failure misattribution can reverse comparative conclusions, advocating paired scoring as a standard protocol.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Test-time reasoning methods (iterative refinement, decomposition, repeated sampling) are evaluated in isolation across different models, benchmarks, and pipelines, making their relative gains incomparable. The authors aim to unify these methods under a single framework and evaluate them under identical conditions to determine which operator is genuinely best.

Method: The paper formalizes test-time reasoning methods as three recursion operators over reasoning traces: GROW (deepening a single path), PRUNE (decomposing and recomposing the problem), and BRANCH (sampling alternative paths and selecting among them). All three are evaluated against a single-pass chain-of-thought baseline using a shared harness with identical prompts, token budgets, and grading code across five benchmarks and three frontier models, comprising 14 model-benchmark settings, 49,327 graded items, and 151,876 model calls.

Result: BRANCH improves accuracy in all 14 settings with an average gain of 5.98 percentage points and is the best operator in 12 settings. GROW yields a mean gain of 2.18 points and degrades performance in two settings. PRUNE improves accuracy by 0.94 points on average. BRANCH's advantage correlates strongly with baseline rates of empty, budget-exhausted outputs (r=0.72), indicating its effectiveness partly stems from recovering from truncation. The paper also shows unpaired evaluation and treating scoring-pipeline failures as model errors can reverse conclusions, favoring paired scoring.

Conclusion: At the abstraction level of recursion operators, repeated branching (BRANCH) is consistently dominant, weakening the hypothesis that different problems require routing among different test-time reasoning operators. The paper advocates paired scoring as a standard evaluation protocol for test-time-compute methods to avoid misleading comparative conclusions.

Abstract: Test-time reasoning methods such as iterative refinement, decomposition, and repeated sampling are often evaluated in isolation, making their gains difficult to compare across models, benchmarks, and evaluation pipelines. We introduce a unified view of these methods as recursion operators over an agent's reasoning trace: GROW, which deepens a single reasoning path; PRUNE, which decomposes and recomposes the problem; and BRANCH, which samples alternative reasoning paths and selects among them. We evaluate all three operators against a single-pass chain-of-thought baseline under a shared harness with identical prompts, token budgets, and grading code. Across five benchmarks and three frontier models, comprising 14 model-benchmark settings, 49,327 graded items, and 151,876 model calls, BRANCH improves accuracy in all 14 settings by an average of 5.98 percentage points and is the best-performing operator in 12. In contrast, GROW yields a mean gain of 2.18 points and degrades performance in two settings, while PRUNE improves accuracy by 0.94 points on average. Analysis shows that BRANCH's advantage arises not only from exploring multiple reasoning paths, but also from recovering from truncation: its gains strongly correlate with the baseline rate of empty, budget-exhausted outputs (r = 0.72). These results weaken the hypothesis that different problems require routing among test-time reasoning operators; at this level of abstraction, repeated branching is consistently dominant. Finally, we show that unpaired evaluation and treating scoring-pipeline failures as model errors can materially change, and even reverse, comparative conclusions, motivating paired scoring as a standard protocol for test-time-compute evaluation.

</details>


### [38] [More GPUs or a Smaller Cache? Tensor Parallelism versus KV Compression for Memory-Bound LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23962)
*Srikanta Datta Tumkur,Mehar Simhadri,Anshu Bansal,Jay Iyer,Sai Pavan Kumar,Sai Kapil Kumar,Ramesh Nampelly,Raj Dandekar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper compares two strategies for handling KV cache exhaustion in LLM serving — tensor parallelism (scaling across GPUs) and KV compression (shrinking cache in place) — on a unified cost-normalized axis (cost per million tokens vs. latency). Using a calibrated simulator across A100/A40/H100 and Llama-2 7B/70B, the authors find no cost-equivalence crossover: compression is consistently 1.20x–2.00x cheaper across all settings. The decisive boundary is model size relative to device memory (~36B parameters for an 80GB GPU); below it compression dominates, above it tensor parallelism becomes mandatory since weights become the binding resource. Compression multiplies capacity per dollar 16.5x versus 1.21x for an eightfold GPU spend, while tensor parallelism remains the only latency-improving lever.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper identifies a gap in the literature: compression papers report memory ratios while parallel-scaling papers report throughput curves, but almost nobody places both strategies on the same cost axis. Without a unified cost-normalized comparison, practitioners cannot make principled decisions about when to scale out via tensor parallelism versus compress KV cache in place.

Method: The authors construct a unified cost-normalized axis (cost per million tokens against latency) and evaluate tensor-parallel configurations (degree 1–8) against KV-compressed configurations (16/8/4-bit, keep-ratios down to 0.25). They use a profiled simulator calibrated on A100, A40, and H100 hardware, tested across Llama-2 7B and 70B models, systematically varying memory relief levels to search for a cost-equivalence crossover.

Result: No cost-equivalence crossover was found: compression is consistently cheaper by 1.20x–2.00x across all tested configurations. A 7B model on an 80GB device cannot exhaust its KV budget within its context window. The boundary between strategies is model size relative to device memory (~36B on an 80GB card). Compression increases per-token latency by 8–93% due to batching contention, while tensor parallelism improves latency. Compression multiplies capacity per dollar by 16.5x versus only 1.21x for an eightfold GPU spend. Llama-2-70B is infeasible on a single A100 at any KV setting because weights (not KV cache) become the binding resource.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that for models below a device-memory-relative threshold (~36B on 80GB cards), KV compression dominates and additional GPUs are largely wasted spend. Above that threshold, tensor parallelism ceases to be an optimization choice and becomes a mandatory entry requirement because weights, which compression cannot address, become the limiting resource. The two levers are complementary: tensor parallelism is the only way to improve latency, while compression is the only way to multiply capacity per dollar.

Abstract: When an LLM serving deployment runs out of KVcache room, there are two well-established ways out. Tensor parallelism shards the weights and the KV cache across two, four, or eight devices, buying memory headroom at the price of an all-reduce on every layer and a hardware bill that grows with the device count. The algorithms community shrinks the cache in place, with KV quantisation and eviction keeping a single GPU and spending a little quality instead. Compression papers report memory ratios, parallel-scaling papers report throughput curves, and almost nobody puts the two on the same cost axis. We place tensor-parallel configurations (degree 1 to 8) and KV-compressed configurations (16/8/4-bit, keep-ratios down to 0.25) on one costnormalised axis, cost per million tokens against latency, using a profiled simulator calibrated on A100, A40, and H100 hardware, and we go looking for the cost-equivalence crossover. We do not find one. Across two models (Llama-2 at 7B and 70B), three GPU types, and every level of memory relief we could construct, compression is cheaper by 1.20x to 2.00x. A 7B model on an 80 GB device cannot exhaust its KV budget within its own context window, and the boundary that decides between the strategies is model size relative to device memory, at roughly 36B parameters for an 80 GB card. Below that wall, compression dominates and extra GPUs are largely wasted spend; above it, tensor parallelism stops being a choice and becomes an entry ticket: Llama-2-70B is infeasible on one A100 at any KV setting, because the binding resource is weights, which KV compression does not touch. Tensor parallelism is the only lever that improves latency (compression makes per-token latency worse, by 8 to 93%, through batching contention), while compression is the only lever that multiplies capacity per dollar (16.5x, against 1.21x for an eightfold spend on GPUs).

</details>


### [39] [Giraffe: A Mapping Architecture from Hidden Text Representations to Visual Embeddings for Efficient Graphic Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23970)
*Nejla Ghaboosi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a novel MLLM architecture that maps hidden token representations to visual model embedding spaces using a single [IMG] token per image, employing two shallow MLP blocks with separate compression modules and a shared expansion module trained with six loss functions. The dual-block design allows one block to assist during training but be omitted at inference, yielding a lightweight solution that performs well on image-to-design and text-to-design generation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for MLLM-based media generation translate hidden representations into visual embedding spaces using multiple specialized tokens per image, which significantly increases input length. This becomes a critical bottleneck for tasks like graphic design generation that require seamless blending of thousands of tokens across text, images, and layout information.

Method: The architecture maps hidden token representations to the embedding space of visual models (CLIP ViT-L/14) using a single [IMG] token per image. It employs two shallow MLP blocks, each with a separate compression module followed by a shared expansion module. The training uses six distinct loss functions. Notably, one MLP block assists the other during training but is omitted during inference, resulting in a lightweight deployment.

Result: Strong performance is demonstrated in both image-to-design and text-to-design generation tasks.

Conclusion: The proposed single-token-per-image compression architecture effectively addresses the input-length limitation of prior multi-token approaches while maintaining strong generation performance, offering a lightweight and efficient solution for multimodal design generation.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have made significant progress in understanding and interpreting mul- timedia content. However, their ability to generate me- dia remains limited. Recent approaches have attempted to bridge this gap by translating the hidden representations of token sequences into the embedding space of visual models or directly into raw image data. However, these methods often represent each image using multiple specialised to- kens which significantly increases the input length. This be- comes a major limitation for tasks such as graphic design generation where the output typically involves a seamless blend of thousands of tokens across text, multiple images, and layout information. To address this challenge, a novel architecture is proposed that maps hidden token represen- tations to the embedding space of visual models, such as CLIP ViT-L/14, using a single [IMG] token per image. The architecture employs two shallow MLP blocks, each with a separate compression module followed by a shared expan- sion module, trained with six distinct loss functions. One block aids the other during training and is omitted during inference, resulting in a lightweight solution. Strong perfor- mance is demonstrated in both image-to-design and text-to- design generation tasks.

</details>


### [40] [When Seeing Is Not Enough: Benchmarking Interactive Visual Grounding in LVLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23978)
*Zhengxiang Wang,Owen Rambow*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a controlled evaluation framework for interactive visual grounding in large vision-language models (LVLMs), finding that current models perform significantly below human baselines, especially when target information must be acquired proactively through questioning rather than provided upfront. LVLMs are also poorly calibrated, and interactive visual grounding remains an open challenge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard visual grounding evaluation uses a one-shot mapping from a complete referring expression to a visual target, which misses the real-world nature of reference where target information is often incomplete, ambiguous, and established through dialogue. This gap motivates a controlled framework to study interactive visual grounding.

Method: The authors construct a controlled evaluation framework varying (1) how much target information is provided upfront versus acquired through dialogue, (2) across four human-grounded visual contexts and four interaction protocols. They evaluate current LVLMs and run follow-up studies varying description sources, reasoning efforts, repeated interactions, providers, and visual contexts.

Result: LVLMs perform significantly below task-level human baselines. Interaction helps when follow-up questions refine or repair an initial description, but performance is lowest when no initial description is provided. LVLMs are poorly calibrated, with confidence exceeding empirical accuracy. Findings are consistent across varied settings.

Conclusion: Interactive visual grounding remains a significant challenge for LVLMs, requiring a combination of visual matching, information seeking, and information synthesis capabilities that current models do not yet sufficiently possess.

Abstract: Visual grounding is typically evaluated as a one-shot mapping from an informative referring expression to a visual target. This formulation misses a central property of real-world reference: target information is often incomplete, ambiguous, and established through interaction. We introduce a controlled evaluation framework for interactive visual grounding in large vision-language models (LVLMs), varying how much target information is provided upfront and how much must be acquired through dialogue. Across four human-grounded visual contexts and four interaction protocols, current LVLMs perform significantly below task-level human baselines. Interaction can help when follow-up questions refine or repair an initial target description. Performance is lowest when no initial description is provided and target information must be acquired through questions, indicating that proactive question-driven grounding remains difficult. LVLMs are also poorly calibrated, often reporting confidence that exceeds their empirical accuracy. Follow-up studies confirm these patterns across varied description sources (human versus AI), reasoning efforts, repeated interactions, description providers, and visual contexts. Overall, interactive visual grounding remains an important challenge, requiring visual matching, information seeking and synthesis.

</details>


### [41] [Rules Before Oracles: Auditable, User-Configurable Argument Selection for Deliberative Polling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23979)
*Muntaser Syed,Markus Zanker,Marius Silaghi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper formalizes civic recommenders for deliberative polls as legible, publicly computable rules parameterized by voters, rather than opaque learned rankers. It proposes a one-hop reversed endorsement flow rule meeting seven checkable criteria, and shows via multi-agent simulation (~17,000 runs) that its served slates fall only 0.035 short of an unconstrained label-reading ceiling, perform comparably to random on coverage alone but dominate on recency and endorsement mass (3.3x), and that weight-function choices function as security controls against flooding attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In deliberative polls, opaque learned rankers decide which arguments each voter sees, yet voters cannot recompute or contest the exposure that shaped their vote. The authors want legibility—being able to recompute and contest the mechanism—to be an admissibility condition on usable mechanisms, not merely an accuracy trade-off.

Method: The paper formalizes a poll over bipolar justification sets and defines seven checkable criteria for a civic recommender. It proposes a one-hop reversed endorsement flow rule parameterized by a relation-weight function. An agentic simulator records every slate at every vote across about 17,000 seed-paired runs, comparing the rule against labeling ceilings, random baselines, and weight-policy variants under adversarial flooding.

Result: Served slates fall 0.035 short of a label-reading ceiling upper-bounding every selection procedure including opaque ones. On coverage alone with non-degenerate authoring, the rule is indistinguishable from a random slate (an order-blind, charity-blind null); on the other two criteria it leads at every prefix with a margin widening under adversarial pressure, and dominates on mass by a factor of 3.3. When a realistic fraction of submissions carries no reasons, the coverage margin returns and grows. Label-homogeneous flooding collapses completeness from 0.81 to 0.34 under flat weights but only to 0.44 under author-count normalisation—a 10% completeness-security gap tied to weight-function choice.

Conclusion: The choice between ranking arms is a position on a coverage-versus-mass frontier—a normative decision, not a fact—and only a legible rule can hand that choice to the person it affects. The rule maps onto an open-source peer-to-peer platform.

Abstract: In a deliberative poll, once submissions outnumber what anyone will read, some mechanism chooses which arguments each voter sees, acquiring much of the decision; practice delegates it to opaque learned rankers, so a voter cannot recompute or contest the exposure that shaped their vote. We ask whether it can be a published rule over publicly recomputable evidence with parameters held by the voter, treating legibility as an admissibility condition on usable mechanisms, not an objective traded against accuracy. We formalise a poll over bipolar justification sets, judging a slate by reason coverage, the order it arrives in, and captured endorsement mass; we give seven checkable criteria for a civic recommender and a rule meeting them: a one-hop reversed endorsement flow parameterised by a relation-weight function. An agentic simulator records every slate at every vote, over about 17,000 seed-paired runs. Served slates fall 0.035 short of a label-reading ceiling upper-bounding every selection procedure, opaque ones included: any unconstrained ranker's advantage is bounded and small. On coverage alone, with non-degenerate authoring, the rule is indistinguishable from a random slate, a null due to an order-blind, charity-blind instrument; on the other two it leads at every prefix by a margin widening with adversarial pressure and dominates on mass by a factor of 3.3. Once a realistic fraction of submissions carries no reasons, the coverage margin returns and grows. Label-homogeneous flooding collapses completeness from 0.81 to 0.34 under a flat weight policy, only to 0.44 under author-count normalisation, making the weight function a security control worth 10% of completeness. The choice between ranking arms is a position on a coverage-versus-mass frontier, not a fact, the kind of choice only a legible rule can hand to the person it affects. It maps onto an open-source peer-to-peer platform.

</details>


### [42] [Memory Is Not Always Needed: Characterizing Conditional Memory in Scientific Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23982)
*Zhen Bi,Xueshu Chen,Yan Wang,Zhizhi Peng,Haosen Hong,Zhen Wang,Zhixuan Chu,Bingyu Zhu,Jungang Lou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates when, where, and to what extent conditional memory should be activated for scientific reasoning in language models. The authors characterize the scientific knowledge boundary and memory-enabled knowledge-circuit nodes, then propose a Knowledge Boundary-Aware Router that decides memory activation, injection location, and signal strength based on task-specific input proxies. Experiments across biological and chemical reasoning benchmarks show the router outperforms static and random routing by preserving beneficial memory contributions while suppressing regressions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conditional memory provides an explicit lookup pathway for scientific reasoning, but its usefulness is input- and computation-dependent: it can repair missing scientific associations but may also introduce distracting shortcuts or interfere with already-correct base-model reasoning. The paper aims to systematically understand when memory should participate in scientific reasoning rather than applying it indiscriminately.

Method: The authors (1) characterize the scientific knowledge boundary to understand where base models lack knowledge, (2) perform controlled interventions on memory-enabled knowledge-circuit nodes to understand where memory signals propagate, and (3) propose a Knowledge Boundary-Aware Router that uses pre-generation input proxies to decide whether memory activates, which layer-stage nodes receive memory signals, and how strongly signals contribute.

Result: Experiments on biological and chemical reasoning benchmarks spanning two backbone families and six task types show memory effects vary substantially across inputs, tasks, and injection locations. Compared to static and activation-rate-matched random routing, the proposed approach more consistently preserves beneficial memory contributions while suppressing memory-induced regressions.

Conclusion: Selective memory allocation is an important principle for reliable scientific reasoning—memory should be activated conditionally based on input characteristics and task needs rather than applied uniformly, establishing a foundation for more targeted integration of explicit memory into reasoning systems.

Abstract: Scientific reasoning requires language models to retrieve specialized knowledge and incorporate it reliably into multi-step computation. Conditional memory provides an explicit lookup pathway that complements dense neural representations, but its usefulness is inherently input- and computation-dependent: retrieved information may repair missing scientific associations, yet it may also introduce distracting shortcuts or interfere with reasoning that the base model can already perform correctly. In this work, we systematically investigate when, where, and to what extent conditional memory should participate in scientific reasoning. We characterize the scientific knowledge boundary and controlled interventions on memory-enabled knowledge-circuit nodes. Based on these analyses, we propose a Knowledge Boundary-Aware Router that uses task-specific input proxies available before generation to determine whether memory is activated, which layer-stage nodes receive memory signals, and how strongly these signals contribute. Experiments on biological and chemical reasoning benchmarks, covering two backbone families and six task types, show that memory effects vary substantially across inputs, tasks, and injection locations. Compared with static and activation-rate-matched random routing, our approach more consistently preserves beneficial memory contributions while suppressing memory-induced regressions, establishing selective memory allocation as an important principle for reliable scientific reasoning.

</details>


### [43] [Diverse by Reasoning: Harnessing the Wisdom of LLM Crowds for Future Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24001)
*Nirupam Chetlapalli,Yiming Liao,Min-Chun Chen,Keke Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a behavior-aware framework for constructing diverse LLM crowds, where models are characterized by reasoning traces on independent tasks, clustered by behavioral similarity, and representatives are selected for collective prediction. A three-model medoid crowd from K-means++ behavioral clustering outperforms voting over all 25 models on two future-prediction benchmarks, while cutting model calls by 88% and inference cost by ~80%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLMs are increasingly used for future prediction via wisdom-of-the-crowd mechanisms, simply increasing crowd size does not guarantee effective diversity because different LLMs may exhibit redundant behaviors. There is a need to construct diverse crowds based on actual behavioral differences rather than just model count.

Method: The framework characterizes each LLM using reasoning traces on independent development tasks, clusters models by behavioral similarity, and selects representative models (medoids) via K-means++ clustering for collective prediction.

Result: Evaluation on 25 LLMs with seven development benchmarks for behavioral modeling and two future-prediction benchmarks shows that a three-model medoid crowd outperforms conventional voting over all 25 models on both benchmarks, while reducing model calls by 88% and inference cost by approximately 80%.

Conclusion: Representative behavioral diversity, rather than simply maximizing diversity or crowd size, is key to building effective LLM crowds for prediction tasks. Crowd composition can matter more than crowd size.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for future prediction, motivating the use of multiple models as a wisdom-of-the-crowd mechanism. However, simply increasing crowd size does not guarantee effective diversity, as different LLMs may exhibit redundant behaviors. We propose a behavior-aware framework for constructing diverse LLM crowds.
