The framework characterizes models using their reasoning traces on independent development tasks, clusters models by behavioral similarity, and selects representatives for collective prediction. We evaluate 25 LLMs using seven development benchmarks for behavioral diversity modeling and two future-prediction benchmarks for evaluating diverse crowds' performance. Our results show that crowd composition can matter more than crowd size: a three-model medoid crowd based on K-means++ behavioral clustering outperforms conventional voting over all 25 models on both prediction benchmarks, while reducing model calls by 88% and inference cost by approximately 80%. The results further suggest that representative behavioral diversity, rather than simply maximizing diversity, is important for constructing effective LLM crowds

</details>


### [44] [Incorporating Cognitive Load and Knowledge Transfer for Multi-Domain Knowledge Tracing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24005)
*Haotian Zhang,Shucun Wang,Jinze Wu,Liang Ding,Shuochen Liu,Zhenya Huang,Jing Sha,Shijin Wang,Qi Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes LT-MKT, a novel method for multi-domain Knowledge Tracing that explicitly incorporates cognitive load and knowledge transfer to improve student knowledge state assessment. It constructs a Multi-domain Hierarchical Graph using LLMs to bridge isolated domains, models cross-domain features in temporal and knowledge dimensions for cognitive load, and designs a knowledge transfer module for within- and cross-domain knowledge propagation, achieving state-of-the-art results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most existing KT methods focus on single-domain learning, but real-world scenarios involve multiple domains simultaneously. This introduces two critical factors often overlooked: cognitive load from managing multi-domain learning across temporal and knowledge dimensions, and knowledge transfer where knowledge states in one domain influence related states within and across domains.

Method: LT-MKT first constructs a Multi-domain Hierarchical Graph by integrating textual information from questions and associated concepts using LLMs to bridge isolated domains. It then explicitly models cross-domain features in both temporal and knowledge dimensions to capture cognitive load effects. Finally, a knowledge transfer module models the propagation of knowledge states within and across domains, jointly modeling all factors for prediction.

Result: Extensive experiments on real-world datasets demonstrate that LT-MKT achieves state-of-the-art performance in predicting students' future performance.

Conclusion: By jointly modeling cognitive load and knowledge transfer in multi-domain learning scenarios, LT-MKT enables more accurate assessment of students' knowledge states and prediction of future performance, outperforming existing approaches.

Abstract: Knowledge Tracing (KT) aims to assess students' dynamic knowledge states from their learning histories. While most existing KT methods focus on single-domain learning with notable success, real-world learning scenarios often involve multiple domains simultaneously, introducing two critical factors: 1) Cognitive load, arising from managing learning across domains in both temporal and knowledge dimensions. 2) Knowledge transfer, where knowledge states in one domain influence related states both within and across domains. In this paper, we focus on exploring these factors to improve students' knowledge state assessment in multi-domain learning scenarios and propose a novel method incorporating cognitive Load and knowledge Transfer for Multi-domain Knowledge Tracing (LT-MKT). Specifically, to bridge isolated domains, LT-MKT first integrates textual information from questions and their associated concepts to construct a Multi-domain Hierarchical Graph, leveraging the advanced representational capabilities of large language models (LLMs). Then, cross-domain features in both the temporal and knowledge dimensions are explicitly modeled to capture the effects of cognitive load. Additionally, a knowledge transfer module is designed to model the propagation of knowledge states within and across domains. By jointly modeling these factors, LT-MKT enables more accurate prediction of students' future performance. Finally, extensive experiments on real-world datasets demonstrate that our method achieves state-of-the-art performance.

</details>


### [45] [Reflection with Action-Induced Visual Differences for Desktop GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24015)
*Yijie Ma,Chaoyue Niu,Fan Wu,Guihai Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Evidence-First Reflection (EFR), a two-stage reflector for desktop GUI agents that decouples action-induced change detection from outcome verification, improving reflector accuracy by 7.11% and end-to-end task success by 5.94% and 4.95% on two benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Planner-Operator-Reflector framework is widely used in GUI agents, but desktop GUIs present challenges due to large, dense interfaces with subtle or scattered state changes. Existing reflectors collapse change detection and outcome verification into a single step, leaving evidence implicit and yielding weakly grounded decisions.

Method: EFR is a two-stage reflector that explicitly decouples action-induced visual difference extraction from outcome verification. It first identifies the action location and candidate changed regions using Set-of-Marks annotations, then describes and filters action-relevant changes, and finally makes judgments based on the cleaned evidence.

Result: On OSWorld-Verified and WindowsAgentArena benchmarks, EFR improves reflector accuracy by 7.11%, with end-to-end task success gains of 5.94% and 4.95% on the two benchmarks respectively.

Conclusion: The evidence-reasoning decoupled design makes reflection better grounded in screen transitions while reducing both visual search complexity and reasoning burden, demonstrating the effectiveness of explicit evidence extraction before outcome verification for desktop GUI agents.

Abstract: The Planner-Operator-Reflector (POR) framework is widely used in GUI agents to maintain objective alignment in complex tasks through modular collaboration. However, desktop GUIs introduce a key challenge: large, dense interfaces often exhibit subtle or scattered state changes, placing most of the burden on the reflector, which must compare pre- and post-action screens, while the planner and operator reason over a single state. Existing reflectors collapse change detection and outcome verification into one step, leaving evidence implicit and yielding weakly grounded decisions. To address this limitation, we propose Evidence-First Reflection (EFR), a two-stage reflector that explicitly decouples action-induced visual differences extraction from outcome verification. EFR identifies the action location and candidate changed regions with Set-of-Marks annotations, describes and filters action-relevant changes, and makes the final judgment from the cleaned evidence. This evidence-reasoning decoupled design makes reflection better grounded in screen transitions, while reducing both visual search complexity and reasoning burden. Experiments on OSWorld-Verified and WindowsAgentArena demonstrate that EFR improves reflector accuracy by 7.11%, yielding average end-to-end task success gains of 5.94% and 4.95% on the two benchmarks, respectively.

</details>


### [46] [Beyond Confidence: Test-Time Scaling for Multi-Turn Search Agents via Retrieval Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24024)
*Hyunho Kook,Junhyuk So,Tianyu Fu,Haizhong Zheng,Beidi Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper identifies a failure mode of confidence-based voting in multi-turn LLM search agents—dubbed "copy inflation"—where tokens copied from retrieved documents receive inflated log probabilities, flattening confidence scores and weakening weighted voting. The authors propose Retrieval-Grounded Voting (RGV), which scores rollouts by lexical overlap between final answers and retrieved documents, sidestepping log probabilities and additional LLM calls. RGV consistently outperforms confidence-based voting across four search-agent benchmarks and five LLMs, with gains up to +5.4% accuracy and +35% on minority-correct questions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While confidence-based voting works well for single-turn reasoning tasks, modern LLMs increasingly operate as multi-turn search agents that retrieve and condition on external documents. The authors find that confidence-based voting transfers poorly to this setting due to "copy inflation"—retrieved document tokens receive artificially inflated log probabilities, which flattens confidence scores and weakens weighted voting.

Method: The paper proposes Retrieval-Grounded Voting (RGV), which scores each rollout by the lexical overlap between its final answer and the documents it retrieved. This signal is computed outside the contaminated context, avoiding both token log probabilities and additional LLM inference calls.

Result: Across four search-agent benchmarks and five LLMs, RGV consistently outperforms confidence-based voting. It achieves gains of up to +5.4% accuracy and +35% on minority-correct questions where the correct answer appears in only 1-2 of 8 rollouts.

Conclusion: Traditional confidence-based voting is unreliable for multi-turn search-agent settings because context contamination from retrieved documents distorts log-probability signals. RGV offers a lightweight, effective alternative that leverages retrieval-answer grounding without requiring additional LLM calls, substantially improving aggregate performance especially on questions where only a minority of rollouts contain the correct answer.

Abstract: Confidence-based voting aggregates parallel LLM rollouts by weighting each with internal signals such as token log probabilities, and has been actively studied for single-turn reasoning. However, modern LLMs increasingly act as multi-turn search agents that retrieve and condition on external documents. In this paper, we show that confidence-based voting transfers poorly to this multi-turn setting, and identify the underlying failure reason as copy inflation: when retrieved documents are appended to an agent's context, tokens copied from those documents receive systematically inflated log probabilities. This flattens confidence scores within each question and weakens the resulting weighted vote. To address this issue, we propose Retrieval-Grounded Voting (RGV), which scores each rollout by the lexical overlap between its final answer and the documents it retrieved. By computing the signal outside the contaminated context, RGV sidesteps both token log probabilities and additional LLM calls. Across four search-agent benchmarks and five LLMs, RGV consistently outperforms confidence-based voting, with gains of up to +5.4% accuracy and +35% on minority-correct questions, where the correct answer appears in only 1-2 of 8 rollouts.

</details>


### [47] [Relative Time Intervals Representation for Word-level Timestamping with Masked Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24041)
*Quanwei Tang,Zhiyu Tang,Xu Li,Dong Zhang,Shoushan,Guodong Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper enhances SpeechLLMs with fine-grained temporal awareness by introducing relative timestamps, a hybrid fine-tuning strategy (full fine-tuning of embedding/head layers plus LoRA on decoder layers), and a masked timestamp training objective, achieving improved timestamp prediction accuracy without sacrificing transcription quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SpeechLLMs have strong capabilities in speech understanding and generation, but their ability to produce fine-grained, temporally aligned outputs is underexplored. The authors aim to transform SpeechLLMs from purely content-focused models into temporal-aware ones.

Method: The method includes three key components: (1) replacing absolute timestamps with relative timestamps for a more compact vocabulary and better generalization; (2) a hybrid fine-tuning strategy combining full-parameter fine-tuning of the timestamp-augmented embedding layer and language model head with LoRA fine-tuning of decoder layers; (3) a masked timestamp training objective to prevent over-reliance on ground-truth timestamps and improve robustness against noisy annotations.

Result: Extensive experiments show significant improvements in timestamp prediction accuracy while maintaining strong speech transcription performance.

Conclusion: The proposed approach effectively equips SpeechLLMs with temporal awareness, enabling them to jointly model speech content and temporal structure without sacrificing their existing transcription capabilities.

Abstract: Although Speech Large Language Models (SpeechLLMs) excel at speech understanding and generation, their capacity for fine-grained, temporally aligned outputs remains underexplored. Our work addresses this gap by enabling SpeechLLMs to jointly model speech content and temporal structure, effectively transforming them from ``content understanding machines" into ``temporal-aware content understanding machines". Specifically, we replace traditional absolute timestamps with relative timestamps, achieving a more compact vocabulary and stronger generalization capabilities. To efficiently infuse timestamp prediction ability into pre-trained large language models, we introduce a hybrid fine-tuning strategy: full-parameter fine-tuning of the timestamp-augmented embedding layer and language model head, combined with LoRA fine-tuning of the decoder layers. Moreover, we design a masked timestamp training objective, preventing the model from over-relying on ground-truth timestamps, and thereby enhancing robustness against noisy real-world annotations. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach achieves significant improvements in timestamp prediction accuracy while maintaining strong speech transcription performance.

</details>


### [48] [Algorithmic Impact Reveals the Hidden Social Choice Structure of Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24046)
*Zachary Wojtowicz,Michelle Si,Finale Doshi-Velez,Ariel Procaccia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper reformulates AI alignment for multi-agent settings as linear optimization over a convex impact space, bridging welfare economics and mechanism design with RLHF. It shows how alignment protocols map to welfare consequences and vice versa, proving strategyproofness and unanimity for voting-by-issues and random-dictatorship mechanisms, and deriving welfare-maximizing alignment protocols with constraints on harm, validated empirically on kidney allocation, food distribution, LLM responses, and trolley problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When AI systems affect multiple people, aligning them becomes a social choice problem—how to aggregate divergent preferences. Standard RLHF largely sidesteps this and lacks social choice guarantees, yet no clear alternative exists. The paper aims to provide a principled framework connecting alignment to welfare economics.

Method: The authors reformulate alignment as linear optimization over a convex impact space, enabling application of welfare economics and mechanism design tools. They demonstrate a forward mapping (alignment protocols to welfare consequences) and a reverse mapping (social planner constraints to alignment protocols), and validate their approach empirically across four real preference datasets.

Result: The reformulation shows voting-by-issues and random-dictatorship mechanisms are strategyproof and unanimous. The reverse direction yields a family of alignment protocols maximizing utilitarian social welfare under constraints on individual or group harm. Empirical results on kidney allocation, charitable food distribution, LLM responses, and trolley problems illustrate the welfare implications of these protocols.

Conclusion: The convex impact space reformulation provides a rigorous bridge between AI alignment and social choice theory, offering alternatives to RLHF with provable welfare guarantees, and enabling principled construction of alignment protocols from normative constraints.

Abstract: When an AI algorithm makes decisions that affect more than one person, aligning it becomes a problem of social choice: how should people's divergent preferences about system behavior be reconciled and aggregated into a single coherent model? The standard approach to aligning frontier AI models$\unicode{x2013}$reinforcement learning from human feedback$\unicode{x2013}$largely sidesteps this question and has poor social choice guarantees. However, it remains unclear what alternative should replace it. We show that, by focusing directly on an algorithm's welfare consequences, the alignment problem can be reformulated as linear optimization over a convex impact space, which makes it amenable to the standard toolkit of welfare economics and mechanism design. This reformulation clarifies how alignment protocols translate into welfare consequences and, conversely, how a social planner's desired constraints on welfare consequences can be translated back into alignment protocols. We apply this transformation to show that voting-by-issues and random-dictatorship mechanisms are strategyproof and unanimous. Demonstrating the reverse direction, we also apply the impact representation to derive a family of alignment protocols that maximize utilitarian social welfare subject to various social desiderata, such as bounds on individual or group harm. We illustrate the welfare implications of these alignment protocols empirically using real human preferences over kidney allocation, charitable food distribution, LLM responses, and trolley problems.

</details>


### [49] [Poisoning Agentic Alpha: Adversarial Vulnerabilities Across Roles and Architectures in Multi-Agent Trading Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24069)
*CheolWon Na,Hao Ni,Lukasz Szpruch,Zhangyang Wang,Dhagash Mehta,Saurabh Nagrecha,Alejandro Lopez-Lira,Chanyeol Choi,Yongjae Lee,Jee-Hyong Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents the first systematic empirical study of adversarial attacks on LLM-based multi-agent trading systems, introducing a practical threat model where adversaries only access source data and prompts rather than system internals. The authors decompose a trading pipeline into four roles (Analyst, Researcher, Trader, Risk Manager), test attacks across four communication topologies using a new metric called Adversarial Signal Preservation Score (APS), and find that no architecture is inherently robust to such attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent LLM trading systems are transitioning from research to live deployment controlling real assets, making their inter-agent communication a critical attack surface. Unlike prior work that assumes privileged adversary access to system internals, the authors argue for a more realistic, low-barrier threat model where adversaries only manipulate source data and prompts—a democratized attack vector that could cause real financial losses.

Method: The study decomposes a widely-used trading pipeline into four functional roles (Analyst, Researcher, Trader, Risk Manager), each paired with attacks matched to its interface. It evaluates four communication topologies under both data-level and agent-level attacks across five assets, two backbone models, and two target directions. A novel metric, the Adversarial Signal Preservation Score (APS), is introduced as a post-hoc diagnostic to explain robustness differences across designs.

Result: The empirical results show that adversarial signals can enter trading systems through source data and prompts, and propagate through inter-agent communication to influence final trading decisions. The central finding is that no communication topology or architecture is inherently robust against these role-specific attacks, with robustness varying across roles, topologies, assets, and backbones.

Conclusion: Since no architecture is inherently robust, future multi-agent trading system design must account for adversarial exposure as a first-class concern. The APS metric and role/structure decomposition provide a framework for reasoning about and developing safer, more robust agentic trading systems.

Abstract: LLM-based multi-agent trading systems, in which specialized agents collaborate through structured communication to produce trading decisions, are moving rapidly from research prototypes to live deployments that control real assets. The same inter-agent communication that makes them effective also exposes them: a corrupted signal can propagate to the final decision and translate into realized financial loss. Unlike prior attacks that presume privileged access to system internals, we restrict the adversary to what is practically reachable---the source data and prompts agents consume---yielding a low-barrier, and thus democratized threat model instantiated as role-specific adversaries.
  We present the first systematic empirical study in the financial domain to characterize how an adversarial signal enters a multi-agent trading system and how far it survives toward the decision. Along the role axis, we decompose a widely-used trading pipeline into four functional roles---Analyst, Researcher, Trader, and Risk Manager---and pair each with an attack matched to its interface. Along the structural axis, we evaluate four communication topologies under data- and agent-level attacks, using the Adversarial Signal Preservation Score (APS) as a post-hoc lens on why some designs are more robust than others. We conduct experiments across five assets, two backbones, and two target directions. A central finding is that no architecture is inherently robust. These findings provide insights for the future design of safer and more robust agentic trading systems.

</details>


### [50] [Compression Trinity: Exploring Sparsity, Quantization, and Low-Rank Approximations for LLM Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24070)
*Mohammad Mozaffari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This thesis proposes the "Compression Trinity," a unified framework that jointly applies sparsity, quantization, and low-rank approximations to compress LLMs. It introduces four methods: MKOR for accelerated pretraining via curvature approximation, SLoPe for faster training with N:M sparsity and lazy adapters, OPTIMA for zero-training static mask stabilization, PATCH for fine-tuning with dynamic hybrid sparsity, and SLiM as the full one-shot compression solution. Results show up to 1.85x pretraining speedup, 1.38x fine-tuning speedup, and up to 5.66% accuracy gains over state-of-the-art methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional LLM compression techniques (sparsity, quantization, low-rank approximation) are applied in isolation and each hits an accuracy-efficiency wall. Additionally, prohibitive computational and environmental costs impede scalable deployment of LLMs.

Method: The Compression Trinity framework jointly applies three compression pillars: sparsity (reduces computation), quantization (minimizes memory bandwidth), and low-rank approximations (recovers accuracy). It is applied across pretraining and post-training: (1) MKOR uses block-diagonal sparsity and low-rank inversion to approximate curvature for quantized optimizer states, reducing complexity from O(d^3) to O(d^2); (2) SLoPe accelerates training via double-pruned backward pass for N:M sparsity with low-rank lazy adapters in the final 1% of training; (3) OPTIMA formulates weight reconstruction as globally optimal column-wise quadratic programs for zero-training static mask stabilization; (4) PATCH learns a dynamic hybrid sparsity ratio between 0% and 50% during fine-tuning; (5) SLiM integrates all three pillars in one shot with mathematically derived low-rank adapters to recover information lost to quantization and sparsity.

Result: MKOR accelerates convergence by up to 1.85x over KFAC; SLoPe accelerates training by up to 1.25x; OPTIMA improves zero-shot accuracy by up to 3.97%; PATCH yields up to 1.38x speedups; SLiM improves accuracy by up to 5.66% over state-of-the-art methods and outperforms uncompressed dense models at equal parameter budgets by 0.6%.

Conclusion: Jointly applying the Compression Trinity—sparsity, quantization, and low-rank approximation—is essential for efficient, scalable, high-performance LLMs. Each pillar alone hits an accuracy-efficiency wall, but combined they achieve significant gains in both training speed and post-training compression quality.

Abstract: Prohibitive computational and environmental costs impede the scalable deployment of Large Language Models (LLMs). Traditional compression techniques (sparsity, quantization, low-rank approximations) are typically applied in isolation, and each hits an accuracy-efficiency wall. This thesis proposes the "Compression Trinity," a unified framework that applies the three pillars jointly: sparsity to reduce computation, quantization to minimize memory bandwidth, and low-rank approximations to recover accuracy. To accelerate pretraining, we apply the Trinity to the optimizer and model architecture. MKOR approximates curvature via block-diagonal sparsity and low-rank inversion, maintaining numerical stability for quantized states; it reduces curvature update complexity from $O(d^3)$ to $O(d^2)$ and accelerates convergence by up to 1.85x over KFAC. SLoPe accelerates training by up to 1.25x via a double-pruned backward pass for N:M sparsity, using low-rank "lazy" adapters in the final 1% of training to recover accuracy. For post-training compression, OPTIMA stabilizes static masks in a zero-training regime by formulating weight reconstruction as globally optimal column-wise quadratic programs, improving zero-shot accuracy by up to 3.97%. Given a fine-tuning budget, PATCH breaks the ceiling of static masks by learning a dynamic hybrid sparsity ratio between 0% and 50%, yielding up to 1.38x speedups. Finally, SLiM realizes the full Compression Trinity in one shot, using mathematically derived low-rank adapters to recover information lost to quantization and sparsity, improving accuracy by up to 5.66% over state-of-the-art methods and outperforming uncompressed dense models at equal parameter budgets by 0.6%. Together, these results show that jointly applying the Compression Trinity is essential for efficient, scalable, high-performance LLMs.

</details>


### [51] [AgentWorld: Personality-Aware Reliability Evaluation for Agentic Information Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24076)
*Gunja Agarwal,Arup Kumar Das,Arun Menon,Jitesh Chandra Mishra,Vignesh Divakaran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentWorld is a simulation framework for evaluating agentic information retrieval systems using Big Five personality-driven user populations, stateful tool-use environments, a pass^k consistency metric with structured fault classification, score-thresholded training-data export, and an adversarial Risk Analyser that quantifies trajectory-level brittleness via Monte-Carlo rollouts, Dempster-Shafer evidence fusion, and Shapley attribution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluation of agentic information retrieval is limited to scripted interactions with uniform users, failing to capture both natural personality diversity and adversarial brittleness in agent behavior.

Method: The framework combines four components: (i) Big Five (OCEAN) personality-driven user populations with stateful tool-use environments; (ii) a pass^k consistency metric with structured fault classification, partial-credit scoring, and dual-control handoff verification; (iii) score-thresholded training-data export in six fine-tuning formats; (iv) an adversarial Risk Analyser that snapshots required-intermediate-state spines, runs Monte-Carlo rollouts under four task-aware perturbation types, and quantifies risk via ΔP/ΔT scoring, Dempster-Shafer evidence fusion, and Shapley attack-category attribution.

Result: Three experiments validate the framework: a conversational analytics agent across 10 OCEAN personas (240 evaluator judgments), a customer-support agent across 5 tasks × 4 persona variants, and adversarial stress-testing of 5 tasks revealing pre-existing trajectory brittleness (V_min=0.375 without perturbation) and tool/infrastructure-layer attack dominance (Shapley: 46% system, 38% action). Personality variation exposed failure modes uniform testing cannot, including cross-domain leakage, contextual drift, a 0.27-point quality gap, and 50% vs. 100% pass-rate across personas on the same task.

Conclusion: The Risk Analyser quantifies trajectory-level brittleness that pass^k alone cannot measure, demonstrating that personality-aware and adversarial evaluation are both necessary for robust assessment of agentic information retrieval systems.

Abstract: Evaluation of agentic information retrieval remains limited to scripted interactions with uniform users, missing both natural personality diversity and adversarial brittleness. We present AgentWorld, a simulation framework combining (i)Big Five (OCEAN) personality-driven user populations with stateful tool-use environments; (ii)the pass$^k$ consistency metric with structured fault classification, partial-credit scoring, and dual-control handoff verification; (iii)score-thresholded training-data export in six fine-tuning formats; and (iv)an adversarial Risk Analyser that snapshots required-intermediate-state spines, branches Monte-Carlo rollouts under four task-aware perturbation types, and quantifies risk via $ΔP / ΔT$ scoring, Dempster--Shafer evidence fusion, and Shapley attack-category attribution. Three experiments demonstrate the framework: a conversational analytics agent across 10 OCEAN personas (240 evaluator judgments); a customer-support agent across 5 tasks $\times$ 4 persona variants; and adversarial stress-testing of 5 tasks revealing pre-existing trajectory brittleness ($V_{\min}=0.375$ without perturbation) and tool/infrastructure-layer attack dominance (Shapley: 46% system, 38% action). Personality variation surfaces failure modes uniform testing cannot expose---cross-domain leakage, contextual drift, a 0.27-point quality gap, and 50% vs. 100% pass-rate across personas on the same task---while the Risk Analyser quantifies trajectory-level brittleness that pass$^k$ alone cannot measure.

</details>


### [52] [EMRB: A Multi-Level Benchmark for Evaluating LLM Reasoning over Raw Electromagnetic Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24086)
*Mingxu Zhang,Ying Sun,Yuhan Li,Yang Ji,Dazhong Shen,Ke Zhang,Shan Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces EMRB, a benchmark evaluating whether LLMs can analyze raw electromagnetic I/Q data by writing and running code, along with ReconPilot, a structured method that improves LLM performance on such tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used as code agents for scientific and engineering analysis, but their ability to analyze raw physical-layer electromagnetic measurements (I/Q data) has not been tested. Existing benchmarks rely on preprocessed features or structured tables rather than raw captures.

Method: EMRB contains 200 problems across five difficulty levels and 27 question types, generated from 11 signal types with verified ground truth, where only raw I/Q captures are provided and quantities must be discovered through code. ReconPilot is a structured method separating signal reconnaissance, targeted analysis, and self-verification.

Result: Evaluating 14 LLMs (proprietary, open-weight, reasoning-oriented), scores range from 24.1% to 78.9%, with mean accuracy dropping from 84.9% on basic measurement to 21.2% on system design. ReconPilot raises overall scores by 3.8-17.6 points and improves 13 of 15 backbone-level combinations.

Conclusion: Raw physical-layer reasoning remains challenging for current LLMs, and structured methods like ReconPilot can meaningfully improve their performance on electromagnetic signal analysis tasks.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as code agents for scientific and engineering analysis, but their ability to analyze raw physical-layer measurements remains untested. We introduce \textbf{EMRB} (\textbf{E}lectro\textbf{m}agnetic \textbf{R}easoning \textbf{B}enchmark), which evaluates whether LLMs can analyze raw I/Q data by writing and running code. EMRB contains 200 problems across five difficulty levels and 27 question types, from signal detection to OFDM design, generated from 11 signal types with verified ground truth. Unlike benchmarks built on preprocessed features or structured tables, EMRB provides only the raw capture; the quantities each question refers to must first be discovered through code. We evaluate 14 LLMs spanning proprietary, open-weight, and reasoning-oriented families. Scores range from 24.1\% to 78.9\%, with the mean dropping from 84.9\% on basic measurement to 21.2\% on system design. We also propose \textbf{ReconPilot}, a structured method that separates signal reconnaissance, targeted analysis, and self-verification. Across three backbones, ReconPilot raises the overall score by 3.8 to 17.6 points and improves 13 of 15 backbone-level combinations tested. All data and code are publicly released in \href{https://github.com/mingxuZhang2/EMRB}{\textcolor{blue}{our GitHub repository}}.

</details>


### [53] [Are Android GUI Agents Robust Against Runtime Anomalies? AnTrap: Evaluating Agents in Dynamic Adversarial Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24099)
*Guo Gan,Yilun Zhao,Cong Chen,Jinbiao Wei,Tingyu Song,Zheyuan Yang,Lin Fu,Hong Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AnTrap is a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating GUI agent robustness against dynamic runtime anomalies on Android devices. It introduces a four-layer taxonomy (State, Thinking, Action, Round) with ten subcategories of anomalies, evaluates 16 leading GUI models showing universal vulnerability, and uses GRPO training to distinguish environment-learnable anomalies from reasoning-bottlenecked ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GUI agent benchmarks lack systematic evaluation of agent robustness against dynamic anomalies (e.g., unexpected pop-ups, action misuse) that commonly occur during real-world Android deployment.

Method: AnTrap injects dynamic perturbations into agent execution trajectories following a taxonomy of real-world anomalies organized into four layers (State, Thinking, Action, Round) with ten fine-grained subcategories. A construction pipeline preserves task solvability under adversarial conditions. GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) training is conducted in both original and adversarial environments.

Result: Evaluation of 16 leading GUI models reveals universal vulnerability to dynamic anomalies, with even the strongest models suffering significant performance degradation. Single-step traps at state and action layers are largely addressable through adversarial reinforcement learning, while deep contextual traps like state deadlock expose intrinsic limitations that training with traps alone cannot resolve.

Conclusion: AnTrap reveals that current GUI agents are universally fragile to runtime anomalies. While some anomalies can be mitigated via adversarial training, deep contextual traps represent reasoning bottlenecks that require fundamentally different approaches beyond environment-based training.

Abstract: GUI agents often encounter dynamic anomalies when deployed on Android devices, from unexpected pop-ups to action misuse, yet existing benchmarks lack systematic evaluation of agent robustness against runtime anomalies. We introduce AnTrap, a comprehensive benchmark that injects dynamic perturbations into agent execution trajectories. We propose a taxonomy organizing real-world anomalies into four layers (State, Thinking, Action and Round) with ten fine-grained subcategories, and develop a construction pipeline that preserves task solvability while introducing realistic adversarial conditions. Evaluating 16 leading GUI models, we reveal universal vulnerability to dynamic anomalies, with even the strongest models suffering significant performance degradation. Furthermore, we conduct GRPO training in both original and adversarial environments to validate our benchmark, separating environment-learnable anomalies from reasoning-bottlenecked ones. Our findings show that while single-step traps at state and action layers are largely addressable through adversarial reinforcement learning, deep contextual traps, like state deadlock, expose intrinsic limitations that cannot be resolved by training in environments with traps alone.

</details>


### [54] [ACE: A Self-Correcting Agentic Canvas Editor for Multi-Slide Presentation Automation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24103)
*JooYoung Jang,Taegyeong Lee,Jihyeon Park,Nojun Kwak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ACE is an agentic canvas editor built on a hierarchical scene-graph with a 98-tool presentation-specific action space, paired with CARE (a content-aware router reducing input tokens by ~89%) and a ground-truth-free self-correction loop with an instruction-following judge. It outperforms a same-backbone agentic HTML pipeline on instruction following (IF 4.23 vs. 3.81, p=.010) at 1.75x speed and ~44% lower cost, with blind raters preferring ACE 58.7% of the time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two practical problems block reliable LLM-agent deployment on commercial design platforms: (1) legacy document formats expose only flat, absolutely positioned elements causing agents to recompute coordinates and break layouts; (2) design has no unique ground truth, so diff-against-reference metrics penalize valid-but-different outputs.

Method: ACE is an agentic canvas editor over a hierarchical scene-graph with a presentation-specialized action space of 98 tools. It is paired with CARE, a content-aware router that feeds only relevant deck slices (~89% avg. input-token reduction), and a self-correction loop driven by a ground-truth-free instruction-following judge whose natural-language critique is fed back as the next-turn instruction.

Result: With a fixed backbone, single-turn scene-graph editing matches a same-backbone agentic HTML pipeline; adding self-correction lifts ACE above it (IF 4.23 vs. 3.81, paired p=.010, replicated by an out-of-loop judge) at 1.75x speed and ~44% lower cost. VQ means are statistically indistinguishable, but 26 blind raters prefer ACE (58.7% decisive win-rate, 81% for self-corrected output). Ranking is invariant across three judge families; out-of-loop judges retain two-thirds of the self-correction gain. 66% of cases halt after one pass and strict-peak rollback removes all observed regressions.

Conclusion: A hierarchical scene-graph representation with presentation-specialized actions, content-aware context routing, and ground-truth-free self-correction enables reliable and efficient LLM-agent document editing on commercial design platforms, outperforming internal-iteration HTML pipelines in instruction following while being faster and cheaper, with evidence that self-correction gains are genuine (not circular).

Abstract: Commercial design platforms increasingly edit documents through large language model (LLM) agents, but two practical problems block reliable deployment: legacy document formats expose only \emph{flat}, absolutely positioned elements, so agents must recompute coordinates and routinely break layouts; and design has no unique ground truth, so diff-against-reference metrics penalize valid-but-different outputs. We present \textbf{ACE}, an agentic canvas editor over a \emph{hierarchical scene-graph} with a presentation-specialized action space (98 tools), paired with \textbf{CARE}, a content-aware router that feeds the agent only the relevant slice of each deck (avg.\ $\sim$89\% input-token reduction), and a \emph{self-correction} loop driven by a \emph{ground-truth-free} instruction-following (IF) judge whose natural-language critique is fed back as the next-turn instruction. With a fixed backbone, a scene-graph editor in a \emph{single turn} already matches a same-backbone \emph{agentic} HTML pipeline that iterates internally; adding self-correction lifts ACE significantly above it on instruction following (IF 4.23 vs.\ 3.81 on the full 94-task benchmark, paired $p{=}.010$, replicated by an out-of-loop judge) at 1.75$\times$ the speed and $\sim$44\% lower cost. VQ means are statistically indistinguishable, but 26 blind raters prefer ACE overall (58.7\% decisive win-rate) and prefer the self-corrected output 81\% of the time; the ranking is invariant across three judge families, and out-of-loop judges retain two-thirds of the self-correction gain, bounding circularity. 66\% of cases halt after one pass, and a strict-peak rollback removes every observed regression.

</details>


### [55] [Scalable Question-Centric Text-to-Image Evaluation: Reliable Ranking, Fine-Grained Diagnosis, and Cost-Aware Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24112)
*Shaoan Zhao,Fang Zhao,Xueqiang Guo,Xinpei Su,Huanlin Gao,Qiang Hui,Ting Lu,Fuyuan Shi,Chao Tan,Bikun Yang,Kai Wang,Shiguo Lian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: QC-T2I-Bench is a question-centric benchmark for text-to-image models that converts prompts into attributed atomic questions organized by Davidsonian Scene Graphs, enabling fine-grained attribution of failures, hierarchy-constrained aggregation, and training-free cost-aware model routing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing T2I benchmarks struggle because models often have similar total scores but different strengths, fine-grained benchmarks return to prompt-level scores with fixed categories, and related requirements are scored separately or as one total, obscuring the distinction between basic and compositional failures.

Method: The paper introduces a question-centric framework that converts open prompts into attributed atomic questions, organizes their dependencies using Davidsonian Scene Graphs (DSGs), uses hierarchy-constrained question aggregation to exclude downstream questions after a prerequisite fails, measures joint success within prompts, and compares repeated entities across prompts to separate basic realization from compositional failures.

Result: Evaluation on multiple open-source T2I models with English and Chinese prompts shows joint completion drops from 80.7% for components with two capabilities to 37.2% for those with seven or more. The cost-aware router matches ERNIE's 89.51-point estimate with 21.3% less GPU-s/MP.

Conclusion: The question-level evidence supports reliable model ranking and fine-grained diagnosis, and the same records can be reused for training-free, cost-aware routing.

Abstract: Modern text-to-image (T2I) models often have similar total scores but different strengths, making practical selection difficult. Fine-grained benchmarks decompose prompts into questions, yet often return them to prompt scores and fixed categories, weakening attribution and ignoring complexity. Related requirements are also scored separately or as one total, obscuring basic versus compositional failure. We present QC-T2I-Bench, a question-centric framework that converts open prompts into attributed atomic questions and organizes their dependencies with Davidsonian Scene Graphs (DSGs). We use hierarchy-constrained question aggregation to exclude downstream questions after a prerequisite fails and to prevent simple and complex prompts from receiving the same total weight. We then use the DSG structure to measure joint success within prompts and compare repeated entities across prompts, separating basic realization failures from failures under additional requirements. We evaluate multiple open-source T2I models on English and Chinese prompts. The resulting question-level evidence supports reliable ranking and fine-grained diagnosis: joint completion falls from 80.7\% for components with two capabilities to 37.2\% for those with seven or more. Finally, we reuse the same records for training-free routing; our cost-aware router matches ERNIE's 89.51-point estimate with 21.3\% less GPU-s/MP.

</details>


### [56] [AHEAD: Adaptive Hindsight with Environment-Augmented Distillation for Agentic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24114)
*Xiaolong Jin,Dingmin Wang,Vijay Lingam,Varun Kumar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AHEAD is a step-aware framework for multi-turn LLM agent RL that assigns different supervision sources to different step types: environment feedback serves as a dense signal for all steps, while LLM-generated corrective hints are provided to critical error steps. It minimally modifies the GRPO algorithm and improves task success by +13.3 points on ALFWorld and +11.0 on WebShop at 7B scale over GRPO.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing trajectory-level RL assigns uniform advantages to all steps, failing to identify which decisions caused success or failure. Prior self-distillation methods apply the same type of privileged information to every step, ignoring the asymmetry that routine steps need little guidance while critical error steps require corrective direction that environment feedback alone cannot provide.

Method: AHEAD matches different supervision sources to different step types within a step-aware framework. The teacher receives environment feedback on all steps as a grounded dense signal and LLM-generated corrective hints on error steps to supply direction lacking from environment feedback. The method introduces minimal modifications to the standard GRPO algorithm.

Result: Across ALFWorld, WebShop, and Search-based QA, and across three model scales, AHEAD raises task success (+13.3 points on ALFWorld and +11.0 on WebShop at 7B over GRPO), reaches a given success rate in fewer training steps, and solves tasks within tighter interaction budgets than outcome-only RL and prior self-distillation baselines.

Conclusion: AHEAD demonstrates that matching supervision sources to step types—dense environment feedback for routine steps and corrective hints for error steps—is an effective and minimally invasive approach to improving multi-turn LLM agent training, outperforming outcome-only RL and uniform self-distillation methods across multiple benchmarks and model scales.

Abstract: Training multi-turn LLM agents with reinforcement learning typically relies on trajectory-level rewards, which assign a uniform advantage to every step and cannot identify which decisions led to success or failure. Self-distillation methods can provide finer-grained supervision by augmenting RL with privileged information. However, existing approaches usually apply the same type of privileged information to every step in an indistinguishable manner, ignoring a key asymmetry: routine steps need little additional guidance, while critical error steps require corrective direction that environment feedback alone cannot provide. We propose AHEAD, a step-aware framework that matches different supervision sources to different step types. The teacher receives environment feedback on all steps as a grounded dense signal, and additionally receives LLM-generated corrective hints on error steps to supply the direction that environment feedback lacks. The method introduces minimal changes to the standard GRPO algorithm. Across ALFWorld, WebShop, and Search-based QA, and across three model scales, AHEAD raises task success (+13.3 points on ALFWorld and +11.0 on WebShop at 7B over GRPO), reaches a given success rate in fewer training steps, and solves tasks within tighter interaction budgets than outcome-only RL and prior self-distillation baselines.

</details>


### [57] [Robust Code RL via Faulty-Code-Driven Test case Synthesis and Dense Reward Shaping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24135)
*Yiwen Zhang,Xiaodong Yan,Zhenyu Huang,Deng Zhao,Liang Jiang,Qing Cui,Zujie Wen,Zhiqiang Zhang,Jun Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RobustTests is a framework that improves RLVR for code generation by using faulty-code-driven test case synthesis, validator agents with behavioral feature clustering for test filtering, and a stepwise dense reward function based on pass rates. It achieves a 3% absolute improvement over baselines on LiveCodeBench when fine-tuning Qwen3-32B on CodeContests data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RLVR for code generation is limited by test case comprehensiveness—insufficient test coverage causes false positives, leading to reward hacking and policy degradation. Existing automated test generation methods suffer from suboptimal quality, introducing reward bias and hallucination noise.

Method: RobustTests proposes three key components: (1) a faulty-code-driven test case synthesis strategy that uses "near correct" faulty codes to guide models in capturing latent logical discrepancies; (2) validator agents with behavioral feature clustering for granular filtering of invalid and redundant test cases; (3) a stepwise dense reward function based on pass rates to counter false negatives from hallucination noise in synthetic tests.

Result: The framework constructs a high-quality augmented test dataset for CodeContests. RL fine-tuning of Qwen3-32B with RobustTests achieves an absolute 3% performance gain on LiveCodeBench compared to baseline methods using a moderately challenging subset of CodeContests problems.

Conclusion: RobustTests effectively advances LLM code generation proficiency by mitigating reward bias from test coverage limitations and hallucination noise, confirming its efficacy as a robust training framework for RLVR in coding tasks.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR) has emerged as a pivotal technique for enhancing the code generation capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, the efficacy of RLVR in coding implementations is fundamentally limited by the comprehensiveness of test cases, because insufficient test coverage in code validation often causes false positives, further leading to reward hacking and policy degradation. To mitigate the reward bias stemming from the suboptimal quality of current automated generation methods, we propose the RobustTests framework, which introduces a faulty-code-driven test case synthesis strategy that leverages "near correct" faulty codes to guide the model in precisely capturing latent logical discrepancies and further integrates validator agents with behavioral feature clustering to facilitate the granular filtering of invalid and redundant test cases. To address false negatives caused by inherent hallucination noise in synthetic test cases, RobustTests also incorporates a stepwise dense reward function based on pass rates, bolstering training robustness through fine-grained feedback. By employing this pipeline, we construct a high-quality dataset that augmented the test cases in CodeContests, encompassing a broader spectrum of faulty code scenarios and significantly enhances diagnostic utility. Experimental results demonstrate that, by leveraging a moderately challenging subset of problems from CodeContests for training, RL fine-tuning of Qwen3-32B via RobustTests achieves an absolute 3% performance gain on the LiveCodeBench benchmark compared to baseline methods, confirming the effectiveness of the RobustTests framework in advancing the code generation proficiency of LLMs.

</details>


### [58] [OmniJudge or OmniBias? Diagnosing Multimodal Judges through Balanced, Decoupled Lenses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24160)
*Guangzheng Hu,Ziyue Jiang,Weixu Qiao,Lixin Zhang,Jianye Kang,Yuru Wu,Rong Bao,Niantong Li,Wei Wang,Ziyi Cheng,Xinfa Zhu,HangRui Hu,Ting He,Bing Zhao,Lin Qu,Hu Wei,Jin Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: D3-Omni is a balanced and decoupled benchmark for diagnosing fine-grained multimodal understanding in OmniJudges (T2I, T2V, TTS evaluators), containing 53 orthogonal binary dimensions and 10,671 samples. It uses verified fully positive seeds with controlled prompt rewriting and atomic dimension-isolating perturbations to derive negatives, achieving near 1:1 per-dimension parity and uniform total-score distribution. Findings reveal that strong OmniJudges struggle on modality-related dimensions, detect violations far less reliably than confirming satisfied requirements, and treat nominally distinct attributes as a single decision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for multimodal understanding judges (OmniJudges) overemphasize positive examples and conflate distinct failure modes, hiding capability gaps. The authors aim to create a benchmark that reliably exposes systematic blind spots in these models' failure detection abilities.

Method: The benchmark constructs negatives by fixing verified fully positive seeds and deriving negatives through controlled prompt rewriting and atomic, dimension-isolating perturbations, avoiding output re-generation that may leak information. The D3 design is Dual-balanced (alleviating negative-sample scarcity and per-dimension label imbalance), Decoupled (each error attributable to a single capability), and Dynamic (steering construction toward under-represented label regions as generative models improve).

Result: The benchmark achieves near 1:1 per-dimension parity and a uniform distribution over all total-score levels. Strong OmniJudges tend to struggle on modality-related dimensions, confirm satisfied requirements far more reliably than detecting violated ones, and treat nominally distinct attributes as largely a single decision.

Conclusion: Aggregate accuracy metrics can hide systematic blind spots in OmniJudges; a balanced and decoupled evaluation lens helps expose these capability gaps and can guide addressing them.

Abstract: Multimodal understanding models that can jointly judge text-to-image (T2I), text-to-video (T2V) and text-to-speech (TTS) generation are increasingly used as "OmniJudges" for evaluation and automatic annotation. How reliably they understand what they score remains unclear, since existing benchmarks and training data tend to overemphasize positive examples and to conflate distinct failure modes, so a judge may score well without recognizing failures while its capability gaps stay hidden. Motivated by this, we introduce D3-Omni, a balanced and decoupled benchmark for diagnosing fine-grained multimodal understanding, covering 53 orthogonal binary dimensions (17/22/14) and 10,671 samples (3,526/1,998/5,147) across the three tasks. Rather than re-generating outputs, which may leak information across dimensions, we fix verified fully positive seeds and derive negatives through controlled prompt rewriting and atomic, dimension-isolating perturbations. The resulting D3 design is Dual-balanced, which helps alleviate negative-sample scarcity and per-dimension label imbalance; Decoupled, so that each error is attributable to a single capability; and Dynamic, steering construction toward under-represented regions of the label distribution as generative models improve.The suite reaches near 1:1 per-dimension parity and a uniform distribution over all total-score levels. Under this balanced view, even strong OmniJudges tend to struggle on modality-related dimensions, to confirm satisfied requirements far more reliably than they detect violated ones, and to treat nominally distinct attributes as largely a single decision, suggesting that aggregate accuracy may hide systematic blind spots that a balanced and decoupled lens can help expose and, in turn, address.

</details>


### [59] [Task-Adaptive Rubrics for GUI Reward Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24174)
*Tao Xiong,Xavier Hu,Wenkai Wang,Qinzhuo Wu,Changqiao Wu,Pengzhi Gao,Wei Liu,Jian Luan,Shengyu Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AdaptRubric is a coarse-to-fine rubric framework for GUI agents that constructs task-adaptive judging criteria through category-level rubric retrieval and instance-level rubric generation, improving reward evaluation quality and online RL optimization performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GUI reward verifiers under-specify how success criteria should be constructed for each task instance. Their judging criteria are not sufficiently task-adaptive—they can transfer checks across tasks, overlook concrete constraints in the current instruction, or become overly strict by enforcing unstated requirements.

Method: AdaptRubric uses a two-stage framework: (1) a category-level coarse stage that routes the instruction to a GUI task family and retrieves reusable task-family criteria, and (2) an instance-level fine stage that generates compact cues for concrete values, scopes, and constraints specific to the current instruction.

Result: AdaptRubric consistently outperforms prior reward agents in both offline reward evaluation and online reinforcement learning optimization, improving F1 by 3.6 points over the baseline average under a matched image budget and yielding a 4.23-point task-success gain.

Conclusion: Task-adaptive judging criteria constructed via coarse-to-fine rubric generation significantly improve GUI agent reward modeling, leading to better offline evaluation accuracy and downstream online RL task success.

Abstract: Recent studies on GUI agents have increasingly focused on outcome reward modeling, which assigns outcome rewards by judging whether an executed trajectory satisfies the success criteria implied by the user instruction. Existing GUI reward verifiers, however, often under-specify how these criteria should be constructed for each task instance. Whether using generic rubric structures or implicit model reasoning, their judging criteria are not sufficiently task-adaptive: they can transfer checks across tasks, overlook concrete constraints in the current instruction, or become overly strict by enforcing unstated requirements. To address this limitation, we propose AdaptRubric, a Coarse-to-Fine Rubrics Framework that constructs task-adaptive judging criteria through a category-level coarse stage and an instance-level fine stage. AdaptRubric performs category-level coarse rubric retrieval by routing the instruction to a GUI task family and retrieving reusable task-family criteria, then conducts instance-level fine rubric generation to surface compact cues for concrete values, scopes, and constraints in the current instruction. Across offline reward evaluation and online reinforcement learning optimization, AdaptRubric consistently outperforms prior reward agents, improving F1 by 3.6 points over the baseline average under a matched image budget and yielding a 4.23-point task-success gain.

</details>


### [60] [Paritok-4B: Intent-Conditioned Context Compression for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24188)
*Jiayu Shi,Luzhuo Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Paritok-4B is a 4B LoRA extractive, intent-conditioned prompt compressor for coding-agent trajectories, distilled from gpt-4.1-mini on 67K real OpenHands trajectories. It compresses agent context to ~25.7% of original size on SWE-bench Lite (2x harder than gpt-4.1-mini compressors), retains 86.5% of uncompressed solve quality, and self-hosts economically on a single 24GB GPU with open-source weights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coding agents re-send large file reads and tool outputs every turn, and this context dominates their token bill. General-purpose prompt compressors are trained on prose and suit code poorly—they paraphrase identifiers and drop the exact spans an agent needs to edit.

Method: Builds on two commitments: (1) extractive compression—selecting spans rather than rewriting, with 96.0% of emitted identifiers/paths/numbers already present in input; (2) intent-conditioning—told the agent's task, it acts within a retained segment selecting which lines survive. Distills a gpt-4.1-mini teacher over 67,074 OpenHands trajectories into 40,606 validated examples and fine-tunes Qwen3-4B as a 264MB LoRA adapter.

Result: On all 300 SWE-bench Lite instances, Paritok-4B compresses to 25.7% of context size (vs 50.2% for gpt-4.1-mini, 61.9% for gpt-5), retains 86.5% of uncompressed solve quality. With cat -n line-numbered real input it compresses slightly less (27.8%) and retains more (89.3%), with McNemar p=0.079 indicating no significant solve-rate reduction. Identifier fidelity holds at 96.2% on held-out SWE-bench Lite.

Conclusion: Paritok-4B makes compressor economics decisive: as an open-source, self-hosted 264MB adapter on one 24GB GPU with no per-token fee, it outperforms frontier-LLM compressors while retaining downstream solve quality. In contrast, gpt-5 as a compressor is net-negative, costing more than the downstream tokens it saves. Weights, data, and evaluation scripts are release open (Apache 2.0).

Abstract: Coding agents re-send large file reads and tool outputs to a frontier LLM every turn, and this context dominates their token bill. General-purpose prompt compressors are trained on prose and suit code poorly: they paraphrase identifiers and drop the exact spans an agent needs to edit. We present Paritok-4B, a 4B LoRA compressor for coding-agent trajectories built on two commitments. It is extractive: it selects spans rather than rewriting them, and 96.0% of the identifiers, paths, and numbers it emits already appear in its input, holding at 96.2% on held-out SWE-bench Lite output. It is intent-conditioned: told the agent's current task, it acts chiefly inside a retained segment, selecting which lines survive (retained lines are +0.067 more intent-relevant than removed ones, paired 95% CI [+0.056, +0.078]) rather than changing how much is retained. We distil a gpt-4.1-mini teacher over 67,074 real OpenHands trajectories into 40,606 validated examples and fine-tune Qwen3-4B. On all 300 SWE-bench Lite instances, Paritok-4B compresses agent context to 25.7% of its size, 2.0x harder than a gpt-4.1-mini compressor (50.2%) and 2.4x harder than gpt-5 (61.9%), while retaining 86.5% of uncompressed single-shot solve quality. Fed the cat -n line-numbered input real agents produce, it compresses slightly less (27.8%) and retains more (89.3%); there the paired test is informative, with 30 instances solved only uncompressed and 17 only compressed, an exact McNemar p=0.079, so at this sample size compressing context to roughly a quarter of its size does not significantly reduce the solve rate. The model is a 264 MB adapter that self-hosts on one 24 GB GPU with no per-token compressor fee, which at list prices decides the economics: gpt-5 as a compressor is net-negative, costing more than the downstream tokens it saves. Weights, data, and evaluation scripts are open (Apache 2.0).

</details>


### [61] [Preference Data Selection for Mitigating the Alignment Tax in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24192)
*Minsu Kim,Jianxun Lian,Xing Xie,Steven Euijong Whang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BALIGN is a balanced data selection strategy that mitigates catastrophic forgetting (alignment tax) during LLM preference alignment by filtering high-risk training samples based on three data-centric features: reference model log-probability margin, token length difference between chosen and rejected responses, and TF-IDF similarity to general capability corpora.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Alignment with human preferences often causes catastrophic forgetting of pre-trained general capabilities (alignment tax). Previous works treat this as an optimization or architectural problem, but the inherent data characteristics driving this degradation remain underexplored. This paper addresses this gap by investigating data-centric features that dictate parameter drift during preference optimization.

Method: BALIGN identifies three key data features through theoretical and empirical analysis of the preference optimization gradient: (1) the reference model's log-probability margin, (2) the token length difference between chosen and rejected responses, and (3) TF-IDF similarity to general capability corpora. These orthogonal features are aggregated into a unified composite risk score, which is used to systematically filter out high-risk preference samples that either disrupt intrinsic model parameters or provide minimal alignment utility.

Result: Extensive experiments on standard human preference datasets show that BALIGN strongly preserves foundational capabilities without compromising alignment gains, consistently achieving the optimal Pareto frontier while incurring minimal computational overhead.

Conclusion: Data selection is an effective and efficient lever for mitigating the alignment tax in preference optimization. By filtering high-risk samples based on a composite risk score computed from three data-centric features, BALIGN achieves a better trade-off between alignment efficacy and general capability preservation than prior optimization or architectural approaches.

Abstract: Aligning large language models to human preferences is crucial for real-world deployment but frequently incurs an alignment tax, leading to the catastrophic forgetting of pre-trained general capabilities. While previous works primarily frame this problem as an optimization or architectural challenge, the inherent characteristics of preference data that drive this degradation remain largely underexplored. In this paper, we propose BALIGN, a balanced data selection strategy that explicitly mitigates catastrophic forgetting while optimizing alignment efficacy. Through theoretical and empirical analyses of the preference optimization gradient, we identify three key data-centric features that dictate parameter drift: the reference model's log-probability margin, the token length difference between chosen and rejected responses, and the TF-IDF similarity to general capability corpora. By aggregating these orthogonal features into a unified composite risk score, BALIGN systematically filters out high-risk preference samples that disrupt intrinsic model parameters or provide minimal alignment utility. Extensive experiments on standard human preference datasets demonstrate that BALIGN strongly preserves foundational capabilities without compromising alignment gains, consistently achieving the optimal Pareto frontier with minimal computational overhead.

</details>


### [62] [MetaRAG: Belief-Action Aligned Policy Optimization for Agentic RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24214)
*Qiuyi Qi,Tian Liang,Jiamu Wang,Jinjian Zhang,Wei Zhou,Pengcheng Zhu,Linjian Mo,Ming Kong,Jie Liu,Qiang Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MetaRAG is a belief-action aligned policy optimization framework for agentic RAG that uses Verify-first Action Generation and Internal Belief Probing to align search decisions with the model's internal answerability belief, improving the accuracy-efficiency trade-off on seven QA benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL-based methods for agentic RAG rely on external supervision and fail to leverage the agent's internal belief about whether the current evidence is sufficient to answer the question, leading to suboptimal search decisions.

Method: MetaRAG reformulates search decision quality as belief-action alignment. It uses Verify-first Action Generation to elicit explicit verification before each action, and Internal Belief Probing to estimate the policy model's own answerability belief from the same question-history context. A consistency reward, gated by answer correctness, is derived to avoid reinforcing internally consistent but incorrect trajectories. The belief probe is training-only with no inference overhead.

Result: Experiments on seven public QA benchmarks show consistent improvements in accuracy-efficiency trade-off over strong RL-based agentic RAG baselines, with gains transferring to deep research settings, different optimizers, and multiple model backbones.

Conclusion: Aligning the agent's search actions with its internal answerability belief provides an effective and general training signal for agentic RAG, improving performance without added inference cost.

Abstract: Agentic retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) requires language models to decide when to continue searching and when to answer. Existing RL-based methods rely on external supervision and overlook the agent's internal belief about whether the current evidence is sufficient. To address this problem, we reformulate the search decision quality as belief-action alignment and propose MetaRAG, a belief-action aligned policy optimization framework for agentic RAG. MetaRAG uses Verify-first Action Generation to elicit an explicit verification process before each actual action, and Internal Belief Probing to estimate the policy model's own answerability belief from the same question-history context. Based on these, MetaRAG derives a consistency reward that is further gated by answer correctness, avoiding reinforcement of internally consistent but incorrect trajectories. The belief probe is used only during training and introduces no inference-time overhead. Experiments on seven public QA benchmarks show that MetaRAG consistently improves the accuracy-efficiency trade-off over strong RL-based agentic RAG baselines, with gains that transfer to deep research settings, different optimizers, and multiple model backbones.

</details>


### [63] [Constraint-Guided Enterprise Data Mapping with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24218)
*Sebastian Monka,Pramod Anantharam,Thien Vo Minh,Lavdim Halilaj*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes constraint-guided mapping (CGM), a neuro-symbolic method for enterprise entity alignment that uses schema-grounded admissibility constraints as hypothesis-space operators (not post-hoc validators) to restrict candidate generation, with cascade relaxation for robustness and neural ranking with bounded LLM disambiguation. Constraints shrink candidate space by ~480x, lift F1 from 0.08 to 0.66, match frontier LLM performance at ~28x lower cost, transfer across seven enterprise datasets (macro F1 0.70), and reduce expert effort by ~7x.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise entity alignment faces challenges from semi-structured records, implicit attributes, and unit/granularity mismatches. Manual matching doesn't scale, and LLM-only matching can violate structural and physical invariants, producing fluent but operationally invalid correspondences.

Method: CGM is a three-stage neuro-symbolic method: (i) schema-grounded admissibility constraints defined by metadata mc = <tau_c, delta_c> (constraint type and executable relation/normalization logic); (ii) constraint-restricted candidate generation with cascade relaxation to guarantee a nonempty feasible set; (iii) neural ranking with bounded LLM disambiguation restricted to the feasible set. Constraints act as hypothesis-space operators rather than post-hoc validators, enabling controlled degradation and auditable decisions.

Result: Hard admissibility shrinks candidate space by ~480x without dropping ground truth; a layer-by-layer ablation shows the constraint gate (not the LLM) is decisive (F1 from 0.08 to 0.66). The benefit is model-independent with no extra inference cost—a small model with constraints matches a frontier LLM without them at ~28x lower cost. The method transfers across seven enterprise datasets (macro F1 0.70) with automatically discovered, expert-refinable constraints, reducing expert effort by ~7x versus spreadsheets. Valentine results serve as an external ranking sanity check, marking that constraints should be hard only where structural invariants are match-determining.

Conclusion: Constraint-guided mapping demonstrates that incorporating hard structural constraints into the hypothesis space—rather than applying them post-hoc or relying solely on LLM semantics—is the decisive factor for enterprise entity alignment. The approach is model-independent, cost-efficient, and transferable across domains, with constraints serving as auditable, human-guidable operators that provide controlled degradation under noise.

Abstract: Enterprise entity alignment must handle semi-structured records, implicit attributes, and unit or granularity mismatches. Manual matching is still common in practice, but does not scale as schemas and providers evolve. LLM-only matching improves semantic recall, yet can violate structural and physical invariants, producing fluent yet operationally invalid correspondences.
  We propose constraint-guided mapping (CGM), a neuro-symbolic method with three stages: (i) schema-grounded admissibility constraints with metadata mc = <tau_c, delta_c>, where tau_c denotes the constraint type and delta_c provides executable relation and normalization logic; (ii) constraint-restricted candidate generation with cascade relaxation to guarantee a nonempty feasible set under noise; and (iii) neural ranking with bounded LLM disambiguation restricted to that feasible set.
  Methodologically, constraints operate as hypothesis-space operators rather than post-hoc validators, enabling controlled degradation under relaxation and auditable, human-guidable decisions. On a controlled structural-decoy benchmark, hard admissibility shrinks the candidate space by ~480x without dropping the GT, and a layer-by-layer ablation shows this gate, not the LLM, is the decisive lift (F1 0.08 to 0.66). The benefit is model-independent and adds no extra inference cost: a small model with constraints matches a frontier LLM used without them at ~28x lower cost. The method, not a single tuned configuration, transfers across seven enterprise makes (macro F1 0.70), each under its own automatically discovered, expert-refinable constraints, and lowers expert effort by ~7x versus spreadsheet workflows. Public Valentine results add an external ranking sanity check and mark the boundary: constraints should be hard only where structural invariants are match-determining.

</details>


### [64] [Evaluating Multiple LLM Generations with Validated Task Coverage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24228)
*Florian Le Bronnec,Rio Yokota*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces VTC-Bench, a five-domain benchmark for evaluating LLMs in multi-candidate settings, along with Validated Task Coverage (VTC) as its core metric. VTC measures how many distinct useful results are obtained within k attempts, and the benchmark reveals that configurations strongest in single-draw quality are not necessarily those with the best coverage, showing that evaluating candidate sets directly uncovers model behavior differences invisible to conventional per-output evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many LLM applications produce multiple candidate outputs for comparison, validation, or combination, yet predominant evaluation settings still focus on individual outputs or reduce multiple samples to a single success. This misses whether outputs include several genuinely different useful results.

Method: The authors introduce VTC-Bench, a five-domain benchmark built from carefully selected real-data tasks where both output quality and task-relevant distinctness can be checked automatically and reproducibly without model-based judges. They propose Validated Task Coverage (VTC) as the core evaluation quantity, measuring how many distinct useful results are obtained within k attempts.

Result: Across multiple models and inference settings, the benchmark leads to different conclusions from conventional evaluation: configurations strongest in single-draw quality are not necessarily those with the best coverage, and simple measures of output variation do not reliably recover task-relevant coverage.

Conclusion: Finite candidate sets can be evaluated directly as objects of interest, revealing differences in model behavior that are not apparent from conventional per-output evaluation.

Abstract: Many LLM applications are most useful when they provide several candidate outputs for comparison, validation, or combination. Predominant evaluation settings, however, still focus on individual outputs or reduce multiple samples to a single success or selected answer. This can miss whether the outputs include several genuinely different useful results. We introduce VTC-Bench, a five-domain benchmark for this setting, together with Validated Task Coverage (VTC) as its core evaluation quantity. The benchmark is built from carefully selected real-data tasks where both output quality and task-relevant distinctness can be checked automatically and reproducibly, without model-based judges. VTC measures how many distinct useful results are obtained within $k$ attempts. Across multiple models and inference settings, the benchmark leads to different conclusions from conventional evaluation: configurations that look strongest from single-draw quality are not necessarily those with the best coverage, and simple measures of output variation do not reliably recover task-relevant coverage. These results show that finite candidate sets can be evaluated directly as objects of interest, revealing differences in model behavior that are not apparent from conventional per-output evaluation.

</details>


### [65] [TRACE: An Evidence-Grounded Benchmark for Safety Evaluation of Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24232)
*Zhenyu Wu,Siyuan Chen,Changchun Yang,Jiaqi Dong,Min Zhou,Ali Almadan,Talal Hammad,Faisal Wahbo,Aminullah Tora,Mona Alshahrani,Xin Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRACE is a new evidence-grounded safety evaluation benchmark covering prompts, reasoning traces, and final responses from Large Reasoning Models. Evaluating 18 guardrail models reveals that detecting unsafe content in reasoning traces is substantially harder than in other components, and current models struggle to extract supporting evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LRMs generate intermediate reasoning traces that may contain unsafe content even when final responses appear safe. Existing safety benchmarks focus on prompts and final responses with binary labels only, leaving reasoning traces unexamined and lacking evidence annotations to justify safety judgments.

Method: The authors introduce TRACE, a benchmark covering the full LRM inference pipeline (prompts, reasoning traces, final responses) with prompts in two languages spanning nine risk categories and ten attack strategies. Four LRMs generate reasoning traces and final responses, and each component is annotated for safety with supporting evidence extracted from source text. They evaluate 18 guardrail models on this benchmark.

Result: Safety judgment for reasoning traces is substantially more challenging than for prompts or final responses. Current guardrail models also struggle to accurately extract supporting evidence from reasoning traces.

Conclusion: There is a critical need for guardrail models that can reliably detect and precisely localize unsafe content across the entire LRM inference pipeline, not just at the prompt or final response level.

Abstract: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) generate intermediate reasoning traces that may contain unsafe content, even when their final responses appear safe. Guardrail models are designed to detect and block unsafe content, yet existing benchmarks for unsafe content detection focus primarily on prompts and final responses, leaving reasoning traces largely unexamined.
