Moreover, these benchmarks typically provide only binary safety labels, without evidence annotations that justify the judgments. To address these limitations, we introduce TRACE, an evidence-grounded safety evaluation benchmark that covers the entire LRM inference pipeline: prompts, reasoning traces, and final responses. TRACE includes prompts in two languages spanning nine risk categories and ten attack strategies. For each prompt, four LRMs generate reasoning traces and final responses, and we annotate the safety of each component and extract supporting evidence from the corresponding source text. Evaluating 18 guardrail models on TRACE reveals that safety judgment for reasoning traces is substantially more challenging than for prompts or final responses, and that current models struggle to accurately extract supporting evidence. These findings highlight the need for guardrail models that can reliably detect and precisely localize unsafe content across the LRM inference pipeline.

</details>


### [66] [STRIVE: Multi-Agent Structured Temporal Reasoning with Integrated Verification for Longitudinal Radiology Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24237)
*Junyeong Maeng,Eunsong Kang,Heung-Il Suk*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: STRIVE is a multi-agent framework for longitudinal radiology report generation that decomposes clinical reasoning into specialized Diagnosis, Attribute, and Temporal Change Agents with explicit intermediate evidence, uses Progression-Aware GRPO for the temporal agent to respect ordered progression states, and employs two-stage verification (Consistency Gate and Validation Agent). It achieves state-of-the-art clinical efficacy and more than doubles Longitudinal Change Concordance over the strongest baseline on Longitudinal-MIMIC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LRRG methods jointly model diagnosis, attribute estimation, temporal comparison, and language generation in implicit representations, causing task interference, obscuring evidence, and limiting error traceability. They also model progression states as independent labels, ignoring their ordered structure and treating missed changes and direction reversals equally.

Method: STRIVE decomposes reasoning into specialized Diagnosis, Attribute, and Temporal Change Agents producing explicit intermediate evidence. The Temporal Change Agent is post-trained with Progression-Aware GRPO, a verifiable shaped reward giving partial credit to direction-preserving errors and lowest scores to direction reversals. Two-stage verification: a deterministic Consistency Gate reconciles agent outputs before generation, and a Validation Agent checks whether the generated report is supported by aggregated evidence.

Result: On Longitudinal-MIMIC, STRIVE achieves the best clinical efficacy among recent methods and more than doubles Longitudinal Change Concordance (LCC) over the strongest baseline.

Conclusion: Structured multi-agent decomposition with progression-aware temporal reasoning and integrated verification yields superior clinical efficacy and temporal agreement in longitudinal radiology report generation compared to implicit joint modeling approaches.

Abstract: Longitudinal radiology report generation (LRRG) requires identifying both current findings and their changes relative to a prior study. Existing methods jointly model diagnosis, attribute estimation, temporal comparison, and language generation within implicit representations, which can cause task interference, obscure the evidence underlying each decision, and limit error traceability. They also model progression states as independent labels, ignoring their ordered structure and thus treating missed changes and direction reversals equally. We present STRIVE, Multi-Agent Structured Temporal Reasoning with Integrated Verification for LRRG, which decomposes clinical reasoning into specialized Diagnosis, Attribute, and Temporal Change Agents that produce explicit intermediate evidence. In particular, the Temporal Change Agent is further post-trained using Progression-Aware GRPO, a verifiable, shaped reward that assigns partial credit to direction-preserving errors while scoring direction reversals lowest. STRIVE performs verification at two stages: a deterministic Consistency Gate reconciles the agent outputs before report generation, and a Validation Agent checks whether the generated report is supported by the aggregated clinical evidence. On Longitudinal-MIMIC, STRIVE attains the best clinical efficacy among recent methods and more than doubles Longitudinal Change Concordance (LCC), a measure of temporal agreement with the reference report, over the strongest baseline.

</details>


### [67] [SA-Bench: Evaluating Semantic Alignment in LLM-Based Paper Reproduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24252)
*Xue Hu,Zewei Pan,Zeli Su,Zhou Liu,Wentao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces SemanticAlign-Bench (SA-Bench), a diagnostic benchmark for evaluating LLM agents on scientific paper reproduction. It decomposes 30 papers from top ML conferences into 1,491 atomic verifiable claims (SAUs) and evaluates 12 generator configurations across four drift dimensions, finding that even the best configuration achieves only 0.301 SAU score out of 1.0, revealing significant semantic drift in generated reproduction code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents frequently generate code for paper reproduction that runs but is scientifically unfaithful—silently diverging from paper specifications. This "semantic drift" failure mode lacks systematic measurement, motivating a diagnostic benchmark to quantify and categorize how generated code deviates from original paper requirements.

Method: The authors construct SA-Bench covering 30 papers from ICLR, ICML, and NeurIPS 2025. They decompose each paper's specifications into atomic, verifiable implementation claims called Semantic Alignment Units (SAUs), evaluate generated repositories along four diagnostic dimensions (numerical, methodological, protocol, and ordering drift), and build 1,491 SAUs across five ML domains. They evaluate 12 generator configurations (4 models × 3 scaffolds) and analyze failures through a taxonomy.

Result: The strongest configuration (Claude + PaperCoder) achieves a mean SAU score of only 0.301 out of 1.0, with an overall mean of 0.221 across 360 evaluations. Failure taxonomy reveals agents attempt most requirements but implement them incorrectly, with implementation mismatch and stubs constituting the majority of zero-scored claims. Scaffolds optimized for executability provide limited benefit for scientific reproduction.

Conclusion: Current LLM agents are largely inadequate at faithful scientific code reproduction, and existing scaffolds that prioritize executability do not address the core problem. Closing the gap requires scaffolds that emphasize semantic specification verification rather than mere code execution, suggesting a fundamental shift in how reproduction benchmarks and agent scaffolds should be designed.

Abstract: LLM agents can generate paper reproduction code, yet often produce scientifically unfaithful implementations. We define this failure mode as semantic drift, where generated code silently diverges from the paper's specifications. We introduce SemanticAlign-Bench(SA-Bench), a diagnostic benchmark covering 30 papers from ICLR, ICML and NeurIPS 2025. For each paper, we decompose its specifications into atomic and verifiable implementation claims, which we call Semantic Alignment Units (SAUs) and evaluate repositories along four diagnostic dimensions spanning numerical, methodological, protocol and ordering drift. In total, we construct 1,491 SAUs across five ML domains and evaluate 12 generator configurations (4 models $\times$ 3 scaffolds). Even the strongest configuration (Claude+PaperCoder) achieves a mean SAU score of only 0.301 out of 1.0, with an overall mean of 0.221 across 360 evaluations. A failure taxonomy reveals that agents attempt most requirements but implement them incorrectly, with implementation mismatch and stubs accounting for the majority of zero-scored claims. Our analysis further indicates that scaffolds optimized for executability provide limited leverage for scientific reproduction; narrowing the gap requires scaffolds that prioritize semantic specification verification. The benchmark, annotations and evaluation pipeline are publicly available.

</details>


### [68] [Beyond Accuracy: A Dual-Judge Evaluation Protocol for Vision-Language Models in Legally Grounded Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24258)
*Su Myat Noe,Ha Thanh Nguyen,May Myo Zin,Ken Satoh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a dual-judge evaluation protocol pairing a standard 0-10 quality judge with a strict binary semantic-equivalence judge against human-curated references, applied to UK traffic-sign interpretation. Across 4,680 evaluations, the judges show moderate association (r=0.644) but reveal an asymmetric Type II error pattern (8.0% of evaluations) where high quality scores become least trustworthy under heavy image occlusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI systems are increasingly deployed in legally accountable settings where outputs must not only be correct but justifiable against legal standards. Existing benchmarks and LLM-as-judge protocols measure task performance and response quality but do not capture whether a high-quality output is actually semantically equivalent to the correct reference, especially under degraded inputs.

Method: The authors develop a dual-judge protocol: one judge scores responses on a 0-10 quality scale, while a second judge performs strict binary semantic-equivalence checking against a human-curated reference. They apply this to a visually grounded UK traffic-sign interpretation task across 4,680 evaluations spanning seven visibility levels and two occlusion modes, and validate the 0-10 judge against human ratings (Pearson r=0.81).

Result: The two judges are moderately associated (point-biserial r=0.644) with an asymmetric Type II pattern affecting 8.0% of evaluations. Marginal Type II rate peaks at high visibility (14.2% at v=0.8) due to answer frequency, but conditioned on scores above 7, the rate is highest under heavy occlusion (54-63% at v<=0.3), meaning high quality scores are least trustworthy with degraded inputs. Human validation shows the 0-10 judge aligns well with everyday readers (r=0.81).

Conclusion: The dual-judge protocol adds a complementary evaluation signal with just one additional LLM call per evaluation, surfacing information that single-judge protocols miss—specifically, the conditional untrustworthiness of high quality scores under heavy degradation. The authors release the prompt template, occluded variants, and full evaluation results.

Abstract: AI systems are increasingly evaluated for legally accountable settings, where correct outputs must also be justifiable against an applicable legal standard. Existing legal-AI benchmarks and LLM-as-judge protocols provide important infrastructure for measuring task performance and open-ended response quality. We contribute one additional evaluation signal: a dual-judge protocol that pairs a standard 0-10 quality judge with a strict binary semantic-equivalence judge against a human-curated reference.
  We study a controlled, visually grounded regulatory task - UK traffic-sign interpretation, whose meaning is a codified question with a known reference for every input - and measure not merely whether the two judges disagree (by construction they must) but how much and where. On 4,680 evaluations under seven visibility levels and two occlusion modes, the two judges are moderately associated (point-biserial r = 0.644), while revealing an asymmetric Type II pattern affecting 8.0% of all evaluations.
  Its distribution is instructive: the marginal rate peaks at high visibility (14.2% at v = 0.8) simply because high-scoring answers are common there, but conditioned on the answer already scoring above 7, the rate is highest under heavy occlusion (54-63% at v <= 0.3), so a high quality score is least trustworthy when the input is most degraded.
  We are explicit that the signal is a property of this judge and reference: a 49-row human check shows the 0-10 judge aligns closely with everyday-reader judgement (Pearson r = 0.81; r = 0.80 with the LLM accuracy sub-score), while the equivalence judge is fairly but one-directionally stricter. The protocol adds one LLM call per evaluation and surfaces a signal single-judge protocols do not report. We release the prompt template, occluded variants, and full evaluation results.

</details>


### [69] [Real-World Knowledge-Guided Change Data Synthesis for Remote Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24263)
*Yaoyi Qi,Xingxing Weng,Chao Pang,Yongkang Cui,Xiangyu Hao,Xiaokang Zhang,Guibo Zhu,Gui-Song Xia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KnowChange is a knowledge-guided change data synthesis framework that uses pretrained vision-language models as knowledge sources to reason about plausible change locations and class transitions, enabling flexible and diverse synthetic change data generation for change detection model training, outperforming existing synthetic datasets in transfer and augmentation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing change data synthesis methods rely on handcrafted rules to simulate changes, which limits the diversity of synthesized data due to insufficient coverage of class transitions and restricts flexibility in accommodating varied change types due to predefined transition designs.

Method: KnowChange leverages pretrained vision-language models as knowledge sources to reason about plausible change locations and class transitions from pre-change scenes and desired change types. It integrates knowledge-guided change simulation with generalizable synthesis models within a unified framework, enabling flexible synthesis of diverse change types.

Result: Extensive experiments demonstrate that KnowChange-generated data consistently outperforms existing synthetic datasets in both synthetic-to-real transfer and synthetic data augmentation, despite being generated at a compact scale. The knowledge-guided change simulation can also be seamlessly integrated into existing synthesis pipelines to enhance downstream utility.

Conclusion: Knowledge-guided synthesis leveraging pretrained vision-language models offers a scalable and flexible alternative to handcrafted rule-based change data synthesis, improving both the diversity and utility of synthetic change detection training data.

Abstract: Change data synthesis provides a cost-effective solution for expanding training data and improving the performance of change detection models. However, existing synthesis methods typically rely on handcrafted rules to simulate changes, where limited coverage of class transitions restricts the diversity of synthesized data, while predefined transition designs limit their flexibility in accommodating varied change types. In this work, we introduce KnowChange, a knowledge-guided change data synthesis framework that leverages pretrained vision-language models as knowledge sources to reason about plausible change locations and class transitions from pre-change scenes and desired change types. By integrating knowledge-guided change simulation with generalizable synthesis models, KnowChange enables flexible synthesis of diverse change types within a unified framework. Extensive experiments demonstrate that KnowChange-generated data consistently outperforms existing synthetic datasets in both synthetic-to-real transfer and synthetic data augmentation, despite being generated at a compact scale. Further analyses show that the knowledge-guided change simulation can be seamlessly integrated into existing synthesis pipelines and enhance the downstream utility of synthesized data.

</details>


### [70] [Matched Excess-Outranker Regularization for Candidate-Set Interference in Continual Knowledge Graph Embedding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24273)
*Hao Ren,Junbin Gao,Jiaojiao Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Matched Excess-Outranker Regularization (MEOR) to address candidate-set interference in continual knowledge graph embedding, where newly added entities cause historical answers to lose rank despite preserved scores and old-entity ordering. MEOR compares newcomer pressure against matched old references with a one-sided penalty, improving historical MRR by 0.0057 over replay and reducing interference by 0.0055 across eight paired runs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing continual knowledge graph embedding methods focus on catastrophic forgetting but overlook that adding entities changes the candidate universe for all queries. Even when historical answers preserve their scores and ordering among old entities, they can still lose rank due to newcomer competition—a distinct form of continual rank loss that existing approaches do not address.

Method: The paper formalizes candidate-set interference and proposes MEOR, a host-level objective that compares smooth answer-relative newcomer pressure with score-blind, structurally matched old references. The one-sided penalty activates only when newcomer competition exceeds the matched reference, thereby preserving the host learner's signal for legitimate new entities.

Result: On ENTITY-ComplEx, MEOR improves historical current-universe MRR by 0.0057 over replay and reduces candidate-set interference by 0.0055, with one-sided 95% lower bounds of 0.0052 and 0.0051. It outperforms persistent calibration, matched maximum regularizer (MMR), and unmatched old regularizer (UOR), and improves historical ranking in all ten FBInc-S and FBInc-L settings with every paired 95% CI excluding zero.

Conclusion: Candidate admission is a distinct source of continual rank loss beyond catastrophic forgetting, and it can be controlled via MEOR without replacing the underlying embedding architecture or continual learner.

Abstract: Continual knowledge graph embedding updates entity and relation representations as a graph grows. Existing methods primarily address catastrophic forgetting, but entity admission also changes the candidate universe of every compatible query. A historical answer can therefore lose rank even when its score and its ordering among old entities are preserved. We formalize this effect as candidate-set interference and introduce Matched Excess-Outranker Regularization (MEOR), a host-level objective that compares smooth answer-relative newcomer pressure with score-blind, structurally matched old references. Its one-sided penalty acts only when newcomer competition exceeds the matched reference, preserving the host learner's signal for legitimate new entities. Across eight paired runs on ENTITY-ComplEx, MEOR improves historical current-universe mean reciprocal rank (MRR) by 0.0057 over replay and reduces candidate-set interference by 0.0055, with one-sided 95% lower bounds of 0.0052 and 0.0051, respectively. It satisfies the preservation criteria for old-universe ranking and newcomer acquisition and improves historical current-universe MRR over persistent calibration, matched maximum regularizer (MMR), and unmatched old regularizer (UOR). Direct ablations support each component of its reference construction and aggregation. Adding MEOR also improves historical ranking in all ten reported FBInc-S and FBInc-L host and backbone settings, with every paired 95% confidence interval excluding zero. These results establish candidate admission as a distinct source of continual rank loss and show that it can be controlled without replacing the underlying embedding architecture or continual learner.

</details>


### [71] [Eating for a Sustainable Planet: Personalized Sustainable Diet Recommendation via Constraint-Aware Decision-Making Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24274)
*Ying Jin,Weiqing Min,Mingyu Huang,Shuqiang Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a personalized sustainable diet recommendation model that treats sustainability as learnable constraints rather than user preferences, and introduces SusDiet—a dataset of ~150k recipes with broad sustainability indicators—to evaluate the approach, showing it promotes sustainable choices without compromising individual preferences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Population-level sustainability modeling exists but practical implementation requires individual-level adoption. The gap between sustainable diet requirements and individual preferences is hindered by inter-individual heterogeneity, making it challenging to align the two.

Method: The authors propose a personalized sustainable diet recommendation model based on a constraint-aware decision-making mechanism, where sustainability is incorporated through learnable constraints rather than modeled as user preferences. They also construct the SusDiet dataset with ~150k recipes featuring broad coverage of sustainability indicators.

Result: Experiments on SusDiet show the method promotes more sustainable choices without compromising individual preference.

Conclusion: The work establishes a framework for aligning individual dietary choices with planetary health, providing quantitative evidence to guide future sustainable diet interventions and policy-making for sustainable development.

Abstract: A sustainable diet represents a multi-dimensional synergy among four essential pillars: nutrition adequacy, economic affordability, cultural acceptability, and environmental respect. Despite the prevalence of population-level sustainability modeling, practical implementation relies on effective individual-level adoption. This transition is often hindered by inter-individual heterogeneity, posing a formidable challenge in aligning sustainable diet requirements with individual preferences. To address this issue, we propose a personalized sustainable diet recommendation model based on a constraint-aware decision-making mechanism, where sustainability is incorporated through learnable constraints rather than modeled as user preferences. To systematically evaluate the proposed approach, we construct a sustainable diet dataset named SusDiet with about 150k recipes, characterized by broad coverage of sustainability indicators. Experimental results on this dataset show that our method promotes more sustainable choices without compromising individual preference. This work establishes a framework for aligning individual dietary choices with planetary health, offering quantitative evidence to guide future sustainable diet interventions and policy-making for sustainable development.

</details>


### [72] [RePolicy: Reinforcement Learning for Safety-Policy Invocation in Agent Safeguards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24275)
*Houcheng Jiang,Boxuan Zhang,Qiyong Zhong,Junfeng Fang,Xiang Wang,Xiangnan He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RePolicy is an RL-based agent safeguard that learns to invoke safety policies from a dynamic policy library, generating policy-grounded rationales and safety judgments for complete agent trajectories. It uses a supervised initialization on the PolicyTraj-20K dataset followed by GRPO with verifiable rewards and policy-context perturbation, achieving strong safety detection and robust policy invocation across six benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing policy-aware safeguards rely on prompting or supervised fine-tuning, which limits their ability to adapt to unseen trajectories and changing policy contexts. There is a need for a safeguard that can learn safety-policy invocation dynamically rather than through static supervision.

Method: RePolicy employs reinforcement learning (GRPO) to learn safety-policy invocation. It takes an agent trajectory and a dynamic policy library as input, invokes applicable policies, and produces policy-grounded rationales and safety judgments. Training uses supervised initialization on the constructed PolicyTraj-20K dataset, followed by GRPO with verifiable rewards and policy-context perturbation for robustness.

Result: Across six agent safety benchmarks, RePolicy achieves strong overall safety-detection performance and robust policy invocation under varying policy contexts.

Conclusion: RePolicy demonstrates that reinforcement learning-based policy invocation enables more adaptive and context-sensitive safety judgment for language model agents, outperforming traditional prompting and supervised fine-tuning approaches.

Abstract: Safeguarding language model agents requires assessing complete execution trajectories under context-dependent safety policies. Existing policy-aware safeguards mainly rely on prompting or supervised fine-tuning, limiting their ability to adapt to unseen trajectories and changing policy contexts. We propose RePolicy, an agent safeguard that learns safety-policy invocation through reinforcement learning. Given an agent trajectory and a dynamic policy library, RePolicy invokes the applicable policy and uses its content to produce a policy-grounded rationale and safety judgment. We construct PolicyTraj-20K to support supervised initialization, followed by GRPO with verifiable rewards and policy-context perturbation. Experiments across six agent safety benchmarks show that RePolicy achieves strong overall safety-detection performance and robust policy invocation under varying policy contexts.

</details>


### [73] [ReproAgent: Contract-Guided Paper-to-Code Reproduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24291)
*Xue Hu,Zewei Pan,Zhongyuan Wang,Zhou Liu,Zeli Su,Wentao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReproAgent is a four-stage pipeline (Prepare-Plan-Generate-Repair) for converting research papers into executable code repositories, using a persistent implementation contract with two channels (implementation-requirement and reference-evidence) to manage both explicit paper content and implicit framework conventions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Paper-to-code reproduction is challenging because specifications are split: explicit paper content (algorithms, metrics, artifacts) is often lost across long agent trajectories, while implicit details (framework defaults, conventions from related work) are absent from the paper entirely.

Method: ReproAgent uses a four-stage Prepare-Plan-Generate-Repair pipeline built around a persistent implementation contract with two channels: an implementation-requirement channel that converts paper snippets into code obligations, and a reference-evidence channel that retrieves content and structure evidence from related repositories. Both channels are bound to work packages, projected into file-level contracts, and consumed across generation and repair stages.

Result: On PaperBench Code-Dev, ReproAgent achieves the highest mean score among same-backbone scaffolds under both Claude-Sonnet-4.5 and Gemini-3-Flash. End-to-end channel ablations and per-paper cases support the contribution of both channels.

Conclusion: The persistent implementation contract with dual channels effectively addresses the split-specification problem in paper-to-code reproduction, and both channels make measurable contributions to performance.

Abstract: Paper-to-code reproduction asks scientific AI agents to turn research papers into executable repositories that preserve the paper's method, protocol and artifacts. This is difficult because the specification is split: explicit paper content such as algorithms, metrics and artifacts is often lost across long agent trajectories, while implicit details such as framework defaults and conventions inherited from related work are absent from the paper. We introduce ReproAgent, a four-stage Prepare--Plan--Generate--Repair pipeline built around a persistent implementation contract with two channels: an implementation-requirement channel that turns paper snippets into code obligations, and a reference-evidence channel that retrieves content and structure evidence from related repositories. Both are bound to work packages, projected into file-level contracts, and consumed across generation and repair. On PaperBench Code-Dev, ReproAgent reaches the highest mean score among same-backbone scaffolds under both Claude-Sonnet-4.5 and Gemini-3-Flash. End-to-end channel ablations and per-paper cases support the contribution of both channels. Code and experimental artifacts are publicly available.

</details>


### [74] [VideoHarness-RSI: Recursive Harness Self-Improvement for Long-Video Understanding with Frozen Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24302)
*Guoyang Xu,Hao Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces VIDEOHARNESS-RSI, a recursive self-improvement framework that searches for executable context-construction programs (harnesses) around a frozen vision-language model for long-video understanding. By keeping the VLM and interface fixed, the authors isolate the question of how much can be gained purely from improving the executable context-construction program itself, showing consistent improvements over hand-crafted baselines and transfer to other benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-video understanding depends on how limited model context is constructed from much longer videos. Existing approaches (compression, retrieval, memory, agentic evidence acquisition) mix context construction with other components, making it hard to isolate a simpler question: how much can be gained by improving the executable context-construction program alone?

Method: The method is VIDEOHARNESS-RSI, a controlled baseline consisting of a recursive search over executable context constructors around a frozen VLM. An outer-loop proposer generates candidate harnesses using prior programs, evaluation outcomes, and execution traces. Candidates are executed and evaluated end-to-end; successful variants are retained for further recursive search. The answering model and interface remain fixed throughout.

Result: Starting from uniform sampling, recursive harness search finds consistent improvements and surpasses several weaker hand-crafted baselines. Starting from a stronger hand-crafted baseline, the same RSI process yields further improvement. The selected harness also transfers to additional long-video benchmarks without additional search.

Conclusion: Executable context construction is established as a distinct optimization layer for long-video understanding. VIDEOHARNESS-RSI provides a reproducible baseline for studying harness discovery and transfer around frozen vision-language models.

Abstract: Long-video understanding depends critically on how a limited model context is constructed from a much longer video. Existing approaches improve this process through compression, retrieval, memory, and agentic evidence acquisition, but these mechanisms are typically introduced as part of a manually designed inference system or optimized together with other components. This makes it difficult to isolate a simpler question: how much can be gained by improving the executable context-construction program alone? We study this question through VIDEOHARNESS-RSI, a controlled baseline for recursively searching executable context constructors around a frozen vision-language model (VLM). An outer-loop proposer uses prior programs, evaluation outcomes, and execution traces to generate candidate harnesses, which are executed and evaluated end to end before successful variants are retained for further search. This makes long-video understanding a controlled instance of automated harness design: the searchable object is executable program structure, while the answering model and interface remain fixed. Starting from uniform sampling, recursive harness search consistently finds room for improvement and surpasses several weaker hand-crafted baselines. Starting instead from a stronger hand-crafted baseline, the same RSI process yields a further improvement. The selected harness also transfers to additional long-video benchmarks without further search. Together, these results establish executable context construction as a distinct optimization layer and provide a reproducible baseline for studying harness discovery and transfer around frozen VLMs.

</details>


### [75] [OPDSearch+: On-Policy Distillation with RL Refinement for Search-Augmented Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24310)
*Qinglin Ye,Zhiyuan Gu,Jingjie Xia,Yiheng Zhang,Kaiyan Zhao,Shunchao Zheng,Yuhang Mu,Wenchao Du,Yiming Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OPDSearch+ is the first teacher-fine-tuning-free distillation paradigm for search-augmented reasoning in small language models. It uses a frozen off-the-shelf instruct model as a teacher with a per-position forward KL objective for on-policy distillation, followed by RL refinement, achieving significant gains over prior 3B RL baselines across seven QA benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-policy distillation (OPD) from trained teachers faces two key challenges: (1) collecting high-quality multi-turn search trajectories at scale is prohibitively expensive due to dynamic retriever responses; (2) task-specific teacher training is costly, while using off-the-shelf teachers without fine-tuning constrains student performance and causes training instability. The goal is to enable effective distillation for small language models without task-specific teacher fine-tuning.

Method: The method has two stages. Stage one performs on-policy distillation where the student interacts with a live search engine and is distilled via a per-position forward KL objective, using a frozen off-the-shelf instruct model as the teacher. This transfers reasoning decomposition and evidence integration skills without task-specific teacher training. Stage two applies reinforcement learning to refine the distilled student from a richer behavioral foundation.

Result: Across seven QA benchmarks, OPDSearch+ with a 3B model consistently outperforms all prior 3B RL baselines, achieving improvements of 13.1% on HotpotQA and 8.5% on 2WikiMultihopQA.

Conclusion: The key insight is that a frozen teacher reshapes the student's policy distribution such that subsequent RL converges to a superior solution that RL alone cannot reach. The two-stage distillation-then-RL paradigm provides a cost-effective and stable alternative for search-augmented reasoning in small language models.

Abstract: Search-augmented reasoning remains difficult for small language models. On-policy distillation (OPD) from trained teachers offers a promising direction, but suffers from two issues: (1) high-quality multi-turn search trajectories depend on dynamic retriever responses, making SFT data prohibitively expensive to collect at scale; (2) task-specifically trained teachers incur substantial training cost, while directly applying OPD with an off-the-shelf teacher without task-specific fine-tuning constrains the student to the teacher's performance ceiling and suffers from severe training instability. We propose OPDSearch+, the first distillation paradigm that requires no teacher fine-tuning for search-augmented reasoning. We investigate the role of a frozen off-the-shelf instruct model as the teacher in on-policy distillation, and reveal a key insight: the teacher reshapes the student's policy distribution so that subsequent RL converges to a superior solution that RL alone cannot reach. In stage one, the student interacts with a live search engine and is distilled via a per-position forward KL objective, transferring reasoning decomposition and evidence integration skills without any task-specific teacher training. In stage two, RL refines the distilled student from a richer behavioral foundation, achieving performance that RL alone cannot reach from scratch. Across seven QA benchmarks, OPDSearch+ with a 3B model consistently outperforms all prior 3B RL baselines, achieving gains of 13.1% on HotpotQA and 8.5% on 2WikiMultihopQA.

</details>


### [76] [Benchmarking LLM Judges for Voice-Agent Evaluation: Reliability, Calibration, and Human Oversight](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24314)
*Anupam Purwar,Shashank Singh,Kritika Srivastava*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper evaluates LLM-as-a-Judge methods for assessing conversational voice agents, comparing human judgments against GPT-4.1 and GPT-5 across telecom and retail conversations under three evaluation configurations (p0, p1, p2). The study finds that LLM-based evaluation is effective for large-scale voice-agent assessment but its reliability is metric- and configuration-dependent, supporting hybrid pipelines where LLMs handle scalable evaluation while humans handle context-sensitive metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating conversational voice agents at scale requires reliable assessment methods that capture both observable interaction quality and contextual human judgment, but current automated approaches lack evidence on where they can reliably substitute for human evaluators.

Method: Human judgments are compared with GPT-4.1 and GPT-5 on telecom and retail voice-agent conversations across quality and safety dimensions. Interactions are scored under three evaluation configurations (p0, p1, p2) to test sensitivity to setup. Analysis includes aggregate agreement, metric-level correlations, evaluator consistency, and systematic human-LLM disagreement patterns.

Result: LLM-based evaluation can effectively serve large-scale voice-agent assessment, but reliability varies by metric and evaluation configuration rather than being uniformly dependable across all dimensions.

Conclusion: The study provides an empirical framework for identifying which metrics suit automated evaluation and supports hybrid pipelines where LLM judges handle scalable assessment while human evaluators remain engaged for metrics requiring contextual interpretation and higher-confidence judgment.

Abstract: Evaluating conversational voice agents at scale re- quires reliable assessment methods that capture both observ- able interaction quality and the contextual judgment typically provided by human evaluators. We investigate LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation by comparing human judgments with GPT-4.1 and GPT-5 on telecom and retail voice-agent conversations, across conversational quality and safety dimensions. The same interac- tions are scored under three evaluation configurations, p0, p1, and p2, to test whether automated judgments are sensitive to the evaluation setup and whether observed patterns generalize across configurations and judge models. Beyond aggregate agreement, we examine metric-level correlations, evaluator consistency, and systematic human-LLM disagreement to identify which conver- sational attributes can be judged reliably by automation and which remain sensitive to interpretation and context. Effective voice-agent evaluation is also shaped by pipeline-level factors such as speech generation, streaming, and error propagation across ASR, reasoning, and tool-calling stages, motivating our focus on comparing how human and LLM judges score the same interactions end to end. Our results show that LLM- based evaluation can serve as an effective component of large- scale voice-agent assessment, but that its reliability is metric- and configuration-dependent rather than uniform. This pro- vides an empirical framework for identifying which metrics suit automated evaluation and supports hybrid pipelines in which LLM judges handle scalable assessment while human evaluators remain engaged for metrics that demand contextual interpretation and higher-confidence judgment.

</details>


### [77] [Can a Dynamic Internal Field Govern a Transformer's Cognition? Certifiability, not Superiority, in Homeostatic Compute Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24319)
*Francisco M. Arrabal-Campos,Ignacio Fernandez,Francisco G. Montoya,Alfredo Alcayde*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a dynamic internal field—a low-dimensional homeostatic state with explicit physics and certified stability—that acts as a "compute governor" on a module graph, modulating reasoning (how much to compute, when to stop, which module to activate) without performing cognition itself. It introduces an adaptive-depth reasoner advanced by a field governed by PDE families on the graph Laplacian, and proves a discrete Schur-Cohn criterion for Verlet with velocity coupling (necessary and sufficient, no commutation hypothesis). Experimental campaigns show the field's second-order structural effect is strong in one family (+0.087) but not detected in another, that at matched interfaces a GRU is indistinguishable or even exceeds the field, and that the field provides no evidence as an evidence accumulator. The key distinction is certifiability—its one-step operator admits an exact runtime stability check—not capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Intelligent systems need not only to reason but to govern their own reasoning—controlling compute budget, stopping criteria, and module selection. The authors investigate whether a dynamic internal field (a homeostatic low-dimensional state with explicit physics) can serve this governance role, and whether such a role can be certified for stability.

Method: The field lives on the module graph and is governed by a family of PDEs on the graph Laplacian, advanced jointly with an adaptive-depth reasoner. A discrete Schur-Cohn criterion for Verlet integration with velocity coupling is proven (necessary and sufficient per latent root, no commutation hypothesis), producing an integrator certificate (not closed-loop). Extensive preregistered deconfounding experiments compare field types (wave, diffusion, gated mixtures, 2D Navier-Stokes substrate) and confront matched GRU baselines, with kill-gate positive controls.

Result: Field physics type does not affect accuracy. A twenty-seed preregistered deconfounding campaign shows the second-order structural effect is strong in one family (+0.087 [ +0.042, +0.132], t=4.0) but absent in another (+0.014 [-0.013, +0.040], n.s.), indicating part of the original contrast was capacity, not order. A matched-interface GRU is indistinguishable in the first family and nominally exceeds the field in the second (-0.035 [-0.067, -0.002]). A kill-gate with positive control finds no evidence of the field as evidence accumulator (Delta AUC +0.0007 vs 0.03 threshold).

Conclusion: A dynamic internal field is a viable and certifiable compute governor but not an enhancer of cognition. Its distinguishing feature is not capability but an exact runtime stability certificate on its one-step operator—a difference of kind (certifiability) rather than of existence (learned recurrences also carry sufficient but conservative certificates). The field modulates; it does not think.

Abstract: An intelligent system does not merely reason: it governs its own reasoning - how much to compute, when to stop, which module to activate. Can that role be played by a dynamic internal field - a low-dimensional homeostatic state with explicit physics and certified stability - that modulates cognition without performing it? Ours is a field on the module graph governed by a family of PDEs on the graph Laplacian, advancing with an adaptive-depth reasoner. We certify the stability of the integrator of the whole family - an integrator certificate, not a closed-loop one. New, and proved here: a discrete Schur-Cohn criterion for Verlet with velocity coupling, necessary and sufficient per latent root, with no commutation hypothesis. The answer is threefold: substance no, structure only in part, certifiability yes. The type of the field's physics is irrelevant for accuracy: wave, diffusion, gated mixtures and a 2D Navier-Stokes substrate tie. A twenty-seed preregistered deconfounding campaign bounds the structural claim: at equalized caps the second-order effect is strong in one family (+0.087 [+0.042, +0.132], t=4.0) but is not detected in the other (+0.014 [-0.013, +0.040], n.s.), so part of the original contrast was capacity, not order; and a matched-interface GRU is indistinguishable in the first and nominally exceeds the field in the second (-0.035 [-0.067, -0.002]). What distinguishes the field is not capability but that its one-step operator admits an exact runtime stability check - a difference of kind, not of existence: learned recurrences carry certificates too, sufficient and conservative ones. A kill-gate with a positive control finds no evidence for the field as evidence accumulator (Delta AUC +0.0007 [-0.0065, +0.0079] vs a 0.03 threshold). A dynamic internal field is a viable, certifiable compute governor, but not an enhancer of cognition: it modulates, it does not think.

</details>


### [78] [SonarLLM: A Native Sonar--Optical Multimodal Large Language Model for Underwater Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24325)
*Cong Su,longxuan ma,Ling Dong,Guofeng Tang,Weijie Yin,Haohui Chen,Zhengtao Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces SonarLLM, a multimodal large language model (MLLM) that treats imaging sonar as a native perceptual modality alongside optical cameras, using a sonar-specific encoder, physics-aware feature enhancement, and reliability-aware hierarchical fusion. It also presents SonarBench, a paired benchmark spanning recognition, counting, VQA, and captioning across sonar-only, optical-only, and fusion settings. SonarLLM outperforms strong baselines by 34.4 points (sonar-only) and 25.1 points (fusion), demonstrating that fusion gains increase with optical degradation and that representing and weighting sonar according to its sensing characteristics is key to robust heterogeneous perception.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal large language models are built primarily on optical encoders and therefore cannot model imaging sonar's distinct range-azimuth structure and acoustic artifacts, nor adaptively exploit the complementarity between sonar and optical sensing under variable underwater visibility (e.g., turbidity).

Method: SonarLLM combines a sonar-specific encoder, modality-specific physics-aware feature enhancement, and reliability-aware hierarchical fusion to align acoustic structure with optical semantics and dynamically adjust each modality's contribution as sensing quality changes. The authors also construct SonarBench, a paired benchmark with four tasks (recognition, counting, VQA, captioning) and three input settings (sonar-only, optical-only, fusion), fixing scene and sonar observation while varying optical degradation to enable controlled measurement of cross-modal complementarity.

Result: SonarLLM achieves 72.0% macro accuracy across sonar-only recognition, counting, and VQA, outperforming the strongest baseline by 34.4 percentage points, and 68.7% under fusion, exceeding the best baseline by 25.1 points. The fusion-over-optical gain grows monotonically from 6.0 to 36.0 points as turbidity increases, demonstrating the increasing complementary value of sonar under controlled optical degradation.

Conclusion: Robust heterogeneous underwater perception depends not merely on adding sonar as an input, but on representing it natively and weighting it according to its sensing characteristics, so that sonar-optical complementarity is dynamically exploited as visibility conditions change.

Abstract: Reliable underwater perception requires complementary sensing under variable visibility. Optical cameras capture appearance and semantics but degrade rapidly with turbidity, whereas imaging sonar preserves geometry while exhibiting distinct range-azimuth structure and acoustic artifacts. Existing MLLMs, built primarily on optical encoders, are therefore ill-suited to model sonar or adaptively exploit sonar-optical complementarity. We propose SonarLLM, a sonar-optical MLLM that treats sonar as a native perceptual modality. It combines a sonar-specific encoder, modality-specific physics-aware feature enhancement, and reliability-aware hierarchical fusion to align acoustic structure with optical semantics and dynamically adjust their contributions as sensing quality changes. We also introduce SonarBench, a paired benchmark that spans four tasks: recognition, counting, visual question answering, and captioning; and, across the benchmark, three input settings: sonar-only, optical-only, and fusion. By fixing the scene and sonar observation while varying optical degradation, SonarBench enables controlled measurement of cross-modal complementarity. SonarLLM achieves 72.0% macro accuracy across sonar-only recognition, counting, and VQA, outperforming the strongest baseline by 34.4 percentage points, and 68.7% under fusion, exceeding the best baseline by 25.1 points. For recognition and counting, the fusion-over-optical gain grows from 6.0 to 36.0 points as turbidity increases, indicating the increasing complementary value of sonar under controlled optical degradation. Together, these results show that robust heterogeneous perception depends not only on adding sonar, but on representing and weighting it according to its sensing characteristics.

</details>


### [79] [Selective Regenerative Decoding: Trajectory-Level Intervention for Inference-Time Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24338)
*Sophia Xiao Pu,Yumo Xu,Sailik Sengupta,Millennium Bismay,Ruixue Lian,James Gung,Yi-an Lai,Arshit Gupta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SRD improves LLM inference-time decoding by selectively regenerating only degraded suffix portions of candidate trajectories instead of discarding or keeping entire trajectories atomically, achieving provable 1.28-1.36x sample efficiency gains over rejection sampling and matching Best-of-N accuracy with fewer generated tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing inference-time decoding methods treat each candidate trajectory as atomic, either fully retaining or irreversibly discarding it. This wastes computation on partially promising candidates whose high-quality prefixes are abandoned alongside degraded suffixes, leaving room for more fine-grained segment-level intervention.

Method: Selective Regenerative Decoding (SRD) routes each candidate to one of three actions: discard, keep, or refine. For borderline candidates, it preserves the useful prefix and regenerates only the degraded suffix portion, without requiring a larger target model. The method provides theoretical guarantees on sample efficiency under mild assumptions.

Result: SRD achieves a provable 1.28-to-1.36-fold gain in sample efficiency over rejection sampling with strictly higher expected trajectory quality, with gains growing as the candidate pool grows. Across MATH500, GPQA Diamond, HotpotQA, and AlpacaEval with multiple generation-reward model pairs, SRD matches Best-of-N accuracy with substantially fewer generated tokens and outperforms speculative rejection in low-compute regimes.

Conclusion: By enabling segment-level intervention rather than whole-trajectory selection, SRD opens a previously underexplored region of the accuracy-compute tradeoff for inference-time reasoning, demonstrating that partial regeneration of degraded suffixes is both theoretically and empirically advantageous.

Abstract: Inference-time decoding methods improve LLM reasoning by exploring multiple candidate trajectories, yet treat each trajectory as atomic: either retaining it whole or discarding it irreversibly. This wastes computation on partially promising candidates whose high-quality prefixes are abandoned alongside degraded suffixes. We introduce Selective Regenerative Decoding (SRD), which routes each candidate to discard, keep, or refine only the degraded portion of the suffix while preserving the useful prefix of borderline candidates, without requiring a larger target model. Under mild assumptions, SRD achieves a provable 1.28-to-1.36-fold gain in sample efficiency over rejection sampling with strictly higher expected trajectory quality, with the gain growing as the candidate pool grows. Across MATH500, GPQA Diamond, HotpotQA, and AlpacaEval with multiple generation-reward model pairs, SRD matches Best-of-N accuracy with substantially fewer generated tokens and outperforms speculative rejection in low-compute regimes. By enabling segment-level intervention rather than whole-trajectory selection, SRD opens a previously underexplored region of the accuracy-compute tradeoff for inference-time reasoning.

</details>


### [80] [The Handoff Tax: Continuing Non-Native Trajectories in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24358)
*Roy Ganz,Mor Shpigel Nacson,Adi Kalyanpur,Ron Litman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies model handoffs in coding agents—switching between low-cost/low-capability (LC) and high-cost/high-capability (HC) models mid-task. It finds that full-trajectory escalation recovers less than half the quality gap while incurring a large cost premium ("handoff tax"), whereas downshifting offers a favorable cost-quality trade-off. Interface preferences reverse with direction: reducing LC trajectory info improves escalation, while removing HC trajectory info harms downshift quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coding agents execute long, multi-call tasks where users face cost-quality trade-offs when escalating to stronger models or downshifting to cheaper ones. Each switch forces the receiving model to continue a trajectory produced by another model, and it is unclear how handoff timing, direction, and inherited trajectory information affect cost and quality.

Method: Using LC and HC model pairs from Claude and GPT families, the authors systematically vary handoff direction (escalation vs. downshift), timing, and interface—comparing full-trajectory transfer, compaction, and trajectory removal while preserving repository state.

Result: Full-trajectory escalation recovers less than half of the LC-to-HC quality gap at substantial extra cost ("handoff tax"). Downshift yields a favorable cost-quality point. Preferred interface reverses with direction: reducing LC trajectory info improves escalation quality, whereas removing the HC trajectory reduces downshift quality.

Conclusion: Model handoffs impose a measurable cost-quality penalty influenced by direction and trajectory information. Designers should tailor handoff interfaces to direction: minimize inherited LC trajectory detail for escalations, but preserve HC trajectory content for downshifts.

Abstract: Coding agents perform long-running tasks spanning dozens of model calls, tool uses, and code edits. As these runs unfold, users face a practical cost-quality trade-off: escalating to a stronger model when a cheaper one struggles, or downshifting once the hard reasoning is complete. Each switch requires the receiver to continue a non-native trajectory produced by another model. We study how this handoff affects quality and cost, and how varying the trajectory information inherited by the receiver changes the outcome. Using pairs of low-cost, low-capability (LC) and high-cost, high-capability (HC) models from the Claude and GPT families, we vary handoff direction, timing, and interface, comparing full-trajectory transfer, compaction, and trajectory removal while preserving the repository state. Across both model families, full-trajectory escalation recovers less than half of the LC-to-HC quality gap while incurring a substantial cost premium. We term this cost-quality penalty the handoff tax. By contrast, downshift offers a favorable cost-quality point. Interestingly, the preferred interface also reverses with direction: reducing LC-model trajectory information improves escalation quality, whereas removing the HC-model trajectory reduces downshift quality.

</details>


### [81] [Adaptive Influence Graphs for Failure Attribution in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24361)
*Yarden Bakish,Amir Dudai,Roy Ganz,Oren Nuriel,Elad Ben Avraham,Mor Shpigel Nacson,Ron Litman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Adaptive Influence Graphs (AIGs), a two-stage agentic framework that transforms failed multi-agent LLM traces into structured graphs and navigates them to identify critical errors, achieving state-of-the-art results on the Who&When failure attribution benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent LLM systems are deployed in real-world applications where failures are costly to localize, yet diagnosing failed runs still relies on human engineers. Engineers don't debug by reading raw logs end to end; they use observability tools that organize traces around components, actions, and dependencies. The authors hypothesize that modern LLMs can benefit from the same structured-trace paradigm for failure attribution.

Method: AIGs is a two-stage agentic framework: (1) it transforms a failed trace into a structured graph, and (2) navigates the graph to identify the critical error. The framework supports adaptive graph construction and agent-directed traversal.

Result: Across multiple models, richer trace representations consistently improve failure attribution, with adaptive graph construction and agent-directed traversal yielding the strongest results. AIGs establishes a new state of the art on the Who&When benchmark for multi-agent failure attribution.

Conclusion: The results affirm that failure attribution depends not only on the diagnosing model, but critically on how the trace is represented and explored—supporting the hypothesis that LLMs benefit from structured, observability-style trace representations.

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM systems are increasingly deployed in real-world applications, where failures can be costly and difficult to localize. Despite growing efforts to automate failure attribution, diagnosing failed runs still largely relies on human engineers. Yet engineers rarely debug complex systems by reading raw logs end to end. Instead, observability tools organize traces around components, actions, and dependencies to support targeted navigation. We hypothesize that modern LLMs can benefit from the same paradigm. To test this hypothesis, we introduce Adaptive Influence Graphs (AIGs), a two-stage agentic framework that first transforms a failed trace into a structured graph and then navigates it to identify the critical error. Across multiple models, we show that richer trace representations consistently improve failure attribution, with adaptive graph construction and agent-directed traversal yielding the strongest results. AIGs establish a new state of the art on Who&When, the standard benchmark for multi-agent failure attribution. This affirms our hypothesis that attribution depends not only on the diagnosing model, but also on how the trace is represented and explored.

</details>


### [82] [From State to Action: OODA-Tool for Reliable Multi-Turn Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24368)
*Rongfeng Guo,Yinxuan Huang,Yusen Wu,Maoqing Zhong,Yunlu Chen,Meng Tang,Teng Long,Vincent Tao Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OODA-Tool is a typed closed-loop policy inspired by Boyd's Observe-Orient-Decide-Act cycle that separates state preservation from action realization in multi-turn tool use, routing decisions through controller-checked intermediate states to mitigate state-action competition in autoregressive policies. It consistently outperforms direct function-calling and ReAct policies across Qwen3 models (0.6B-14B) in multi-turn, multi-tool, and incomplete-information settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct function-calling and ReAct-style policies couple state tracking and action generation within the same autoregressive trajectory, creating state-action competition where pressure to produce the next call can overwrite or ignore accumulated information, degrading reliability in multi-turn tool use.

Method: OODA-Tool implements a typed closed-loop policy with four stages: Observe reconstructs the current task state, Orient determines if execution is warranted, Decide forms an admissible action structure, and Act realizes the external output. This architecture routes decisions through controller-checked intermediate states, separating state preservation from action realization.

Result: OODA-Tool consistently improves task success across model sizes (0.6B to 14B) compared to direct function-calling and ReAct policies. Larger gains are observed on smaller models and on tasks whose actions depend heavily on cross-turn accumulated information and prior tool results. Controlled variants, stage-level ablations, and transfer evaluations confirm robustness.

Conclusion: Separating state tracking from action generation via explicit intermediate state routing effectively mitigates state-action competition in tool-use agents, with the OODA-inspired architecture providing reliable improvements particularly for smaller models and information-dependent tasks.

Abstract: Reliable multi-turn tool use requires an agent to preserve an evolving task state and ensure that each action remains consistent with it. However, direct function-calling and ReAct-style policies learn state tracking and action generation within the same autoregressive trajectory. This coupling creates state-action competition: the pressure to produce the next call can overwrite or ignore information accumulated earlier in the interaction. Inspired by Boyd's Observe-Orient-Decide-Act cycle, we introduce OODA-Tool, a typed closed-loop policy designed to mitigate this competition by separating state preservation from action realization. Rather than generating an action directly from the interaction history, OODA-Tool routes each decision through controller-checked intermediate states, ensuring that the final output remains grounded in the current task state. Specifically, Observe reconstructs the task state, Orient determines whether execution is warranted, Decide forms an admissible action structure, and Act realizes the external output. We evaluate OODA-Tool against direct function-calling and ReAct policies using Qwen3 models ranging from 0.6B to 14B across multi-turn, multi-tool, and incomplete-information settings. OODA-Tool consistently improves task success across model sizes, with larger gains on smaller models and on tasks whose actions depend strongly on information accumulated across turns and prior tool results. Controlled variants, stage-level ablations, and transfer evaluations further demonstrate the robustness of these improvements.

</details>


### [83] [Do Recipes Have Personas? Characterizing and Generating Creator Style in Attributed Procedural Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24369)
*Lei Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces procedural stylometry—the computational discovery of individual creator personas from procedural execution graphs—using a new dataset (ViralRecipesTrans) of culinary video transcripts. It formulates the task as graph learning and process discovery, revealing that lexical classifiers overfit while topological metrics capture physical workflow constraints. The authors extend this to a generative task of predicting a creator's execution graph for unseen dishes, showing LLMs excel at semantic assignment but lack macro-planning, while a structured two-stage model achieves superior topological control. An ensemble approach synthesizes both strengths for personalized workflow generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models homogenize procedural logic, obscuring the unique execution processes of individual creators. The authors aim to computationally discover and preserve these idiosyncratic "procedural personas" from unstructured data such as video transcripts.

Method: The paper introduces ViralRecipesTrans, a dataset of procedurally aligned execution flow graphs from culinary video transcripts mapped to specific creators. Procedural stylometry is formulated as graph learning and process discovery, comparing traditional lexical classifiers against discrete topological metrics. The framework is extended to a generative task of predicting a creator's exact structural execution graph for unseen dishes, using a structured two-stage model with Markovian priors and an ensemble approach with LLMs.

Result: Lexical classifiers overfit via semantic leakage, while discrete topological metrics successfully capture rigid physical workflow constraints. Few-shot LLMs dominate semantic assignment but suffer from macro-planning deficits, whereas the structured two-stage model achieves superior topological control via rigid Markovian priors. An ensemble approach dynamically synthesizes global semantic reasoning with localized topological footprints to outperform both individual approaches.

Conclusion: Procedural persona generation requires a dual approach: semantic reasoning for global macro-planning (best handled by LLMs) and topological control for local structural execution (best handled by structured models). An ensemble framework combining both yields the most effective automated discovery and generation of personalized workflows.

Abstract: While large language models (LLMs) possess vast zero-shot procedural knowledge, their tendency to produce homogenized logic often obscures the unique, idiosyncratic execution processes of individual human creators. In this paper, we investigate the computational discovery of procedural personas from unstructured data. To achieve this, we introduce ViralRecipesTrans, a new dataset of procedurally aligned execution flow graphs extracted from popular culinary video transcripts and explicitly mapped to specific creators. We formulate procedural stylometry as a graph learning and process discovery task, revealing a fundamental duality: while traditional lexical classifiers overfit via semantic leakage, discrete topological metrics successfully capture the rigid physical constraints of a creator's workflow. Building upon this characterization, we extend our framework into a novel generative task--predicting a creator's exact structural execution graph for unseen dishes. We expose a fundamental dichotomy in style generation between global macro-planning and local structural execution. Our results demonstrate that few-shot LLMs dominate semantic assignment but suffer from persistent macro-planning deficits, whereas our structured two-stage model achieves superior topological control via rigid Markovian priors. Together, an ensemble approach to procedural generation combines the strengths from both sides, dynamically synthesizing global semantic reasoning with localized topological footprints to automate the discovery and generation of personalized workflows.

</details>


### [84] [ResiSpec: Enhancing Multi-Candidate Speculative Sampling via Residual Distribution Shaping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24411)
*Zhi-Kai Chen,Jun-Jie Tao,Wei-Xiang Mao,De-Chuan Zhan,Han-Jia Ye*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ResiSpec is a framework that addresses Residual Drift in multi-candidate speculative decoding by strategically reforming the proposal distribution during verification, achieving up to 1.92x speedup over state-of-the-art multi-candidate methods without compromising output exactness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-candidate speculative decoding schemes are bottlenecked by a phenomenon called Residual Drift: when initial candidates are rejected, the residual target distribution diverges from the draft model's predictions, rendering subsequent candidates ineffective and forcing expensive resampling. This undermines the efficiency gains of multi-candidate approaches.

Method: ResiSpec proposes a framework that strategically reforms the proposal distribution during the verification phase to anchor the residual target mass within the draft model's high-confidence regions. This is achieved by mathematically re-aligning the verification process while preserving output exactness, thereby preventing candidate obsolescence.

Result: ResiSpec achieves up to 1.92x speedup over state-of-the-art multi-candidate speculative decoding methods.

Conclusion: ResiSpec effectively resolves the Residual Drift bottleneck in multi-candidate speculative decoding by re-aligning the proposal distribution, delivering significant speedups while maintaining output exactness.

Abstract: The efficiency of Large Language Model (LLM) serving is fundamentally limited by the sequential nature of autoregressive decoding. Speculative Decoding (SD) mitigates this by using a lightweight draft model to speculate future tokens, which are then validated by the LLM in a single parallel forward pass. To further boost efficiency, multi-candidate schemes propose diverse candidate sets to increase the likelihood of token acceptance. However, we show that these schemes are bottlenecked by Residual Drift: a phenomenon where the rejection of initial candidates causes the residual target distribution to diverge from the draft model's predictions. This shift renders subsequent candidates ineffective and forces the system into expensive resampling. To resolve this, we propose ResiSpec, a framework that strategically reforms the proposal distribution during verification to anchor the residual target mass within the draft model's high-confidence regions. By mathematically re-aligning the verification process without compromising output exactness, ResiSpec prevents candidate obsolescence and achieves up to 1.92$\times$ speedup over state-of-the-art multi-candidate methods. Code is available at https://github.com/Czzzk/Resispec.

</details>


### [85] [A Judge Should Know What Changed:Construct Validity for LLM-as-a-Judge Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24419)
*Jianlin Chen,Wenhui Chen,Ziyao Lin,Chi Man Vong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper formalizes construct validity for LLM-as-a-judge evaluators using a two-dimensional profile (invariance S and construct sensitivity R), showing these are independent, that no scalar summary preserves all comparisons, and that high agreement can coexist with weak sensitivity. Empirical results across 7 judges and 4 domains show judges at S >= 0.90 average S = 0.945 but R = 0.319, with sensitivity differing between scope (0.383) and strength (0.262) edits. Audits of five public label sets reveal surface-only predictors reproduce 55%-67% of labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-as-a-judge evaluation relies on agreement and robustness to surface perturbations, but reliability does not establish construct validity—whether the judge actually responds to meaningful changes in the construct being evaluated.

Method: Formalizes construct validity as a two-dimensional profile (invariance S and construct sensitivity R); measures this profile across 7 judges and 4 domains using 7 construct-changing intervention types and 5 register-only controls; intervention direction determined by human annotators with generation, verification, and judging assigned to disjoint model families; also audits five public label sets for surface-only predictor reproduction.

Result: S and R are shown to be independent with no scalar summary preserving all relevant comparisons. At matched invariance S >= 0.90, judges average S = 0.945 but R = 0.319. Scope vs. strength sensitivity differs significantly (0.383 vs 0.262, +0.121 gap consistent across all 7 judges). Surface-only predictors reproduce 55%-67% of labels in paired mode, including 67.4% of MT-Bench human votes.

Conclusion: High judge agreement can coexist with weak sensitivity to construct changes, motivating joint reporting of invariance and sensitivity and auditing the validation set itself rather than relying on agreement metrics alone.

Abstract: LLM-as-a-judge evaluation is usually assessed by agreement and robustness to surface perturbations, but reliability does not establish construct validity. We formalize construct validity for an evaluator as a two-dimensional profile: invariance S, the probability that a verdict is unchanged under construct-preserving edits, and construct sensitivity R, the probability that it changes under minimal construct-changing edits. We show that S and R are independent and that no scalar summary preserves all relevant comparisons. We measure the profile across 7 judges and 4 domains using 7 construct-changing intervention types and 5 register-only controls, with intervention direction determined by human annotators and generation, verification, and judging assigned to disjoint model families. At matched invariance S >= 0.90, judges average S = 0.945 but R = 0.319. Sensitivity also differs between scope and strength edits: R_scope = 0.383 versus R_strength = 0.262, a +0.121 gap with the same sign for all 7 judges. We further audit five public label sets and find that surface-only predictors reproduce 55%-67% of labels in paired mode, including 67.4% of MT-Bench human votes. These results show that high judge agreement can coexist with weak sensitivity to changes in the construct being evaluated, motivating joint reporting of invariance and sensitivity and auditing the validation set itself.

</details>


### [86] [Partial Identification under Causal Orders by Linear Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24427)
*Eric Rossetto,Alessandro Antonucci*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a framework for bounding counterfactual and nested counterfactual queries without requiring a fully specified causal graph. The key insight is that any counterfactual query induces a (mostly partial) topological ordering over relevant variables, which can be used to parametrize the query and reduce identification to a linear program. The bounds are proven tight via constructive structural causal models, and the approach generalizes the classical bounding framework of Tian and Pearl (2000) for probabilities of causation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional non-parametric (partial) identification of counterfactual queries requires a fully specified causal graph, which is often unavailable due to incomplete domain knowledge. The authors seek to relax this requirement by exploiting structural constraints inherently implied by the query itself, enabling identification even without an input causal graph.

Method: The paper shows that any counterfactual inquiry induces a (mostly partial) topological ordering over relevant variables. This ordering enables explicit query parametrization, reducing the identification problem to a linear programming task. The framework generalizes the Tian and Pearl (2000) bounding approach, and tightness is proven by constructing structural causal models that attain the bounds while remaining compatible with observed data and the query-implied order.

Result: The proposed bounding procedure can bound arbitrary counterfactual and nested counterfactual queries. The bounds are proven tight via constructive structural causal models. The generality and practical utility are demonstrated through multiple case studies from the literature, showing informative insights can be derived even in the absence of an input causal graph.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that complete causal graph knowledge is not necessary for meaningful counterfactual identification. Instead, query-implied structural assumptions suffice to produce tight, informative bounds, generalizing prior work and broadening the applicability of counterfactual analysis to settings with incomplete domain knowledge.

Abstract: Non-parametric (partial) identification of counterfactual queries typically relies on a fully specified causal graph. Motivated by settings with incomplete domain knowledge, we challenge this requirement by leveraging structural assumptions that are inherently implied by the query itself. We show that any counterfactual inquiry induces a, mostly partial, topological ordering over relevant variables, which, in turn, enables an explicit query parametrisation reducing the identification task to a linear program. This allows bounding arbitrary counterfactual and nested counterfactual queries. Our work can be viewed as a generalisation of the classical bounding framework of Tian and Pearl (2000), originally developed for probabilities of causation. We also prove the \emph{tightness} of our bounds by constructing structural causal models that attain the bounds whilst being compatible with both the observed data and the query-implied order. To assess both the generality and practical utility of the proposed bounding procedure, we revisit several case studies from the literature, demonstrating how the derived bounds can be used to yield informative insights even in the absence of an input causal graph.

</details>


### [87] [A Behavior-Guided Online Probabilistic Forecasting Method for Electric vehicle Charging Loads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24441)
*Chenghan Li,Qingxiang Liu,Yinliang Xu,Yuxuan Liang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a behavior-guided online probabilistic forecasting framework for EV charging loads that explicitly models persistent station-specific patterns and recent behavioral changes. It uses a dual-timescale behavior representation, semantic encoding of changes for drift-aware adaptation, and a delayed-feedback mechanism for consistent online updates. Experiments on ten real-world stations show 15.3%/17.8% improvements in MSE and Pinball loss for 1-h-ahead forecasts, and 16.8%/22.6% for 4-h-ahead forecasts over the best baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EV charging loads exhibit strong behavioral heterogeneity and temporal variability, making online probabilistic forecasting difficult under evolving operating conditions. Persistent charging patterns differ across stations, and recent behavioral changes continuously alter underlying load distributions, challenging conventional and drift-aware forecasting models.

Method: The framework constructs a dual-timescale behavior representation distinguishing long-term charging characteristics from recent behavioral states and quantifying their deviations. These behavioral changes are semantically encoded to guide drift-aware forecasting adaptation. A delayed-feedback mechanism ensures temporally consistent online updates when observations arrive across different forecasting horizons.

Result: On ten heterogeneous real-world charging stations, the method consistently outperforms conventional and concept-drift-aware online baselines. For 1-h-ahead forecasting, MSE and Pinball loss are reduced by 15.3% and 17.8% respectively. For 4-h-ahead forecasting, improvements reach 16.8% and 22.6% respectively.

Conclusion: The behavior-guided approach effectively handles evolving charging behaviors and extended forecasting horizons, delivering consistent performance gains in both forecasting accuracy and probabilistic reliability across heterogeneous real-world EV charging stations.

Abstract: Electric vehicle (EV) charging loads exhibit strong behavioral heterogeneity and temporal variability, posing significant challenges for online probabilistic forecasting under evolving operating conditions. In particular, persistent charging patterns may differ substantially across stations, while recent behavioral changes can continuously alter the underlying load distributions. This paper proposes a behavior-guided online probabilistic forecasting framework that explicitly characterizes persistent station-specific patterns and recent behavioral changes.
