A dual-timescale behavior representation is constructed to distinguish long-term charging characteristics from recent behavioral states and quantify their deviations. These behavioral changes are further semantically encoded to guide drift-aware forecasting adaptation, while a delayed-feedback mechanism ensures temporally consistent online updates when observations become available across different forecasting horizons. Experiments on ten heterogeneous real-world charging stations demonstrate that the proposed method consistently outperforms conventional forecasting models and concept-drift-aware online baselines in forecasting accuracy and probabilistic reliability. For 1-h-ahead forecasting, the proposed method reduces MSE and Pinball loss by 15.3\% and 17.8\%, respectively, over the corresponding best baselines. For 4-h-ahead forecasting, the improvements further reach 16.8\% and 22.6\%, respectively, demonstrating consistent performance gains under evolving charging behaviors and extended forecasting horizons.

</details>


### [88] [Mahalanobis-Based Multi-Head Attention for Complex State Propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24462)
*Xiaohe Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes MHA-CSP, a Mahalanobis distance-based RBF kernel attention mechanism that replaces standard dot-product attention, enabling direct construction of Tree Attention via positive definiteness, plus an attention meshing mechanism for cross-head collaboration. With only 119K parameters, it outperforms Transformer and GCN baselines on long-sequence state tracking tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard dot-product attention and graph-based baselines struggle to achieve efficient structured reasoning on long-sequence state tracking tasks, motivating the need for a novel attention mechanism that can capture symbolic structures with better efficiency-performance trade-offs.

Method: MHA-CSP replaces dot-product attention with a Mahalanobis distance-based RBF kernel, computing attention in an infinite-dimensional feature space without extra parameters. The positive definiteness of Mahalanobis distance enables direct Tree Attention construction from accumulated distances with a LogSumExp correction. Multi-head Mahalanobis distance matrices are repurposed for an attention meshing mechanism enabling cross-head kernel collaboration. The model uses teacher forcing only at the final hidden state and inherits efficient information bypass from the CSP backbone.

Result: With only 119K parameters and teacher forcing applied exclusively at the final hidden state, MHA-CSP consistently outperforms Transformer and GCN baselines trained from scratch under identical conditions on long-sequence state tracking tasks, achieving robust structured reasoning via synthetic distance rectification.

Conclusion: Complex-valued state propagation with collaborative multi-head rectification effectively captures symbolic structures, establishing a new efficiency-performance trade-off for structured reasoning.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose \textbf{Mahalanobis-Based Multi-Head Attention} (MHA-CSP), a novel attention mechanism that replaces the standard dot-product with a \textbf{Mahalanobis distance-based RBF kernel}, which effectively computes attention in an infinite-dimensional feature space without increasing the parameter count. Crucially, the positive definiteness of the Mahalanobis distance enables a \textbf{direct construction of Tree Attention}: attention scores are built directly from accumulated distances, with a LogSumExp correction that rectifies the raw distance by subtracting the log-sum of edge exponentials. Moreover, the multi-head Mahalanobis distance matrices are themselves repurposed to construct an \textbf{attention meshing mechanism}, enabling cross-head kernel collaboration that simultaneously boosts accuracy and training efficiency.
  Extensive experiments demonstrate that MHA-CSP, with only 119K parameters and \textbf{teacher forcing applied exclusively at the final hidden state}, consistently outperforms Transformer and GCN baselines trained from scratch under identical conditions on long-sequence state tracking tasks. While these baselines rely on dense attention or graph propagation, MHA-CSP achieves robust structured reasoning via synthetic distance rectification---powered by Mahalanobis-based attention---and efficient information bypass inherited from the CSP backbone.
  This result highlights the effectiveness of complex-valued state propagation with collaborative multi-head rectification in capturing symbolic structures, establishing a new efficiency-performance trade-off for structured reasoning.

</details>


### [89] [HMGCLIP: Heterogeneous Multi-Granularity Contrastive Learning for E-commerce Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24467)
*Qiuyu Zhu,Yi Gao,Zhichao Wan,Mingyang Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HMGCLIP is a unified multimodal embedding framework for e-commerce that uses heterogeneous hypergraph construction to mine structure-aware hard negatives and align multi-granular semantics, enabling dual-granularity inference for both fine-grained and coarse-grained product understanding tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Multimodal Large Language Models encode product information into global embeddings, which limits their ability to capture fine-grained attributes needed for precise discrimination tasks such as distinguishing subtle material differences among visually similar products.

Method: HMGCLIP constructs a heterogeneous hypergraph to leverage hypergraph topology for mining structure-aware hard negatives and aligning multi-granular semantics at relation and hyperedge levels. It employs a dual-granularity inference mechanism that dynamically fuses attribute evidence for both fine-grained and coarse-grained downstream tasks.

Result: Extensive experiments on a newly released fine-grained e-commerce dataset and the public MAVE benchmark show HMGCLIP outperforms strong multimodal encoders, MLLMs, and e-commerce baselines.

Conclusion: HMGCLIP validates that hypergraph-based heterogeneous structure modeling with multi-granular semantic alignment can effectively improve fine-grained product understanding in e-commerce, and the released comprehensive fine-grained dataset supports future benchmarking efforts.

Abstract: Although recent Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have advanced general product understanding, they implicitly encode product information into global embeddings, thereby limiting their ability to capture fine-grained attributes. This limitation hinders performance in tasks requiring precise attribute discrimination, such as distinguishing subtle material differences among visually similar products. To address this challenge, we propose HMGCLIP, a unified multimodal embedding framework. By constructing a heterogeneous hypergraph, we leverage hypergraph topology to mine structure-aware hard negatives and align multi-granular semantics at both relation and hyperedge levels. This design enables a dual-granularity inference mechanism that dynamically fuses attribute evidence for both fine-grained and coarse-grained downstream tasks. Furthermore, we release a comprehensive fine-grained e-commerce dataset to facilitate future benchmarking. Extensive experiments on this new dataset and the public MAVE benchmark show that HMGCLIP outperforms strong multimodal encoders, MLLMs, and e-commerce baselines, validating the superiority of HMGCLIP.

</details>


### [90] [Reinforcement Learning-Guided Evolutionary Policy Optimization for Preference-Adjustable Heterogeneous Agile Earth Observation Satellite Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24470)
*He Wang,Junyu Wu,Hui Li,Yanjie Song,Witold Pedrycz,Liang Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a reinforcement-learning-assisted operator-selection memetic evolutionary algorithm (RLOSMEA) for heterogeneous agile Earth observation satellite scheduling. It decouples schedule decoding, population-based search, and online actor-critic operator control, using assignment-based indirect encoding and decoder-based equivalent-cost evaluation to handle satellite-dependent constraints while optimizing task gain, energy saving, and load balance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Heterogeneous AEOS scheduling is challenging because satellites differ in orbital access, maneuvering capability, and payload resources, making the same task have different feasible windows, transition costs, and resource-consumption patterns across platforms, which complicates unified modeling and efficient optimization.

Method: The framework combines an assignment-based indirect encoding with decoder-based equivalent-cost evaluation in the modeling layer, and decouples schedule decoding, population-based search, and online actor-critic operator control in the optimization layer. Reinforcement learning is used to select high-level search operators rather than constructing schedules directly, coordinating global exploration, feasibility recovery, and local refinement within a limited function-evaluation budget.

Result: Experiments on different heterogeneous AEOS scenarios show that RLOSMEA achieves higher overall weighted utility and more stable convergence than representative metaheuristic baselines. Sensitivity and learning-behavior analyses confirm the method's robustness and the effectiveness of RL-guided operator selection.

Conclusion: The proposed RLOSMEA effectively addresses heterogeneous AEOS scheduling by integrating evolutionary optimization with RL-guided operator selection, achieving superior performance in weighted utility and convergence stability while maintaining robustness across diverse scenarios.

Abstract: Heterogeneous agile Earth observation satellite (AEOS) scheduling requires task selection, satellite assignment, and observation sequencing under satellite-dependent visibility windows, attitude maneuvering requirements, energy consumption, and onboard storage constraints. Since satellites differ in orbital access, maneuvering capability, and payload resources, the same task may have different feasible windows, transition costs, and resource-consumption patterns on different platforms, which increases the difficulty of unified modeling and efficient optimization. To address this problem, this paper proposes an evolutionary policy optimization framework for heterogeneous AEOS scheduling with preference-adjustable weighted objectives. In the modeling layer, assignment-based indirect encoding is combined with decoder-based equivalent-cost evaluation to retain satellite-dependent constraints while integrating task gain, energy saving, and load balance into an interpretable scalar utility. In the optimization layer, schedule decoding, population-based search, and online actor-critic operator control are decoupled, so that reinforcement learning selects high-level search operators rather than constructing schedules directly. Based on this framework, a reinforcement-learning-assisted operator-selection memetic evolutionary algorithm (RLOSMEA) is developed to coordinate global exploration, feasibility recovery, and local refinement under a limited function-evaluation budget. Experiments on different heterogeneous AEOS scenarios show that RLOSMEA achieves higher overall weighted utility and more stable convergence than representative metaheuristic baselines. Sensitivity and learning-behavior analyses further confirm the robustness of the proposed method and the effectiveness of reinforcement-learning-guided operator selection.

</details>


### [91] [Implicit Q-learning-bootstrapped ant colony optimization for maritime moving-target observation scheduling with agile satellites](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24471)
*He Wang,Junyu Wu,Yeye Liu,Yifan Zhou,Jie Zhang,Hui Li,Yanjie Song,Liang Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes IQACO, an implicit Q-learning-bootstrapped ant colony optimization method for multi-satellite maritime moving-target observation scheduling. It embeds an offline Q-learning module that adaptively adjusts ACO parameters (pheromone factor, heuristic factor, evaporation rate) based on a compact search-state representation, achieving the highest mean observation benefit across 14 test scenarios with 3.40%–9.40% improvement over conventional ACO.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Maritime moving-target observation scheduling with agile Earth observation satellites is a dynamic, sequence-dependent combinatorial optimization problem. Sea-surface targets move continuously, causing feasible observation windows to vary with target motion and satellite orbital geometry, requiring joint optimization of task selection, satellite assignment, window selection, and ordering under multiple constraints.

Method: IQACO embeds an offline implicit Q-learning module into constructive ant colony optimization. Rather than learning a direct task-selection policy, it learns to adaptively adjust the pheromone factor, heuristic factor, and evaporation rate. A compact search-state representation captures pheromone distribution, current and historical-best solution quality, and iteration progress. During online scheduling, ACO constructs feasible observation sequences while the learned policy regulates exploration vs. exploitation based on the current search state.

Result: Experiments on 14 scenarios with different scales and satellite configurations show IQACO obtains the highest mean observation benefit in every scenario, improves conventional ACO results by 3.40%–9.40%, accelerates convergence, and remains stable under different objective-weight settings.

Conclusion: Offline value learning provides an effective adaptive search-control mechanism for constrained maritime moving-target observation scheduling.

Abstract: Maritime moving-target observation scheduling with agile Earth observation satellites is a dynamic, sequence-dependent combinatorial optimization problem. Sea-surface targets move continuously, causing feasible observation windows to vary with target motion and satellite orbital geometry. The scheduler must jointly determine task selection, satellite assignment, observation-window selection, and observation ordering under time-window, attitude-maneuvering, onboard-resource, and cloud-affected availability constraints. This paper proposes an implicit Q-learning-bootstrapped ant colony optimization method, termed IQACO, for multi-satellite maritime moving-target observation scheduling. Rather than directly learning a task-selection policy, IQACO embeds an offline implicit Q-learning module into constructive ant colony optimization to adaptively adjust the pheromone factor, heuristic factor, and evaporation rate. A compact search-state representation captures pheromone distribution, current and historical-best solution quality, and iteration progress. During online scheduling, ant colony optimization constructs feasible observation sequences, while the learned policy regulates exploration and exploitation according to the current search state. Experiments on 14 scenarios with different scales and satellite configurations show that IQACO obtains the highest mean observation benefit in every scenario, improves the result of conventional ant colony optimization by 3.40\%--9.40\%, accelerates convergence, and remains stable under different objective-weight settings. These results demonstrate that offline value learning provides an effective adaptive search-control mechanism for constrained maritime moving-target observation scheduling.

</details>


### [92] [PeakBench: Benchmarking Resource-Aware Tool Invocation in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24509)
*Zhi-Kai Chen,Xu-Xiang Zhong,Song-Yan Li,De-Chuan Zhan,Han-Jia Ye*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PeakBench is a benchmark for evaluating LLM agents on multi-tool workflows with parallel execution, featuring executable workflows with dependency annotations and resource profiles. It uses a two-part evaluation framework that separates logical planning (dependency correctness) from physical scheduling (resource-constrained execution), with dedicated metrics for each dimension. Results show strong logical planning doesn't guarantee safe/efficient execution under resource constraints, and exposing resource information helps reduce overflows and improve utilization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agent benchmarks focus on tool selection, argument generation, and serial execution, neglecting parallelization and resource-constrained scheduling. This creates a gap where serial execution is safe but slow, while resource-agnostic parallel execution is fast but causes avoidable resource overflows. The missing scheduling dimension needs to be addressed.

Method: PeakBench introduces a benchmark of executable multi-tool workflows with execution-grounded dependency annotations and measured resource profiles. It proposes a two-part evaluation framework that disentangles logical planning (dependency correctness) from physical scheduling (resource-constrained execution), with dedicated metrics for each dimension.

Result: The paper shows that strong logical planning does not reliably translate into safe or efficient execution under resource constraints. Additionally, exposing resource information to agents can reduce avoidable overflows and improve resource utilization, demonstrating PeakBench's utility as a diagnostic testbed for resource-aware agent behavior.

Conclusion: PeakBench fills a critical gap in agent evaluation by adding a resource-constrained scheduling dimension alongside logical planning. It provides a framework for diagnosing whether failures stem from dependency planning or scheduling issues, and demonstrates that resource awareness is crucial for safe and efficient parallel tool execution in LLM agents.

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly solve tasks by invoking multiple tools, where parallel execution is essential for low latency but difficult to manage safely. Existing agent benchmarks primarily evaluate tool selection, argument generation, and end-to-end success under mostly serial execution, largely overlooking valid parallelization and resource-constrained scheduling. This missing scheduling dimension creates a practical failure mode: serial execution is safe but slow, while resource-agnostic parallel execution is fast but prone to avoidable resource overflows. To address this gap, we introduce PeakBench, a benchmark of executable multi-tool workflows with execution-grounded dependency annotations and measured resource profiles. A central challenge in evaluating such workflows is attribution: failures and inefficiencies may arise from incorrect dependency planning, poor resource-constrained scheduling, or both. PeakBench addresses this challenge with a two-part evaluation framework that disentangles logical planning from physical scheduling, with dedicated metrics for each dimension. Using this framework, we show that strong logical planning does not reliably translate into safe or efficient execution under resource constraints. We further show that exposing resource information can reduce avoidable overflows and improve resource utilization, making PeakBench a useful testbed for diagnosing resource-aware agent behavior. Code is available at https://github.com/Czzzk/Staggering-the-Peaks.

</details>


### [93] [Neurosymbolic Alignment for Physiologically-Safe Clinical Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24534)
*Abdulhady Abas Abdullah,Erik Cambria,Milena Zivkovic*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Neurosymbolic Alignment, a training-time framework that grounds preference optimization of a 7B clinical LLM in structured physiological knowledge via an HGNN-based Physiological World Model over an 847K-node biomedical knowledge graph, achieving substantial safety improvements on the Clinical Safety Benchmark (CSS: 69.5%→90.8%) over text-only ORPO baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical LLMs can produce recommendations that are factually plausible but physiologically unsafe. Safety alignment based solely on text supervision fails to capture underlying physiological constraints, motivating the need for grounding in structured physiological knowledge rather than text-only signals.

Method: A training-time framework coupling a 7B clinical LLM with an HGNN-based Physiological World Model built on an 847K-node biomedical knowledge graph. Candidate responses are scored via homeostatic constraints, multi-hop path plausibility, and drug-interaction penalties. These scores generate rankings that drive iterative on-policy ORPO updates. Evaluation uses the Clinical Safety Benchmark (CSB), a 2,500-scenario benchmark.

Result: CSS improves from 69.5% to 90.8% (+21.3 pp) over ORPO; physician-evaluated harm rate drops from 14.1% to 5.1%; DID improves from 72.8% to 91.6%. The method surpasses GPT-4 (5-shot) on all safety metrics despite a 10x parameter disadvantage, outperforms SFT+SelfCorrect by 11.4 pp CSS, retains 84.2% CSS under synthetic EHR noise, and shows strong calibration (ECE=0.038, kappa=0.91). Ablations identify HGNN scoring and iterative training as key contributors.

Conclusion: Training-time physiological grounding yields measurable, independently verifiable safety improvements in open-weight clinical LLMs under controlled evaluation. External validation on real clinical data is required to confirm transfer to deployment settings.

Abstract: Clinical LLMs can generate recommendations that are factually plausible yet physiologically unsafe. We investigate whether safety alignment can be improved by grounding preference optimization in structured physiological knowledge rather than text-only supervision. Methods: We propose Neurosymbolic Alignment, a training-time framework that couples a 7B clinical LLM with an HGNN-based Physiological World Model over an 847K-node biomedical knowledge graph. Candidate responses are scored using homeostatic constraints, multi-hop path plausibility, and drug-interaction penalties, and the resulting rankings drive iterative on-policy ORPO updates. Evaluation is performed on the Clinical Safety Benchmark (CSB), a 2,500-scenario benchmark for physiological constraint violations in generative clinical reasoning. Results: Relative to ORPO, the proposed method improves CSS from 69.5% to 90.8% (+21.3 pp), reduces physician-evaluated HR from 14.1% to 5.1% on the blinded subset, and improves DID from 72.8% to 91.6%. These gains are corroborated by an HGNN-independent Rule-Engine Safety Score (RSS: 86.4%, +21.2 pp over ORPO; r=0.97 concordance with CSS). The method also exceeds GPT-4 (5-shot) on all safety metrics despite a 10x parameter disadvantage, and outperforms an inference-time self-correction pipeline (SFT+SelfCorrect) by 11.4 pp CSS. Under synthetic EHR-style noise, 84.2% CSS is retained. Ablation analysis shows that HGNN scoring (-16.2 pp) and iterative training (-11.5 pp) are the dominant contributors. PhysioScore calibration against 200 clinician labels yielded ECE = 0.038 and kappa = 0.91. Conclusion: Training-time physiological grounding produces measurable and independently verifiable safety improvements in open-weight clinical LLMs under controlled evaluation. External validation on real clinical data is required to determine whether these gains transfer to deployment settings

</details>


### [94] [Discovering Adaptive Transmission Programs for Collective Innovation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24545)
*Cédric Colas,Jérémy Perez,Eleni Nisioti,Akhilesh Mocherla,Pierre-Yves Oudeyer,Clément Moulin-Frier,Maxime Derex*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes state-aware transmission protocols (programs that route information based on agent and collective states) for collective intelligence, discovered via LLM-guided evolutionary search. Evolved protocols outperform standard baselines by up to 37% in a collective discovery task, with ablations confirming state-awareness is the key driver, and showing transferability across domains and populations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on transmission processes in collective intelligence has focused primarily on network structure (who shares with whom and when). However, networks are state-agnostic and cannot condition transmission on what agents know or the collective's state. This paper aims to formalize and explore state-aware transmission protocols that can adaptively route information based on agent and collective knowledge states.

Method: The authors formalize transmission protocols as state-aware programs that condition routing on agent and collective states. They use LLM-guided evolutionary search to discover effective protocols in a collective discovery task. They compare against standard baselines from the literature and run ablation studies (removing content-dependence while preserving network topology and timing) to isolate the role of state-awareness. They also test transferability across domain variations and agent populations.

Result: Evolved state-aware protocols increase collective performance over standard baselines by up to 37%. Ablations show that removing content-dependence eliminates performance gains, confirming state-awareness as the driving factor. The evolved protocols also transfer across domain variations and different agent populations, demonstrating generality.

Conclusion: Effective and generalizable transmission protocols can be discovered in silico via LLM-guided evolutionary search, suggesting a path toward AI-assisted design of coordination infrastructure that can enhance human collective intelligence beyond what network-structure-only approaches achieve.

Abstract: Human collective intelligence depends on transmission processes: who shares what with whom, how, and when. While these processes emerge from individual cognition, they can also be directed by deliberate top-down protocols. Prior work has studied how transmission shapes collective outcomes primarily through the lens of network structure, varying who shares with whom and when. But networks are state-agnostic: they cannot condition transmission on what agents know or on the state of the collective. Here, we formalize transmission protocols as state-aware programs that route information and resources based on agent and collective states, and we use LLM-guided evolutionary search to design effective protocols in a collective discovery task. Evolved protocols increase collective performance over standard baselines from the literature by up to 37%. Ablations confirm that state-awareness drives this advantage: removing content-dependence while preserving network topology and timing eliminates performance gains. We find that evolved protocols also transfer across domain variations and agent populations. These results demonstrate that effective and generalizable transmission protocols can be discovered in silico, suggesting a path toward AI-assisted design of coordination infrastructure that enhances human collective intelligence.

</details>


### [95] [When "Must" Becomes "Maybe": Constraint Weakening in LLM Agent Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24569)
*Yiheng Sun,Huifei Wang,Yancheng Zhu,Zhenyu Li,Zebin Zhao,Yifan Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that LLM agent workflows suffer from "operational state preservation" failures: handoff transformations (compression, plan assimilation, convergence, ownership deferral, precedent substitution) can retain the topical content of safety-critical state while deactivating its action-constraining force. In 1,296 synthetic episodes, compression causes 100% blocker deactivation and 54.2% forbidden actions, though restoring all four state fields (prerequisite, authority, fallback, execution consequence) achieves 100% preservation and 0% forbidden actions. Notably, downstream verification eliminates forbidden actions while artifact deactivation remains at 95.3%, showing that semantic availability does not guarantee operational preservation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents coordinate via intermediate language artifacts, and while prior work has focused on topical information retention across transformations, the binding force of state—whether a condition still constrains downstream action or merely informs it—has not been systematically studied. Safety blockers provide a controlled setting with explicit prerequisites, authority, fallback, and execution consequences, enabling a rigorous investigation of this state-transmission failure.

Method: The authors condition on correct upstream identification of safety blockers, systematically vary handoff transformation types (direct handoff, compression, plan assimilation, convergence, ownership deferral, precedent substitution), and evaluate an executor restricted to only the resulting artifact. They measure two outcomes: artifact deactivation (the blocker losing its binding status) and forbidden action (the executor performing a prohibited action). A controlled synthetic episode framework with 1,296 episodes is used, with interventions that restore all four state fields or add downstream verification as fixed-artifact interventions.

Result: Direct-handoff controls preserve every blocker. Normal handoff compression produces 100.0% deactivation and 54.2% forbidden action. Restoring all four state fields raises preservation to 100.0% and reduces forbidden action to 0.0%. Fixed-artifact downstream verification eliminates forbidden action while artifact deactivation remains at 95.3%, demonstrating that containment (preventing forbidden actions) and preservation (maintaining binding state) are separable.

Conclusion: Handoff transformations can retain state content while weakening its constraints on downstream action. Semantic availability does not guarantee operational preservation—a critical failure mode for LLM agents coordinating safety-relevant workflows where intermediate artifacts must bind, not merely inform, subsequent behavior. The results motivate explicit mechanisms to preserve action-binding state fields through handoffs.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents coordinate complex tasks through multi-role and multi-stage workflows. Upstream state is repeatedly transformed into intermediate language artifacts, such as summaries, plans, tickets, memories, and handoff notes, from which downstream components act. For action-constraining state, topical retention is insufficient: an artifact may mention an unresolved condition while changing it from a requirement that must be resolved before execution into information that may merely inform the next action. We study this action-binding role as operational state preservation. Safety blockers provide a controlled instance because each source state has an explicit prerequisite, authority, fallback, and execution consequence. We condition on correct upstream identification, vary the handoff transformation, and evaluate an executor restricted to the resulting artifact. Across 1,296 controlled synthetic episodes, direct-handoff controls preserve every blocker, whereas compression, plan assimilation, convergence, ownership deferral, and precedent substitution repeatedly turn binding state into caveats or non-binding considerations. Normal handoff compression produces 100.0% deactivation and 54.2% forbidden action. Restoring all four state fields raises preservation to 100.0% and reduces forbidden action to 0.0%. Fixed-artifact interventions further separate preservation from containment: downstream verification eliminates forbidden action while artifact deactivation remains 95.3%. These results identify a state-transmission failure between information extraction and action. Handoff transformations can retain state content while weakening its constraints on downstream action. Semantic availability does not guarantee operational preservation.

</details>


### [96] [EviDx: Evidence-Aware Active Diagnosis with Scaffolded LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24570)
*Lihang Zeng,Shaoting Zhang,Xiaofan Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EviDx is an evidence-aware active diagnosis framework for LLM-based clinical diagnosis systems, featuring patient-specific interactive environments, a clinical diagnostic scaffold with role-specialized agents and evidence tools, and an observer-guided runtime harness that regulates diagnostic termination via uncertainty and evidence coverage tracking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based medical diagnosis systems treat diagnosis as static case-to-answer prediction with limited evidence acquisition support, whereas real clinical diagnosis is an active, iterative evidence-seeking process. While agentic LLMs offer dynamic alternatives through tool use, existing systems under-specify how patient evidence is exposed, scaffolded, and controlled at runtime.

Method: EviDx consists of three components: (1) E-Synthesis constructs interactive environments from raw clinical cases; (2) a clinical diagnostic scaffold organizes role-specialized agents, evidence tools, and evolving evidence states; (3) an observer-guided runtime harness regulates diagnostic termination by tracking uncertainty and evidence coverage. Evaluation uses a 3-level pyramid assessing execution robustness, reasoning dynamics, and diagnostic outcomes.

Result: Experiments show EviDx improves diagnostic performance and process stability while revealing model-dependent capability boundaries across different LLMs.

Conclusion: EviDx demonstrates that shifting from static case-to-answer prediction to structured, evidence-aware active diagnosis improves both diagnostic outcomes and process reliability in LLM-based clinical systems, though capability boundaries vary by underlying model.

Abstract: Clinical diagnosis is an active evidence-seeking process in which clinicians acquire evidence, update competing hypotheses, and decide when the available evidence is sufficient for diagnosis. Yet many medical diagnosis systems built around large language models (LLMs) still formulate diagnosis as static case-to-answer prediction, with limited support for evidence acquisition. Agentic LLMs offer a dynamic alternative through tool use and intermediate diagnostic trajectories, but existing systems often under-specify how patient evidence should be exposed, scaffolded, and controlled at runtime. We introduce EviDx, an evidence-aware active diagnosis framework that pairs patient-specific diagnostic environments with a clinical diagnostic scaffold and an observer-guided runtime harness. In EviDx, $\mathcal{E}$-Synthesis constructs interactive environments from raw clinical cases; the scaffold organizes role-specialized agents, evidence tools, and evolving evidence states; and the harness regulates diagnostic termination by tracking uncertainty and evidence coverage. A 3-level evaluation pyramid assesses execution robustness, reasoning dynamics, and diagnostic outcomes. Experiments show that EviDx improves diagnostic performance and process stability while revealing model-dependent capability boundaries.

</details>


### [97] [Joint Optimization of Tool Creation and Use for Large Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24571)
*Zhi Rui Tam,Chieh-Yen Lin,Yun-Nung Chen,Shao-Hua Sun,Hung-yi Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SMITH is a reinforcement learning framework that jointly trains tool creation and tool use within a single policy, achieving 79.8% macro-average accuracy on held-out procedural reasoning tasks with a 4B model, outperforming stronger baselines and generalizing to out-of-domain tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing tool-augmented language models are limited by pre-written APIs, and current tool-creation systems decouple the tool-writer from the tool-user with no feedback signal connecting schema generation to actual invocability.

Method: SMITH uses reinforcement learning to jointly train tool creation and tool use in a single policy. Each rollout is either a build task (write a tool from examples) or a use task (invoke a pooled tool on a question). Three separate reward axes independently track schema, code, and outcome failures, each contributing its own gradient.

Result: A 4B Qwen3 trained with SMITH on 13 procedural reasoning tasks reaches 79.8% macro-average accuracy on held-out tasks (best across all evaluated methods), 40.4 on TabMWP-Hard, and 42.6 on out-of-domain GQA (+7.6 over best same-backbone baseline), without visual or tabular training data. Tools written by the 4B model also improve performance of LFM-2.5-350M and Qwen3-30B-A3B.

Conclusion: Jointly training tool creation and tool use via RL with multi-axis rewards yields a stronger, more generalizable tool-augmented reasoning system, and the tools produced are transferable to other models.

Abstract: Tool-augmented language models are bounded by the APIs humans bothered to write; existing tool-creation systems patch this by prompting a frozen LLM at inference time, leaving the model that writes a tool decoupled from the one that uses it, with no signal that the schemas it produces are schemas it can invoke. We propose SMITH (Schema-grounded Multi-task Iterative Tool Honing), a reinforcement learning framework that jointly trains tool creation and tool use inside a single policy. Each rollout is either a build task (write a tool from a few examples) or a use task (invoke a pooled tool on a held-out question). Three separate reward axes catch schema, code, and outcome failures independently, so each failure mode contributes its own gradient. A 4B Qwen3 trained with SMITH on 13 procedural reasoning tasks with exact verifiers reaches 79.8 macro-average accuracy on held-out tasks, the best across all evaluated methods and ahead of an untrained 30B-A3B tool-writer. It also reaches 40.4 on TabMWP-Hard and 42.6 on out-of-domain GQA (+7.6 over the best same-backbone inference-time baseline), without any visual or tabular training data. Tools written by our 4B models also lifted the performance of LFM-2.5-350M and Qwen3-30B-A3B under same reasoning tasks.

</details>


### [98] [PhysMLLMs: Spatial Priors for Unified Referring Segmentation and Grounded Reasoning of Images and Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24574)
*Siyao Yan,Bo Han,Jisheng Dang,Bimei Wang,Shude Wang,Hong Peng,Yulan Guo,Jianhuang Lai,Bin Hu,Tat-SengChua*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PhysMLLMs is a training-stage prior injection architecture that adds physics-inspired spatial continuity priors to Video MLLMs via Global Representation Prior Alignment (REPA-Global), which distills global visual representations from a frozen DINOv2 teacher using an offline embedding cache. It improves video segmentation mask quality and cross-frame consistency, especially on challenging cases (small targets, fast motion, occlusion, distractors), without adding inference cost or compromising image-level grounding and general multimodal capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video MLLMs for language-guided video segmentation exhibit spatio-temporal inconsistencies (jitter, drift, identity switches), particularly when targets are partially hidden or similar objects appear nearby. The likely cause is that current training lacks explicit spatial priors, making it difficult to maintain stable spatial identity and shape over time.

Method: PhysMLLMs injects physics-inspired spatial continuity priors at the training stage by aligning a student model's global visual representation with a frozen DINOv2 teacher model. The core mechanism, REPA-Global, uses an offline embedding cache and a scheduled distillation plan to distill global visual representations during training, leaving inference unchanged.

Result: Across multiple video benchmarks, PhysMLLMs improves video segmentation mask quality and cross-frame consistency, with larger gains on challenging cases (small targets, fast motion, occlusion, distractors, reasoning queries). It maintains comparable performance on single-frame referring image segmentation and general VLM benchmarks.

Conclusion: Physics-inspired spatial prior injection can improve temporal stability in video segmentation while preserving image-level grounding and general multimodal capability, demonstrating that explicit spatial priors during training help overcome spatio-temporal inconsistencies in Video MLLMs.

Abstract: Video multimodal large language models support language guided video segmentation, but they often show spatio temporal inconsistencies, e.g., jitter, drift, and identity switches. These failures are more common when targets are partly hidden or when similar objects appear nearby.One likely reason is that current training lacks explicit spatial priors, which makes it difficult to maintain stable spatial identity and shape over time. We present PhysMLLMs, a training-stage prior injection architecture that injects physics-inspired spatial continuity priors into Video MLLMs. PhysMLLMs is designed to encourage more stable object-centered representations by aligning the student global visual representation with a frozen teacher model during training. Our core mechanism, Global Representation Prior Alignment (REPA-Global), distills global visual representations from a frozen DINOv2 teacher using an offline embedding cache and a scheduled distillation plan. This design keeps inference unchanged and does not add inference time cost. Across multiple video benchmarks, PhysMLLMs improves video segmentation mask quality and cross-frame consistency, with larger gains on challenging cases involving small targets, fast motion, occlusion, distractors, and reasoning queries. On single-frame referring image segmentation and representative general VLM benchmarks, PhysMLLMs maintains comparable performance, demonstrating that the injected spatial prior improves video consistency without compromising image-level grounding or general multimodal capability. These results suggest that physics-inspired spatial prior injection can improve temporal stability while preserving general capability. The code is available at https://github.com/tusu-code/20260121-icml2026-2.git.

</details>


### [99] [Pivot-and-Station Multi-Agent Path Finding: Solvability, Complexity, and Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24585)
*Andrea Di Nezza,Mihir Patel,Fabio Fagnani,Sara Bernardini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces PS-MAPF, a new Multi-Agent Path Finding variant where tasked agents must visit interchangeable pivots before parking at anonymous stations. It provides complete solvability characterization, proves NP-hardness of optimization objectives with a single pivot, and presents three algorithms including a SAT-based optimal solver and PPP which solves 74-89% of benchmarks with dramatically better performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automated high-density storage systems (warehouses, robotic parking, plant logistics) require agents to traverse task-critical resources and park without obstructing operations, motivating a new MAPF variant that captures pivot visitation constraints combined with anonymous station termination.

Method: The paper formally defines PS-MAPF, provides a complete solvability characterization on 2-edge-connected graphs and arbitrary connected graphs using an effective-distance measure, proves NP-hardness results, and develops three algorithms: a complete baseline, a SAT-based optimal solver, and Pivot-Prioritized Planning (PPP).

Result: Every instance on 2-edge-connected graphs is solvable; on arbitrary connected graphs, a structural effective-distance measure relative to unoccupied vertices is necessary and sufficient for solvability. Minimizing station-makespan or station-flowtime is NP-hard even with a single pivot. PPP solves 74-89% of benchmark instances with makespan and flowtime orders of magnitude better than the baseline.

Conclusion: PS-MAPF is a tractable and practically solvable problem class with complete theoretical characterization, though optimization is NP-hard; the PPP algorithm offers scalable near-optimal solutions for real-world automated storage systems.

Abstract: Automated high-density storage systems (warehouses, robotic parking, plant logistics, etc.) require fleets of agents to move through scarce task-critical resources and then park without obstructing future operations. We introduce Pivot-and-Station Multi-Agent Path Finding (PS-MAPF), a MAPF variant in which a subset of tasked agents must each visit one of a set of interchangeable pivots (e.g., workstations) before the entire fleet terminates at anonymous stations, one agent per station. We characterize solvability completely: every instance on a 2-edge-connected graph is solvable, and, on arbitrary connected graphs, a structural effective-distance measure relative to the number of unoccupied vertices gives a necessary and sufficient condition. We prove that minimizing station-makespan or station-flowtime is NP-hard already with a single pivot. We present three algorithms, a complete baseline, a SAT-based optimal solver, and Pivot-Prioritized Planning (PPP), the last solving 74-89% of benchmark instances with makespan and flowtime orders of magnitude below the baseline.

</details>


### [100] [Causal Modelling of Support Interventions for Student Competency Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24632)
*Francesca Mangili,Alessandro Antonucci,Rafael Cabañas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper advocates for adopting structural causal modelling (SCM) in educational assessment, moving beyond probabilistic psychometric models like item response theory toward a framework that supports interventional and counterfactual reasoning. The authors propose a protocol for constructing such models, where structural equations are elicited from experts using purely logical information, and illustrate the approach with data from an assessment measuring algorithmic skills in compulsory school students.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard psychometric approaches (e.g., item response theory) only support probabilistic belief updating and associative reasoning, which are insufficient for explicitly modelling interventions (such as hints) and counterfactual scenarios in educational assessment. A causal framework would better support educators in designing targeted interventions and evaluating educational strategies.

Method: The paper proposes a protocol for constructing structural causal models for educational assessment, where structural equations are elicited from domain experts. The required information is purely logical in nature (not probabilistic), avoiding less tenable probabilistic assumptions. The approach is demonstrated using data from complex assessment tasks measuring algorithmic skills in compulsory school students.

Result: The paper demonstrates the practical relevance of interventional and counterfactual reasoning in educational assessment, showing how these forms of reasoning enable scenario analysis (e.g., evaluating the effect of hints) that remain inaccessible to standard associative psychometric models. The feasibility of the protocol is illustrated with a concrete assessment example.

Conclusion: Adopting structural causal modelling for educational assessment provides a principled framework that extends beyond probabilistic updating, enabling educators to reason about interventions and counterfactuals. Although this requires expert-elicited structural equations, the logical (non-probabilistic) nature of the required information makes the approach viable and tenable.

Abstract: Accurate assessment of student competencies is essential for enabling educators to identify individual needs, design targeted interventions, and evaluate the effectiveness of educational strategies. Empirical assessment procedures are typically grounded in psychometric models, such as item response theory, which relate student competence levels to performance on assessment tasks. In this paper, we advocate adopting a structural causal modelling approach to educational assessment, moving beyond probabilistic belief updating toward a framework that explicitly supports interventional and counterfactual reasoning. We propose a corresponding protocol for its construction and analyse the practical relevance of forms of reasoning that remain inaccessible to standard associative models, including the explicit modelling of interventions such as hints and the related counterfactual scenario analysis. Although our protocol requires the structural equations to be elicited from experts, the necessary information is purely logical and does not rely on probabilistic, less tenable assumptions. We illustrate the approach using data from an assessment that employs complex tasks designed to measure compulsory school student algorithmic skills.

</details>


### [101] [Parason: Revealing Subtask and Trial Parallelism in LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24658)
*Zhengyang Zhang,Zijian Zhang,Jiaxuan Gao,Shusheng Xu,Yi Wu,Song Han,Ligeng Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Parason is a framework that identifies and learns two forms of parallel reasoning in LLMs—Subtask Parallelism and Trial Parallelism—converting sequential reasoning traces into structured parallel trajectories, training with a parallelism-aware reinforcement learning objective (PA-GRPO), and executing via tool calls to achieve ~1.7x wall-clock acceleration on math benchmarks while maintaining accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long autoregressive reasoning traces in LLMs create severe latency (up to days/weeks) for difficult tasks. Prior parallel reasoning systems focus only on Subtask Parallelism (task decomposition), overlooking Trial Parallelism (speculative attempts with verification and aggregation), which the authors show constitutes the majority (65.5%) of parallelizable computation, especially on hard problems.

Method: Parason (1) analyzes reasoning traces to reveal Trial Parallelism as the dominant form of parallelizable reasoning; (2) converts sequential reasoning traces into structured parallel trajectories using a context-free grammar; (3) trains models with Parallelism-Aware Group Relative Policy Optimization (PA-GRPO), whose reward jointly balances accuracy, latency, and both parallelism ratios; (4) executes learned parallel structures at inference via tool calls for real wall-clock speedup.

Result: On mathematical reasoning benchmarks including AIME24 and AIME25, Parason achieves an average acceleration of approximately 1.7x while maintaining competitive accuracy.

Conclusion: Trial Parallelism is a largely overlooked but dominant form of parallelizable reasoning computation, and explicitly learning both Subtask and Trial parallelism through structured trajectory transformation and parallelism-aware RL can translate theoretical computational savings into real wall-clock latency reduction without sacrificing accuracy.

Abstract: Scaling test-time reasoning has substantially improved the problem-solving ability of large language models (LLMs), but standard autoregressive decoding still executes long reasoning traces sequentially, creating severe latency for difficult tasks (up to days and weeks). Parallel reasoning offers a natural remedy. However, prior systems primarily focus on Subtask Parallelism, where the model learns to decompose a high-level task into smaller chunks that can be solved independently. This approach overlooks another pervasive form of parallelism: Trial Parallelism, where multiple speculative attempts explore, verify, and aggregate competing hypotheses in parallel. In this paper, we introduce Parason, which reveals and learns both forms of parallelism in LLM reasoning. Our analysis identifies Trial Parallelism as the majority of parallelizable reasoning computation (65.5% in DeepSeek-V4's reasoning steps in HLE), and it becomes increasingly dominant on hard problems. Guided by this taxonomy, Parason converts sequential reasoning traces into structured parallel trajectories with a context-free grammar, then trains models with Parallelism-Aware Group Relative Policy Optimization (PA-GRPO), whose reward jointly balances accuracy, latency, and the two parallelism ratios. At inference time, Parason executes the learned parallel structure through tool calls, translating theoretical savings to real-world wall-clock acceleration. Experiments on mathematical reasoning benchmarks including AIME24 and AIME25 show that Parason achieves an average acceleration about 1.7$\times$ while maintaining competitive accuracy.

</details>


### [102] [The Invisible Editorial Layer: Formalizing Undisclosed Inference-Time Steering, Probability Placement, and the Attribution Problem in Deployed Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24662)
*Augusto Camargo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper argues that LLM evaluation is incomplete because it ignores inference-time interventions on the probability distribution between model weights and token sampling. It introduces "inference-time framing bias," formalizes the distinction "Model != Deployed System," and proposes three concepts: the Inference Attribution Problem, Probability Placement (a commercial advertising primitive via probability shifts), and Inference Policy Transparency. The paper connects these concepts to existing governance frameworks including the EU AI Act, Digital Services Act, and FTC doctrines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM evaluation assumes observable behavior is primarily driven by weights, data, alignment, and prompts—overlooking that modern inference pipelines can systematically modify probability distributions before token selection. While technical work on controlled generation and watermarking matures, the governance, security, and economic implications of undisclosed inference policies remain underexplored.

Method: The paper formalizes the operational distinction between model weights and deployed systems, and introduces three conceptual frameworks: (1) Inference Attribution Problem—why behavioral bias cannot be causally attributed to weights under limited observability; (2) Probability Placement—an advertising primitive where commercial influence is achieved via systematic probability shifts rather than explicit product mentions; (3) Inference Policy Transparency—a governance principle for auditable deployment-layer interventions. It then maps these concepts onto existing legal frameworks (EU AI Act Article 5, DSA, FTC doctrines).

Result: The paper establishes a formal conceptual foundation for understanding inference-time framing bias and highlights the gap between technical maturity of decoding/logit-level interventions and the lack of governance attention to their undisclosed deployment. It demonstrates the relevance of existing legal frameworks to this emerging concern.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that inference-time interventions constitute a significant, under-examined layer of control over LLM output, and argues for Inference Policy Transparency as a necessary governance principle to ensure that deployed LLM systems' behavior is auditable and attributable.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are commonly evaluated under the assumption that their observable behavior is primarily determined by model weights, training data, alignment procedures, and user prompts. This view is incomplete. Modern inference pipelines may systematically modify the probability distribution produced by a model immediately before token selection, creating an additional layer of control between frozen weights and observed text.
  While controlled generation (e.g., PPLM, GeDi, DExperts, FUDGE) and text-watermarking systems (e.g., SynthID-Text) demonstrate the technical maturity of decoding- and logit-level interventions, the governance, security, and economic implications of an undisclosed inference policy remain comparatively underexplored. This paper examines the emergence of inference-time framing bias: the systematic modification of generated language toward political, ideological, institutional, or commercial frames via interventions applied after model inference but before token sampling.
  We formalize the operational reality Model != Deployed System and introduce three concepts: (1) the Inference Attribution Problem, characterizing why observed behavioral bias cannot generally be causally attributed to model weights alone under limited observability; (2) Probability Placement, defining a hypothetical advertising primitive in which commercial influence is implemented through systematic shifts in generation probabilities rather than explicit product insertions; and (3) Inference Policy Transparency, a governance principle for making deployment-layer interventions auditable. We examine these concepts in relation to Article 5 of the EU AI Act, the EU Digital Services Act, and FTC doctrines.

</details>


### [103] [Confident at the moment of action: belief miscalibration in LLM play under hidden information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24691)
*Bhushan Kashinath Joshi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: In a hidden-information chess variant where agents state confidence about a secretly relocated royal piece, high-confidence captures ($\geq 0.5$) were correct in only 1 of 62 cases, with 99.3% and 98.7% of calibration deficit concentrated in these events across two batches. Conventional evaluation axes (legality, cost, latency, completion rate) dissociate entirely from belief quality, and outcome-based evaluation fails to detect this misalignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic systems increasingly gate actions on a model's own stated confidence, under the assumption that confidence tracks correctness at the moment of acting. This paper tests whether that assumption holds in contexts involving hidden information and sequential decision-making.

Method: The authors use a hidden-information chess variant where the royal piece's location is secretly and repeatedly relocated. They elicit the agent's stated probability distribution over the hidden piece's location every turn, separately from its chosen move, and score these distributions against ground truth recovered post-game. They run two independent batches plus four additional model configurations across two providers, and compare belief quality against conventional evaluation axes.

Result: High-confidence captures were correct in only 1 of 62 cases, with calibration deficit concentrated almost entirely in these events (99.3% and 98.7% across batches). The pattern orders consistently but with statistically weak pairwise gaps at this sample size. A deliberation-budget change alone moved the metric nearly as much as a large cross-model gap, and the configuration winning on every conventional axis produced the worst belief quality. A model can still win the game despite this misalignment.

Conclusion: Stated confidence can severely misrepresent correctness in hidden-information settings, and this misalignment is invisible to outcome-only or conventional evaluation metrics. Capability does not predict calibration, and evaluation must separately assess belief quality when models gate actions on self-reported confidence.

Abstract: Agentic systems increasingly gate actions on a model's own stated confidence, which assumes confidence tracks correctness at the moment of acting. We test this in a hidden-information chess variant where royal status can be secretly, repeatedly relocated between pieces, and where an agent's stated probability distribution over the opponent's hidden royal piece -- elicited every turn, separately from the move it chooses -- is scored against ground truth recoverable after the game. Across two independent batches, captures made at high stated confidence ($\geq 0.5$) about the hidden piece's location were correct in 1 of 62 cases. The calibration deficit is concentrated almost entirely in these events: 99.3% of it in the original batch, 98.7% in the replication. The same pattern, in weaker form, orders consistently (point estimates only; most pairwise gaps are not statistically distinguishable at this sample size) across four further model configurations spanning a second provider -- reported as scope for the finding, not as evidence that capability predicts calibration: a same-model comparison at a fixed external leaderboard score shows a deliberation-budget change alone moves the metric by nearly as much as a large cross-model gap. In a separate seat, conventional evaluation axes -- legality, cost, latency, completion rate -- can dissociate entirely from belief quality, with the configuration winning on every conventional axis producing the worst belief quality tested. A model exhibiting this pattern can still win the game its belief was about, which is why outcome-only evaluation would not detect it.

</details>


### [104] [Lifted Model Construction under Approximate Commutativity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24713)
*Malte Luttermann,Jan Speller,Tanya Braun,Marcel Gehrke,Ralf Möller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces the concept of ε-commutativity, a relaxation of commutativity for factors in probabilistic graphical models where output values are only approximately invariant under input permutations. The authors show how ε-commutativity enables lifted inference on approximately commutative factors, provide strict bounds on approximation error, and empirically demonstrate comparable query accuracy with lower runtime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lifted inference requires exactly commutative factors to construct lifted representations, but in practice parameters learned from data are only approximately commutative due to unavoidable noise. This gap between theoretical requirements and practical data prevents the application of lifted inference to real-world learned models.

Method: The paper formalizes ε-commutativity as a relaxation of standard commutativity, where factor outputs are approximately invariant under input permutations within a bounded error ε. It demonstrates how such approximately commutative factors can be exploited for lifted model construction and downstream probabilistic inference, and derives strict theoretical bounds on the induced approximation error.

Result: The theoretical guarantees are confirmed empirically, showing that the proposed ε-commutativity approach achieves comparable query accuracy to exact inference while achieving lower runtime, demonstrating the practical applicability of lifted inference on real-world learned models.

Conclusion: ε-commutativity bridges the gap between the theoretical requirements of lifted inference and the practical reality of learned parameters, enabling scalable probabilistic inference with bounded approximation error on approximately symmetric models.

Abstract: Lifted inference algorithms enable scalable probabilistic inference even for large object domains by leveraging the indistinguishability of objects in a probability distribution. An essential prerequisite for constructing a lifted representation is to identify commutative factors, i.e., functions whose output values are invariant under permutations of a subset of their input values, in a potential-based factorisation. In practice, however, parameters learned from data inevitably deviate even if associated objects are indistinguishable, causing their corresponding factors to be only approximately commutative instead of being exactly commutative. We address this problem by introducing the concept of ε-commutativity, a relaxation of commutativity where output values are only approximately invariant under permutations of input values. Specifically, we show how ε-commutativity can be exploited for lifted model construction, downstream probabilistic inference, and prove strict bounds on the induced approximation error, thereby ensuring the practical applicability of lifted model construction while maintaining highly accurate query results. These theoretical guarantees are confirmed empirically, demonstrating comparable query accuracy at lower runtime.

</details>


### [105] [Meta$^n$: Recursive Self-Improvement through Emergent Depth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24735)
*Zae Myung Kim,Young-Jun Lee,Seungyeon Jwa,Dongyeop Kang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Meta$^n$ is a self-improving LLM agent framework that fixes a single meta-operation Ω and recurses it on its own products (traces + code), reading the solver stack below and writing the next layer as a strategic pre-process and callable helper library. Depth is determined by convergence and searched over via an evolutionary archive. It outperforms prior self-improving agents across eight benchmark families on two backbones, uniquely scoring above zero on ARC-AGI-2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-improving LLM agents refine answers rather than the process that produces them. Systems that add a meta-level keep that level fixed, and self-editing systems must leave part of their machinery untouched for stability, capping meta-depth at roughly two. The goal is to achieve deeper, stable recursive self-improvement.

Method: Meta$^n$ keeps the meta-operation Ω fixed and recurses on its input. Ω is repeatedly applied to its own products: it reads the traces of the solver stack below along with the code that produced them, then writes the next layer as a strategic pre-process and a library of callable helpers. Depth is set by convergence rather than fixed in advance, and an evolutionary archive searches over layer chains.

Result: Across two backbones, Meta$^n$ outperforms prior self-improving agents on all eight benchmark families. It is the only method scoring above zero on ARC-AGI-2, a benchmark designed to resist skill memorization. Ablations show most gain from recursion comes from conditioning passed between layers, and distinct layer roles emerge with depth without any prompt prescribing them.

Conclusion: By fixing the meta-operation and recursing on its input, Meta$^n$ unlocks deeper and more stable self-improvement, as evidenced by broad benchmark superiority and emergence of distinct layer roles. Code is publicly released.

Abstract: Self-improving LLM agents refine answers, not the process that produces those answers. Systems that add a meta-level hold that level fixed, and those that edit themselves must leave part of their own editing machinery untouched to stay stable, capping the meta-depth they realize at roughly two. We present Meta$^n$, which keeps the meta-operation fixed and recurses on its input instead. That operation, $Ω$, is applied repeatedly to its own products, reading the traces of the solver stack below together with the code that produced them, then writing the next layer as a strategic pre-process and a library of callable helpers. Because $Ω$ never changes, it cannot destabilize the system, and because its input strictly grows, each layer reasons from a higher vantage than the last. Depth is set by convergence rather than fixed in advance, and an evolutionary archive searches over layer chains. Across two backbones, Meta$^n$ outperforms prior self-improving agents on all eight benchmark families. The sharpest case is ARC-AGI-2, built to resist skill memorization, where it alone scores above zero. Ablations indicate that most of the gain from recursion comes from the conditioning each layer passes to the next, and distinct layer roles emerge with depth although no prompt prescribes them. Code available at https://github.com/minnesotanlp/meta-n

</details>


### [106] [RACE: Scalable Statistical Estimation of Functional Consistency in LLM Neurons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24758)
*Runyu Wang,Bo Liu,Xiaxin Zhang,Yu Han,Jiawei Cao,Xiaoye Zhang,Zhe Zhang,Yifan Yang,Peng Ping*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RACE (Residual Alignment for Consistency Estimation) is a forward-pass statistical framework for evaluating the domain-wide functional consistency of Transformer neurons, achieving superior domain specificity compared to gradient-based methods with two orders of magnitude lower computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for discovering stable neuron behavior across domains rely on instance-level point estimates that obscure population-level variability or computationally expensive procedures that limit scalable domain-wide analysis, creating a gap in efficient population-level mechanistic interpretability.

Method: RACE uses a forward-pass statistical framework based on residual alignment to estimate the domain-wide functional consistency of Transformer neurons, avoiding backpropagation and enabling efficient population-level analysis.

Result: Perturbation experiments show RACE achieves superior domain specificity compared to gradient-based point estimates; token-distribution-level results confirm the association between selected neurons and the target domain; computational overhead is two orders of magnitude lower than gradient-based methods.

Conclusion: RACE provides a scalable and statistically grounded approach for domain-wide mechanistic interpretability of Transformer neurons, overcoming the limitations of instance-level point estimates and computationally expensive gradient-based procedures.

Abstract: Discovering stable neuron behavior across entire domains remains a challenge in mechanistic interpretability. Existing methods often rely on instance-level point estimates or computationally expensive procedures, which either obscure population-level variability or limit scalable domain-wide analysis. We present RACE (Residual Alignment for Consistency Estimation), a forward-pass statistical framework that evaluates the domain-wide functional consistency of Transformer neurons. Perturbation experiments demonstrate that RACE achieves superior domain specificity compared to gradient-based point estimates. Meanwhile, token-distribution-level results verify the association between the selected neurons and the target domain. Furthermore, its computational overhead is two orders of magnitude lower than that of gradient-based methods.

</details>


### [107] [Evidence Blindness in Direct Corpus Interaction: Persistent Navigation with AtlasNav](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24764)
*Hongyu Guo,Zhiyu Zheng,Zhao Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces AtlasNav, a persistent multi-view corpus-navigation framework for large language model agents that organizes corpora once into a "Corpus Atlas" structure, enabling finite-budget agentic search as navigation over reusable structure rather than online reconstruction. AtlasNav achieves 92.05% strict accuracy on BrowseComp-Plus while reducing online inference cost by 30.21% over prior state-of-the-art, and introduces the concept of "Evidence Blindness" — progressive silent loss of evidence across interaction stages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current DCI (Direct Corpus Interaction) agents face "Evidence Blindness" — required evidence may fail to surface, surfaced documents may remain unopened, or opened documents may not expose their decisive fragments — all under finite interaction budgets. Existing methods either use raw interaction (adding little reusable structure) or dynamic-workspace approaches that reconstruct query-conditioned interaction spaces from scratch per query, recovering useful structure mostly online and incurring high cost.

Method: AtlasNav formulates large-scale agentic search as finite-budget navigation over reusable corpus structure. It retains direct corpus interaction but organizes the corpus once into a persistent "Corpus Atlas" — a multi-view navigation representation — allowing each query to adaptively navigate this shared structure rather than reconstructing it per query. The paper introduces stage-wise evidence realization for quantifying Evidence Blindness across interaction stages.

Result: On BrowseComp-Plus, AtlasNav achieves 92.05% strict accuracy while reducing recorded online inference cost by 30.21% relative to the prior dynamic-workspace state of the art. Under matched budgets, it realizes complete required evidence earlier and approaches the model's evidence-supplied empirical reference more rapidly. The representation principle is robust under PhantomWiki's distinct corpus organization, controlled 10K-1M scaling, and transfers competitively to heterogeneous enterprise knowledge.

Conclusion: Agentic search depends not only on accessible evidence but critically on how the corpus is represented so that limited interaction becomes effective navigation. Persistent, reusable corpus organization (Corpus Atlas) outperforms both raw interaction and per-query dynamic workspace reconstruction, demonstrating that proactive structural organization of corpora is a key factor in effective finite-budget agentic search.

Abstract: Large language model agents are moving beyond conventional retrieval-augmented generation toward direct interaction with external corpora. Direct Corpus Interaction (DCI) keeps the full corpus accessible, yet reachable evidence can remain unusable under finite interaction budgets. Required evidence may fail to surface, a surfaced supporting document may remain unopened, or an opened document may fail to expose its decisive fragment. We call this progressive silent loss Evidence Blindness and quantify it through stage-wise evidence realization. Within the DCI paradigm, raw interaction adds little reusable corpus organization, while dynamic-workspace methods reconstruct a query-conditioned interaction space from each query and trajectory. In both cases, useful structure is recovered largely online. We instead formulate large-scale agentic search as finite-budget navigation over reusable corpus structure. We introduce AtlasNav, a persistent multi-view corpus-navigation framework that retains direct corpus interaction but organizes the corpus once into a Corpus Atlas, allowing each query to navigate adaptively rather than reconstruct shared structure. On BrowseComp-Plus, AtlasNav achieves 92.05% strict accuracy while reducing recorded online inference cost by 30.21% relative to the prior dynamic-workspace state of the art. Under matched budgets, it realizes the complete required evidence earlier and approaches the same model's evidence-supplied empirical reference more rapidly. The same representation principle remains effective under PhantomWiki's distinct corpus organization and controlled 10K-1M scaling, and transfers competitively to heterogeneous enterprise knowledge. These results show that agentic search depends not only on accessible evidence, but also on how the corpus is represented so that limited interaction becomes effective navigation.

</details>


### [108] [StepGuard: Learning Step-Level Guardrails with Scalable Supervision and Safety-Utility Balancing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24777)
*Zhijie Zheng,Yu Li,Chen Qian,Yuqian Fu,Yanwei Fu,Lu Sheng,Jing Shao,Dongrui Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: StepGuard is a step-level guard model that audits completed agent trajectories and pre-checks tool actions before execution, trained via an automatic data engine (StepGen) and a dynamic balancing method (Balance-GRPO), achieving state-of-the-art accuracy among open-weight guard models and reducing mean attack success rate by 77.3% with only a 2.8-point utility drop.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based agents face security risks (file modification, information leakage, unauthorized actions) when invoking tools. Existing guardrails evaluate completed trajectories only, leaving step-level pre-execution monitoring of individual tool actions underexplored, which is critical for preventing harmful actions before they occur.

Method: The authors propose (1) StepGuard, a step-level guard model that both audits completed agent trajectories and checks tool actions before execution; (2) StepGen, an automatic data engine that generates paired safe/unsafe trajectories sharing the same context but differing only in the action at the risky step; and (3) Balance-GRPO, a reinforcement learning method that dynamically balances learning between safe and unsafe actions based on observed accuracy to mitigate over-defense and under-defense.

Result: StepGuard achieves the highest average accuracy among open-weight guard models, with performance comparable to GPT-5.4. When deployed to guard agents on AgentDojo and AgentDyn, it reduces mean attack success rate by 77.3% relative to the no-guard setting, while mean utility drops by only 2.8 percentage points.

Conclusion: Step-level pre-execution monitoring is both feasible and effective for agent safety. StepGuard demonstrates that guarding individual tool actions before execution can substantially reduce attack success rates while maintaining high utility, and Balance-GRPO successfully addresses the trade-off between over-defense and under-defense.

Abstract: LLM-based agents can interact with external environments through tool invocation, but this capability also introduces security risks such as file modification, information leakage, and unauthorized actions. Existing guardrails often evaluate completed trajectories, leaving pre-execution monitoring of step-level actions underexplored. We propose StepGuard, a step-level guard model that can audit completed agent trajectories and check tool actions before they are executed. To train StepGuard, we introduce StepGen, an automatic data engine that generates safe and unsafe trajectories with the same context but different actions at the risky step. To further reduce over-defense and under-defense, we propose Balance-GRPO, which dynamically balances learning between safe and unsafe actions based on their observed accuracy. Experiments show that StepGuard achieves the highest average accuracy among open-weight guard models, with performance comparable to GPT-5.4. When used to guard agents on AgentDojo and AgentDyn, StepGuard reduces mean attack success rate by 77.3% relative to the no-guard setting, while mean utility drops by only 2.8 percentage points.

</details>


### [109] [Right Diagnoses, Decorative Reasoning:A Perturbation Audit of Medical Chain-of-Thought](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24790)
*Mengzhu Xu,Jifan Gao,Xia Jiang,Yaoxin Wu,Xi Long*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A medical perturbation audit using 30 clinically motivated operators and a chain-update vs. answer-flip joint analysis reveals that chain-of-thought reasoning in 14 medical LLMs is largely decoupled from final answers (72.9% Chain-Decoupling Rate on destructive edits), suggesting CoT serves as documentation rather than faithful reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinicians rely on CoT rationales as evidence of medical reasoning in LLMs, yet existing faithfulness probes neglect clinical cost and medical evaluations treat chains as black boxes—leaving a gap in testing whether visible chains actually determine answers in safety-critical medical contexts.

Method: The authors build a 30-operator perturbation battery that edits both the chain and the question using clinically motivated operators (severity reversal, negation flip, demographic swap, evidence ablation). They combine this with a chain-update-times-answer-flip joint analysis to classify failure modes across 14 LLMs and four medical QA benchmarks.
