Two board-certified clinicians re-annotate N=197 perturbed questions to validate gold label defensibility.

Result: Three independent tests converge: CDR is 72.9% panel-wide on clinically meaningful destructive edits, chain corruption leaves accuracy unchanged, and removing CoT prompting does not reduce accuracy. Clinician re-annotation confirms 98.5% of golds remain defensible. The pattern persists across fine-tuning approaches, scale, and is consistent in closed-source models via answer-side signals.

Conclusion: Medical CoT in current LLMs is largely decoupled from answer generation—serving as post-hoc documentation rather than faithful reasoning. The proposed framework and CDR metric offer a reusable standard for auditing CoT faithfulness in medical settings.

Abstract: Clinicians read chain-of-thought (CoT) rationales as evidence of medical reasoning, but whether the visible chain plays that role is rarely tested. General-domain CoT-faithfulness probes ignore clinical cost, and medical LLM evaluations treat the chain as a black box. We close this gap with a medical perturbation audit: a 30-operator battery edits both the chain and the question with clinically motivated operators (severity reversal, negation flip, demographic swap, evidence ablation), paired with a chain-update times answer-flip joint analysis that classifies each model by its failure mode. Applied to 14 LLMs on four medical QA benchmarks, three independent tests converge: the Chain-Decoupling Rate (CDR; chain does not register the edit and the answer does not flip) is 72.9% panel-wide on clinically meaningful destructive edits, chain corruption leaves accuracy unchanged, and removing CoT prompting does not reduce accuracy. Two board-certified clinicians re-annotate N=197 perturbed questions; 98.5% leave the gold defensible. The pattern holds across medical and reasoning fine-tuning and scale; on the closed-source tier, where the chain text is unavailable, the answer-side signals are consistent with the same decoupling. Our framework and CDR provide a reusable yardstick for auditing whether medical CoT is faithful or merely documentation.

</details>


### [110] [CAFE: Self-Improving Search Agents Need Co-Evolving Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24794)
*Boyang Liu,Senjie Jin,Peixin Wang,Zhangyue Yin,Yibo Wang,Yuhao Zhou,Xinbing Liang,Shizheng Zhu,Yuhui Wang,Jingqi Tong,Zhiheng Xi,Jiazheng Zhang,Clive Bai,Clarenceai,Blaze Chen,Tao Gui,Qi Zhang,Xuanjing Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CAFE is a framework where a shared-parameter model alternates between search-agent and critic roles, enabling feedback that co-evolves with the agent's policy. It couples online RL (comparative feedback estimation and feedback-aware advantage shaping) with offline preference optimization from matched trajectories, outperforming existing RL-based search agents across seven benchmarks and six out-of-domain benchmarks while reducing hallucinations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Outcome-supervised search agents cannot localize intermediate errors or redirect ongoing trajectories before mistakes compound, because terminal rewards arrive too late. Corrective feedback as a learned in-trajectory intervention introduces a coupled problem: the agent must learn when to request/use feedback, while the critic must infer useful corrections from outcome-confounded rollouts whose failure patterns shift as the agent improves.

Method: CAFE uses a shared-parameter model alternating between agent and critic roles. It (1) initializes feedback-conditioned recovery from trajectories built around the base agent's own failures, (2) couples online and offline optimization: online RL employs a comparative feedback estimate using a prompt-level call-skip success gap to shape request returns, plus feedback-aware advantage shaping that reweights token advantages before/after feedback; offline, rollout-derived preference optimization learns feedback from matched successful and unsuccessful trajectories.

Result: CAFE outperforms evaluated RL-based search agents on average across seven agentic search benchmarks, retains gains across all six out-of-domain benchmarks, and reduces answer-level hallucinations. One-sided ablations show improving only the agent or only the critic plateaus, whereas alternating both updates yields continued improvement.

Conclusion: A self-improving search agent requires feedback that co-evolves with the policy it guides; decoupling agent and critic improvement leads to saturation, while coupled alternating optimization sustains performance gains.

Abstract: Outcome-supervised search agents learn when and how to retrieve evidence, but terminal rewards neither localize intermediate errors nor redirect an ongoing trajectory before those errors compound. Treating corrective feedback as a learned in-trajectory intervention couples the two roles: the agent must decide when to request and use feedback, while the critic must infer useful corrections from outcome-confounded rollouts whose failure patterns shift as the agent improves. We introduce CAFE (Coupled Agent--Feedback Evolution), a framework in which a shared-parameter model alternates between search-agent and critic roles. CAFE initializes feedback-conditioned recovery from trajectories built around the base agent's own failures, then couples online and offline optimization. During online RL, a comparative feedback estimate uses a prompt-level call--skip success gap to shape request returns, while feedback-aware advantage shaping reweights token advantages before and after feedback. Offline, rollout-derived preference optimization learns feedback from matched successful and unsuccessful trajectories. On seven agentic search benchmarks, CAFE outperforms the evaluated RL-based search agents on average, retains its gains across all six out-of-domain benchmarks, and reduces answer-level hallucinations. One-sided ablations show that improving only the agent or only the critic eventually plateaus, whereas alternating the two updates continues to improve performance. These findings suggest that a self-improving search agent needs feedback that co-evolves with the policy it guides.

</details>


### [111] [StarHarness: Evolving Harnesses with Stratified Search for Enterprise Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24804)
*Esakkivel Esakkiraja,Denis Akhiyarov,Vikas Yadav,Sai Rajeswar,Patrice Bechard,Sridhar Nemala,Sagar Davasam*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: StarHarness is a framework that evolves environment-specific agent harnesses (prompts, tools, skills, subagent structures, and agent-loop configs) while keeping model weights fixed. It improves full-benchmark performance by 20-35 percentage points across IT, ITSM, and Finance benchmarks, with gains that generalize to held-out tasks and transfer across model families.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tool-rich enterprise tasks suffer from persistent model-environment mismatch that limits agent performance. Rather than retraining model weights, the authors propose evolving the harness itself—the surrounding configuration and interface—to better fit each environment.

Method: StarHarness constructs a compact evolution pool by stratifying tasks based on baseline failure behavior. It separates proposer-visible search tasks from proposer-hidden selection tasks to avoid overfitting, and reserves held-out tasks for evaluating generalization. The evolved harness can modify prompt/task framing, tool interfaces, skills, MCP-backed providers, subagent structure, and agent-loop configuration.

Result: Across ITBench SRE, EnterpriseOps-Gym ITSM, and AutomationBench Finance, harness evolution improves full-benchmark performance by 20-35 percentage points after only 4-12 accepted changes per environment. Gains persist on tasks excluded from evolution and transfer without re-evolution across GPT and Qwen model families. Trace analysis shows improvements come from interface repairs, environment conventions, and operational knowledge that compresses search, with fewer false-positive diagnoses and shorter trajectories.

Conclusion: StarHarness provides a practical path to reduce persistent model-environment mismatch in tool-rich enterprise tasks by evolving the harness rather than the model, achieving substantial and generalizable performance improvements at low cost.

Abstract: We present StarHarness, a framework for evolving environment-specific agent harnesses while keeping model weights fixed. The evolved harness can include prompt and task framing, tool interfaces, skills, MCP-backed providers, subagent structure, and agent-loop configuration. StarHarness constructs a compact evolution pool by stratifying tasks according to baseline failure behavior, separates proposer-visible search tasks from proposer-hidden selection tasks, and reserves held-out tasks for evaluating generalization. Across ITBench SRE, EnterpriseOps-Gym ITSM, and AutomationBench Finance, harness evolution improves full-benchmark performance by 20-35 percentage points over the default harness after 4-12 accepted changes per environment. These gains persist on tasks excluded from evolution and transfer without re-evolution across GPT and Qwen model families. Trace analysis links the improvements to interface repairs, environment conventions, and operational knowledge that compresses search, with fewer false-positive diagnoses and shorter trajectories in several settings. StarHarness therefore offers a practical way to reduce persistent model-environment mismatch in tool-rich enterprise tasks.

</details>


### [112] [Strictly Causal Streaming Video Anomaly Detection with a Theoretically-Grounded State-Space Core](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24810)
*Yogesh Kumar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a strictly causal streaming video anomaly detector using a diagonal linear state space recurrence with input/state-dependent decay gating. It achieves O(1) per-frame updates with no lookahead or clip buffering, derives a closed-form relationship between recurrence decay and detection delay (theoretical settling bound of 57-59 frames vs measured lag of 1.6-18.4 frames), reports real edge-hardware latency (0.74-0.77 ms/frame on Apple M3 Pro, >1300 FPS), and achieves 67.9% and 70.2% frame-level AUC on UCSD Ped2 and CUHK Avenue—trailing prior non-causal SSM baselines, with gate effects being dataset-size dependent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Mamba-style SSM video anomaly detectors rely on clip/window buffering, lack theoretical understanding of how temporal memory relates to detection latency, and benchmark throughput only on simulated GPU numbers rather than the edge hardware these methods target. This work aims to address these gaps with a strictly causal, streaming formulation.

Method: The method is a strictly causal streaming anomaly detector built on a diagonal linear state space recurrence with an input- and state-dependent decay gate, trained self-supervised via causal next-embedding prediction on a frozen visual backbone. The fixed-size state updates in O(1) time/memory per frame with no buffering or lookahead. The paper derives a closed-form relationship between the recurrence decay spectrum and both detection delay and shortest detectable anomaly duration.

Result: The predicted settling delay from base decay (57-59 frames) far exceeds measured detection delay (1.6 frames on Ped2, 18.4 frames on Avenue), showing the event boundary gate governs responsiveness. On Apple M3 Pro hardware, end-to-end latency is 0.74-0.77 ms/frame (>1300 FPS). Frame-level AUC reaches 67.9% (Ped2) and 70.2% (Avenue), trailing non-causal SSM baselines. Ablations show gating helps on the larger Avenue dataset but hurts on smaller Ped2.

Conclusion: The causal streaming formulation achieves strong real-time edge performance with theoretical grounding of latency behavior, but leaves an accuracy gap versus non-causal SSM baselines. Immediate next steps are closing this accuracy gap and extending evaluation to a third, larger benchmark.

Abstract: Recent work has applied Mamba style state space models (SSMs) to video anomaly detection, yet existing approaches still rely on buffering clips or windows internally, lack a theoretical account of how temporal memory relates to detection latency, and benchmark efficiency only through GPU throughput rather than the edge hardware these methods are intended to target. We introduce a strictly causal streaming anomaly detector whose fixed size state is updated in O(1) time and memory per incoming frame, with no lookahead and no clip buffering. Its temporal core is a diagonal linear state space recurrence with an input and state dependent decay gate, trained self supervised through causal next embedding prediction on a frozen visual backbone. We derive a closed form relationship between the recurrence decay spectrum and both detection delay and the shortest anomaly it can reliably capture, then validate empirically on UCSD Ped2 and CUHK Avenue. The settling delay bound predicted from the learned base decay (57 to 59 frames) sits far above the measured detection delay (1.6 and 18.4 frames), showing that the event boundary gate, not the base decay, governs responsiveness. We further report end to end latency and throughput measured directly on Apple M3 Pro hardware, 0.74 ms and 0.77 ms per frame (over 1300 FPS), rather than simulated GPU numbers. With an untuned initial configuration the method reaches 67.9 percent and 70.2 percent frame level AUC on Ped2 and Avenue, trailing prior non causal SSM baselines in accuracy. Ablations over decay rate, state size, and gating reveal that the gate contribution is dataset size dependent, hurting accuracy on the smaller Ped2 training set but helping on the larger Avenue one. Closing this accuracy gap and extending evaluation to a third, larger benchmark are immediate next steps.

</details>


### [113] [Constrained Entity Selection under Partial Knowledge for LLM-Based Knowledge Graph QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24824)
*Emanuel Kitzelmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Constrained Entity Selection under Partial Knowledge (CES-PK), a framework for verifying LLM-generated candidate answers in knowledge graph question answering using lightweight symbolic constraints derived from the question, with a three-valued constraint semantics to handle incomplete KGs. Experiments on the Hetionet biomedical KG show precision improvements through filtering invalid candidates while preserving recall.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based KGQA systems either rely on brittle full semantic parsing into executable queries (e.g., SPARQL) which fails on complex or incomplete real-world KGs, or on LLM-reasoning which is robust but lacks formal guarantees. There is a need for a middle-ground approach that verifies LLM-generated answers using lightweight symbolic constraints without requiring full executable logical forms.

Method: The paper formulates the CES-PK problem, where candidate answers generated by an LLM-based system are verified using lightweight symbolic constraints (type, relation, and exclusion constraints) derived from the question. To handle incomplete KGs, a three-valued constraint semantics (satisfied, violated, unknown) is employed under open-world assumptions, avoiding incorrect rejections of valid answers.

Result: Experiments on the Hetionet biomedical knowledge graph demonstrate that filtering invalid candidates improves precision while preserving recall, because candidates whose constraints are not explicitly violated are retained. Satisfied constraints also provide additional positive symbolic evidence for ranking remaining candidates.

Conclusion: Symbolic constraint verification provides an effective complementary mechanism for LLM-based KGQA, offering formal guarantees without requiring full semantic parsing and preserving recall while improving precision under open-world assumptions.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used for knowledge graph question answering (KGQA), but can fail to correctly ground answers in the underlying graph. Current approaches to LLM-based KGQA either rely on full semantic parsing into executable queries such as SPARQL, which is brittle in practice due to complex schemas or incompleteness of real-world KGs, or on LLM-reasoning and answer generation over KGs, which can be more robust but lacks formal guarantees. In this work, we study a complementary setting in which \emph{candidate} answers are generated by an LLM-based system and subsequently verified using lightweight symbolic constraints derived from the question. We introduce \emph{Constrained Entity Selection under Partial Knowledge (CES-PK)}, a problem formulation that focuses on eliminating invalid answers and providing symbolic support for valid ones without requiring construction of executable logical forms. To account for incomplete KGs, we employ a three-valued constraint semantics (\emph{satisfied, violated, unknown}) that avoids incorrect rejections under open-world assumptions. To demonstrate the effects of our method, we instantiate this framework over the Hetionet biomedical knowledge graph and evaluate the impact of type, relation, and exclusion constraints. Experiments show that precision improves by filtering invalid candidates, while recall is preserved due to retaining candidates whose constraints are not explicitly violated. Satisfied constraints provide additional positive symbolic evidence to rank remaining candidates.

</details>


### [114] [A Dual-Dimensional LLM Framework for Automated Item Incidental Content Similarity Analysis in Large-Scale Assessments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24825)
*Jing Huang,Jihong Zhang,Hua-Hua Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a dual-dimensional Automated Item Similarity Analysis (AISA) framework powered by Large Language Models (LLMs), which operationalizes item similarity through Structured Decomposition and Semantic Relatedness. LLM-derived metrics outperform traditional text-based measures (BLEU, cosine similarity) in capturing construct-irrelevant redundancy, align better with psychometric indicators, and improve estimation stability and reduce bias in Computerized Adaptive Testing with minimal efficiency trade-offs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The growing use of automatic item generation in large-scale assessments raises concerns about incidental content redundancy—where construct-irrelevant elements like wording or context become unintentionally repetitive across items. Traditional similarity metrics (BLEU, cosine similarity) fail to capture the nuanced structural and semantic layers that collectively drive perceived redundancy, necessitating a more sophisticated approach.

Method: The study develops a dual-dimensional AISA framework powered by LLMs, operationalizing item similarity through two complementary lenses: Structured Decomposition (analyzing structural layers of items) and Semantic Relatedness (capturing meaning-level overlap). The framework is validated psychometrically against indicators of construct-irrelevant local dependence and item parameter groupings, and evaluated in Computerized Adaptive Testing (CAT) via simulations incorporating LLM-based similarity constraints into item selection.

Result: LLM-derived similarity metrics align more closely with construct-irrelevant local dependence indicators and yield more coherent item parameter groupings than traditional text-based measures. In CAT simulations, LLM-based similarity constraints improve estimation stability and reduce bias with minimal efficiency trade-offs, outperforming constraints based on conventional metrics.

Conclusion: LLM-powered AISA offers a scalable solution for item bank curation, content-aware test assembly, and experience-sensitive adaptive testing across diverse assessment contexts, addressing the limitations of conventional similarity metrics in managing unintended item redundancy.

Abstract: The rapid expansion of large-scale assessments and the growing adoption of automatic item generation have intensified concerns about incidental content redundancy, where construct-irrelevant elements such as wording or contextual framing become unintentionally repetitive across items. Traditional similarity metrics like BLEU or cosine similarity, often fail to capture the nuanced structural and semantic layers that drive perceived redundancy simultaneously. This study proposes a dual-dimensional framework for Automated Item Similarity Analysis (AISA) powered by Large Language Models (LLMs), operationalizing similarity through Structured Decomposition and Semantic Relatedness. Psychometric validation indicates that LLM-derived metrics align more closely with indicators of construct-irrelevant local dependence and yield more coherent item parameter groupings than traditional text-based measures. The framework is further evaluated through its application in Computerized Adaptive Testing (CAT). Simulations reveal that incorporating LLM-based similarity constraints into item selection improves estimation stability and reduces bias with minimal efficiency trade-offs, outperforming constraints based on conventional metrics. These findings highlight the potential of LLM-powered AISA to support scalable bank curation, content-aware test assembly, and experience-sensitive adaptive testing across diverse assessment contexts.

</details>


### [115] [FedV-KGQA: Multi-Hop Question Answering over Vertically Partitioned Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24846)
*Md Saikat Islam Khan Bappy,Oshani Seneviratne*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FedV-KGQA is a federated framework for multi-hop question answering over knowledge graphs where organizations share entities but own disjoint sets of relations. It combines local graph enrichment and knowledge graph embeddings to keep raw triples and relation parameters within each silo, uses a topic entity anchoring mechanism to ground questions without runtime inter-silo communication, and demonstrates strong performance across 12 model configurations on three benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world knowledge graphs for question answering are often vertically partitioned across organizations due to governance and data sovereignty constraints. Centralized systems cannot answer multi-hop questions when required facts are split across silos, motivating the need for a federated approach that respects data boundaries.

Method: FedV-KGQA combines local graph enrichment with knowledge graph embeddings to maintain structural data boundaries (raw triples and relation parameters never leave each silo), and introduces a topic entity anchoring mechanism that grounds questions in the correct graph neighborhood without runtime inter-silo communication.

Result: Evaluations across 12 model configurations on three benchmarks show FedV-KGQA performs strongly, remains close to centralized performance, generalizes to 3-hop reasoning, and is robust to embedding perturbations.

Conclusion: FedV-KGQA provides an effective federated solution for vertical knowledge graph partitioning, achieving near-centralized performance while preserving data sovereignty constraints and avoiding runtime inter-silo communication.

Abstract: Real-world data for knowledge graph question answering is often distributed across different organizations due to governance and data sovereignty constraints. While centralized systems exist, they cannot answer multi-hop questions when the required facts are split across vertically partitioned silos. In this paper, we propose FedV-KGQA, a framework for multi-hop reasoning over knowledge graphs in which organizations share entities but own disjoint sets of relations. Our approach combines local graph enrichment and knowledge graph embeddings to ensure raw triples and relation parameters never leave each silo, establishing a structural data boundary without requiring centralized graph access. We further introduce a topic entity anchoring mechanism that grounds questions in the correct graph neighborhood without any runtime inter-silo communication. We evaluate 12 model configurations across three benchmarks and show that FedV-KGQA performs strongly, remains close to centralized performance, generalizes to 3-hop reasoning, and is robust to embedding perturbations.

</details>


### [116] [SPO++: Stream-Aligned Policy Optimization for Asynchronous Agentic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24870)
*Kai Ruan,Jinghao Lin,Qianshan Wei,Ziqi Zhou,Zihe Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SPO++ improves on Single-stream Policy Optimization (SPO) by fixing a mismatch in advantage normalization—trajectory-level whitening does not center the token-weighted actor loss, so SPO++ standardizes terminal-outcome advantages under the action-token measure and reorganizes prompt evidence by the generating policy event. On ALFWorld (two scales) and Math-TIR, SPO++ outperforms SPO in online learning efficiency, with action-token-measure normalization being the strongest ablation component.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Group-relative reinforcement learning requires sibling rollouts from the same prompt, which is costly for long and variable tool-use trajectories. SPO removes this dependency using a persistent prompt-level value estimate, but its normalization recipe is flawed: whitening one advantage per trajectory does not center the token-weighted quantity actually consumed by the actor loss.

Method: SPO++ standardizes terminal-outcome advantages under the action-token measure (fixing the centering mismatch between trajectory-level whitening and the token-weighted actor objective), and reorganizes prompt evidence by the policy event that generated it rather than by learner receipt order.

Result: Across matched runs on ALFWorld at two model scales and on Math-TIR, SPO++ improves online learning efficiency over SPO. A paired ablation identifies action-token-measure normalization as the strongest tested component.

Conclusion: Correctly normalizing advantages under the action-token measure (rather than per-trajectory) substantially improves single-stream policy optimization for long, variable tool-use trajectories, making it a viable and more efficient alternative to group-relative methods.

Abstract: Group-relative reinforcement learning waits for sibling rollouts of the same prompt, which is costly for long and variable tool-use trajectories. Single-stream Policy Optimization (SPO) removes this dependency with a persistent prompt-level value estimate, but its recipe whitens one advantage per trajectory before optimizing a token-mean actor loss. We show that trajectory centering generally does not center the token-weighted quantity consumed by the actor, and fix the mismatch by standardizing terminal-outcome advantages under the action-token measure. We additionally organize prompt evidence by the policy event that generated it rather than learner receipt order. Across matched runs on ALFWorld at two model scales and on Math-TIR, SPO++ improves online learning efficiency over SPO. A paired ablation identifies action-token-measure normalization as the strongest tested component.

</details>


### [117] [Recursive Experiential-Working Memory Evolution for Long-Horizon Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24876)
*Zhaochen Yu,Yingcheng Wu,Zhenfei Yin,Kaiyuan Chen,Zhe Zhao,Mengdi Wang,Shuicheng Yan,Ling Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Recuris is a recursive Experiential-Working Memory architecture for long-horizon agent tasks, where Working Memory tracks task progress and guides skill selection from Experiential Memory, enabling validation-gated recursive memory evolution. It improves task success in 35/37 model-benchmark pairs across four benchmarks and ten models, achieving SOTA results (e.g., +17.8 on tau-bench for GPT-5.6 Sol, +15.6 for Claude Opus 5 reaching 87.9%), with advantages growing up to +32.2 points on longest tasks and reducing common failures by up to 80%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recursive self-improvement (RSI) is difficult in long-horizon tasks because growing interaction histories obscure the task state and misalign skill invocation, degrading agent performance and making failure localization challenging.

Method: Recuris couples Working Memory (tracking task progress) with Experiential Memory (guiding skill selection), using execution as structured evidence to localize failures. A fixed Meta-Agent converts this evidence into localized, validation-gated updates to Skill Memory, forming a bounded recursive memory-evolution loop.

Result: Recuris improves task success in 35 of 37 completed model-benchmark pairs across four long-horizon benchmarks and ten models. It achieves SOTA results including +17.8 on tau-bench for GPT-5.6 Sol, +15.6 for Claude Opus 5 (reaching 87.9%), and +16.6/+13.5 on Qwen3.6-27B/35B for SkillFlow. Advantage grows to +32.2 points on longest tasks, with common long-horizon failures reduced by up to 80%.

Conclusion: Recursively evolving memory serves as a scalable foundation for recursive self-improvement, enabling agents to continuously transform accumulated experience into increasingly effective long-horizon behavior.

Abstract: Recursive self-improvement (RSI) remains hard in long-horizon tasks, where growing histories obscure the task state and misalign skill invocation. We introduce Recuris, a recursive Experiential-Working Memory architecture for long-horizon agent harnesses, in which Working Memory tracks task progress and guides skill selection from Experiential Memory, grounding skill use in current needs rather than the full history. This coupling also turns execution into structured evidence that localizes failures to specific memory components. Across tasks, a fixed Meta-Agent turns that evidence into localized, validation-gated updates to Skill Memory that reshape execution and yield new evidence, forming a bounded recursive memory-evolution loop. Across four long-horizon benchmarks and ten models, Recuris improves task success in 35 of the 37 completed model-benchmark pairs, carrying frontier models to SOTA-level task success: on tau-bench it adds +17.8 points to GPT-5.6 Sol and +15.6 to Claude Opus 5, taking Opus 5 to 87.9%, and +16.6/+13.5 points on Qwen3.6-27B/35B on SkillFlow. The advantage widens as the interaction horizon grows, to +32.2 points on the longest tasks, and common long-horizon failures fall by up to 80%. These results position recursively evolving memory as a scalable foundation for RSI, enabling agents to continuously transform accumulated experience into increasingly effective long-horizon behavior. Code: https://github.com/Gen-Verse/Recuris

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [118] [Pattern-Derived Visual Swarm Games: Multi-Scale Drone-Vision States for Interception and Sustainability Audits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23575)
*Faruk Alpay,Levent Sarioglu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper converts drone-vision annotation streams into virtual swarm-game states without controlling physical drones, using Bloom representations and deterministic probes to produce capability vectors and finite zero-sum games. It scales from 6×6 to 32×32 games, adds a repeated Markov layer with nine state variables, and diagnoses image-plane bandwidth shrinking as the error source. A finite empirical-risk encoder over scale-normalized Gaussian bandwidths achieves 77.6% accuracy at 128×128, reducing joint loss by 0.185, while a 32-round repeated-game audit over 16M trajectories selects a budget-adaptive policy with value 0.461.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To audit and validate drone-vision systems by converting annotation streams into virtual game states, enabling scalable evaluation of capability, robustness, and decision-making without requiring physical drone control.

Method: Compresses VisDrone and UAVSwarm metadata into Bloom representations with deterministic probes to generate bounded capability vectors, image-space formations, zero-sum payoffs, and visual overlays. The audit scales finite games from 6×6 to 32×32 (locally tuned to value 0.616 from baseline 0.526 at 32×32). Adds a repeated Markov layer with nine state variables (stock, fatigue, adaptation, exposure, stress, budget, data-growth, model-improvement, entropy-budget). A fixed-pixel raster audit reveals non-monotonic accuracy (67.2% at 128×128, hotspot error 0.136), diagnosed as shrinking image-plane bandwidth. A finite empirical-risk encoder over scale-normalized Gaussian bandwidths selects λ=1.50, reaching 77.6% accuracy and reducing joint loss by 0.185. A server-side audit checks 16,777,216 target-localization states, and a 32-round repeated-game audit over 16,777,216 trajectories selects a budget-adaptive policy with value 0.461.

Result: Local screen tuning improves robust screen security from 0.526 to 0.593, and the 32×32 tuned screen reaches value 0.616. The empirical-risk encoder with λ=1.50 achieves 77.6% accuracy at 128×128 (up from 67.2% baseline), reducing joint loss by 0.185. The budget-adaptive policy from the repeated-game audit achieves value 0.461.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates a scalable virtual game-state audit framework for drone-vision systems, identifies shrinking image-plane bandwidth as the key error source, and shows that scale-normalized Gaussian-bandwidth encoders plus budget-adaptive repeated-game policies substantially improve robustness and accuracy.

Abstract: We convert drone-vision annotation streams into virtual swarm-game states without controlling physical drones. VisDrone and UAVSwarm metadata are compressed into a Bloom representation; deterministic probes produce bounded capability vectors, image-space formations, finite zero-sum payoffs, and human-readable visual overlays. The audit scales from $6\times 6$ to $32\times 32$ finite games and adds a repeated Markov layer with stock, fatigue, adaptation, exposure, stress, budget, data-growth, model-improvement, and entropy-budget state variables. Local screen tuning raises robust screen security from $0.526$ to $0.593$, and the $32\times 32$ tuned screen reaches value $0.616$. A field readout audit shows that fixed-pixel rasters do not improve monotonically: $128\times 128$ accuracy is $67.2\%$ and hotspot error is $0.136$. The diagnosed error is shrinking image-plane bandwidth. A finite empirical-risk encoder over scale-normalized Gaussian bandwidths selects a scale-normalized encoder with $λ=1.50$, reaching $77.6\%$ accuracy at $128\times 128$ and reducing joint loss by $0.185$. A server-side audit checks $16{,}777{,}216$ target-localization states, and a 32-round repeated-game audit over $16{,}777{,}216$ trajectories selects a budget-adaptive policy with value $0.461$.

</details>


### [119] [Macro-Operator Generation and Predicate Selection for TAMP Operator Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23629)
*Can Emir Bora,Emre Ugur*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a system that accelerates Task and Motion Planning (TAMP) by automatically discovering macro-operators from demonstration data—composite actions that compress recurring sequences of individual actions into single planning steps—and by pruning predicates not referenced by any learned operator, resulting in up to 4.6x planning speedup and enabling solution of long sequential tasks that baseline methods cannot solve.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hand-crafting symbolic operators is a major bottleneck in deploying TAMP systems. While recent works learn operators from demonstrations, existing methods treat each action in isolation, failing to capture recurring multi-step manipulation structures, which makes search intractable for long sequential tasks. Additionally, evaluating all provided predicates at every search node wastes computation when most are never used by learned operators.

Method: The system has two central components. First, it automatically generates macro-operators by discovering causally linked action pairs from training data—pairs where one action produces exactly the condition required by the next—and compresses each recurring sequence into a single composite planning step. Second, it prunes all predicates that no learned operator references, shrinking the symbolic state evaluated at each search node. These two mechanisms together shorten the effective planning horizon.

Result: Across four TAMP domains, the method achieves up to a 4.6x planning speedup compared to the baseline (Learning Operators for TAMP). Crucially, it solves a long sequential task that the baseline fails to solve, demonstrating that macro-operator discovery not only accelerates planning but can determine practical solvability in certain domains.

Conclusion: Macro-operator discovery from demonstrations, combined with predicate pruning, significantly improves TAMP planning efficiency. The benefit grows with task length, and in certain domains, macro-operators are the deciding factor between solvability and failure in practice.

Abstract: Creating symbolic operators by hand is one of the main bottlenecks in deploying Task and Motion Planning systems (TAMP). Recent works show that these operators can instead be learned directly from demonstration data. Existing methods, however, typically learn each action in isolation and cannot capture the recurring multi-step structure of manipulation tasks, so the search becomes intractable on long sequential tasks. A further inefficiency arises in the symbolic state: every provided predicate is evaluated at every search node, even when it never appears in any learned operator. We present a system that addresses both problems together. Its central component is the automatic generation of macro-operators, composite actions that compress a recurring sequence of individual actions into a single planning step. Our system discovers causally linked action pairs directly from the training data, where one action produces exactly the condition that the next one requires, and turns each pair into a new operator. Alongside this, our system prunes every predicate that no learned operator references, which shrinks the symbolic state evaluated at each search node. Together, these changes shorten the effective planning horizon, and the benefit they bring grows with the length of the task. Across four TAMP domains, our method reaches up to a 4.6x planning speedup compared to the baseline method, namely Learning Operators for TAMP. More importantly, it solves a long sequential task that the baseline cannot solve. Macro-operator discovery thus not only accelerates planning but, in certain domains, determines solvability in practice.

</details>


### [120] [Concept-Guided Exploration: Building Persistent, Actionable Scene Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23650)
*Noé Zapata,Gerardo Pérez,Alejandro Torrejón,Pedro Núñez,Pablo Bustos*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper introduces a concept-first architecture for 3D spatial perception in mobile robots, where spatial understanding emerges from asynchronous concept agents (room and door) that directly instantiate and manage semantic entities, building a shared scene graph without relying on a pre-existing global metric map.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current approaches to 3D space perception typically build metric maps first and then add semantic layers, which is a bottom-up pipeline that may not efficiently leverage human-interpretable concepts for spatial understanding. The authors explore an alternative paradigm where semantic concepts come first and drive spatial representation.

Method: The architecture employs two spatial concept agents (room and door) as autonomous processes within a cognitive distributed system. These agents cooperatively build a shared scene graph through active exploration and incremental validation. The key principle is hierarchical constraint propagation, where room instantiation provides geometric and semantic priors that guide door detection within wall boundaries, complemented by prediction-matching loops.

Result: The approach yields an actionable, human-interpretable spatial representation without relying on a pre-existing global metric map, supporting scalable operation and persistent task-relevant understanding in structured indoor environments.

Conclusion: A concept-first architecture with asynchronous semantic agents can effectively build scene graph representations of indoor layouts, demonstrating an alternative to the conventional metric-first mapping paradigm for mobile robot spatial perception.

Abstract: The perception of 3D space by mobile robots is rapidly moving from flat metric grid representations to hybrid metric-semantic graphs built from human-interpretable concepts. While most approaches first build metric maps and then add semantic layers, we explore an alternative, concept-first architecture in which spatial understanding emerges from asynchronous concept agents that directly instantiate and manage semantic entities. Our robot employs two spatial concepts (room and door), implemented as autonomous processes within a cognitive distributed architecture. These concept agents cooperatively build a shared scene graph representation of indoor layouts through active exploration and incremental validation. The key architectural principle is hierarchical constraint propagation: Room instantiation provides geometric and semantic priors to guide and support door detection within wall boundaries. The resulting structure is maintained by a complementary functional principle based on prediction-matching loops. This approach is designed to yield an actionable, human-interpretable spatial representation without relying on any pre-existing global metric map, supporting scalable operation and persistent, task-relevant understanding in structured indoor environments.

</details>


### [121] [Learning to Act While Waiting: RL Finetuning of Generalist Robot Policies Under Inference Latency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23831)
*Brian Zhu,Momen Khalil,E Harrison,Emanuele Poggi,Philipp Schmitt,Bernd Kast,Philine Meister,Pranav Atreya,Qiyang Li,Finn Ferchau,Cesar Colmenero,Yash Shahapurkar,Gokul Narayanan,Melih Erdogan,Kai Wurm,Georg von Wichert,Oier Mees,Eugen Solowjow,Andrew Wagenmaker,Sergey Levine*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ARLI is a latency-aware RL framework that enables fine-tuning of large generalist robot policies (e.g., VLAs) under inference delays by augmenting states with committed actions and mid-inference observations to restore near-Markovian structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern generalist robot policies (VLAs) are too large to perform efficient RL-based improvement because their severe inference latency alters environment dynamics and breaks the Markov assumption that RL relies on, causing standard RL algorithms to fail completely.

Method: ARLI builds on asynchronous inference (interleaving action generation with execution to hide latency) and introduces two key contributions: (1) a low-latency RL policy design that maximizes reactivity within the inference window, and (2) state augmentations that restore near-Markovian structure by incorporating committed actions and a mid-inference observation.

Result: Evaluated on simulated and real-world manipulation tasks, ARLI enables effective fine-tuning under inference delays where standard RL fails entirely, and even matches or exceeds the performance of standard RL in idealized no-latency settings.

Conclusion: ARLI demonstrates that RL-based improvement of large generalist policies is feasible under realistic inference delays, overcoming the latency-induced breakdown of the Markov assumption through targeted state augmentation and asynchronous inference design.

Abstract: While reinforcement learning (RL) allows generalist robot policies to continually improve during deployment, the large model size of modern generalist policies, such as VLAs, poses a fundamental obstacle to effective RL improvement. In particular, their severe inference latency---which can lead to pauses or jerky movements---can alter the effective environment dynamics and, if not correctly accounted for, break the Markov assumption that RL relies on, causing standard RL algorithms to fail completely. In this work, we introduce a latency-aware framework, Asynchronous RL with Intermediate Information (ARLI), that enables RL-based improvement of generalist policies under inference delays.
  Our framework builds on asynchronous inference approaches, which interleave action generation with execution to hide latency, and addresses its incompatibility with RL by providing a low-latency RL policy design that maximizes reactivity within the inference window through two contributions: state augmentations that restore near-Markovian structure by incorporating committed actions and a mid-inference observation.
  We evaluate our approach across simulated and real-world manipulation tasks, and find that it enables effective finetuning under inference delays where standard RL fails entirely, even matching or exceeding the performance of standard RL in idealized no-latency settings.

</details>


### [122] [Resilience Matters for Embodied Agents System: New Metrics, Systematic Evaluation, and Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23839)
*Yapeng Liu,Yuanzhao Zhai,Xudong Gong,Dawei Feng,Bo Ding,Lin Wang,Huaimin Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper introduces a resilience evaluation framework for Embodied Agents Systems (EAS), defining a metrics suite covering Rebound, Stability, and Graceful Extensibility to capture process-level behavioral dynamics that outcome-centric metrics like success rate miss. Evaluated across 400 household tasks with 10 EAS, it reveals hidden process distinctions and demonstrates that metrics-guided optimization improves resilience.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing EAS evaluations rely on outcome-centric metrics (success rate, safety scores) that collapse diverse execution trajectories into coarse scores, obscuring dynamic processes. They ignore a critical property—Resilience—that reflects how EAS recover, stabilize, and extend under perturbations and iterative updates, which is particularly critical in open-world environments with continuous unexpected disruptions and directly affects deployment quality and human-agent trust.

Method: The authors draw on resilience-engineering concepts adapted to EAS groundings and propose a flexible resilience evaluation framework. They define a comprehensive resilience metrics suite measuring Rebound (recovery from perturbations), Stability (consistency under disturbances), and Graceful Extensibility (capacity to extend under new demands). They implement a resilience evaluation layer that transforms execution processes into assessments for diagnosis and optimization, applicable to any EAS.

Result: Across 400 household tasks with 10 EAS, process-level distinctions hidden by outcome metrics were revealed, including recovery cost differences among successful episodes (ΔC_rec=25.2), increased instability, and task-family degradation. Metrics-guided optimizations reduced recovery cost and increased stability and graceful extensibility completion. Results also revealed a trade-off among resilience characteristics.

Conclusion: Resilience evaluation provides diagnostic value beyond outcome-centric metrics, enabling optimization of EAS deployment. The observed trade-off among resilience characteristics suggests that a resilient EAS construction should be configured according to deployment-specific requirements rather than optimized uniformly.

Abstract: Embodied Agents System (EAS) are increasingly deployed in open-world physical domains, where reliability directly dictates deployment quality and human-agent trust. However, existing evaluations rely on outcome-centric metrics as success rate or safety scores that collapse diverse execution trajectories into coarse scores, obscuring the dynamic processes underlying agent behavior. Therefore, they ignore a critical property of EAS -- which we define as the Resilience -- that reflects how EASs recover, stabilize, and extend under perturbations and across iterative updates. The lack of resilience is particularly critical in open-world environments due to continuous unexpected disruptions, thus directly affecting the quality of EAS deployment. To address this problem, we gain insight from the resilience-engineering concepts to EAS groundings and propose a novel resilience evaluation framework that can be flexibly applied to any EAS. Specifically, we define the first comprehensive resilience metrics suite for EASs system that exposes Rebound, Stability, and Graceful Extensibility across embodied tasks execution, providing a practical grounding for EAS resilience analysis. We further implement the resilience evaluation layer that transforms execution process into assessments for diagnosis and optimization. Across 400 household tasks with 10 EAS, we reveal the process-level distinction hidden by outcome metrics, including recovery cost differences among successful episodes ($ΔC_{rec}=25.2$), increased instability and task-family degradation. Metrics-guided optimizations reduce recovery cost and increase stability, graceful extensibility completion, showing the diagnostic effect of resilience evaluation. Our results reveal a trade-off among resilience characteristics, suggesting that a resilient EAS construction should be configured according to deployment-specific requirements.

</details>


### [123] [DreamLedger: Execution-Settled Credit Files for World-Model Imagination in Robot Decision Loops](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23863)
*Xianyao Li,Ruitong Tian,Rui Min,Fang Xu,Jing Du*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DreamLedger is an execution-settled credit system that treats robot prediction reliability as a persistent deployment object—recording how often consumed predictions are borne out across operating conditions, regions, and horizons—and gates consumption by these credits to reduce failed predictions, with auditable logs and settlement supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern robots act on world-model predictions, but reliability is expressed through instantaneous, model-internal signals only. There is no persistent, deployment-grounded record of whether consumed predictions actually hold up in the real world, making trust brittle and hard to audit.

Method: DreamLedger registers each consumed prediction as a claim, settles claims against arriving reality at zero labeling cost, excludes measurement-contaminated outcomes via an attribution stage, and uses a settlement-supervised head to complement sparse bins. Credits are indexed by operating condition, region, and prediction horizon, and gate consumption: low-credit predictions shorten horizons or trigger extra observation. Every reliance event is logged via dependency tickets and replayable audit logs.

Result: Across 12 held-out condition-horizon cells, claim failure is dose-monotone. Credit-gated planning cuts burned imagination (consumed claims that later fail) by 62% (95% CI 43–81%) versus blind consumption, with equal success and comparable collision rates. At matched risk targets, verification probes drop from 1.00 to 0.36/episode in manipulation (success 0.94 vs 0.98). The framework mounts on DreamerV3, TD-MPC2, and V-JEPA 2-AC across decoder-, latent-, and token-space interfaces, remains operational on a real Franka manipulator under contact and sensing noise, re-prices a deployment failure loop online, and replays all 1,062 registered spends from audit logs.

Conclusion: Treating reliability as a persistent, settled ledger object yields safer, auditable, and more efficient prediction consumption across diverse world-model stacks—from simulated domains to real hardware—without sacrificing success rates.

Abstract: Robots are beginning to act on world-model predictions, yet reliability is still expressed through instantaneous, model-internal signals. DreamLedger instead treats reliability as a persistent deployment object: an execution-settled credit file recording how often consumed predictions are borne out, indexed by operating condition, region, and prediction horizon, and consulted before each use. Each consumed prediction is registered as a claim; attributable outcomes are settled against arriving reality at zero labeling cost, an attribution stage excludes measurement-contaminated outcomes, and a settlement-supervised head complements sparse bins. The resulting credit gates consumption: low-credit predictions shorten the dependent horizon or trigger additional observation; every reliance event remains auditable via dependency tickets and replayable logs. We evaluate DreamLedger in three simulated domains (indoor flight, tabletop manipulation, 2D navigation), via mounts on unmodified DreamerV3, TD-MPC2, and V-JEPA 2-AC, and on a real Franka manipulator. Claim failure is dose-monotone in all 12 held-out condition-horizon cells. Credit-gated planning reduces burned imagination (consumed claims that later fail to redeem) by 62% (95% CI 43-81%) versus blind consumption, with equal success and comparable collision rates. At matched risk targets, persistent books cut verification probes from 1.00 to 0.36/episode in manipulation, at success 0.94 versus 0.98; settlement-grounded calibration retains moderate, seed-consistent operating points unlike raw instantaneous gates. The same trust layer operates across decoder-, latent-, and token-space interfaces, including V-JEPA 2-AC settled on real robot frames. On hardware, settlement remains operational under real sensing and contact noise, a deployment failure loop is re-priced online, and all 1,062 registered spends replay from the audit logs.

</details>


### [124] [Interpreting Control Latents for System Identification via Conditional Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23887)
*Dingqi Zhang,Ruiqi Zhang,Mark W. Mueller*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper converts latent-conditioned adaptive robot policies' internal latents into distributions of physical quadrotor models using conditional flow matching. The decoded model ensembles enable two downstream applications without modifying the frozen policy: online predictive tuning of high-level controller gains and robustness analysis under disturbances. Results show 23% and 45% reductions in position and heading RMSE respectively under perturbed actuator dynamics, and accurate prediction of lateral error evolution under Gaussian force disturbances.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learned latents in adaptive policies remain opaque internal representations rather than interpretable physical models. A direct latent-to-physical-parameter mapping is under-specified because multiple systems can yield similar closed-loop behavior. This limits closed-loop analysis, diagnosis, and improvement of fixed policies.

Method: The authors use conditional flow matching to decode each operational latent into a distribution of quadrotor models. This probabilistic decoding handles the under-specification problem by representing ambiguity as a distribution rather than a point estimate. The decoded model distribution is then used for (1) online predictive tuning of a high-level controller around the fixed low-level policy, and (2) robustness analysis under specified disturbances, all without modifying the policy itself.

Result: Under perturbed actuator dynamics, decoded-model predictive tuning reduced position tracking RMSE by 23% and heading RMSE by 45% relative to fixed gains. Under Gaussian force disturbances, decoded-model ensembles closely predicted lateral tracking-error evolution.

Conclusion: Control latents can be converted into physical model ensembles, enabling tuning, robustness analysis, and diagnosis of frozen adaptive policies, thereby addressing the interpretability gap in latent-conditioned adaptive control.

Abstract: Latent-conditioned adaptive policies can control robots across changing dynamics, but their learned latents remain internal representations of the policy rather than physical models that can be inspected, rolled out, or used by other control modules. This limits closed-loop analysis, diagnosis, and further improvement of a fixed policy. A direct mapping from latent to physical parameters is also under-specified, because multiple systems can induce similar closed-loop behavior. We therefore decode each operational latent into a distribution of quadrotor models using conditional flow matching. The decoded distribution enables two downstream uses without modifying the policy: online predictive tuning of a high-level controller around the fixed low-level policy, and robustness analysis under specified disturbances. Under perturbed actuator dynamics, decoded-model predictive tuning reduces position tracking RMSE by $23\%$ and heading RMSE by $45\%$ relative to fixed gains. Under Gaussian force disturbances, decoded-model ensembles closely predict the lateral tracking-error evolution. Together, these results show that control latents can be converted into physical model ensembles for tuning, robustness analysis, and diagnosis of frozen adaptive policies.

</details>


### [125] [Safety-aware Model Predictive Path Integral Control with Signal Temporal Logic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23972)
*Yiqi Zhao,Taekyung Kim,Hideki Okamoto,Bardh Hoxha,Jyotirmoy V. Deshmukh,Lars Lindemann,Georgios Fainekos*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes safety-aware-stl-mppi, a sampling-based receding-horizon planning framework that encodes discrete-time Signal Temporal Logic (STL) formulas into time-varying control barrier functions (CBFs) integrated into a model predictive path integral (MPPI) controller for efficient and safe motion planning under complex specifications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety-aware motion planning is challenging in robotics, particularly for time-critical missions with complex specifications. Existing methods struggle to balance computational efficiency with formal constraint satisfaction expressed in temporal logics.

Method: The approach encodes discrete-time STL formulas into candidate time-varying control barrier functions (CBFs), which are integrated into a model predictive path integral (MPPI) controller. The framework is efficiently parallelizable as a sampling-based planner.

Result: The method consistently achieves high safety and efficiency compared to several MPPI baselines across four artificially constructed Mars Rover planning case studies with diverse environments and cost setups, plus a quadcopter planning experiment in NVIDIA Isaac Lab.

Conclusion: The proposed safety-aware-stl-mppi framework demonstrates that combining STL-encoded CBF constraints with sampling-based MPPI planning yields a computationally efficient solution that maintains high safety and performance under complex temporal logic specifications.

Abstract: Safety-aware motion planning remains a challenge in robotics, especially when missions are time-critical and are under complex specifications. In this paper, we propose safety-aware-stl-mppi, a computationally efficient sampling-based receding-horizon planning framework designed to promote satisfaction of constraints expressed in Signal Temporal Logic (STL). Our approach encodes discrete-time STL formulas into candidate time-varying control barrier functions (CBF), which are integrated into a model predictive path integral (MPPI) controller. Our method inherits the benefits of low computational cost from an efficiently parallelizable sampling based planner and utilizes CBF for constraints expressed in STL. We compare against several MPPI baselines using four artificial Mars Rover planning case studies with a diverse environment and cost setups, where we show our method consistently achieving high safety and efficiency. We show a quadcopter planning experiment with NVIDIA Isaac Lab.

</details>


### [126] [Sensorless damage-safe grasping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23983)
*Yusei Shuto,Danilo Vasconcellos Vargas*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A deformation-bounded gripper controller for robotic fruit harvesting that uses only encoder position and motor-effort signals to estimate and limit compression strain, providing a certified, operator-interpretable damage limit across the full ripeness-driven stiffness range.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fruit stiffness varies several-fold with ripeness, making any fixed grip force either too weak (failed grasps) or too strong (bruising). Since force is not a reliable control parameter across ripeness levels, a deformation-based control strategy is needed.

Method: The controller closes the gripper until estimated compression strain reaches a user-specified limit ε, using only encoder position and motor effort. It divides an effort-derived contact force by a lower bound on object stiffness, making the stop provably conservative. The method also quantifies a contact-detection strain floor that scales linearly with closing speed.

Result: In MuJoCo simulation with realistic fruit stiffness and servo-calibrated sensor noise, the controller achieves ≥98% grasp success with 0% damage across all medium-to-firm stiffnesses for the entire certified ε range, outperforming fixed-force baselines. On 3D-printed TPU cubes, it matches baseline success at roughly half the grip force and reduces soft-object damage from 100% to 40%.

Conclusion: Deformation-bounded control with a certified ε parameter is a superior alternative to fixed-force control for variable-stiffness grasping: it provides a principled, operator-interpretable, size-scaling damage limit, exposes closing speed as a throughput–gentleness trade-off knob, and serves as a safe-action parameter for learned policies.

Abstract: Robotic fruit harvesting must hold produce securely without bruising it, yet compression stiffness varies several-fold with ripeness within a single species, so no fixed grip force spans the range. Rather than tune force, we bound deformation: a controller closes the gripper until the object's estimated compression strain reaches a user-specified limit $\varepsilon$, using only the encoder position and motor-effort signal on every servo gripper---no tactile or force-torque sensor. Dividing an effort-based contact force by a lower bound on object stiffness makes the stop provably conservative---true compression stays at or below $\varepsilon$---for any $\varepsilon$ above a contact-detection strain floor we identify and quantify: robust detection itself spends compression, linearly in closing speed, making speed an explicit throughput--gentleness knob. Unlike a hand-tuned force threshold, $\varepsilon$ is a certified, size-scaling, operator-interpretable damage limit, and a ready safe-action parameter for learned grasping policies. In MuJoCo simulation over a realistic fruit-stiffness range, under a sensor-noise model calibrated to the real servo, the controller holds $\ge 98\,\%$ grasp at $0\,\%$ damage across all medium-to-firm stiffnesses for the entire certified $\varepsilon$ range, which neither fixed-force baseline attains; on stiffness-graded 3D-printed TPU cubes it matches baseline grasp success at roughly half the grip force and cuts soft-object damage from $100\,\%$ to $40\,\%$.

</details>


### [127] [Bridging Teacher Expectations and Robot Learning via Coupling Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23994)
*Evan Dallas,Sean Dallas,Wing-Yue Geoffrey Louie*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a new scale for classifying human-robot teaching interactions based on coupling dynamics between human teacher and robot learner, applies it to a subset of the literature, and investigates how coupling dynamics and mental model mismatches affect teaching effectiveness and human perceptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human-robot teaching has shifted from offline learning to coupled teaching-learning interactions, but it is unclear how these coupling dynamics affect teaching effectiveness and human perceptions. This gap motivates the need for a systematic framework to classify and analyze such interactions.

Method: Informed by human learning theories, the authors propose a new scale for classifying human-robot teaching interactions based on coupling dynamics between the human teacher and robot learner. The scale is then applied to a subset of the human-robot teaching literature to analyze how coupling dynamics and teacher mental model mismatches with the robot's learning system impact outcomes.

Result: The application of the proposed scale to the literature reveals how coupling dynamics and mental model mismatches between human teachers and the ground-truth robot learning system affect teaching effectiveness and human perceptions of the teaching process.

Conclusion: Coupling dynamics play a critical role in human-robot teaching effectiveness and human perceptions, and the proposed scale provides a structured way to classify and understand these interactions, with mental model mismatches being a key factor.

Abstract: Human-robot teaching focuses on enabling nontechnical experts to customize robots according to their needs after deployment. With recent advances in machine learning, human-robot teaching is no longer confined to offline learning where the data gathering step from a human teacher is separated from when the robot learns. Instead, more recent approaches for human-robot teaching focus on coupling human teaching with robot learning. This coupling impacts the structure, timing, and content of the teaching and learning interaction. However, it is currently unclear how such coupling dynamics affect humanrobot teaching effectiveness and human perceptions towards the teaching process. Informed by human learning theories, in this paper we propose a new scale for classifying human-robot teaching interactions according to coupling dynamics present between the human teacher and robot learner. We apply this scale to a subset of the human-robot teaching literature to identify how coupling dynamics and human teacher mental model mismatches with the ground truth robot learning system affect teaching effectiveness and human perceptions towards the teaching process

</details>


### [128] [NeurRAFT: Robot Motion Planning via Anchor-Level Flow Matching with Clearance-Aware Preference Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24026)
*Sibo Tian,Chang Liu,Minghui Zheng,Xiao Liang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: NeurRAFT is a generative neural motion planner that uses anchor-level flow matching with a Jacobian-weighted loss to generate compact waypoints, then applies Direct Preference Optimization to reshape the trajectory distribution toward larger obstacle clearance, achieving state-of-the-art performance and zero-shot real-world transfer to a Franka robot.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: End-to-end neural motion planners avoid privileged geometric models but struggle with collision-free planning in cluttered environments. Prior dense waypoint-sequence models waste capacity on redundant local details, and imitation learning from positive demonstrations cannot distinguish collision-free from near-collision trajectories, allowing collision-prone behaviors to persist.

Method: NeurRAFT operates on compact anchor waypoints using a Jacobian-weighted training loss that accounts for task-space impact per anchor. Anchors are generated in two integration steps via flow matching, with cubic-spline interpolation for full-resolution trajectories. Direct Preference Optimization reshapes the pretrained planner's distribution toward trajectories with larger obstacle clearance, directly absorbing improvements into planner parameters without extra inference cost.

Result: The method shows substantial improvements over state-of-the-art neural planners in experiments, and real-world experiments demonstrate zero-shot transfer to a Franka robot under noisy and partially occluded depth observations.

Conclusion: Anchor-level flow matching combined with clearance-aware preference tuning effectively addresses collision-free planning in cluttered environments, eliminating the need for post-hoc corrections while enabling robust real-world deployment.

Abstract: Recent end-to-end neural motion planners generate trajectories from raw sensor observations, avoiding the privileged geometric models required by classical planners. However, collision-free planning in cluttered environments remains challenging. We present NeurRAFT, a generative planning framework based on anchor-level flow matching and clearance-aware preference tuning. Unlike prior neural planners that model dense waypoint sequences and spend capacity on redundant local details and smoothness, NeurRAFT operates on compact anchor waypoints. We train the planner using a Jacobian-weighted loss that accounts for the task-space impact of each anchor. At inference, the anchors are generated in two integration steps, followed by cubic-spline interpolation to recover a smooth, full-resolution trajectory. Since imitation learning from positive demonstrations cannot distinguish collision-free from near-collision trajectories, collision-prone behaviors persist at test time. Rather than relying on post-hoc corrections, we directly reshape the pretrained planner's distribution toward safer solutions without augmenting inference. Specifically, Direct Preference Optimization shifts probability mass toward trajectories with larger obstacle clearance, with the resulting improvement directly absorbed into the planner parameters. Experiments show substantial improvements over state-of-the-art planners, while real-world experiments demonstrate zero-shot transfer to a Franka robot under noisy and partially occluded depth observations. Video results available at https://neurraft.github.io/.

</details>


### [129] [Design-to-Plan: A Large Language Model-Based Multi-Agent Framework for Manufacturing Process Planning from 3D CAD Models and 2D Engineering Drawings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24039)
*Muhammad Tayyab Khan,Lequn Chen,Wenhe Feng,Seung Ki Moon*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Presents Design-to-Plan, an LLM-based multi-agent framework for end-to-end manufacturing process planning that coordinates specialized agents for 3D feature recognition, 2D drawing analysis, context fusion, knowledge retrieval, process sequencing, tool selection, and report generation. Achieves 100% success across downstream agents, 95.9%-97.6% Tool F1, 90% source detection accuracy, and 60%-68% token reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing manufacturing process planning approaches focus on isolated subtasks (feature recognition, drawing interpretation, tool selection) and cannot support the full reasoning chain from design artifacts to complete process plans, particularly when integrating 3D CAD models, 2D drawings, materials, and domain-specific rules.

Method: A hybrid multi-agent framework where an orchestrator coordinates specialized LLM agents (3D feature recognition, 2D drawing analysis, 2D-3D context fusion, knowledge retrieval, process sequencing, tool selection, report generation). LLMs act as reasoning agents interacting with deterministic modules and knowledge sources, with deterministic modules extracting structured information from CAD/drawings while LLM agents perform context-aware reasoning, rule retrieval, conflict resolution, and planning output generation.

Result: Evaluated on 300 benchmark cases: 100% success across three downstream ReAct-enabled agents, Tool F1 scores of 95.9%-97.6%, 90% source detection accuracy in conflict analysis, and a 60%-68% reduction in token usage for key planning tasks.

Conclusion: Structured LLM-based multi-agent coordination can effectively bridge design representations and manufacturing knowledge, enabling scalable, efficient, and traceable design-to-plan automation.

Abstract: Manufacturing process planning transforms heterogeneous design information into coherent manufacturing decisions. However, existing approaches focus on isolated subtasks, such as feature recognition, drawing interpretation, or tool selection, and struggle to support the full reasoning chain from design artifacts to process plans. This is critical when planning must interpret 3D CAD models, 2D engineering drawings, materials, and domain-specific rules. To address this gap, this paper presents Design-to-Plan, a large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent framework for end-to-end manufacturing process planning. An orchestrator coordinates specialized agents for 3D feature recognition, 2D drawing analysis, 2D-3D context fusion, knowledge retrieval, process sequencing, tool selection, and report generation. Rather than using LLMs as standalone text generators, the framework deploys them as reasoning agents that interact with deterministic modules and knowledge sources to produce consistent and traceable decisions. In this hybrid design, deterministic modules and specialized agents extract structured information from CAD and drawing inputs, while LLM agents perform context-aware reasoning, retrieve manufacturing rules, resolve conflicts, and generate planning outputs. The framework is evaluated using 300 benchmark cases across three downstream ReAct-enabled agents, plus separate evaluations of CAD feature recognition, drawing analysis, and 2D-3D context fusion. The parallel architecture achieves 100% success across downstream agents, Tool F1 scores of 95.9%-97.6%, 90% source detection accuracy in conflict analysis, and a 60%-68% reduction in token usage for key planning tasks. Results show that structured LLM-based multi-agent coordination can bridge design representations and manufacturing knowledge, enabling scalable, efficient, and traceable design-to-plan automation.

</details>


### [130] [Hierarchical Skill Retrieval for Data-Efficient Adaptation of Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24042)
*Haoran Hao,Shahram Najam Syed,Jeff Schneider,Jeffrey Ichnowski*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes Hierarchical Skill Retrieval (HSR), a retrieval framework for data-efficient Vision-Language-Action (VLA) model adaptation. HSR decomposes target tasks into candidate skill sequences, selects the best decomposition via semantic plausibility and skill reliability, performs hybrid retrieval combining subtask-level language matching with behavior-feature reranking, and adapts policies through a two-stage pretraining-finetuning pipeline. Experiments on LIBERO and real-world tasks show 10.3% and 21.3% average success rate improvements over the strongest baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models pretrained on large-scale robot data degrade when adapted to new tasks with limited task-specific demonstrations. Existing retrieval methods rely on visual similarity, state-action representations, or task-level language matching, which overlook the hierarchical structure of long-horizon manipulation tasks where complete task matches are rare but reusable skills are abundant.

Method: HSR operates in three stages: (1) decomposing target tasks into candidate skill sequences and evaluating each plan via semantic plausibility and skill reliability estimated from the prior dataset; (2) hybrid retrieval combining subtask-level language retrieval with behavior-feature reranking to select demonstrations that are semantically relevant and behaviorally compatible; (3) a two-stage pretraining and finetuning pipeline separating general skill acquisition from task-specific adaptation.

Result: HSR improves average success rates by 10.3% on the LIBERO benchmark and 21.3% on real-world robot manipulation tasks over the strongest baseline, demonstrating the effectiveness of structured skill-level retrieval.

Conclusion: Structured skill-level retrieval is effective for data-efficient VLA adaptation, showing that exploiting hierarchical task structure with hybrid retrieval significantly outperforms existing retrieval approaches that ignore reusable sub-skills in long-horizon manipulation.

Abstract: While Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models pretrained on large-scale robot datasets provide a strong foundation for robot manipulation, their performance can degrade when adapted to new tasks with limited task-specific demonstrations. Retrieval offers a practical way to reuse existing demonstrations for data-efficient adaptation, but existing methods often rely on visual similarity, state-action representations, or task-level language matching. These approaches may overlook the hierarchical structure of long-horizon manipulation tasks, where complete task matches are rare but reusable skills are often abundant. To address this challenge, we propose Hierarchical Skill Retrieval (HSR), a retrieval framework for data-efficient VLA adaptation. Specifically, HSR first decomposes a target task into candidate skill sequences. It evaluates each plan based on both semantic plausibility and skill reliability estimated from the prior dataset. The selected decomposition is then used for hybrid retrieval. This combines subtask-level language retrieval with behavior-feature reranking to identify demonstrations that are both semantically relevant and compatible with the target task. Finally, we adapt the policy through a two-stage pretraining and finetuning pipeline, which separates general skill acquisition from task-specific adaptation. Experiments on the LIBERO benchmark and several real-world robot manipulation tasks show that HSR improves the average success rate by 10.3% and 21.3% over the strongest baseline, respectively. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of structured skill-level retrieval for data-efficient VLA adaptation. Videos and code are available at https://hoar012.github.io/HSR-Project.

</details>


### [131] [SIREN-Bench: Behavior-Driven Generation and Evaluation of Emergency-Vehicle Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24094)
*Yicheng Zhu,Tianmu Zhao,Haoxin Leng,Fan Zuo,Tao Li,Zilin Bian*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SIREN is a behavior-driven SUMO-CARLA co-simulation platform for generating emergency vehicle (EMV)-civilian traffic interactions, instantiated as SIREN-Bench-v1 with seven parameterized interaction templates across emergency levels L1-L3 and three behavior families, providing synchronized sensor observations and simulator-native annotations for benchmarking detection, trajectory prediction, and vision-language risk understanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating safety-critical EMV-civilian interactions requires behavior-level control over both EMV privileges and civilian responses, together with consistent sensing and ground truth, but existing datasets and simulation benchmarks do not provide this combination.

Method: SIREN couples SUMO's network-level traffic evolution and behavior logic with CARLA's continuous vehicle control and synchronized onboard sensing. Behavior-dependent control splits between SUMO, CARLA, or jointly. The platform is instantiated as SIREN-Bench-v1 with seven parameterized interaction templates across three emergency levels (L1-L3) and three behavior families.
