Result: Evaluations of nine trajectory predictors, four LiDAR-based detectors, and five vision-language models reveal behavior-dependent failure modes: traffic-clearance interactions hardest for detection, privileged intersection traversal hardest for prediction, no learned predictor beats constant-velocity baseline on average, and vision-language models perform better on normal traffic than near-miss/collision events.

Conclusion: Behavior-centered benchmarking reveals critical failure modes in existing models and establishes SIREN as an extensible data-generation and evaluation platform for autonomous-driving and transportation safety research.

Abstract: Emergency vehicles (EMVs) can reorganize surrounding traffic as civilian vehicles brake, change lanes, or form rescue corridors in response to their passage. Evaluating these safety-critical interactions requires behavior-level control over both EMV privileges and civilian responses, together with consistent sensing and ground truth. Existing datasets and simulation benchmarks do not directly provide this combination. We present \textbf{SIREN}, a behavior-driven SUMO--CARLA co-simulation platform for generating EMV--civilian interactions. SIREN couples SUMO's network-level traffic evolution and behavior logic with CARLA's continuous vehicle control and synchronized onboard sensing; depending on the active behavior, the interaction is controlled by SUMO, CARLA, or jointly. We instantiate the platform as \textbf{SIREN-Bench-v1}, comprising seven parameterized interaction templates across emergency levels L1--L3 and three behavior families, with synchronized sensor observations and simulator-native annotations. We demonstrate the benchmark through three representative tasks: 3D object detection, trajectory prediction, and vision-language risk understanding. Evaluations of nine trajectory predictors, four LiDAR-based detectors, and five vision-language models reveal behavior-dependent failure modes. Traffic-clearance interactions are hardest for detection, privileged intersection traversal is hardest for prediction, and no learned predictor outperforms the constant-velocity reference on average. Vision-language models perform substantially better on normal traffic than on near-miss and collision events. These results demonstrate the value of behavior-centered benchmarking and establish SIREN as an extensible data-generation and evaluation platform for autonomous-driving and transportation safety research.

</details>


### [132] [TrAct: Bridging Robot Control and Visual Prediction with Visual Tracks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24101)
*Zhi Cao,Howard Ji,Kevin Zhang,Kuangzhi Ge,Li Fei-Fei,Jiajun Wu,Huang Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TrAct is a world-model-based robot decision-making framework that uses visual tracks as an intermediate interface between control and prediction, improving success rates from 27% to 55% in simulation and 49% to 76% on real-world tasks compared to a strong VLA baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robot actions are embodiment-specific and weakly aligned with image-space visual changes, making them ineffective as conditioning signals for robot world models. Visual tracks offer an embodiment-agnostic representation of how task-relevant points move through a scene, providing dense image-space guidance for accurate future video prediction.

Method: TrAct comprises three components: (1) VLAT, a Vision-Language-Action-and-Track model that jointly predicts candidate actions and corresponding visual tracks from observations and language instructions; (2) TWM, a track-conditioned world model that predicts future visual outcomes conditioned on proposed tracks; and (3) VLAC, a vision-language reward model that scores predicted outcomes. At inference, VLAT generates candidate action-track pairs, TWM rolls out visual consequences, and VLAC selects the best track; the paired action is executed.

Result: On the LIBERO-INTEGRAL benchmark and real-world Franka manipulation tasks, TrAct improves success rates from 27% to 55% in simulation and from 49% to 76% on real-world tasks versus the π0.5 VLA baseline. TWM also consistently improves video prediction quality over action-conditioned world models.

Conclusion: Visual tracks serve as an effective shared interface between robot control and visual prediction, enabling more accurate world modeling and stronger robot generalization.

Abstract: Robot actions are inherently embodiment-specific and only weakly aligned with image-space visual changes, limiting their effectiveness as conditioning signals for robot world models. In contrast, visual tracks provide an embodiment-agnostic representation of how task-relevant points move through a scene, offering dense image-space guidance for accurate and spatially precise future video prediction. Building on this observation, we propose TrAct, a world-model-based robot decision-making framework that uses visual tracks as an intermediate interface between control and prediction. TrAct consists of three components: a Vision-Language-Action-and-Track model (VLAT) that jointly predicts candidate actions and corresponding visual tracks from the current observation and language instruction; a track-conditioned world model (TWM) that predicts future visual outcomes conditioned on the proposed tracks; and a vision-language reward model (VLAC) that scores the predicted outcomes. At inference time, VLAT generates candidate action-track pairs, TWM rolls out their visual consequences, and VLAC selects the track whose predicted outcome best satisfies the instruction; the action paired with the selected track is then executed by the robot. Experiments on the proposed LIBERO-INTEGRAL benchmark and real-world Franka manipulation show that TrAct improves success rates from 27% to 55% in simulation and from 49% to 76% on real-world tasks compared with the strong VLA baseline $π_{0.5}$. Furthermore, TWM consistently improves video prediction quality over the action-conditioned world model (AWM). These results demonstrate that visual tracks provide an effective shared interface between robot control and visual prediction, enabling more accurate world modeling and stronger robot generalization.

</details>


### [133] [Trajectory-Level Continuous Action Representation for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24111)
*Tong Yang,Jingkai Jia,Yuecheng Xu,Xueyao Chen,Chi Zhang,Wenqiang Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CAT is a trajectory-level continuous action representation framework for robotic manipulation that encodes action trajectories within a fixed real-time interval into continuous latent tokens, using frequency-aware positional encoding for temporal consistency across control frequencies and trajectory-level regularization for stability. It outperforms VQ-based and continuous visuomotor baselines on LIBERO, MimicGen, and real-world long-horizon tasks across various model backbones and control frequencies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing visuomotor systems entangle action representation with control frequency or rely on fixed temporal parameterizations, leading to representational redundancy at high sampling rates and limiting the modeling of critical motion.

Method: CAT encodes action trajectories within a fixed real-time interval into a set of continuous latent tokens. It incorporates a frequency-aware positional encoding to establish a shared temporal coordinate system for temporal consistency across varying control frequencies, and applies trajectory-level regularization to stabilize the latent representation. This avoids representation growth with timestep density and eliminates reliance on predefined temporal parameterizations.

Result: Extensive system-level evaluations on LIBERO, MimicGen, and real-world long-horizon manipulation tasks show CAT-based policies consistently outperform competitive VQ-based and continuous visuomotor baselines under matched training settings. Across various model backbones and control frequencies, CAT consistently improves success rates.

Conclusion: Trajectory-level continuous action modeling offers significant advantages for scalable robotic manipulation across varying control rates.

Abstract: We propose CAT, a trajectory-level continuous action representation framework for robotic manipulation. Existing visuomotor systems often entangle action representation with control frequency or rely on fixed temporal parameterizations. This leads to representational redundancy at high sampling rates and limits the modeling of critical motion. CAT instead encodes action trajectories within a fixed real-time interval into a set of continuous latent tokens. To ensure temporal consistency across varying control frequencies, we further incorporate a frequency-aware positional encoding that establishs a shared temporal coordinate system. Trajectory-level regularization further stabilizes the latent representation. This approach prevents representation growth with timestep density and avoids reliance on predefined temporal parameterizations. Extensive system-level evaluations on LIBERO, MimicGen, and real-world long-horizon manipulation tasks demonstrate that CAT-based policies consistently outperform both competitive VQ-based and continuous visuomotor baselines under matched training settings. Across various model backbones and control frequencies, CAT consistently improves success rates. These results highlight the advantages of trajectory-level continuous action modeling for scalable robotic manipulation across varying control rates.

</details>


### [134] [PonderPounce: A Pretrained MLLM as an Episode Context Engine for Robot Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24115)
*Suhwan Choi,Jaeyoon Jung,Sungkyung Kim,Yunsung Lee,Youngjae Yu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PonderPounce reuses an MLLM's native causal context as robot memory, combining a System2 MLLM (Ponder) that accumulates episode cognition in its context with a System1 VLA (Pounce) that asynchronously receives only the newest continuous cognition token. Jointly trained end-to-end without purpose-built memory modules, it achieves competitive results on RoboMME and RoboCasa-DC benchmarks with low-latency serving.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models generally inherit pretrained representations from MLLMs but do not use their contextual capacity as episode memory. Memory-dependent policies address this via purpose-built history mechanisms, which add architectural complexity. The authors propose reusing an MLLM's native causal context as robot memory instead.

Method: Two-component architecture: (1) Ponder, a System2 MLLM that accumulates episode observations, demonstrations, and prior cognition in its native causal context, generating subgoal text and demonstration reasoning for internal use. (2) Pounce, a System1 VLA receiving current observation, instruction, and proprioception directly, asynchronously receiving only the newest continuous cognition token and its age through the Ponder-Pounce interface. Both are trained end-to-end with optimized serving for low latency.

Result: On RoboMME with base-scale training, PonderPounce achieves 60.83% with 9B and 50.04% with 0.8B under the same Pounce architecture, versus 44.51% for FrameSamp+Modul and 17.93% for current-observation π0.5. With 9x data it reaches 75.54% vs 57.88% for FrameSamp+Modul. On RoboCasa-DC it reaches 12.5% vs 11.6% for the strongest published baseline, dropping to 8.6% when cognition is replaced by a null state. Serving achieves p50 latencies of 78ms for cognition refresh and 25ms for action invocation, supporting 20Hz action playback.

Conclusion: PonderPounce demonstrates that an MLLM's native causal context can serve effectively as robot memory without purpose-built memory modules, achieving state-of-the-art results on benchmarks while maintaining low-latency serving suitable for real-time control. The ablation on RoboCasa-DC confirms that the cognition signal is essential to performance.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can integrate long visual histories, reason under partial observability, and infer behavior from a few examples. Yet vision-language-action (VLA) models generally inherit pretrained representations without using this contextual capacity as episode memory. Memory-dependent policies address this gap through purpose-built history mechanisms. PonderPounce instead reuses an MLLM's native causal context as robot memory. Ponder, a System2 MLLM, accumulates episode observations, demonstrations, and prior cognition in its native causal context and can generate subgoal text and demonstration reasoning for internal use. Pounce, a System1 VLA, receives the current observation, instruction, and proprioception directly; through the Ponder--Pounce interface, it asynchronously receives only the newest continuous cognition token and its age. Both are jointly trained end to end without a purpose-built memory module or separate bridge pretraining. Optimized serving achieves p50 latencies of 78ms for cognition refresh and 25ms for action-model invocation, supporting 20Hz action playback. On RoboMME with base-scale training data, PonderPounce reaches 60.83% with 9B and 50.04% with 0.8B under the same Pounce architecture and interface, versus 44.51% for FrameSamp+Modul and 17.93% for the current-observation π_{0.5}. With 9x data, it reaches 75.54% versus 57.88% for FrameSamp+Modul. On RoboCasa-DC, the same interface learns from action supervision alone and reaches 12.5% versus 11.6% for the strongest published demonstration-conditioned baseline, falling to 8.6% when cognition is replaced by a learned null state.

</details>


### [135] [Coverage Planning for Robotic Tooth Preparation in Densely Constrained Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24155)
*Yunwen Li,Chen Chen,Xiangjie Yan,Chang Shu,Jianxia Hou,Shiji Song,Xiang Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a robotic system for autonomous full-crown tooth preparation, featuring an anatomy-aware toolpath planning algorithm and a clearance-oriented end-effector yaw assignment strategy. The system achieves an average geometric deviation of 0.117 mm (RMSE) in milling irregular tooth surfaces, validated through simulations and phantom-head experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tooth preparation is a core restorative dentistry procedure that is challenging to automate because the dental bur must operate in a densely constrained intraoral workspace where sub-millimeter deviations can compromise outcomes or damage adjacent structures. Current approaches lack the precision and safety required for autonomous operation.

Method: The proposed framework consists of two key components: 1) an anatomy-aware toolpath planning algorithm that conforms precisely to a technician-designed preparation model while protecting adjacent teeth, and 2) a clearance-oriented end-effector yaw assignment strategy that enables intraoral access while reducing soft-tissue interference risk.

Result: The robotic system accurately mills irregular tooth surfaces with an average geometric deviation of 0.117 mm (RMSE), achieving restoration quality and clinical safety. Feasibility and effectiveness were validated through simulations and phantom-head experiments.

Conclusion: The proposed robotic system successfully demonstrates autonomous full-crown tooth preparation with high precision and clinical safety, providing a feasible solution for automating this challenging restorative dentistry procedure.

Abstract: Tooth preparation refers to the controlled removal of tooth structure to create an optimal substrate for fixed restorations and is a core procedure in restorative dentistry. Automating this task is particularly challenging for robots because the dental bur must operate within a densely constrained intraoral workspace, where even sub-millimeter deviations can compromise outcomes or damage adjacent structures. This paper presents a novel robotic system for autonomous full-crown tooth preparation. The proposed framework includes: 1) an anatomy-aware toolpath planning algorithm that conforms precisely to a technician-designed preparation model while protecting adjacent teeth, and 2) a clearance-oriented end-effector yaw assignment strategy that allows intraoral access while reducing the risk of soft-tissue interference. Together, these features enable the robot to accurately mill the irregular tooth surface with an average geometric deviation of 0.117 mm (RMSE), achieving both restoration quality and clinical safety. A series of simulations and phantom-head experiments validate the system's feasibility and effectiveness.

</details>


### [136] [Robust Slip Detection and Material Classification via Spatiotemporal Transformers on a Uniformly-Illuminated Visuo-Tactile Sensor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24162)
*Ziyang Ma,Yuhao Sun,Zichen Ai,Xiangyang Ji,Bin Fang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a visuo-tactile sensor with uniform RGB illumination and sub-millimeter depth reconstruction, alongside a unified perception framework featuring a dual-head TimeSformer for slip detection and direction classification, achieving 95.5% (3-class contact state), 91.5% (8-class slip direction), and 98.8% (object recognition) accuracies on unseen objects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing slip detection datasets are predominantly limited to binary classification and lack fine-grained directional perception, which is essential for complex robotic manipulation tasks.

Method: The work combines a hardware-level visuo-tactile sensor with customized uniform RGB illumination enabling high-precision sub-millimeter depth reconstruction, a multi-task visuo-tactile dataset of 15 objects with synchronized depth information, and a dual-head TimeSformer network for dynamic spatiotemporal slip processing, plus a ResNet-50 backbone for static object class recognition.

Result: The dual-head TimeSformer achieves 95.5% accuracy for 3-class contact state prediction and 91.5% for fine-grained 8-class slip direction classification on unseen objects. ResNet-50-based static tactile object recognition reaches 98.8% accuracy across 15 categories.

Conclusion: The proposed hardware-software framework provides high-fidelity tactile feedback and a powerful multi-modal perception baseline for complex robotic manipulation, advancing beyond binary slip detection to fine-grained directional perception.

Abstract: Tactile sensing is central to robotic manipulation, among which slip detection stands out as a quintessential and critical task. However, existing slip datasets are predominantly limited to binary classification, lacking fine-grained directional perception. To address this limitation, we propose a visuo-tactile sensor featuring customized uniform RGB illumination, alongside a unified perception framework. At the hardware level, the sensor achieves high-precision, sub-millimeter depth reconstruction. Based on this capability, we collect a multi-task visuo-tactile dataset encompassing 15 objects, synchronously generating depth information for each data sample. Algorithmically, we design a dual-head TimeSformer network to process dynamic spatiotemporal slip. On unseen objects, this network achieves robust accuracies of 95.5% and 91.5% for 3-class contact state prediction and fine-grained 8-class slip direction classification, respectively. Furthermore, static tactile-based object class recognition utilizing a ResNet-50 backbone yields an outstanding accuracy of 98.8% across 15 categories. The proposed hardware-software framework provides high-fidelity feedback and a powerful multi-modal perception baseline for complex robotic manipulation.

</details>


### [137] [NVIDIA Cosmos-H-Dreams: Real-Time Generative Physics Simulation for Surgical Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24199)
*Javier Gamazo Tejero,Lukas Zbinden,Keyur Sheth,Raghavendra K M,Nadim Daher,Diego Granero Maraña,Filip Binkiewicz,Patrick Thornycroft,Mahdi Azizian,Sean D. Huver*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Cosmos-H-Dreams is the first interactive real-time surgical world-model system, combining an action-conditioned generative model with teacher-to-student distillation to achieve ~160 inference FPS on a single workstation GPU. It is controller-agnostic, supporting live control via browser keyboard, VR headsets, commercial surgical robot consoles, and learned policies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Physical-robot surgical experiments are time-consuming, costly, and hard to reproduce, while classical simulators fail to capture photorealistic visuals and deformable-tissue dynamics. There is a critical lack of real-time interactive generative simulation for surgical robotics.

Method: The system builds on Cosmos-H-Surgical-Simulator, a multi-embodiment action-conditioned surgical video world model fine-tuned on the Open-H-Embodiment corpus. The authors post-train this checkpoint on procedure-specific data, then distill a bidirectional teacher into a causal few-step student using Self Forcing, enabling real-time streaming via the NVIDIA FlashDreams library.

Result: Cosmos-H-Dreams streams at approximately 160 inference FPS on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell workstation GPU. It supports live control through multiple interfaces: browser keyboard over WebRTC, Meta Quest over WebXR, a commercial surgical robot console (CMR Surgical's Versius), and closed-loop learned policies.

Conclusion: This is the first interactive surgical world model supporting live human and policy control. Released as an open system, it provides a foundation for surgical education, scalable synthetic data generation, and future intraoperative decision support.

Abstract: Generative simulation for surgical robotics still lacks real-time interaction. Physical-robot experiments, often involving animal or cadaver labs, are time-consuming, costly, and difficult to reproduce, while classical simulators struggle to capture photorealistic appearance and deformable-tissue dynamics. We address this gap with Cosmos-H-Dreams, an integrated real-time surgical world-model system combining an action-conditioned generative model, a teacher-to-student distillation recipe, and a deployment stack built on the NVIDIA FlashDreams streaming-inference library. Starting from Cosmos-H-Surgical-Simulator, a multi-embodiment action-conditioned surgical video world model fine-tuned on the large-scale Open-H-Embodiment corpus, we post-train this checkpoint on embodiment- and procedure-specific data. By distilling the resulting bidirectional teacher into a causal, few-step student with Self Forcing, we turn a passive video generator into a controllable surgical simulator that streams at $\sim$160 inference FPS on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell workstation GPU. Crucially, Cosmos-H-Dreams is controller-agnostic: any interface that emits a stream of robot kinematics can drive it. We demonstrate live control through a browser keyboard over WebRTC, a Meta Quest headset over WebXR, a commercial surgical robot console such as CMR Surgical's Versius, and learned policies operating in closed loop. To our knowledge, this is the first interactive surgical world model supporting live human and policy control. Human operators and policies alike can act inside the synthesized world and observe the consequences in real time. We release Cosmos-H-Dreams as an open surgical simulation system, providing a common foundation for surgical education, scalable synthetic data generation, and future intraoperative decision support.

</details>


### [138] [CARO: Contact-Agnostic Residual Observation for Zero-Shot Robust Quadruped Locomotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24217)
*Zihan Yang,Shixuan Han,Kexin Guo,Xiang Yu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CARO is a contact-agnostic residual observation framework for policy adaptation that embeds a fixed-base Euler-Lagrange model into the RL control loop, using a disturbance observer to extract dynamics-mismatch signals as residual observations, achieving improved zero-shot robustness without torque sensors, visual measurements, or specialized training curricula.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable policy adaptation to out-of-distribution dynamics without requiring torque sensors, explicit contact estimation, or vision-based floating-base measurements, and without needing specialized disturbance curricula or additional adaptation supervision during training.

Method: CARO embeds a fixed-base Euler-Lagrange model into the reinforcement learning loop and constructs a torque-level residual observation via a disturbance observer that extracts a structured signal representing dynamics mismatch. The policy learns to exploit this residual feedback for online adaptation. Training is conducted under the same terrain, command, and domain-randomization conditions as the nominal policy.

Result: CARO achieves substantially improved zero-shot robustness in simulation and sim-to-real transfer tasks involving out-of-distribution payloads, center-of-mass shifts, terrain geometries, abrupt dynamics changes, and elevated-platform landings.

Conclusion: CARO demonstrates that a contact-agnostic residual observation framework with a disturbance observer can provide effective online adaptation without the typical sensing requirements or specialized training setups, substantially improving zero-shot robustness across diverse out-of-distribution scenarios.

Abstract: We propose CARO, a contact-agnostic residual observation framework for policy adaptation. CARO embeds a fixed-base Euler--Lagrange model into the reinforcement learning control loop and constructs a torque-level residual observation without requiring torque sensors, explicit contact estimation, or vision-based measurements of the floating-base position and linear velocity. A disturbance observer extracts a structured signal representing dynamics mismatch, while the policy learns to exploit this feedback for online adaptation. CARO is trained under the same terrain, command, and domain-randomization conditions as the nominal policy, without specialized disturbance curricula or additional adaptation supervision. Nevertheless, it achieves substantially improved zero-shot robustness in simulation and sim-to-real transfer tasks involving out-of-distribution payloads, center-of-mass shifts, terrain geometries, abrupt dynamics changes, and elevated-platform landings.

</details>


### [139] [A Durable Vision-Based Tactile Fingertip for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24242)
*F. Richard Cottrell,Megha H. Tippur,Edward H. Adelson*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a durable vision-based tactile fingertip sensor that combines a soft silicone gel with a nonpigmented thermoplastic-polyurethane protective film and a replaceable sensing cartridge. Accelerated durability testing showed the developed sensor survived 2-3 hours of sanding and 5-8 days of repetitive 39.2 N probe loading, while commercial GelSight Mini and DIGIT sensors ruptured after seconds to minutes. The durability improvement exceeds two orders of magnitude, with gradual degradation and replaceable cartridges enabling practical maintenance for demanding robotic applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Commercial vision-based tactile sensors provide rich contact information but suffer from vulnerability to abrasion and concentrated repeated loading, which limits their use in demanding robotic applications where durability is critical.

Method: The sensor comprises a soft silicone gel with a nonpigmented, textured, thin thermoplastic-polyurethane protective film and a replaceable sensing cartridge. Durability was evaluated using two accelerated laboratory procedures: a rotating-drum sanding test and a repetitive probe test applying 39.2 N at 45 cycles per minute, and compared against commercial GelSight Mini and DIGIT sensors.

Result: The developed sensor reached protective-film rupture after approximately 2-3 hours of sanding. During repetitive probe testing, all nine sensors remained functionally usable when testing was discontinued: seven after 5 days, one after 6 days, and one after 8 days. Commercial sensors ruptured after 24-30 seconds of sanding and 25-35 minutes of repetitive loading. Damage to the developed sensor progressed gradually with little interference with tactile imaging. Durability improvements of more than two orders of magnitude were established.

Conclusion: Combining increased durability, gradual degradation, and rapid cartridge replacement offers a practical and maintainable approach to vision-based tactile sensing for demanding robotic applications.

Abstract: Currently available commercial vision-based tactile sensors provide rich contact information but remain vulnerable to abrasion and repeated concentrated loading, limiting their use in demanding robotic applications. This work presents a durable tactile fingertip comprising a soft silicone gel with a nonpigmented, textured, thin thermoplastic-polyurethane protective film and a replaceable sensing cartridge. Durability was evaluated using two accelerated laboratory procedures: a rotating-drum sanding test and a repetitive probe test applying 39.2 N (4.0 kgf) at 45 cycles per minute. Under the defined sanding conditions, the developed sensor reached the protective-film rupture endpoint after approximately 2-3 hours. During repetitive probe testing, all nine developed sensors remained functionally usable when testing was discontinued: seven after 5 days, one after 6 days, and one after 8 days. Commercial GelSight Mini and DIGIT specimens exhibited initial surface-film rupture after approximately 24-30 seconds of sanding and 25-35 minutes of repetitive loading. Damage to the developed sensor progressed gradually and produced little interference with tactile imaging at the test endpoints. These observations establish durability improvements of more than two orders of magnitude under the defined accelerated conditions. Combining increased durability, gradual degradation, and rapid cartridge replacement offers a practical approach to maintainable vision-based tactile sensing for demanding robotic applications.

</details>


### [140] [NeuralParker: A Reinforcement Learning Planner for Irregular Parking Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24485)
*Zihan Wang,Bai Huang,Yang Guan,Xiao Li,Haoyu Xu,Naizheng Wang,Shengbo Eben Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: NeuralParker is a reinforcement learning-based hybrid planner for arbitrary-pose parking in irregular bounded environments. It uses a target-relative vertex representation of full-environment geometry for long-range route reasoning, couples a learned curvature-length arc policy with an in-loop terminal ensemble of cubic Hermite connections, and achieves higher planning success and trajectory quality than baselines, validated in simulation and on a real delivery vehicle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing learning-based parking planners rely on local observations and assume marked slots with short approach maneuvers, which restricts long-range route reasoning when delivery/service vehicles must reach an operator-specified pose from a distant start in irregular bounded environments.

Method: NeuralParker is a hybrid RL-based planner. It encodes full-environment obstacle and boundary geometry in a target-relative vertex representation to retain route-defining context, and couples a learned curvature-length arc policy with an in-loop terminal ensemble that selects among diverse cubic Hermite connections using a curvature-regularized cost.

Result: On factorial and long-range route-choice benchmarks, NeuralParker outperforms evaluated baselines in planning success and overall trajectory quality. Ablations confirm benefits of the target-relative global representation and terminal ensemble. A real-vehicle evaluation confirms effective transfer to real delivery-vehicle perception at a working parking site with low computational cost.

Conclusion: NeuralParker demonstrates that combining a target-relative global environment representation with a learned arc policy and terminal pose refinement enables robust arbitrary-pose parking in irregular environments, and transfers effectively to real vehicles at low computational cost.

Abstract: Automated parking commonly assumes marked slots and short approach maneuvers. Delivery and service vehicles, however, may need to reach an operator-specified pose in an irregular bounded environment from a distant start. Existing learning-based parking planners often rely on local observations, which can restrict long-range route reasoning. To address this problem, we present NeuralParker, a reinforcement learning-based hybrid planner for arbitrary-pose parking. NeuralParker encodes full-environment obstacle and boundary geometry in a target-relative vertex representation, allowing the policy to retain route-defining context throughout the approach. It further couples a learned curvature--length arc policy with an in-loop terminal ensemble that selects from diverse cubic Hermite connections using a curvature-regularized cost. We also establish factorial and long-range route-choice benchmarks to evaluate planning success and trajectory quality. Experiments on these benchmarks show that NeuralParker achieves higher planning success and better overall trajectory quality than the evaluated baselines, while ablation studies support the benefits of the target-relative global representation and terminal ensemble. Finally, a real-vehicle evaluation confirms that the planner transfers effectively to real delivery-vehicle perception at a working parking site, planning successfully at low computational cost.

</details>


### [141] [RoG-DAgger: Rollout-Guided Post-Training for End-to-End Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24525)
*Liangyu Zhong,Joachim Sicking,Fabian Hueger,Hanno Gottschalk*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RoG-DAgger is a post-training framework that uses short-horizon kinematic rollouts to generate high-quality expert demonstrations in safety-critical driving states, addressing the training-inference mismatch in end-to-end driving systems. It expands the expert's solution space, times takeover near the point of no return, and aligns expert-student field of view, improving driving scores and success rates across in-distribution and out-of-distribution benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: End-to-end driving systems trained via open-loop imitation learning on fixed expert data suffer from training-inference mismatch, leaving them vulnerable to safety-critical failures in policy-induced states. Existing DAgger-style driving pipelines are limited by a constrained expert solution space, poorly timed takeovers, and privileged information unavailable to the student.

Method: RoG-DAgger constructs expert demonstrations in safety-critical states using short-horizon kinematic rollouts. It (i) expands the expert's trajectory-and-speed solution space by evaluating candidate plans through rollout, (ii) produces preventive supervision based on rollout outcomes, (iii) times takeover using rollout solvability to estimate the point of no return, and (iv) aligns the expert's field of view with the student's to provide compatible supervision.

Result: RoG-DAgger improves SimLingo by 5.3 driving-score points and 6.2 percentage points in success rate on Bench2Drive, doubles driving score from 22 to 44 on Longest6 v2, and improves out-of-distribution success rate from 55% to 66% on Fail2Drive.

Conclusion: Short-horizon kinematic rollout-based expert supervision effectively resolves the training-inference mismatch in end-to-end driving, yielding robust gains in both in-distribution (including long-horizon) and out-of-distribution settings.

Abstract: Recent end-to-end driving systems demonstrate strong performance on closed-loop benchmarks, yet are still predominantly trained on fixed expert-collected data using open-loop imitation learning. This training-inference mismatch leaves the policy vulnerable in policy-induced states, where accumulated errors can lead to safety-critical failures. A promising post-training approach to overcome this issue is Dataset Aggregation (DAgger), which gathers expert demonstrations in policy-induced states and subsequently fine-tunes the policy on the resulting aggregated dataset. Existing driving DAgger pipelines, however, face three challenges: i) the expert is restricted to a limited trajectory-and-speed solution space, ii) takeover may occur too early or too late relative to impending failures, and iii) privileged expert decisions may rely on information unavailable to the student. To address this, we introduce RoG-DAgger, a post-training framework that uses short-horizon kinematic rollouts to construct high-quality expert demonstrations in safety-critical states. Specifically, RoG-DAgger expands the expert's trajectory-and-speed solution space and evaluates candidate plans through rollout to construct preventive supervision. Moreover, it uses rollout solvability to time the takeover near the estimated point of no return. Lastly, it aligns the expert's field of view with that of the student to provide student-compatible supervision. Across in-distribution (including long-horizon) and out-of-distribution evaluations, RoG-DAgger improves the end-to-end model SimLingo by 5.3 driving-score points and 6.2 percentage points in success rate on Bench2Drive, doubles its driving score from 22 to 44 on Longest6 v2, and improves out-of-distribution success rate from 55\% to 66\% on Fail2Drive.

</details>


### [142] [Fiber Optic Sensing Glove for High Performance Dexterous Manipulation Capture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24572)
*J. D. Peiffer,Taylor Niehues,Li Guan,Ziyi Kou,Ergys Ristani*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces a fiber optic sensing glove for full hand pose tracking using multi-core shape-sensing fibers that capture full 3D fiber shapes. A novel registration pipeline and inverse-kinematics solver reconstruct hand pose at 60 Hz, achieving 7.2 mm mean fingertip position error (4.9 mm after factory calibration) on a 2-hour dexterous manipulation dataset across 5 subjects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-based hand tracking degrades under occlusion and challenging lighting conditions, while existing sensorized gloves suffer from drift, magnetic interference, and rarely match motion-capture accuracy. The paper targets these failure modes to enable robust full hand pose tracking during dexterous manipulation.

Method: The glove uses multi-core shape-sensing fibers that capture each fiber's complete 3D shape rather than just curvature. A novel pipeline registers each reconstructed fiber shape to a common hand reference frame, and a new inverse-kinematics solver reconstructs full hand pose at 60 Hz using curve constraints. A one-time factory calibration of the fiber routing hub transfers across users and sessions.

Result: Benchmarked on a 2-hour dexterous object manipulation dataset across 5 subjects, the glove achieves 7.2 mm mean fingertip position error against motion capture ground truth, reduced to 4.9 mm after factory calibration of the fiber routing hub.

Conclusion: The system enables high-fidelity data capture and bimanual virtual teleoperation, both essential for advancing robotics research in dexterous manipulation and teleoperation applications.

Abstract: Capturing hand pose during dexterous manipulation remains difficult: vision-based methods degrade under occlusion and challenging lighting, while sensorized gloves, though occlusion-free, are prone to drift and magnetic interference and rarely match motion-capture accuracy. We introduce a fiber optic sensing glove for full hand pose tracking that targets these failure modes, using multi-core shape-sensing fibers that capture each fiber's full 3D shape rather than curvature alone. A novel pipeline registers each reconstructed fiber shape to a common hand reference frame, and a new inverse-kinematics solver reconstructs full hand pose at 60 Hz using curve constraints. Benchmarked on a 2-hour dataset of dexterous object manipulation tasks across 5 subjects, the glove achieves 7.2 mm mean fingertip position error against motion capture ground truth, reduced to 4.9 mm by a one-time factory calibration of the fiber routing hub that transfers across users and sessions. These capabilities enable high-fidelity data capture and bimanual virtual teleoperation - both essential to advancing the robotics field.

</details>


### [143] [Gripper-aware Vision Language Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24603)
*Hanyi Zhang,Zihong Luo,Tianyu Li,Khang Nguyen,Basu Hela,Shreyas Kumar,Ngoc Duy Tran,Feng Dai,Charith Munasinghe,Jorge Peña Queralta,Giovanni Toffetti,Khoa Vo,Ngan Le,Ravi Prakash,Quan Vuong,Tung D. Ta,Long Hu,Anh Nguyen,Baoru Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces MiGA, a multi-gripper-aware dataset with 103,000 demonstrations across five gripper types, and GVLA, a vision language action model with a multi-gripper tokenizer and adapter-based policy routing, demonstrating improved grasping performance, generalization, and efficient gripper adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VLAs implicitly assume gripper invariance despite grasping strategies being inherently embodiment-dependent. Different gripper types require distinct interaction strategies, yet current datasets predominantly rely on parallel-jaw grippers, limiting gripper-aware learning.

Method: MiGA dataset spans five gripper types across multiple robots with 103,000 demonstrations capturing strategy divergence. GVLA combines a multi-gripper tokenizer with adapter-based policy routing, where gripper encoding induces structured embedding information balancing parameter sharing and strategy differentiation.

Result: GVLA outperforms current baselines in both simulation and real-world experiments, improves zero-shot generalization and few-shot adaptation to new objects or unseen tasks, and enables more efficient gripper adaptation. Layer-wise probing confirms meaningful gripper-conditioned representations.

Conclusion: Gripper-aware modeling is essential for VLA-based robotic grasping, and the MiGA dataset combined with the GVLA architecture effectively addresses embodiment-dependent grasping strategy divergence while improving generalization and transferability across gripper types.

Abstract: Vision language action models (VLAs) have advanced general purpose robotic grasping and manipulation by enabling robots to interpret visual observations and natural language instructions to generate executable action sequences. However, existing VLAs often implicitly assume gripper invariance, despite grasping strategies being inherently embodiment-dependent. Different gripper types, such as parallel-jaw and suction, usually require distinct interaction strategies to achieve the same grasping objective. Moreover, current datasets for VLAs predominantly rely on parallel-jaw grippers, limiting gripper-aware learning. To address this gap, we introduce MiGA, a multi-gripper-aware dataset spanning five distinct gripper types across multiple robots with 103,000 demonstrations, explicitly capturing strategy divergence under shared task objectives. We further propose GVLA, which combines a new multi-gripper tokenizer with adapter-based policy routing. Our new gripper encoding induces structured embedding information that balances parameter sharing and strategy differentiation, while layer-wise probing confirms meaningful gripper-conditioned representations for VLAs. Intensive experiments in both simulation and real-world robots show that our GVLA outperforms the current baselines across evaluated settings. Our method also improves zero-shot generalization or few-shot adaptation to new objects or unseen tasks, and enable more efficient gripper adaptation.

</details>


### [144] [VIP: Variation-based Iterative-learning Planning for Robotic Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24618)
*Shuli Lv,Pengda Mao,Chen Min,Li Hong,Runxiao Liu,Shuai Wang,Quan Quan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper introduces VIP, a variation-based iterative-learning planning framework for motion planning in single robots and robotic swarms. Instead of optimizing discrete trajectory variables, VIP updates planning commands as continuous functions in infinite-dimensional function space, achieving O(n) per-iteration computational complexity. The framework is validated through simulations and real-world experiments across diverse planning objectives, platforms, and swarm configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional motion planning methods rely on finite-dimensional trajectory parameterization or long prediction horizons, causing rapidly growing computational costs especially in multi-robot and obstacle-dense environments. There is a need for a scalable and efficient planning framework that can operate under limited onboard computing resources.

Method: VIP formulates planning commands as continuous functions in an infinite-dimensional function space and updates them via variation-based iterative learning. The same variation-based update supports two implementation modes: model-in-the-loop for offline planning and robot-in-the-loop for online execution. This avoids computational costs from horizon expansion and high-dimensional discretization, yielding O(n) per-iteration complexity where n is the number of spatial discretization points.

Result: Extensive simulations and real-world experiments demonstrate that VIP efficiently generates and iteratively improves motion plans across different planning objectives, robotic platforms, and swarm configurations, confirming its effectiveness, computational efficiency, and scalability.

Conclusion: VIP serves as a general and scalable planning methodology that overcomes the computational limitations of conventional trajectory-parameterized approaches, making it suitable for single and multi-robot applications in large, complex environments with constrained onboard resources.

Abstract: Over the past decade, autonomous robotic systems have been increasingly deployed in applications such as surveying, search and rescue, and last-mile delivery. These applications require robots to generate safe and efficient motion plans in large, complex, and obstacle-dense environments, often under limited onboard computing resources. However, conventional planning methods commonly rely on finite-dimensional trajectory parameterization or increasingly long prediction horizons, leading to rapidly growing computational costs, particularly in multi-robot scenarios. This paper presents a novel variation-based iterative-learning planning (VIP) framework for efficient motion planning of both single robots and robotic swarms. Instead of optimizing a large number of discrete trajectory variables, VIP directly updates the planning command as a continuous function in an infinite-dimensional function space. The same variation-based update can be implemented in a model-in-the-loop manner for offline planning or in a robot-in-the-loop manner between online physical executions. By avoiding the computational burden associated with horizon expansion and high-dimensional trajectory discretization, VIP maintains a per-iteration computational complexity of $\mathcal{O}(n)$, where $n$ denotes the number of spatial discretization points. Extensive simulations and real-world experiments demonstrate that the proposed framework can efficiently generate and iteratively improve motion plans for different planning objectives, robotic platforms, and swarm configurations, highlighting its effectiveness, computational efficiency, and scalability as a general planning methodology.

</details>


### [145] [GaussianWAM: Distilling Geometry and Semantics from 3D Gaussian Fields into World-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24714)
*Zijian Zhang,Yuqing Jiang,Weitao Zhou,Minglei Li,Jinhao Zhang,Yao Mu,Xiaofan Li,Hao Zhao,Haibao Yu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GaussianWAM is a training-time framework that enhances World-Action Model (WAM) representations by organizing geometric and semantic supervision through a 3D Gaussian field, improving robotic manipulation performance without changing deployment architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: WAMs jointly learn visual prediction and action generation but their video latents are optimized for visual prediction only, lacking explicit encouragement to preserve cross-view geometric structure and spatially localized, object-relevant semantics.

Method: GaussianWAM uses synchronized multi-view observations with frozen foundation models (providing depth, camera parameters, dense semantic features) to bind heterogeneous signals to shared 3D Gaussian primitives. It renders spatially aligned semantic, depth, and coverage targets that are distilled into current-observation representations of the WAM during training. All auxiliary components (teacher models, Gaussian components, prediction heads) are removed after training.

Result: On LIBERO-Plus, FastWAM improves from 52.05% to 71.29% and Cosmos Policy from 71.52% to 77.30%. Direct CLIP/VGGT distillation gives 69.37% baseline, while Gaussian-field unification improves to 71.29%. Improvements also shown on standard LIBERO, RoboTwin, and real-world manipulation.

Conclusion: Training-time Gaussian distillation provides a practical way to inject geometry- and semantics-related supervision into WAM representations without changing their deployment architecture, demonstrating substantial performance gains across benchmarks.

Abstract: World-Action Models (WAMs) jointly learn future visual prediction and action generation, using video dynamics as a representation-learning signal for robotic manipulation. However, their video latents are primarily optimized for visual prediction and are not explicitly encouraged to preserve cross-view geometric structure or spatially localized, object-relevant semantics. We propose \textbf{GaussianWAM}, a training-time representation-enhancement framework that organizes geometric and semantic supervision through a 3D Gaussian field. Given synchronized multi-view observations, frozen geometry and vision foundation models provide depth, camera parameters, and dense semantic features. GaussianWAM binds these heterogeneous signals to shared Gaussian primitives and renders spatially aligned semantic, depth, and coverage targets, which are distilled into the current-observation representations of the WAM. All teacher models, Gaussian components, and auxiliary prediction heads are removed after training, leaving the original WAM inference path without additional modules or forward computation. On LIBERO-Plus, GaussianWAM improves FastWAM from 52.05\% to 71.29\% and Cosmos Policy from 71.52\% to 77.30\%. Direct CLIP and VGGT distillation already establishes a strong FastWAM baseline of 69.37\%, while Gaussian-field unification further improves it to 71.29\%, supporting the benefit of spatially organizing heterogeneous teacher signals. GaussianWAM also improves performance on standard LIBERO and shows positive transfer trends on RoboTwin and real-world manipulation. These results suggest that training-time Gaussian distillation provides a practical way to inject geometry- and semantics-related supervision into WAM representations without changing their deployment architecture.

</details>


### [146] [Fiber Bragg Grating Whiskers for Bioinspired Hydrodynamic Perception on Underwater Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24724)
*Hao Li,Tianyu Tu,Siyue Yao,Ziyang Chang,Juhyun Jung,Xiaochi Xie,Long Yin Chung,Tian-Ao Ren,Genliang Chen,Mark Cutkosky*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper presents compact fiber Bragg grating (FBG) whiskers inspired by harbor seal vibrissae for underwater robots. The whiskers, featuring non-uniform taper and elliptical cross-section like seal whiskers, demonstrate monotonic flow sensing from 0.1–0.6 m/s, reduced self-induced oscillations versus cylindrical designs, and the ability to detect vortices from pitching foils seconds after passage. A single front-mounted whisker enabled a small underwater robot to correctly distinguish turning from straight movement in 17/20 trials (85%) using only whisker signals, connecting bioinspired hydrodynamic sensing to autonomous robot action.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Harbor seals can passively perceive and track moving targets in dark or turbid water using vibrissae that sense hydrodynamic trails. The authors aim to translate this biological capability into a compact, practical sensor for underwater robots, enabling them to perform passive hydrodynamic detection and tracking where vision and sonar may be limited.

Method: The researchers designed compact fiber Bragg grating (FBG) whiskers modeled after seal vibrissae, incorporating a non-uniform taper and elliptical cross-section for flow noise suppression. They characterized the whiskers through controlled towing experiments across flow speeds (0.1–0.6 m/s), compared self-induced vibrations against a cylindrical baseline, and examined angle-of-attack dependence. They also conducted pitching-foil experiments to test vortex detection, followed by behavioral trials where a single front-mounted whisker on a small underwater robot classified straight vs. turning trajectories from whisker signals alone.

Result: The whiskers exhibited monotonic relative-flow response over 0.1–0.6 m/s, significantly reduced self-induced oscillation relative to a cylindrical baseline, and pronounced sensitivity to angle of attack. They successfully detected characteristic vortices shed by both stationary and moving pitching foils, with detection possible several seconds after the source passed. In robot trials, a single whisker enabled correct branch selection (straight vs. turn) in 17 of 20 trials (85.0%) using whisker signals exclusively.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate that bioinspired FBG whiskers can effectively bridge passive hydrodynamic sensing and autonomous robot action, proving useful for tracking underwater objects in visually obscured environments. The work supports the viability of whisker-based hydrodynamic perception as a practical sensing modality for marine robotics.

Abstract: Harbor seals track hydrodynamic trails with their vibrissae, enabling passive perception of moving targets in dark or turbid water. Inspired by this capability, we present compact fiber Bragg grating (FBG) whiskers for underwater robots. Like seal whiskers, they have a non-uniform taper and elliptical cross-section. Controlled towing experiments show a monotonic relative-flow response from 0.1 to 0.6 m/s, a strong reduction of self-induced oscillation relative to a cylindrical baseline, and a pronounced dependence on angle of attack. Experiments with a pitching foil show that the whiskers can detect the characteristic vortices shed by a stationary or moving source, detectable several seconds after the source has passed. Using this information, a single front-mounted whisker enabled a small underwater robot to distinguish between continuing straight and executing a turn, selecting the correct branch in 17 of 20 trials (85.0%) from whisker signals alone. These results connect bioinspired hydrodynamic sensing to robot action and suggest the utility of whiskers for tracking underwater objects.

</details>


### [147] [One-Shot Learning from Demonstration of Contact-Rich Robotic Manipulation by Identifying Physical Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24741)
*A. H. G. Overbeek,H. van der Kooij,M. Vlutters*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a Learning from Demonstration (LfD) method that explicitly models physical interactions (when and where contact is made/broken) between a robot and its environment, enabling reproduction of contact-rich manipulation tasks using a hybrid position-force controller. The method achieves robust, generalizable, and adaptive task reproduction from a single demonstration without prior task knowledge, demonstrated on real robot tasks like door opening, lock opening, bolt picking, screwing, dislodging, and surface contouring.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most LfD methods do not explicitly model physical interactions (e.g., making and breaking of contact) between a robot and its environment, despite these being crucial during manipulation tasks. Since the same basic physical interactions recur often, they can serve as a basis for robust, generalizable, and adaptive task reproduction.

Method: The proposed LfD method explicitly encodes what physical interactions take place, where they occur, and when they occur from demonstrations. A hybrid position-force controller tracks demonstrated trajectories until contact-based transition conditions derived from the demonstrations are satisfied.

Result: Evaluated on real robot experiments including opening doors and locks, bolt picking and screwing, dislodging, and surface contouring. The method provides four benefits: (1) reproduction of complex, sequential, contact-rich manipulation tasks from a single demonstration with no prior task knowledge; (2) robustness to unknown geometric variations; (3) generalization when geometric variations are known; and (4) online adaptation using geometric information explored during task reproduction.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling physical interactions benefits LfD in robustness, generalization, and adaptivity, which are generally lacking in the LfD literature. The work aims to close a gap in interpretable few-shot LfD of robotic manipulation.

Abstract: Learning from Demonstration (LfD) allows robots to learn manipulation tasks directly from humans, thereby supporting the versatile application of robots. Most LfD methods do not explicitly model the physical interactions between a robot and its environment, such as the making and breaking of contact, while these are crucial during manipulation tasks. Because the same basic physical interactions recur often, they can be a basis for robust, generalizable, and adaptive task reproduction. We propose an LfD method that explicitly uses what physical interactions take place where and when. Using that information, a hybrid position-force controller tracks demonstrated trajectories until contact-based transition conditions from the demonstrations are met. We evaluate our method in real robot experiments consisting of opening doors and locks, bolt picking and screwing, dislodging, and surface contouring. We show that explicitly modeling physical interactions benefits LfD in four ways. First, by allowing reproduction of complex, sequential, and contact-rich manipulation tasks using only a single demonstration and no prior knowledge of the task. Second, by facilitating robustness to unknown geometric variations in the environment. Third, by facilitating generalization when geometric variations are known. Fourth, by facilitating online adaptation using geometric information explored during task reproduction. We discuss how robustness, generalization, and adaptivity can be explicitly implemented, which is generally lacking in the LfD literature. Thereby, our work aims to close a gap in interpretable few-shot LfD of robotic manipulation.

</details>


### [148] [Latent Action as Intention Enables Efficient Future Imagination for World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24882)
*Xiang Li,Yupeng Zheng,Songen Gu,Huailiang Ma,Feng Yu,Xian Nie,Shanshuai Yuan,Yujie Zang,Weize Li,Shuai Tian,Moyang Liu,Ya-Qin Zhang,Wenchao Ding*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: LAWA is a world action model architecture that uses compact latent actions as an operational representation of future intentions, enabling efficient test-time future imagination without generating future observations. It achieves state-of-the-art success rates on RoboCasa (65.6% few-shot, 80.8% full data), improves over the Fast-WAM baseline by 9.6 and 4.5 points respectively, matches Joint-WAM performance while reducing inference latency by 42.9%, and demonstrates competitive robustness on LIBERO-Plus and real-world tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World action models improve robot control via future observation modeling, but generating future observations at test time incurs high latency. Fast-WAM removes this for efficiency but suffers from lower generalization, especially with scarce demonstrations and out-of-distribution scenarios. The paper aims to bridge the gap between efficiency and generalization by retaining future imagination in a lightweight form.

Method: LAWA introduces a WAM architecture that employs compact latent actions as an operational representation of future intentions. It uses a discrete tokenizer enhanced by action-free pre-training to produce manipulation-centric codebook targets. The model jointly denoises a continuous latent state anchored to these targets along with executable action chunks, while omitting the future-video branch at inference time to reduce latency.

Result: On RoboCasa, LAWA achieves state-of-the-art average success rates of 65.6% and 80.8% in few-shot and full data settings, improving over Fast-WAM by 9.6 and 4.5 points respectively. It preserves performance comparable to Joint-WAM while reducing inference latency by 42.9%. LAWA also shows competitive zero-shot robustness on LIBERO-Plus and superior performance on real-world tasks.

Conclusion: Future imagination does not need to be discarded for efficiency. Retaining it through compact latent actions provides an effective trade-off among performance, generalization, and latency, making LAWA a compelling approach for robot control with world action models.

Abstract: World action models (WAMs) improve robot control by modeling how observations evolve, but generating future observations at test time incurs substantial latency. Fast-WAM removes this process for efficiency; however, our matched implementations show lower generalization for Fast-WAM than for future-aware alternatives, especially with scarce robot demonstrations and in out-of-distribution scenarios. To bridge this gap, we introduce **LAWA**, a WAM architecture that uses compact latent actions as an operational representation of future intentions, enabling efficient test-time future imagination without generating future observations. Specifically, a discrete tokenizer enhanced by action-free pre-training produces manipulation-centric codebook targets. LAWA jointly denoises a continuous latent state anchored to these targets with executable action chunks while omitting the future-video branch at inference. On RoboCasa, LAWA achieves state-of-the-art average success rates of 65.6% and 80.8% in the few-shot and full data settings, improving over the matched Fast-WAM baseline by 9.6 and 4.5 points, respectively. It also preserves the performance level of the matched Joint-WAM variant while requiring 42.9% lower inference latency. LAWA also demonstrates competitive zero-shot robustness on LIBERO-Plus and superior performance on real-world tasks. These results show that future imagination need not be discarded: retaining it with compact latent actions yields an effective trade-off among performance, generalization, and latency. Code and models will be released.

</details>


### [149] [Do Robotic World Models Really Follow Actions? Diagnosing and Aligning Action-Conditioned Generation for Policy Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24885)
*Sixiang Chen,Jiaming Liu,Jixian Wu,Yichen Guo,Tinghao Wang,Siyuan Qian,Hao Chen,Jiajun Cao,Jian Tang,Shanghang Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces WorldEcho, a benchmark for evaluating action-conditioned world models on off-expert action following using visual integrity and SE(3) trajectory alignment, and WorldSync, a training method that improves action following through distributional coverage, representational grounding, and intervention-effect alignment, demonstrating improved simulation reliability for policy learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Action-conditioned world models assume their generated futures faithfully reflect arbitrary valid actions, but existing benchmarks only evaluate on expert demonstrations, leaving off-expert action following inadequately assessed. This unverified assumption undermines the reliability of world models as learned simulators for policy evaluation and improvement.

Method: The paper proposes (1) WorldEcho, a diagnostic benchmark that probes action following over a broader action distribution using visual integrity checks and SE(3) trajectory alignment, and (2) WorldSync, a training approach with three complementary axes: distributional coverage (broadening the training distribution over action consequences), representational grounding (grounding intermediate video representations in action-induced robot dynamics via an Action-Forcing Expert), and intervention-effect alignment (aligning predicted changes under action interventions with ground-truth future changes).

Result: Diagnosis shows current world models execute expert actions reasonably but struggle with diverse off-expert trajectories, either ignoring commanded actions or producing visually invalid rollouts. Experiments on RoboTwin benchmarks and real-robot tasks show WorldSync improves WorldEcho metrics and serves as a more reliable simulator for iterative policy improvement, enabling higher success rates.

Conclusion: WorldEcho reveals the gap between expert and off-expert action following in current world models, and WorldSync effectively addresses this gap, producing world models that serve as more reliable learned simulators for iterative policy improvement with higher policy success rates.

Abstract: Action-conditioned world models are increasingly used as learned simulators for policy evaluation and improvement, yet their effectiveness rests on an unverified assumption: generated futures faithfully reflect arbitrary valid actions. Existing benchmarks are typically confined to expert demonstrations, leaving off-expert action following inadequately evaluated. To address this gap, we introduce WorldEcho, which probes action following over a broader action distribution using visual integrity and SE(3) trajectory alignment. Our diagnosis shows that current world models reasonably execute expert actions but struggle with diverse off-expert trajectories, either ignoring the commanded actions or producing visually invalid rollouts. We further propose WorldSync, which strengthens action following along three complementary axes: distributional coverage, representational grounding, and intervention-effect alignment. It broadens the training distribution over action consequences, grounds intermediate video representations in action-induced robot dynamics through an Action-Forcing Expert, and aligns predicted changes under action interventions with the corresponding changes in ground-truth futures. Experiments on RoboTwin benchmarks and real-robot tasks show that WorldSync improves WorldEcho metrics and serves as a more reliable simulator for iterative policy improvement, enabling policies to achieve higher success rates.

</details>
